In return, Bijapur granted the Portuguese a factory at the port of Dabhol with extraterritorial jurisdiction — a precedent for later European “factory” privileges in Mughal ports.

The Ahmadnagar Sultanate, under the Nizam Shahi dynasty, pursued a more confrontational stance. Sultan Burhan Nizam Shah II (r. 1591‑1595) attempted to blockade Portuguese shipping by fortifying the island of Janjira and allying with the Ottoman admiral Kurtoğlu Muslihiddin. The Portuguese countered by forging an alliance with the Maratha chieftain Maloji Bhosale, who provided light cavalry for raids on Ahmadnagar’s coastal supply lines. This triangular contest culminated in the 1600 Portuguese‑Maratha joint operation that captured the fort of Revdanda, securing the northern Konkan coast for Portuguese commerce.

Cultural and technological transfers accompanied these political manoeuvres. Portuguese shipwrights introduced the “caravel” hull design to Deccan shipyards at Chaul, enabling the construction of larger, ocean‑going vessels that could carry heavier artillery. Conversely, Deccan metallurgists supplied high‑quality wootz steel for Portuguese cannon barrels, a trade documented in the “Livro das Armadas” of 1558. The exchange of gunpowder recipes — particularly the use of saltpetre from the Coromandel coast — accelerated the adoption of more reliable artillery across the Deccan Sultanates.

By the 1630s, the Mughal annexation of Ahmadnagar (1636) and the decline of Bijapur’s independence altered the balance. The Portuguese shifted their diplomatic focus to the Mughal court, securing a farman from Shah Jahan in 1638 that confirmed their trading rights at Surat and exempted them from the “rahdari” (road toll) on inland routes. Nevertheless, the legacy of the earlier Deccan‑Portuguese engagements persisted in the fortified coastal settlements, the hybrid Indo‑Portuguese naval architecture, and the continued flow of Deccan textiles through Goa to Lisbon — a commercial circuit that survived well into the eighteenth century.

Mughal Peasant Differentiation under the Zabt System: From Raiyat to Zamindar

The Mughal zabt revenue system, codified in the Ain‑i‑Akbari, classified cultivators into a hierarchy that shaped rural power relations for centuries. At the base stood the “raiyat” (peasant cultivator) who held a hereditary right (khudkasht) to till a specific plot, paying a fixed share of the produce (usually one‑third to one‑half) as land revenue (mal). Above the raiyat were the “khudkasht” holders with larger holdings, often employing hired labour (majurs) during peak seasons. The “zamindar” occupied the next tier: a local notable who collected revenue from a cluster of villages (pargana) on behalf of the state, retaining a “zamindari” share (typically 10‑15 % of the jama) as remuneration.

Revenue assessment relied on the “dahsala” method — a ten‑year rolling average of crop yields and prices — which aimed to smooth fluctuations but also entrenched the position of zamindars who could manipulate local yield reports (kamin) to reduce the assessed jama. The “amil” (revenue officer) and the “qanungo” (hereditary record‑keeper) jointly verified the “paimaish” (measurement) of each field, yet the qanungo’s hereditary tenure often aligned his interests with the zamindar, creating a collusive network that could under‑report cultivated area.

Peasant differentiation intensified under Aurangzeb’s Deccan campaigns. The constant demand for cash to fund the army led to the expansion of “ijara” (revenue farming), where the right to collect revenue was auctioned to the highest bidder — often a wealthy zamindar or a moneylender (sahukar). The ijara‑holder extracted surplus beyond the state’s fixed demand, pushing many raiyats into indebtedness. Those who defaulted lost their khudkasht rights and became “paikast” (tenant‑at‑will) or landless labourers, a process documented in the “Bengal Revenue Records” of the 1680s where the proportion of paikast cultivators rose from 12 % to 27 % within two decades.

Women’s roles in this hierarchy are visible in the “stridhana” (women’s property) registers that survived in Rajasthan and Gujarat. Widowed raiyat women could retain cultivation rights if they paid the revenue, but zamindari claims often superseded these rights, especially when the zamindar invoked the “haq‑i‑zamindari” to seize the land for arrears. The Mughal court occasionally intervened — Akbar’s 1582 farman protected widows from summary eviction — yet enforcement depended on local officials who were frequently zamindars themselves.

The long‑term effect of this differentiation was the emergence of a semi‑feudal agrarian structure that persisted into the colonial period. The Permanent Settlement of 1793 essentially codified the zamindar’s revenue‑collecting role into a proprietary right, while the raiyat’s customary occupancy was reduced to a tenancy at will. Understanding the Mughal zabt’s internal logic — its reliance on measurement, its delegation to zamindars, and its vulnerability to revenue‑farming — is essential for analysing both pre‑colonial agrarian unrest (e.g., the 1669 Jat revolt) and the colonial transformation of land tenure.

  • Confusing the Gujarat Sultanate’s iqta grants with Mughal jagir assignments; the former were revenue‑sharing contracts, the latter were salary‑linked military tenures.
  • Assuming Maratha artillery was entirely imported; indigenous foundries at Pune and Satara produced the bulk of zamzama and farangi guns.
  • Treating Vijayanagara temple grants as purely religious endowments; they carried explicit fiscal obligations and state audit mechanisms.
  • Believing Portuguese cartazes were voluntary trade licences; they were enforced by naval patrols and non‑compliance invited seizure of vessels.
  • Equating the Mughal raiyat’s hereditary right with full ownership; the state retained ultimate title and could reassign land for revenue arrears.
  • Overlooking the role of the qanungo in colluding with zamindars to under‑report yields, a structural feature that amplified peasant indebtedness.

Mughal Harem Politics: The Influence of Royal Women on Statecraft

The Mughal harem was not merely a domestic enclosure but a structured political institution that channelled the influence of royal women into the machinery of empire. From the reign of Akbar, the harem acquired a formal hierarchy headed by the chief queen (padshah begum) and managed by a corps of eunuchs, female attendants, and a separate treasury. This arrangement allowed women of the imperial household to intervene in appointments, revenue assignments, and diplomatic correspondence, especially when the emperor was campaigning or incapacitated. The harem’s political weight derived from its control over the emperor’s personal seal and its access to the inner council (diwan‑i‑khas), where petitions could be presented without the mediation of the wazir.

Nur Jahan, the favourite wife of Jahangir, exemplifies the apex of harem power. After her marriage in 1611 she secured the title “Nur Jahan Begum” and effectively co‑ruled for over a decade. She issued farmans in her own name, struck coins bearing her legend, and directed the appointment of governors and mansabdars through her brother Asaf Khan and her father Itimad-ud-Daula. Her influence extended to foreign policy: she negotiated the 1615 treaty with the Portuguese at Daman and mediated the succession crisis after Jahangir’s death, backing Shah Jahan against his rival Shahryar. Contemporary chroniclers such as Mutamad Khan record that “the empire’s reins lay in her hands” during Jahangir’s frequent illnesses.

Jahanara Begum, daughter of Shah Jahan, wielded a different but equally potent form of authority. As the senior-most princess after Mumtaz Mahal’s death, she managed the imperial household’s vast endowments, supervised the construction of the Shahjahanabad canals, and acted as the emperor’s confidante during the war of succession (1657‑58). Her diplomatic letters to the Safavid court and to the Deccan sultanates reveal a nuanced grasp of geopolitics. After Aurangzeb’s accession she retained her stipend and the title “Padshah Begum”, using her wealth to patronise Sufi shrines and to mediate disputes among nobles, thereby preserving a channel of soft power within an increasingly orthodox court.

The institutional backbone of harem politics rested on the office of the chief eunuch (khwaja‑sara), who controlled access to the emperor and managed the harem’s finances. The eunuch’s loyalty was secured through jagir grants and the right to collect customs at designated ports. The harem also maintained its own intelligence network (barids) that reported on noble factions, provincial unrest, and market prices. This information flow allowed royal women to advise the emperor on military campaigns and revenue reforms, as seen in Nur Jahan’s advocacy for the reduction of the land revenue demand in the Punjab during a famine.

By the late seventeenth century the harem’s direct political role waned. Aurangzeb’s puritanical policies curtailed the public visibility of royal women, and the rise of powerful noble factions (the Irani, Turani, and Deccani groups) shifted decision‑making to the wazir and the bakhshi. Nevertheless, the precedent of female participation in governance persisted in the princely states of the nineteenth century, where begums such as Qudsia Begum of Bhopal exercised sovereign authority. The Mughal harem thus represents a distinctive model of gendered power that operated within, rather than outside, the formal structures of the empire.

Mughal Legal Institutions: Qazi, Mufti, and the Adalat System

The Mughal judicial framework combined Islamic jurisprudence (fiqh) with indigenous customary law (riwaj) to create a layered system of courts that functioned from the capital down to the village. At the apex stood the emperor as the supreme judicial authority (sultan‑i‑adalat), who could hear appeals and issue binding decrees (farmans). Below him, the chief qazi (qazi‑ul‑quzzat) appointed by the emperor oversaw the hierarchy of provincial qazis, each responsible for a subah. The qazi’s jurisdiction covered civil disputes (marriage, inheritance, contracts) and certain criminal matters (theft, assault) governed by the Hanafi school, which the Mughals adopted as the official madhhab.

The qazi was assisted by a mufti, a legal scholar tasked with issuing fatwas (legal opinions) on points of law referred by the qazi or by private litigants. The mufti did not adjudicate but provided the interpretive basis for the qazi’s judgment. In practice, the mufti’s opinion carried great weight because the qazi was expected to follow the consensus (ijma) of recognized jurists. The office of the sadr‑us‑sudur, the chief religious endowments officer, supervised the appointment of qazis and muftis, ensuring that they met the educational standards of madrasas such as those at Delhi, Agra, and Lahore.

At the provincial level, the faujdar (military governor) and the kotwal (city magistrate) exercised criminal jurisdiction, especially over offences against public order (highway robbery, rebellion). The faujdar’s court (faujdari adalat) applied a mixture of Islamic penal law (hudud, ta‘zir) and local customary punishments. The kotwal maintained urban policing, regulated markets, and presided over summary trials for minor infractions. Both officers reported to the subahdar, who could confirm or modify sentences, creating a dual track of military and judicial authority that the Mughals inherited from the Delhi Sultanate.

Village‑level justice was dispensed by the panchayat (council of elders) under the supervision of the qanungo (hereditary record‑keeper) and the patwari (village accountant). The panchayat applied riwaj, which varied regionally but generally recognised the rights of cultivators (raiyats) and the obligations of zamindars. The Mughal state encouraged this decentralized adjudication because it reduced the burden on imperial courts and legitimised local power structures. The Ain‑i‑Akbari records that Akbar issued a dastur‑ul‑amal (administrative manual) instructing qazis to respect riwaj where it did not contradict sharia, a principle that later British administrators codified in the Anglo‑Muhammadan law.

The integration of Islamic and customary law produced a pragmatic legal pluralism that sustained Mughal rule across diverse societies. However, the system’s reliance on personal appointment of qazis and the absence of a codified statute book meant that judicial outcomes could vary widely. The eighteenth‑century fragmentation saw the emergence of regional courts (e.g., the Nizam’s adalat in Hyderabad) that retained the Mughal structure but adapted it to local political realities. The British East India Company’s early judicial reforms (Regulating Act 1773, Cornwallis Code 1793) deliberately borrowed the Mughal distinction between civil (diwani) and criminal (faujdari) jurisdiction, underscoring the lasting institutional legacy of the Mughal adalat system.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Which Mughal officer was primarily responsible for issuing fatwas that guided the qazi's judgment?" Many candidates answer "qazi" or "faujdar". Identify the correct officer and explain why the others are incorrect.
The correct officer is the mufti. The mufti was a legal scholar whose sole function was to provide authoritative legal opinions (fatwas) on points of Islamic law referred by the qazi or private parties. The qazi adjudicated cases but was expected to follow the mufti’s fatwa; the faujdar was a military‑cum‑police magistrate dealing with criminal order, not legal interpretation. The sadr‑us‑sudur supervised appointments but did not issue fatwas. Therefore, only the mufti fits the description.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question lists several Mughal officials, first match each to its core function: qazi = judge, mufti = legal opinion, faujdar = criminal/military police, kotwal = urban magistrate, sadr‑us‑sudur = religious endowments. Eliminate options that conflate judicial and executive roles. This functional mapping works for both direct‑fact and assertion‑reason questions.

Maratha Confederal Diplomacy: Treaties with the Mughals, Nizam, and European Powers

The Maratha confederacy conducted diplomacy through a blend of personal emissaries (vakils), formal treaties (sanads), and the strategic use of chauth‑sardeshmukhi claims as diplomatic leverage. After Shivaji’s death, the Peshwa’s secretariat (Huzur Daftar) institutionalised treaty‑making, maintaining a registry of engagements with the Mughal emperor, the Nizam of Hyderabad, the Portuguese at Goa, and later the British East India Company. The 1665 Treaty of Purandar, imposed by Jai Singh I, forced Shivaji to cede twenty‑three forts and accept a mansab of 5,000, yet it also recognised Maratha control over the Konkan coast — a precedent for later negotiations where territorial concessions were traded for revenue rights.

In the early eighteenth century, the Marathas exploited Mughal weakness after Aurangzeb’s death. The 1718 Treaty of Delhi (also called the Maratha‑Mughal settlement) granted the Marathas the right to collect chauth and sardeshmukhi from six Deccan subahs in return for a nominal tribute. The Nizam‑ul‑Mulk, seeking to secure his own position, signed the 1728 Treaty of Mungi Shevgaon, ceding the chauth of the Carnatic to the Marathas while retaining administrative control. These agreements were recorded in Persian and Marathi, sealed with the Peshwa’s signet, and enforced by Maratha cavalry stationed at strategic forts such as Pune, Satara, and Gwalior.

European powers entered Maratha diplomacy as both rivals and allies. The 1739 Treaty of Bassein with the Portuguese confirmed Maratha possession of Salsette and Bassein in exchange for a yearly payment, while the 1775 Treaty of Surat with the British recognised the Peshwa’s claim to the revenues of Surat and Broach. The 1782 Treaty of Salbai ended the First Anglo‑Maratha War, restoring the status quo ante bellum and guaranteeing Maratha control over territories north of the Narmada. The decisive 1802 Treaty of Bassein (with the British) turned the Peshwa into a subsidiary ally, allowing British troops to be stationed in Poona and effectively ending the confederacy’s independent foreign policy.

Internal confederacy diplomacy was equally critical. The Peshwa acted as primus inter pares among the great houses — Scindia of Gwalior, Holkar of Indore, Bhonsle of Nagpur, and Gaekwad of Baroda. The 1795 Treaty of Pune attempted to regulate the distribution of chauth among these chiefs, but rivalries over the control of the Mughal emperor (the “emperor‑making” policy) led to the 1795‑96 civil war between Scindia and Holkar. The Peshwa’s inability to enforce a unified diplomatic line weakened the confederacy’s bargaining position vis‑à‑vis the British, who exploited these divisions through the subsidiary alliance system.

By the 1818 Treaty of Poona, the Maratha confederacy was dissolved, its territories annexed or placed under princely states. The diplomatic corpus — over two hundred sanads, farmans, and correspondence — survives in the Peshwa Daftar at Pune and the British India Office Records. These documents illustrate a sophisticated diplomatic culture that combined Mughal ceremonial forms (khilat, nazar) with Maratha fiscal concepts (chauth, sardeshmukhi) and adapted to the treaty‑based international law introduced by European powers.

Vijayanagara's Overseas Connections: Trade and Cultural Exchange with Southeast Asia

Vijayanagara’s maritime outreach was anchored in a network of west‑coast ports — Bhatkal, Honavar, Mangalore, and the coastal port of Goa — that linked the empire’s agrarian surplus and textile production to the Indian Ocean trading system. The state maintained a modest navy (the “kadal‑pattam”) primarily for anti‑piracy patrols and for escorting merchant convoys to the Malabar coast. Revenue from port duties (sungam) and the royal monopoly on pepper and cardamom financed the navy and contributed to the imperial treasury, as recorded in the inscriptions of the Tuluva period.

Trade with Southeast Asia flourished from the late fifteenth to the mid‑sixteenth century. Vijayanagara exported cotton textiles (especially the famed “calico” from the Coromandel), indigo, and iron ore to ports such as Malacca, Aceh, and Ayutthaya. In return, the empire imported aromatic woods (sandalwood, eaglewood), Chinese ceramics, and Persian horses trans‑shipped through Malacca. The Portuguese capture of Goa in 1510 initially disrupted this circuit, but the Vijayanagara rulers negotiated a 1518 agreement allowing Portuguese ships to load pepper at Bhatkal in exchange for a fixed customs share, thereby preserving a degree of commercial autonomy.

Cultural exchange accompanied commerce. Sanskrit inscriptions dated to the reign of Krishnadevaraya have been found in Java and Bali, attesting to the movement of Brahmins and scribes. The architectural vocabulary of Vijayanagara’s temples — particularly the use of the gopura and the mandapa — influenced the temple complexes of the Majapahit successor states. Conversely, Southeast Asian motifs such as the kala‑makara doorway appear in the sculptural programme of the Vitthala temple at Hampi, suggesting a two‑way flow of artistic ideas mediated by merchant guilds (nanadesis) operating across the Bay of Bengal.

The Portuguese presence introduced new naval technology (carracks, lateen rigs) and cartographic knowledge that Vijayanagara’s shipwrights adopted for their own coastal vessels. However, the 1565 Battle of Talikota and the subsequent sack of Hampi severed the empire’s central coordination of maritime policy. The successor Nayaka states (Madurai, Tanjavur, Ikkeri) continued individual trade relations with the Portuguese and the Dutch, but the unified imperial naval strategy collapsed. By the early seventeenth century, the English and Dutch East India Companies had established factories at Masulipatnam and Pulicat, redirecting the bulk of Coromandel textile exports away from the traditional Vijayanagara‑controlled routes.

Despite the political fragmentation, the legacy of Vijayanagara’s overseas connections persisted in the continued use of the “Vijayanagara peso” (a gold coin standard) in the Malabar‑Southeast Asian trade well into the eighteenth century. The empire’s integration of agrarian revenue, port customs, and a state‑supported merchant marine represents an early example of a South Indian polity actively shaping Indian Ocean commerce rather than merely reacting to it.

Delhi Sultanate Urban Planning: The Foundation and Layout of Sultanate Capitals

The Delhi Sultanate’s capital cities were deliberately engineered to project sovereign authority, accommodate a growing military‑administrative elite, and control strategic trade routes. Qutb al‑Din Aibak (r. 1206‑10) established the first sultanate capital at Mehrauli, centred on the Qutb Minar complex. The layout followed a citadel‑mosque‑market triad: the fortified citadel (Lal Kot/Qutb complex) housed the royal palace and treasury; the Quwwat‑ul‑Islam mosque symbolised Islamic legitimacy; and the surrounding bazaar (the “chahar‑suq”) linked the city to the imperial highway, facilitating revenue collection from the Doab.

Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296‑1316) responded to Mongol pressure and population growth by founding Siri, a new fortified city northeast of Mehrauli. Siri’s plan incorporated a massive rectangular wall with seven gates, a central palace complex (the “Hazar Sutun”), and a dedicated garrison area for the standing army (the “lashkar”). The city’s water supply was secured by the Hauz‑i‑Alai reservoir, fed by a canal from the Yamuna, demonstrating an early integration of hydraulic engineering into urban design. The market reforms of Alauddin were enforced through the “shahna‑i‑mandi” (market superintendent) stationed in Siri’s main square.

Muhammad bin Tughluq (r. 1325‑51) undertook the most ambitious — and controversial — urban experiment by ordering the transfer of the capital to Daulatabad (Deogir) in the Deccan. The new capital, renamed “Tughluqabad”, was laid out on a grid with a massive stone fort, a congregational mosque, and a planned residential sector for the transplanted Delhi elite. The forced migration (the “Daulatabad experiment”) aimed to consolidate control over the newly conquered southern territories but resulted in massive demographic loss and economic disruption, leading to the capital’s return to Delhi within a decade.

Firuz Shah Tughluq (r. 1351‑88) shifted focus to public works and welfare architecture. He founded Firuzabad on the banks of the Yamuna, incorporating a network of canals (the “Yamuna‑Firuz” canal) that irrigated the surrounding countryside and supplied the city’s numerous gardens (bagh), hospitals (dar‑ul‑shifa), and madrasas. The city’s layout emphasized axial symmetry: the royal palace faced the Jami Masjid, while the “Kushk‑i‑Firuz” (pavilion) overlooked a large artificial lake. Firuz Shah’s building inscriptions claim the construction of 1,200 gardens, 200 towns, and 30 reservoirs, underscoring the sultanate’s capacity for large‑scale urban infrastructure.

The Lodi period (1451‑1526) saw a return to a more modest urban scale. Sikandar Lodi established Agra as a secondary capital, laying out the “Lodi Gardens” as a funerary complex that doubled as a public space. The Lodi sultans fortified the existing Delhi walls, added the “Bara‑Gumbad” and “Shish‑Gumbad” tombs, and reorganised the city’s wards (mohallas) around craft guilds. By the time Babur captured Delhi in 1526, the sultanate’s urban legacy — citadel‑mosque‑market complexes, hydraulic works, and planned residential wards — provided a template that the early Mughals adapted and expanded in their own capital projects at Agra, Fatehpur Sikri, and Shahjahanabad.

  • Confusing the harem’s informal influence with formal institutional authority; the harem never held a constitutional role.
  • Attributing the issuance of fatwas to the qazi rather than the mufti; the qazi adjudicates, the mufti opines.
  • Assuming Maratha treaties were always honoured; many were repudiated when military circumstances changed.
  • Overstating Vijayanagara’s naval power; its fleet was coastal and defensive, not a blue‑water force.
  • Believing the Daulatabad transfer succeeded; it was abandoned within ten years due to logistical failure.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common misconceptions.

Mughal Military Logistics in the Deccan: Banjara Caravans, Supply Depots, and the Campaigns of Aurangzeb

The protracted Deccan campaigns of Aurangzeb (1681‑1707) exposed the limits of a centrally‑controlled supply chain. The Mughal army, often exceeding 200,000 combatants plus camp followers, depended on a hybrid logistics network that combined state‑run depots (faujdari‑khana) with the private enterprise of Banjara grain‑carriers. Banjaras, organised into tanda (caravan) units of 500‑1,000 bullock‑carts, contracted directly with the imperial commissariat (mir‑i‑saman) to move wheat, barley, and fodder from the fertile Doab and Malwa to forward bases at Burhanpur, Aurangabad, and Jinji. Each tanda carried a sealed manifest (parwana) specifying quantity, destination, and a fixed price (zabt‑rate) that insulated the state from market spikes.

State depots were positioned at strategic river crossings — notably at the Tapti near Burhanpur and the Godavari near Paithan — where grain was stored in mud‑brick silos (kothi) protected by a garrison of 200‑300 matchlockmen. The depots operated on a “first‑in‑first‑out” rotation to minimise spoilage, and their accounts were audited quarterly by the diwan‑i‑tan (revenue auditor). When the monsoon disrupted river transport, the Banjaras shifted to overland routes through the Satpura passes, a maneuver that added 15‑20 days to delivery but kept the army fed during the 1689‑90 siege of Bijapur.

The system’s fragility lay in its reliance on a single commodity corridor. Maratha light cavalry (bargirs) repeatedly intercepted Banjara convoys, forcing the Mughals to allocate 10‑15 percent of their cavalry to escort duty. Aurangzeb’s 1695 order to fortify the “Banjara Road” with a series of watch‑towers (burj) and to impose a toll (rahdari) on all non‑imperial traffic reduced losses by an estimated one‑third, yet the overall cost of logistics consumed nearly 40 percent of the Deccan expedition’s annual budget, contributing to the fiscal strain that precipitated the jagirdari crisis of the 1700s.

The Maratha Dak System: Postal Relays, Intelligence Gathering, and Administrative Communication

Shivaji’s creation of a dedicated postal network (dak) in the 1660s gave the Maratha state a communication speed unmatched by contemporary Indian powers. The system rested on a chain of relay stations (dak‑chowki) spaced roughly 12‑15 km apart along the main arteries linking Raigad, Pune, Satara, and the coastal forts of Kolaba and Suvarnadurg. Each chowki maintained a complement of two mounted couriers (dak‑sawar) and a foot‑messenger (paik) who could traverse the ghats during monsoon when horses were unusable. Messages were written on palm‑leaf folios (patra) sealed with the royal mudra, and a time‑stamp (ghadi) recorded at every handover, allowing the Peshwa’s secretariat to calculate transit times with an accuracy of ±30 minutes over a 300 km stretch.

Beyond official orders, the dak carried intelligence reports (khabar) from village headmen (patil) and spies (gupte) embedded in Mughal and Bijapur camps. The Peshwa’s “Khabar‑Navis” office at Pune compiled these into daily digests (roz‑nama) that informed strategic decisions such as the timing of the 1674 raid on Surat. The network also facilitated revenue administration: assessment registers (mali‑patra) travelled from the district (pargana) to the central treasury (raj‑khazana) within a fortnight, enabling the swift adjustment of chauth demands.

Under the Peshwas (1713‑1761) the dak was professionalised. A salaried cadre of “dak‑adhyaksha” oversaw station masters, standardized the breed of Maratha horses (the “Deccani” stallion), and introduced a coded cipher (sanket) for sensitive diplomatic correspondence with the Nizam and the English at Bombay. The system’s reach extended to the Carnatic, where Maratha agents at Tanjore and Madurai used the same relay logic to coordinate the 1740‑41 campaigns. By the 1750s the dak comprised over 1,200 stations, a density that allowed a message from Pune to reach Delhi in 12‑14 days — a speed the Mughal imperial post (barid) could not match after the 1739 Nadir Shah invasion disrupted its northern routes.

Technique: Comparative Speed Analysis
A question asks: “Compare the average transmission speed of the Maratha dak (c. 1750) with the Mughal barid (c. 1650) for a 1,200 km distance. Which system was faster and by how many days?”
The Maratha dak covered ~1,200 km in 12‑14 days (average 13 days). The Mughal barid, using horse‑relay stations every 8‑10 km, averaged 20‑22 days for the same distance in the mid‑17th century. Therefore the Maratha dak was faster by roughly 7‑9 days. The answer should cite the relay spacing, the use of foot‑messengers in ghats, and the coded cipher that reduced handling time.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a quantitative comparison, first identify the two systems, then note the key variables: relay distance, messenger type, and administrative overhead. Write the formula “days = distance ÷ (relay‑speed × efficiency‑factor)” in the margin; plugging the textbook‑standard values yields the answer quickly and shows the examiner you grasp the mechanics, not just the narrative.

Ahom‑Mughal Diplomatic Encounters: The Treaty of Ghilajharighat and Its Aftermath

The Ahom kingdom’s resistance to Mughal expansion in the Brahmaputra valley culminated in the 1663 Treaty of Ghilajharighat, negotiated between Ahom king Jayadhwaj Singha and Mughal governor Mir Jumla. The treaty obliged the Ahoms to cede the western territories up to the Barnadi River, pay an indemnity of 300,000 rupees and 20 elephants, and accept a Mughal thana (garrison) at Guwahati. Crucially, the Ahoms retained internal sovereignty, their own paiks (militia) and the hereditary office of the Borphukan, which the Mughals recognised as a “zamindari‑type” authority.

Mir Jumla’s subsequent death (1663) and the Mughal preoccupation with the Deccan wars allowed the Ahoms to regroup. Under king Chakradhwaj Singha (r. 1663‑1670) the Ahoms launched a counter‑offensive, recapturing Guwahati in 1667 and expelling the Mughal thana. The Mughal response, led by Raja Ram Singh, ended in the decisive Ahom victory at Saraighat (1671), where the Ahom navy — a fleet of 2,000 war‑boats (bachari) armed with swivel‑guns — exploited the river’s narrow channels to neutralise Mughal artillery.

The diplomatic fallout reshaped Mughal frontier policy. Emperor Aurangzeb, recognising the impossibility of a permanent garrison in the malaria‑prone valley, shifted to a “tribute‑only” model: the Ahoms sent annual presents (nazrana) of gold, musk, and elephants to Delhi, while the Mughals refrained from stationing troops east of the Barnadi. This arrangement persisted until the 1760s, when the Burmese invasions forced the Ahoms to seek British assistance, ending the unique Mughal‑Ahom diplomatic equilibrium.

Mughal Bengal Administration: The Diwan‑i‑Bengal, Zamindari Settlements, and Revenue Farming

Bengal’s integration into the Mughal fiscal apparatus after Akbar’s 1576 conquest created a distinctive administrative template that blended the imperial diwan’s oversight with the pre‑existing zamindari hierarchy. The Diwan‑i‑Bengal, headquartered at Dhaka (later Murshidabad), supervised a two‑tier revenue collection: the “zabt” cash assessment on directly‑managed khalisa lands, and the “ijara” (revenue‑farming) contracts granted to zamindars and taluqdars for the remaining parganas. Zamindars, often former Afghan or Bengali chieftains, received a sanad (patent) confirming hereditary rights in return for a fixed annual peshkash (tribute) calibrated to the Ain‑i‑Akbari’s dahsala averages.

The revenue‑farming system incentivised zamindars to expand cultivation; they advanced seed‑loans (taqavi) to peasants (raiyats) and constructed irrigation bunds (bandh) that increased the measured area (bigha) by up to 15 percent in the 1620s. However, the system also generated friction: the imperial diwan periodically revised the peshkash upward (the “re‑assessment” of 1658‑59 under Shah Shuja), prompting zamindar revolts such as the 1660 uprising of the Bara Bhuiyans. The Mughal response combined military suppression with the appointment of loyal faujdars who oversaw the collection of the “mal‑guzari” (land‑tax) directly from villages, bypassing recalcitrant intermediaries.

By the late 17th century, Bengal’s revenue yield — estimated at 12‑13 million rupees annually — financed a significant portion of the imperial army’s Deccan expenditures. The province’s monetary surplus also attracted European factories; the English East India Company’s 1698 farman granted them duty‑free trade in return for a fixed annual payment of 3,000 rupees, a precedent that later underpinned the 1765 Diwani grant. The administrative continuity from Akbar’s zabt to the Nawabi “mal‑guzari” illustrates how Mughal fiscal institutions adapted to regional agrarian structures while preserving central revenue claims.

The Mughal Imperial Household Treasury: The Khazana, the Diwan‑i‑Khas, and the Mir Bakhshi’s Fiscal Role

Beyond the public exchequer (khazana‑i‑amm), the Mughal emperors maintained a private treasury (khazana‑i‑khas) that financed the imperial household, the harem, the royal workshops (karkhana), and personal military retinues (ahadis). The Diwan‑i‑Khas, a senior finance minister distinct from the Diwan‑i‑Aam (revenue minister), administered this private purse. He received daily cash inflows from the “sair‑jihat” (customs on luxury imports), the “zar‑i‑zamin” (gold‑mine royalties from Kolar and Hutti), and the “nazrana” (tribute) presented by provincial governors on accession and festivals.

The Mir Bakhshi, traditionally the head of the military establishment (mir‑i‑arzi), also exercised fiscal authority over the household cavalry (ahadis) and the imperial guard (khass‑daran). His office audited the pay‑rolls (tankhwa) of these troops, which were drawn from the khazana‑i‑khas rather than the jagirdari assignments. This dual control created a checks‑and‑balances mechanism: the Diwan‑i‑Khas could not disburse funds without the Mir Bakhshi’s certification of troop strength, while the Mir Bakhshi could not recruit beyond the budget sanctioned by the Diwan‑i‑Khas.

Under Jahangir (1605‑27) the khazana‑i‑khas financed the construction of the Shalimar Gardens and the patronage of European painters, while Shah Jahan (1628‑58) expanded it to fund the Taj Mahal’s marble procurement from Makrana. Aurangzeb, however, curtailed household expenditure after 1680, redirecting surplus to the Deccan war chest; the Diwan‑i‑Khas’s annual accounts (bahi‑khata) show a 30 percent decline in “karkhana” outlays between 1680 and 1700. The household treasury thus functioned as a flexible fiscal reservoir, enabling emperors to bypass the rigid jagirdari system for prestige projects and emergency military needs.

  • Confusing the Banjara caravan contracts with the state‑run faujdari depots; they operated under different accounting systems.
  • Assuming the Maratha dak used only horse‑mounted couriers; foot‑messengers were essential in the Western Ghats during monsoon.
  • Treating the Treaty of Ghilajharighat as a permanent Mughal annexation; it was a temporary cession later reversed by Ahom counter‑offensives.
  • Equating the Diwan‑i‑Bengal’s ijara contracts with the later Permanent Settlement; the Mughal system retained periodic re‑assessment.
  • Believing the Mir Bakhshi controlled the entire imperial treasury; his fiscal remit was limited to the household cavalry and guard.
  • Overstating the khazana‑i‑khas’s independence; it remained subordinate to the emperor’s direct orders and the Diwan‑i‑Khas’s audit.
  • Neglecting the role of the “nazrana” in linking provincial loyalty to household finance; it was a key revenue stream for the private purse.

These advanced sections illustrate how logistical, communicative, diplomatic, fiscal, and household mechanisms intertwined to shape the political economy of medieval India.

The Mughal Provincial Governors: Subahdars, Faujdars, and the Balance of Central Control

The Mughal empire divided its territory into subahs (provinces), each headed by a subahdar (also called nazim) appointed directly by the emperor. The subahdar combined military, judicial, and fiscal authority: he commanded the provincial garrison, supervised the faujdars (district‑level military governors), and oversaw revenue collection through the diwan of the subah. The emperor retained leverage through the mansabdari system — subahdars held high mansabs (often 5,000‑7,000 zat) and were frequently transferred to prevent the growth of regional power bases. The faujdar administered a sarkar (district), maintaining law‑and‑order, collecting the mal‑guzari from villages, and reporting to the subahdar. In practice, the balance between central control and provincial autonomy shifted with the emperor’s strength. Under Akbar and Jahangir, frequent rotations and the presence of imperial waqia‑navis (news‑writers) kept governors accountable. Under Aurangzeb, the prolonged Deccan wars forced the emperor to grant longer tenures and larger jagirs to subahdars, which eroded central oversight and contributed to the emergence of semi‑independent successor states such as Bengal, Awadh, and Hyderabad.

Mechanisms of Oversight

  • Imperial news‑writers (waqia‑navis): stationed in each subah, they sent daily reports to the mir bakhshi and the emperor, creating an information loop independent of the governor.
  • Periodic audits: the diwan‑i‑ala dispatched revenue auditors (amils) to verify the zabt assessments and the jama (actual collection) against the dahsala averages.
  • Mansab transfers: a subahdar’s mansab could be increased, decreased, or reassigned at the emperor’s discretion, making the post a conditional privilege rather than a hereditary right.

Limits of Central Authority

Despite these mechanisms, the vast distances and the reliance on local intermediaries (zamindars, qanungos) meant that subahdars often negotiated revenue settlements that deviated from the imperial zabt norms. In Bengal, the diwan‑i‑Bengal operated with considerable autonomy, and the nawab of Bengal eventually transformed the subahdar’s office into a hereditary governorship. The Mughal inability to enforce uniform fiscal standards across all subahs illustrates the structural tension between a centralized bureaucratic ideal and the realities of early modern governance.

The Maratha Ashta Pradhan: Evolution of the Council of Ministers under Shivaji and the Peshwas

Shivaji’s coronation in 1674 formalised an eight‑member council known as the Ashta Pradhan (eight ministers), each assigned a distinct portfolio that mirrored the military‑administrative needs of a nascent guerilla state. The Peshwa (prime minister) coordinated overall policy; the Mujumdar (finance minister) managed revenue, treasury, and accounts; the Senapati (commander‑in‑chief) oversaw the army; the Sachiv (secretary) handled correspondence and royal decrees; the Mantri (chronicler) maintained records of the king’s daily activities; the Sumant (foreign minister) conducted diplomacy; the Nyayadhish (chief justice) administered justice; and the Panditrao (ecclesiastical head) supervised religious endowments and ceremonies. Under the Peshwa era (1713‑1818), the council’s composition shifted: the Peshwa became the de facto hereditary head of state, the Mujumdar evolved into a more sophisticated revenue bureaucracy managing chauth and sardeshmukhi collections, and the Senapati’s role diminished as the confederacy’s military command devolved to regional sardars (Holkar, Scindia, Bhonsle). The Sachiv and Mantri merged into a secretariat that issued sanads and managed the dak (postal) network. The Sumant negotiated treaties with the Mughals, the Nizam, and European powers, while the Nyayadhish presided over a hybrid legal system blending dharmaśāstra principles with customary Maratha law. The Panditrao’s influence waned as the state secularised its fiscal operations, though he retained ceremonial duties at the Satara court.

Institutional Continuities and Changes

  • Merit‑based appointments: Shivaji selected ministers on competence rather than birth; the Peshwas initially continued this but later allowed hereditary succession in key posts (e.g., the Bhat family for the Peshwaship).
  • Revenue integration: The Mujumdar’s office standardised the assessment of chauth (one‑fourth of revenue) and sardeshmukhi (additional ten percent) across the confederacy, creating a fiscal framework that bound the sardars to the central treasury.
  • Diplomatic professionalization: The Sumant maintained a corps of vakils (envoys) stationed at Mughal, Nizam, and European courts, enabling real‑time intelligence gathering.

Decline of the Council’s Cohesion

After the Third Battle of Panipat (1761), the Peshwa’s authority fragmented; the Ashta Pradhan became a ceremonial body while real power resided with the great sardars. The British exploited this disunity, negotiating separate treaties with each sardar, effectively bypassing the council. By 1818, the Ashta Pradhan ceased to function as an executive organ, surviving only as a symbolic remnant in the princely state of Satara.

Examiner's technique
Question: Explain how the Ashta Pradhan's structure reflected the Maratha state's military‑administrative priorities during Shivaji's reign and how it changed under the Peshwas.
Answer: Shivaji designed the Ashta Pradhan to centralise command of a guerilla polity: the Peshwa coordinated strategy, the Senapati led mobile cavalry, the Mujumdar secured revenue from chauth/sardeshmukhi to fund campaigns, and the Sumant managed alliances with Mughal and European powers. The Nyayadhish and Panditrao provided legal and religious legitimacy. Under the Peshwas, the council became more bureaucratic: the Peshwa turned hereditary, the Mujumdar expanded a professional revenue staff, the Senapati’s field command passed to regional sardars, and the Sumant institutionalised a resident envoy system. The shift mirrors the transition from a personalist warrior state to a confederacy requiring fiscal administration and diplomacy.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for “evolution” or “change over time”, structure the answer in two clear phases (Shivaji → Peshwa) and use a table or bullet list to contrast portfolios, appointment methods, and functional scope. Examiners reward explicit comparison over narrative description.

The Deccan Sultanates' Administrative Framework: The Wazir, the Diwan, and the Provincial Governors

The five Deccan Sultanates — Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, Golconda, Bidar, and Berar — shared a Persianate bureaucratic template but adapted it to local conditions. At the apex stood the wazir (prime minister), who supervised the central diwan (revenue department) and the mir bakhshi (military paymaster). The diwan maintained the jama (assessed revenue) and hasil (actual collection) registers, employing qanungos (hereditary revenue accountants) to measure cultivated area (bigha) and apply the zabt‑style cash assessment where feasible. In the fertile Krishna‑Godavari delta, Golconda’s diwan implemented a detailed kankut (crop‑cut) survey, while in the arid zones of Ahmadnagar the batai (crop‑sharing) method prevailed. Provincial governance was entrusted to tarafdars (governors of tarafs or districts), who held military commands and collected revenue through amirs and jagirdars. The tarafdar reported to the wazir but often enjoyed de facto autonomy, especially in Bijapur where the tarafdar of the Karnatak region minted his own copper coins. The sultan’s court also maintained a barid (intelligence) network of news‑writers (waqia‑navis) mirroring the Mughal system, ensuring the centre received independent reports on provincial governors’ loyalty and fiscal performance.

Fiscal Instruments and Coinage

  • Standardised silver rupee: Golconda and Bijapur issued rupees of 11.6 g silver (≈97 % purity) aligned with the Mughal rupiya, facilitating inter‑regional trade.
  • Revenue farming (ijara): The diwan auctioned revenue contracts to ijaradars for a fixed sum, transferring collection risk to private financiers.
  • Land grants (inam, madad‑i‑ma'ash): Tax‑free grants to scholars, Sufi shrines, and military officers created a class of intermediaries who mediated between the state and peasantry.

Military‑Administrative Integration

The mir bakhshi coordinated the mansabdari-like ranking of nobles (amirs) who received jagirs in lieu of cash salaries. However, unlike the Mughals, the Deccan sultans frequently granted hereditary jagirs to secure loyalty, which over time weakened central fiscal control. The adoption of gunpowder artillery (see “Gunpowder Fortifications in the Deccan Sultanates”) required a specialised topkhana (artillery corps) directly funded from the central treasury, bypassing the jagirdari system. This dual military financing — jagir‑based cavalry and treasury‑funded artillery — characterised Deccan military administration until the Mughal conquests of the late seventeenth century.

The Ahom Military Organization: The Paik System, the Barphukan, and the Naval Forces on the Brahmaputra

The Ahom kingdom (1228‑1826) sustained a unique military‑labour system called the paik system, which mobilised every able‑bodied male for state service. Each paik was assigned to a khel (guild) based on hereditary occupation — infantry, artillery, boat‑building, or logistics. The paik owed a fixed period of labour (usually four months annually) to the state, during which he served in the army, constructed roads, or manned the riverine fleet. The highest military commander was the Barphukan, stationed at Guwahati, who commanded the western frontier and the Brahmaputra naval flotilla. The Barphukan coordinated with the Burhagohain (prime minister) and the Borgohain (commander of the eastern forces) to execute a defence‑in‑depth strategy against Mughal incursions. The Ahom navy, composed of bachhari (small war‑canoes) and larger panchgarhia vessels armed with swivel guns, exploited the Brahmaputra’s seasonal floods to launch amphibious raids on Mughal supply lines. During the 1671 Battle of Saraighat, the Ahom fleet under Lachit Borphukan (a later Barphukan) used night‑time boarding actions and fire‑ships to neutralise the larger Mughal war‑boats, securing a decisive victory.

Logistics and Intelligence

  • Paik rotation: The rotational labour ensured a standing reserve without a permanent standing army, reducing fiscal burden.
  • Signal system: A network of hilltop watchtowers (dangaria) relayed smoke‑by‑day and fire‑by‑night signals across the valley, providing early warning of enemy movements.
  • Supply depots: Granaries (kothas) stocked rice and salt at strategic points along the river, enabling rapid mobilisation.

Adaptation to Gunpowder

From the late sixteenth century, the Ahoms imported matchlock muskets (tufang) and cast bronze cannons with Portuguese assistance. The paik guilds incorporated a dedicated topkhana (artillery corps) trained by European gunners. This integration allowed the Ahoms to besiege Mughal forts such as Itakhuli (1682) using combined infantry‑artillery tactics, demonstrating the system’s flexibility.

The Mughal Educational and Scholarly Networks: Madrasas, Libraries, and the Translation Bureau (Maktab Khana)

Mughal patronage of learning created a layered educational infrastructure that linked imperial authority with Persianate intellectual culture. At the apex stood the Maktab Khana (translation bureau) established by Akbar in 1574 at Fatehpur Sikri. Headed by the Sadr‑us‑Sudur, it employed scholars fluent in Sanskrit, Arabic, Persian, and Turkish to translate key texts — the Mahabharata (as Razmnama), the Ramayana, the Atharvaveda, and works on astronomy, medicine, and philosophy — into Persian. The bureau’s output legitimised Mughal rule by presenting Indian knowledge within the imperial lingua franca and provided reference works for administrators. Below the bureau, a network of madrasas (colleges) operated in major cities: Delhi, Agra, Lahore, Ahmadabad, and Burhanpur. Each madrasa followed the dars‑i‑nizami curriculum — Quranic exegesis (tafsir), Hadith, jurisprudence (fiqh), logic (mantiq), and mathematics (hisab). Endowments (waqf) from nobles and the imperial treasury funded stipends (wazifa) for students and salaries for teachers (mudarris). The imperial library (kitabkhana) at Agra and later at Shahjahanabad housed thousands of manuscripts, catalogued by the musahib‑i‑kitabkhana. Emperors personally inspected acquisitions; Jahangir’s memoirs record his commissioning of illustrated copies of the Akbarnama and the Tuzuk‑i‑Jahangiri. The library also served as a scriptorium where calligraphers (katib) produced luxury manuscripts for diplomatic gifts.

Regional Language Development

While Persian remained the administrative language, Mughal courts encouraged vernacular literary production. In the Deccan, the Dakhni dialect flourished under Bijapur and Golconda patronage, producing poets such as Wali Muhammad Wali. In Bengal, the puthi tradition (Bengali Islamic literature) received support from local governors. The translation bureau’s Sanskrit‑Persian projects indirectly stimulated bilingual scholarship, enabling later figures like Dara Shikoh to compose the Majma‑ul‑Bahrain (The Confluence of Two Oceans), a comparative study of Vedantic and Sufi metaphysics.

Institutional Continuities and Decline

  • Waqf resilience: Even after imperial patronage waned under Aurangzeb’s austerity, many madrasas survived on waqf income, preserving the scholarly network into the eighteenth century.
  • British appropriation: The East India Company’s College of Fort William (1800) modelled its Persian department on the Mughal madrasa curriculum, employing former Mughal mudarris as professors.
  • Manuscript dispersal: The 1739 Nadir Shah sack of Delhi scattered the imperial library’s holdings; many manuscripts entered European collections, shaping early Orientalist scholarship.
  • Confusing the subahdar’s fiscal role with the diwan’s; the subahdar supervised but did not directly assess revenue.
  • Assuming the Ashta Pradhan remained a functional executive council throughout the Peshwa era; after 1761 it became largely ceremonial.
  • Treating the Deccan Sultanates’ wazir as equivalent to the Mughal vakil; the wazir’s authority varied widely among the five sultanates.
  • Overlooking the rotational nature of the Ahom paik system and equating it with a standing professional army.
  • Believing the Maktab Khana translated only religious texts; its portfolio included scientific, medical, and historical works.
  • Neglecting the role of waqf endowments in sustaining madrasas after imperial funding declined; they were the primary financial backbone.
  • Each of these traps reflects a common misreading of institutional nuance that examiners target in analytical questions.

Mughal Imperial Workshops (Karkhanas) and the Production of Luxury Textiles for Export

The Mughal state maintained a network of imperial workshops known as karkhanas that produced luxury textiles, metalwork, arms, and manuscripts for the court and for export. Under Akbar the system was formalised: each karkhana was headed by an ustad (master craftsman) who supervised a hierarchy of shagirds (apprentices) and karigars (skilled artisans). The most prestigious textile karkhanas were located at Agra, Fatehpur Sikri, Lahore, and Ahmedabad. They specialised in zari (gold‑thread) brocades, khaddar (fine cotton), velvet (makhmal), and carpet weaving (especially the qaleen of Kashmir and the durrie of Agra).

Production was organised on a piece‑rate basis but the state supplied raw materials — silk from Bengal, cotton from Gujarat, wool from the Punjab, and gold thread from the imperial mint. The diwan‑i‑buyutat (store‑keeper) recorded inputs and outputs, ensuring that a fixed proportion of output was earmarked for the imperial wardrobe (khilat) and the remainder could be sold through the sahukar (merchant) network at Surat, Cambay, and later at the English and Dutch factories. Contemporary European accounts (e.g., Tavernier, 1665) note that Mughal brocades fetched prices in Europe comparable to Persian and Ottoman silks, making textiles a major component of the Mughal export surplus.

Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan the karkhanas expanded: the karkhana‑i‑khass (royal workshop) at Agra employed over 4,000 artisans at its peak, and the karkhana‑i‑firozi (turquoise‑glazed pottery) at Lahore supplied the court and the Ottoman market. The emperor personally inspected samples; Jahangir’s memoirs record his commissioning of a jama (robe) woven with a new buta (paisley) motif that later became a hallmark of Indian textile design. Revenue from the karkhanas was accounted in the ain‑i‑akbari as part of the mal‑i‑khas (imperial private income), distinct from land revenue.

The system began to contract under Aurangzeb’s austerity measures. The emperor reduced the number of state‑run workshops, transferring many to private sahukars who operated on a contract (ijara) basis. This shift lowered quality control and contributed to the loss of the European market to cheaper Persian and Ottoman imitations by the early eighteenth century. Nevertheless, the technical repertoire — especially the jamdani muslin of Dhaka and the kashida embroidery of Kashmir — survived in regional workshops and later fed the British East India Company’s textile procurement.

The Sikh Khalsa's Institutionalisation under Guru Gobind Singh and the Formation of the Dal Khalsa

Guru Gobind Singh’s creation of the Khalsa on 30 March 1699 at Anandpur Sahib transformed the Sikh community from a devotional fellowship into a corporate martial order. The ceremony introduced the five Kakars — kesh (uncut hair), kangha (comb), kara (steel bracelet), kirpan (sword), and kachera (cotton breeches) — as visible markers of identity and discipline. The Guru also promulgated the Rehat Maryada (code of conduct), which prescribed daily prayers (nitnem), prohibition of tobacco and halal meat, and the obligation to defend the oppressed.

To operationalise the Khalsa as a fighting force, Guru Gobind Singh organised the Dal Khalsa (the Army of the Pure) into a confederacy of misls (brigades), each led by a sardar chosen on merit rather than heredity. The first major commander was Banda Singh Bahadur, who captured Sirhind (1710) and established a short‑lived Sikh administration in the Punjab. After Banda’s execution (1716), the misls operated semi‑independently, controlling territories from the Sutlej to the Indus. They maintained a shared treasury (khalsa) at Amritsar, funded by dasvandh (tithe) contributions from the faithful and by plunder from Mughal and Afghan campaigns.

The Dal Khalsa’s decision‑making body was the Sarbat Khalsa, a biannual assembly held at the Akal Takht where sardars resolved disputes, allocated resources, and elected a Jathedar (supreme commander) for joint operations. This institutional framework allowed the Sikhs to survive repeated Mughal and Durrani invasions (e.g., the 1762 Vadda Ghalughara) and to re‑consolidate under Maharaja Ranjit Singh in the early nineteenth century. The Khalsa’s emphasis on collective leadership, codified discipline, and a distinct religious‑military identity distinguishes it from contemporary Rajput or Maratha confederacies.

Source Correlation Technique
A question asks: "Explain how the institutional features of the Khalsa under Guru Gobind Singh differed from the Mughal mansabdari system in terms of recruitment, loyalty, and revenue extraction."
The Khalsa recruited volunteers bound by a religious oath (Amrit Sanskar), whereas mansabdars were appointed by the emperor and held rank (zat/sawar) contingent on imperial favour. Khalsa loyalty derived from shared faith and the collective Sarbat Khalsa, while mansabdar loyalty was personal to the emperor and enforced through jagir transfers. Revenue for the Khalsa came from dasvandh and war booty, managed communally; mansabdars extracted revenue from assigned jagirs, remitting a portion to the centre. The answer must contrast the corporate, faith‑based cohesion of the Khalsa with the hierarchical, state‑centric mansabdari.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When comparing institutions, tabulate three dimensions — recruitment, loyalty mechanism, revenue flow — and cite one primary source for each (e.g., Guru Gobind Singh’s Hukamnamas for Khalsa; Ain‑i‑Akbari for mansabdari). This structure earns full marks for analytical depth.

Mughal Environmental Management: Forests, Hunting Reserves, and Waterworks

The Mughal state treated natural resources as strategic assets. Imperial hunting reserves (shikargahs) such as the Bari Doab and the Kashmir valley were demarcated by boundary pillars (nishan) and guarded by shikaris (gamekeepers). These reserves supplied the court with game, falcons, and, crucially, timber for shipbuilding at the Surat and Chittagong yards. The Ain‑i‑Akbari lists over 120 species of trees protected in the khalsa forests, with penalties for unauthorised felling.

Water management was central to agrarian expansion. Akbar’s finance minister, Raja Todar Mal, commissioned the Nahr‑i‑Bihisht (Canal of Paradise) in the Punjab, diverting the Ravi to irrigate the doab between the Beas and Sutlej. Shah Jahan extended the Western Yamuna Canal (originally built by Firoz Shah Tughluq) to feed the Shahjahanabad gardens and the Red Fort moat. The canals were maintained by a cadre of mutsaddis (engineers) funded from the diwan‑i‑khalisa. Revenue records show a 15‑20 % increase in assessed produce (dahsala) in villages served by these canals.

Forest and water policies also served military logistics. The Grand Trunk Road was lined with sarais (caravanserais) and baolis (step‑wells) spaced at 10‑12 km intervals, ensuring water for cavalry horses and infantry. During Aurangzeb’s Deccan campaigns, the state requisitioned teak from the Malwa and Berar forests for artillery carriages and fort repairs at Golconda and Bijapur. The depletion of these forests by the late seventeenth century forced the Mughals to import timber from the Konkan coast, raising campaign costs.

Environmental stewardship thus intersected with fiscal and military imperatives. The decline of centralized oversight after 1707 led to unchecked deforestation, siltation of canals, and a measurable drop in agricultural yields recorded in the zabt surveys of the 1720s.

The Mughal Empire's Diplomatic Exchanges with the Ottoman and Safavid Courts

Mughal diplomacy with the Ottoman Empire and the Safavid Persia was shaped by competing claims to Islamic leadership and by the control of overland trade routes. Akbar dispatched an embassy to Istanbul in 1576 led by Hakim Humam, presenting a khilat and a letter asserting the Mughal emperor’s title Padshah‑i‑Islam. The Ottomans, under Murad III, responded with a ceremonial robe but avoided recognising Mughal caliphal pretensions, reflecting the Ottoman claim to the universal caliphate.

Relations with the Safavids were more volatile. The Safavid Shah Tahmasp I had granted refuge to Humayun (1544), and Akbar reciprocated by sending a mission to Qazvin in 1562. However, sectarian differences — Mughal Sunni orthodoxy versus Safavid Twelver Shi‘ism — and rivalry over Kandahar (captured by Akbar in 1595, lost to Shah Abbas I in 1622, retaken by Shah Jahan in 1638) kept the frontier tense. Diplomatic gifts included Persian manuscripts, Mughal miniatures, and horses; the exchange of artists (e.g., the Persian painter Abd al‑Samad at the Mughal atelier) facilitated the diffusion of the Safavid‑Mughal painting style.

Both courts used the hajj caravan as a diplomatic channel. Mughal nobles financed the Mahmal (ceremonial litter) sent annually to Mecca, which carried letters to the Sharif of Mecca and, indirectly, to Istanbul. The Safavids similarly sponsored caravans, and the Mughal‑Safavid competition for the Sharif’s favour influenced the flow of pilgrims and the distribution of khilafat titles.

These exchanges had concrete economic effects. Ottoman‑Mughal treaties (e.g., the 1612 agreement) secured safe passage for Indian textiles through Aleppo to Mediterranean markets, while Safavid control of the Silk Road segments forced Mughal merchants to rely on the maritime route via Surat and Hormuz. The diplomatic correspondence, preserved in the Maktubat‑i‑Akbari and the Ottoman Mühimme Defterleri, provides a rare window into early modern Muslim‑Muslim statecraft.

The Ahom Kingdom's Cultural Synthesis: Assamese Language, Vaishnavism, and Court Ritual

The Ahom rulers, originally Tai‑speaking migrants from present‑day Yunnan, engineered a deliberate cultural synthesis that legitimised their rule over the Brahmaputra valley. By the early sixteenth century the court adopted Assamese as the administrative language, replacing the Tai Ahom script with the Assamese script for royal edicts (raj‑patra) and land grants (patta). The Buranjis (chronicles) were composed in Assamese prose, preserving both Tai genealogies and local histories.

The pivotal religious transformation came through the patronage of the Vaishnava saint‑reformer Srimanta Sankardev (1449‑1568) and his disciple Madhavdev. The Ahom king Suhungmung (r. 1497‑1539) granted land (satras) for monastic centres (namghar) that became hubs of devotional music (borgeet), dance (sattriya), and manuscript copying. These institutions integrated tribal communities (e.g., the Morans, the Kacharis) into a shared religious framework, reducing ethnic friction.

Court ritual blended Tai coronation rites (Singarigharutha) with Hinduised ceremonies. The king performed the Deul‑puja at the Kamakhya temple, offered the Homa (fire sacrifice) in the Tai tradition, and distributed prasad to both Brahmin priests and tribal elders. The annual Bihu festival was institutionalised as a state event, with the royal treasury funding communal feasts and the distribution of gamocha (traditional cloth).

This synthesis produced a resilient political culture. When the Mughals under Mir Jumla invaded (1662), the Ahom nobility mobilised under a shared Assamese‑Vaishnava identity, enabling the guerrilla defence that culminated in the Treaty of Ghilajharighat (1663). The cultural cohesion forged through language, religion, and ritual thus underpinned both internal stability and external resistance.

  • Confusing the karkhana system with the later British factory system; the former was state‑run, the latter private‑capital.
  • Assuming the Khalsa’s Sarbat Khalsa functioned as a permanent legislature; it was an ad‑hoc assembly convened only twice a year.
  • Attributing all Mughal canal construction to Akbar; major extensions occurred under Shah Jahan and Aurangzeb.
  • Treating Mughal‑Ottoman diplomacy as a formal alliance; it remained ceremonial with no binding military commitments.
  • Equating Ahom adoption of Assamese with wholesale abandonment of Tai identity; the Tai language persisted in royal genealogies and military terminology.
  • Overlooking the role of dasvandh in funding the Dal Khalsa; many answers cite only plunder.
  • Believing the Mughal shikargahs were purely recreational; they were also strategic timber reserves for naval construction.

The advanced sections above integrate institutional mechanics, diplomatic nuance, and cultural synthesis to meet the analytical depth required for high‑stakes examination answers.

Alauddin Khalji's Market Reforms and Price Control Mechanism

Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296‑1316) instituted a comprehensive set of market regulations aimed at stabilising prices in Delhi and the surrounding doab, thereby ensuring a reliable supply of grain, cloth, and other essentials for his standing army. The reforms were administered through a newly created department, the Diwan‑i‑Riyasat, headed by a Shahna‑i‑Mandi (market superintendent) who reported directly to the sultan. Fixed prices were announced for wheat, barley, rice, pulses, sugar, ghee, oil, salt, and a range of textiles; the rates were set well below prevailing market levels and were enforced by a network of barids (intelligence agents) and muhtasibs (market inspectors).

To prevent hoarding, the state established state‑run granaries (karkhanas) at strategic points such as Delhi, Kara, and Multan. Peasants were compelled to sell a prescribed portion of their harvest to the state at the fixed price, while merchants were required to obtain licences (parwanas) and to maintain stipulated stocks. Violations attracted severe penalties: confiscation of goods, fines, imprisonment, and in extreme cases corporal punishment. The sultan also regulated the weight and measure standards, introducing the gaz‑i‑Sikandari for length and the ser‑i‑Shahi for weight, which were periodically verified by royal officials.

The fiscal impact was twofold. First, the army’s procurement costs fell dramatically, allowing Alauddin to maintain a large cavalry (estimated at 475,000 horsemen) without proportionate increases in land revenue demand. Second, the policy created a surplus in the state treasury that financed his Deccan campaigns and the construction of the Alai Darwaza and the Hauz‑i‑Alai. However, the system relied on a highly centralised bureaucracy and on the sultan’s personal oversight; after his death the price controls eroded rapidly under the Tughlaqs, who lacked the administrative capacity to sustain the inspection network.

Historians note that the reforms were not purely benevolent; they served a military‑fiscal logic that prioritised the sultan’s war machine over peasant welfare. The forced sale of grain at below‑market rates depressed rural incomes, and the licensing regime stifled private trade. Nonetheless, the experiment remains a rare pre‑modern instance of state‑directed price administration on a large scale, and it is frequently cited in examinations as an example of “market regulation” versus “free market” in medieval Indian economic history.

Mughal Mansabdari System: Evolution from Akbar to Aurangzeb

The mansabdari system was the backbone of Mughal military‑civil administration, assigning every noble a dual rank: zat (personal status and salary) and sawar (number of cavalrymen to be maintained). Introduced by Akbar in 1574‑75, the initial scale ranged from 10 to 5,000 zat, with the highest grades reserved for princes and senior amirs. The zat determined the noble’s position in the court hierarchy and his entitlement to a jagir (revenue assignment), while the sawar fixed the contingent size and the quality of horses required.

Under Akbar, the system was relatively flexible: promotions were based on merit, loyalty, and battlefield performance. The dagh (branding) and chehra (muster roll) inspections ensured that the stipulated troop numbers were actually present. Jahangir retained the structure but introduced the du‑aspah sih‑aspah innovation, allowing a noble to hold a higher sawar than his zat would normally permit, in exchange for a larger revenue assignment. This measure expanded the cavalry pool without inflating the zat hierarchy.

Shah Jahan formalised the mansab‑i‑khas (special mansabs) for trusted commanders and linked jagir transfers more tightly to the zabt revenue assessments, reducing the scope for arbitrary assignments. He also increased the proportion of tankhwah (cash salary) paid from the imperial treasury, partly to curb the growing tendency of mansabdars to extract excess revenue from their jagirs.

Aurangzeb’s long Deccan wars strained the system. The number of mansabdars swelled to over 1,600, and the average zat fell as the emperor granted lower ranks to accommodate the expanding officer corps. The jagirdari crisis intensified: revenue yields from the Deccan were uncertain, and many mansabdars received “paper jagirs” that could not be collected. To compensate, Aurangzeb increased the sawar obligations without raising zat, leading to widespread under‑maintenance of contingents. The dagh inspections became perfunctory, and the cavalry’s combat effectiveness declined.

By the early eighteenth century the mansabdari framework had lost its integrative function. Regional governors (subahdars) began to treat their assignments as hereditary fiefs, and the central treasury could no longer enforce the chehra‑dagh regime. The system’s evolution illustrates how a centrally designed military‑bureaucratic instrument can adapt — and eventually fracture — under fiscal pressure and territorial overexpansion.

Examiner's Trap: Confusing Zat and Sawar
A question asks: “In the Mughal mansabdari system, which rank determined the number of cavalry a noble was required to maintain?” Many candidates answer “zat”.
The correct answer is sawar. Zat fixed the noble’s personal status and salary; sawar fixed the contingent size. Remember: zat = personal rank, sawar = cavalry obligation. The dual‑rank structure is a favourite testing point; always pair the two concepts in your notes.
Answer‑Writing Strategy
When a question asks for “evolution”, structure the answer chronologically: Akbar’s foundation → Jahangir’s du‑aspah sih‑aspah → Shah Jahan’s tankhwah emphasis → Aurangzeb’s crisis. Use a two‑column table (Emperor / Key Change) in the margin of your answer sheet; examiners reward clear comparative frameworks.

Vijayanagara's Nayankara System and the Role of the Nayakas

The Nayankara system was the principal mechanism through which the Vijayanagara state (c. 1336‑1646) delegated military and administrative authority to regional commanders known as nayakas. Originating under the Sangama dynasty, the institution matured under the Saluva and Tuluva rulers, reaching its zenith during the reign of Krishnadevaraya (r. 1509‑1529). A nayaka received a nayankara (territorial grant) in return for maintaining a stipulated number of troops, usually cavalry and infantry, and for forwarding a fixed share of the agrarian revenue to the central treasury.

Unlike the Mughal mansabdari, the nayankara was often hereditary in practice, especially in the southern provinces (Tamil Nadu, Karnataka). The nayaka exercised near‑autonomous judicial, fiscal, and military powers within his grant, including the right to mint low‑denomination coins, to levy local market taxes, and to administer temples. The central court retained symbolic suzerainty through the Mahanayakacharya (chief of nayakas) and through periodic darshan ceremonies where nayakas presented tribute.

The system facilitated rapid mobilisation: during Krishnadevaraya’s campaigns against the Deccan Sultanates, the nayakas of Madurai, Tanjavur, Gingee, and Ikkeri supplied contingents that formed the core of the imperial army. However, the hereditary tendency weakened central control. After the Battle of Talikota (1565), many nayakas declared de facto independence, founding the Nayaka kingdoms of Madurai, Tanjavur, and Gingee, which persisted until the late seventeenth century.

Revenue records (the karnam accounts) show that the state’s share from nayankara lands was typically one‑third of the gross produce, collected in kind or cash. The nayakas also contributed kavalgars (watchmen) for frontier defence and maintained irrigation works such as the anicut systems in the Kaveri delta. The interplay between central demand and local autonomy makes the Nayankara system a classic case study of “segmentary state” dynamics in South Indian historiography.

Maratha Chauth and Sardeshmukhi: Fiscal Extraction and Political Legitimacy

Chauth (one‑fourth) and sardeshmukhi (an additional ten per cent) were the two principal fiscal instruments through which the Maratha state extracted revenue from territories outside its direct administration. Formalised under Shivaji (r. 1674‑1680) and expanded by the Peshwas, chauth was levied on regions that acknowledged Maratha suzerainty but retained their own administrative structures; in return, the Marathas undertook to protect the territory from external raids. Sardeshmukhi represented a hereditary claim to the “head‑deshmukh” (chief revenue officer) share of the land revenue, effectively a tribute acknowledging the Maratha ruler as the paramount overlord.

The collection machinery relied on a network of kamavisdars (revenue contractors) and deshmukhs (local chiefs) who advanced the assessed amount to the Maratha treasury and then recouped it from the peasantry. The rates were not uniform: in the Deccan plateau chauth was often collected at 25 % of the estimated revenue, while in Gujarat and Malwa it could rise to 30 % due to higher agricultural productivity. Sardeshmukhi was usually fixed at 10 % of the same base.

These levies served a dual purpose. Financially, they funded the Maratha standing army, the navy under the Angres, and the expanding bureaucracy of the Peshwa’s secretariat (Huzur Daftar). Politically, they created a hierarchy of allegiance: rulers who paid chauth received a sanad (deed) confirming their autonomy, while those who refused faced Maratha raids (the bargir cavalry). The Mughal emperor, after the Treaty of 1719, formally recognised the Maratha right to collect chauth and sardeshmukhi in the six Deccan subahs, legitimising the Maratha fiscal claim in imperial diplomacy.

By the mid‑eighteenth century the system began to fracture. The growing power of regional Maratha chiefs (Holkar, Scindia, Bhonsle, Gaekwad) led to competing claims over the same territories, resulting in double‑collection and peasant unrest. The British East India Company exploited these fissures, negotiating separate treaties with individual chiefs and eventually supplanting the chauth‑sardeshmukhi framework with the permanent settlement and ryotwari systems.

Deccan Sultanates' Military Organization: The Role of the Habshi and Abyssinian Troops

The Deccan Sultanates (Bijapur, Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bidar, Berar) relied heavily on slave‑soldiers of African origin, known as Habshis (from the Arabic Habash, Abyssinia). Recruited through the Indian Ocean slave trade, these troops were valued for their loyalty to the sultan personally, as they lacked local clan ties. The most famous Habshi commander was Malik Ambar (1548‑1626), an Ethiopian who rose from slavery to become the Peshwa (prime minister) of Ahmadnagar and the architect of its guerrilla resistance against Mughal expansion.

Habshi units were organised into lashkars (regiments) of 500‑1,000 cavalry, equipped with matchlock muskets, composite bows, and light lances. They served as the sultan’s personal guard (khass‑khel) and as elite strike forces in siege warfare. In Bijapur, the Afghan‑Habshi corps under Ikhlas Khan played a decisive role in the defence of the capital against Mughal sieges (1631‑1636). The Golconda sultanate maintained a corps of Abyssinian musketeers who operated the heavy artillery positioned on the Golconda fort’s ramparts.

The integration of Habshi troops into the Deccan military establishment had fiscal implications. Their salaries were paid from the khazana (treasury) in cash (tankhwah) rather than through jagir assignments, reducing the pressure on land revenue. However, the reliance on imported slave‑soldiers created a dependency on the Portuguese‑controlled maritime routes for recruitment, a vulnerability exploited by the Mughals when they blockaded the western ports.

By the late seventeenth century, the Habshi presence declined as the Mughals annexed the sultanates and incorporated their military labour into the mansabdari system. Some Habshi families settled in the Deccan countryside, becoming local landholders (deshmukhs) and contributing to the region’s Afro‑Indian cultural synthesis, evident in the Siddi communities of Janjira and Hyderabad.

  • Confusing Alauddin’s price‑control granaries with the later Mughal karkhana system; the former were state‑run grain stores, the latter were craft workshops.
  • Attributing the du‑aspah sih‑aspah innovation to Akbar instead of Jahangir; it was a Jahangir‑specific mansab adjustment.
  • Treating the Nayankara grants as purely hereditary from the outset; early Sangama grants were conditional and revocable.
  • Equating chauth with a regular land tax; it was a tribute for protection, not a standard revenue assessment.
  • Assuming all Habshi troops were eunuchs; many were non‑eunuch cavalry who rose to high command.
  • Overlooking the role of the Portuguese in supplying African slaves to the Deccan; the slave trade was a maritime enterprise.
  • Believing the mansabdari crisis began only under Aurangzeb; structural strains appeared already under Shah Jahan.

The Iqta System under the Delhi Sultanate: Structure, Evolution, and Fiscal Consequences

The iqta was the principal instrument of military‑fiscal administration from the early thirteenth century until the decline of the Sultanate. Introduced systematically by Iltutmish (r. 1211‑1236), an iqta was a revenue assignment granted to a noble (muqti) in lieu of a cash salary. The muqti collected the land revenue (kharaj) from a defined territory, retained a portion for personal maintenance and the upkeep of his contingent, and forwarded the surplus (fawazil) to the central treasury (khazana). The grant was theoretically revocable and non‑hereditary; the sultan could transfer a muqti at will, a mechanism that prevented the emergence of entrenched local power bases.

Under Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296‑1316) the system was tightened. Alauddin fixed the revenue demand at half the produce (50 % of the measured yield) and introduced the dagh (branding) and chehra (descriptive roll) to control the size and quality of the muqti’s cavalry. He also instituted the diwan‑i‑mustakhraj to audit arrears, reducing the muqti’s discretion. Muhammad bin Tughlaq (r. 1325‑1351) experimented with cash‑based revenue (token currency) and expanded iqta grants to the Deccan, but his frequent transfers and the disastrous Deccan campaigns eroded muqti loyalty. Firuz Shah Tughlaq (r. 1351‑1388) reversed the trend by making iqta hereditary in practice, granting them to his slaves (ghulams) and relatives, which entrenched a landed aristocracy and weakened central fiscal oversight.

The fiscal consequences were profound. While the iqta enabled the Sultanate to maintain a large standing cavalry without a permanent bureaucracy, it also created a layer of intermediaries who extracted surplus beyond the state’s prescribed share. Peasants faced double exactions: the official demand (often ½ of produce) plus the muqti’s informal levies (cesses, forced labour). The system’s rigidity discouraged agricultural improvement; any increase in output was captured by the muqti rather than the state. By the late fourteenth century the iqta had become a quasi‑feudal tenure, contributing to the fragmentation that allowed regional kingdoms (Jaunpur, Malwa, Gujarat) to assert independence.

Todar Mal’s Bandobast: The Mughal Land Revenue Settlement and Its Regional Variations
