Akbar’s finance minister, Raja Todar Mal, codified the zabt (measurement) system in the 1580s, producing the Ain‑i‑Dahsala (the ten‑year settlement) that became the benchmark for Mughal revenue administration. The settlement rested on three pillars: accurate land measurement (paimaish) using the bigha‑gaz‑jarib standard; classification of soil into four categories — polaj (annually cultivated), parauti (fallow for a year), chachar (fallow for two‑three years), and banjar (uncultivated); and fixation of cash revenue rates (dastur) based on a ten‑year average of yields and prices. The revenue demand was typically one‑third to one‑half of the estimated produce, payable in copper dam or silver rupee.

Implementation relied on a hierarchy of officials: the qanungo (hereditary revenue record‑keeper) maintained the village papers (bahi), the amil (revenue collector) assessed and collected the demand, and the bitikchi (accountant) recorded transactions in the daftar. The settlement was not uniform across the empire. In the fertile Gangetic plains (Bihar, Bengal) the dastur was higher because of assured irrigation and multiple cropping; in Gujarat the rates reflected the commercial value of cotton and indigo; in the Deccan the rugged terrain and lower productivity led to lighter assessments and a greater reliance on the nasaq (crop‑sharing) method. Akbar’s successors — Jahangir and Shah Jahan — retained the framework but introduced the zabt‑i‑kamil (full measurement) in newly conquered territories and allowed local zamindars to act as intermediaries, especially in Bengal where the zamindari class was entrenched.

The Bandobast’s longevity stemmed from its flexibility: the ten‑year cycle permitted periodic revision, and the use of cash rates facilitated integration with the imperial monetary system. However, the system presupposed a stable peasantry and reliable price data — conditions that deteriorated during the late seventeenth‑century wars, leading to widespread arrears and the eventual shift toward revenue farming (ijaradari) under Aurangzeb’s successors.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Which of the following statements correctly distinguishes the zabt system from the ijaradari system?" Options include: (a) Zabt relied on hereditary zamindars; (b) Ijaradari fixed revenue for ten years; (c) Zabt used measurement and cash rates; (d) Ijaradari was introduced by Todar Mal.
Correct answer: (c). The zabt system, codified by Todar Mal, employed systematic land measurement (paimaish) and fixed cash revenue rates (dastur) based on a ten‑year average. Ijaradari, by contrast, was a revenue‑farming arrangement where the right to collect was auctioned to the highest bidder (ijaradar) for a short term, usually one year, and it emerged prominently after Aurangzeb’s death. Options (a), (b) and (d) conflate the two systems.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the three pillars of the Ain‑i‑Dahsala (measurement, classification, cash rates) and the functional chain qanungo‑amil‑bitikchi. For ijaradari, recall the shift from state‑appointed amils to auctioned ijaradars, the typical one‑year term, and the resulting peasant oppression. A quick table comparing zabt vs. ijaradari (basis, term, collector, impact) saves time in both prelims and mains.

Mughal Revenue Farming (Ijaradari): Mechanisms, Impact on Peasantry, and Decline

From the late seventeenth century the Mughal state increasingly resorted to ijaradari — the auction of revenue collection rights to private contractors (ijaradars) — to meet urgent fiscal demands, especially during the Deccan wars of Aurangzeb (r. 1658‑1707). The ijaradar bid a lump sum (ijara) for the right to collect the assessed revenue (jama) from a pargana or sarkar for a fixed period, usually one year. The bid amount became the state’s guaranteed income; any surplus collected above the bid was the ijaradar’s profit. The system bypassed the regular amil‑qanungo apparatus, replacing it with a profit‑driven intermediary.

The mechanics were formalised in the diwan‑i‑tan (revenue department). The auction (chak) was held at the provincial capital; the highest bidder secured a sanad (deed) specifying the jama, the term, and the obligation to maintain law and order. Ijaradars often sub‑let to smaller contractors (mustajirs), creating a pyramid of extraction. Because the ijaradar’s incentive was short‑term maximisation, they imposed exorbitant cesses (abwabs), forced peasants to sell produce at below‑market prices, and resorted to coercion (land seizure, imprisonment) to meet the bid. Contemporary chroniclers such as Khafi Khan note that in the Deccan the ijaradari led to widespread abandonment of villages (desertion) and a decline in cultivated area.

The impact on peasantry was severe. The traditional zabt safeguards — fixed rates, ten‑year revisions, and the qanungo’s record‑keeping — were eroded. Peasants lost the protection of the state’s direct assessment and became subject to the ijaradar’s arbitrary demands. In regions like Gujarat and Malwa, the system accelerated the conversion of peasant proprietors (raiyats) into tenants-at‑will. The fiscal strain also weakened the mansabdari system, as nobles pressed for ijaradari grants to supplement dwindling jagir incomes.

By the early eighteenth century the Mughal centre could no longer enforce the auction process; provincial governors (subahdars) began granting ijaradari rights to their own clients, effectively privatising revenue. The Maratha chauth‑sardeshmukhi system exploited this vacuum, collecting tribute from territories already burdened by ijaradari. The British East India Company, after acquiring the diwani of Bengal (1765), initially retained ijaradari but soon replaced it with the permanent settlement (1793) and ryotwari systems, ending the Mughal revenue‑farming experiment.

The Integration of Rajput Principalities into the Mughal Imperial Framework: Marriage Alliances, Mansabs, and Autonomy

The Mughal‑Rajput alliance was a cornerstone of imperial stability from Akbar’s reign onward. Akbar (r. 1556‑1605) initiated a policy of matrimonial ties: his marriage to the daughter of Raja Bharmal of Amber (1562) set a precedent followed by unions with the houses of Marwar (Jodhpur), Bikaner, and Jaisalmer. These marriages were not merely symbolic; they secured the loyalty of powerful Rajput clans and provided the Mughal court with a reservoir of trusted military commanders.

In return, Rajput rulers received high mansabs (ranks) — often 5,000 zat/5,000 sawar for the heads of major houses — and were allowed to retain their ancestral territories (watan jagirs) as hereditary holdings. The Mughal emperor recognised their internal autonomy: they administered justice, collected revenue through their own officials, and maintained distinct cultural practices (e.g., the Rajput code of honour, the practice of jauhar). However, they were obligated to furnish contingents for imperial campaigns, attend court at prescribed intervals, and accept the emperor’s suzerainty in foreign affairs.

The relationship evolved under successive emperors. Jahangir (r. 1605‑1627) continued the policy, granting the title of “Mirza” to Rajput princes and integrating them into the imperial household. Shah Jahan (r. 1628‑1658) relied heavily on Rajput generals such as Maharaja Jaswant Singh of Marwar and Raja Jai Singh of Amber for Deccan campaigns. Aurangzeb (r. 1658‑1707) initially upheld the alliance but his rigid orthodoxy and the imposition of jizya (1679) strained relations. The Rathore rebellion (1679‑1681) and the prolonged resistance of Mewar under Maharana Raj Singh illustrated the limits of integration when religious policy clashed with Rajput identity.

Despite tensions, the Mughal‑Rajput framework persisted until the empire’s disintegration. In the eighteenth century, Rajput states like Jaipur and Jodhpur negotiated directly with the Marathas and later the British, leveraging their Mughal‑granted mansabs and watan jagirs as diplomatic assets. The legacy of this integration is evident in the continued use of Mughal administrative terminology (subah, faujdar) in Rajput courts well into the colonial period.

Deccan Sultanates’ Architectural and Literary Patronage: The Bijapur and Golconda Schools

The Deccan Sultanates — particularly Bijapur (Adil Shahi) and Golconda (Qutb Shahi) — cultivated a distinctive Indo‑Islamic cultural synthesis that manifested in architecture, painting, and literature. In Bijapur, Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) transformed the capital into a centre of the “Deccani” style. The Gol Gumbaz (completed 1656) exemplifies monumental scale: a massive dome (44 m diameter) resting on a square base without external buttresses, employing a whispering gallery that demonstrates advanced acoustic engineering. The Ibrahim Rauza (tomb of Ibrahim II) combines Persian iwan motifs with local stone‑carving, featuring intricate jali screens and calligraphic bands in thuluth script.

Golconda’s architectural hallmark is the Charminar (1591), a four‑arched monument that also functioned as a mosque and a market hub. The Qutb Shahi tombs, set in a garden complex, display a progression from early Persianate domes to later structures with bulbous domes, corner minarets, and extensive stucco ornamentation. Both sultanates employed Persian architects (e.g., Ustad Ahmad Lahauri’s disciples) alongside local artisans, resulting in a hybrid vocabulary of arches, pendentives, and decorative tile work (kashi kari) imported from Safavid Iran.

Literary patronage flourished in parallel. The Adil Shahi court nurtured Dakhni Urdu (Deccani) poetry; the poet‑king Ibrahim II himself composed the Kitab‑i‑Nauras, a treatise on music and aesthetics in Dakhni. The Golconda court supported the Telugu poet‑scholar Pingali Suranna and the Persian historian Ferishta, whose Tarikh‑i‑Ferishta remains a primary source for Deccan history. Manuscript production thrived: the Bijapur school of miniature painting, characterised by vivid colours, elongated figures, and a lyrical treatment of landscape, produced the celebrated “Najm al‑Ulum” (The Stars of Sciences) manuscript (c. 1570). Golconda’s ateliers blended Persian Safavid conventions with indigenous motifs, evident in the “Shahnama” copies commissioned by Sultan Abdullah Qutb Shah.

This cultural efflorescence was underpinned by the sultanates’ commercial wealth — diamond trade from Golconda, textile exports from Bijapur — and their strategic position linking the Indian Ocean world with the interior. The Mughal annexation of Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687) dispersed many artists and scholars to Mughal courts, where Deccani aesthetics influenced the later Mughal “provincial” style seen in the paintings of the Lucknow and Murshidabad schools.

  • Confusing the iqta’s revocable nature with the later hereditary jagir; the iqta remained theoretically non‑hereditary until Firuz Shah’s practice.
  • Attributing the ten‑year zabt cycle to a permanent settlement; the Ain‑i‑Dahsala was a periodic reassessment, not a perpetual fixation.
  • Equating ijaradari with the zamindari system; ijaradari was a short‑term auction, whereas zamindari involved hereditary revenue rights.
  • Assuming all Rajput houses entered matrimonial alliances; Mewar (Sisodia) resisted marriage ties until the late seventeenth century.
  • Overlooking the role of Dakhni Urdu as a court language distinct from Persian; it was the medium of poetry and administration in Bijapur and Golconda.
  • Believing the Gol Gumbaz’s dome was built with European engineering; it employed indigenous corbelling and pendentive techniques.
  • Treating the Charminar solely as a commemorative monument; it was simultaneously a mosque, a market, and a symbolic centre of the city’s grid.

The sections above provide a layered understanding of fiscal, military, and cultural mechanisms that shaped medieval Indian polities from the Sultanate to the Marathas.

The Mughal Imperial Post (Barid) and Intelligence Network: Structure, Operations, and Political Impact

The Mughal communication apparatus, known as the Barid, was a state‑run relay system that combined postal delivery with systematic intelligence gathering. Its origins lie in the Sultanate barid tradition, but Akbar (r. 1556‑1605) reorganised it into a hierarchical network described in the Ain‑i‑Akbari. At the apex stood the Barid‑i‑Mumalik (Postmaster General), a mansabdar of 5,000 zat who reported directly to the emperor. Below him, provincial Barid‑i‑Subah officers managed the dak chowkis (relay stations) spaced roughly 12‑15 km apart along the major highways — the Grand Trunk Road, the Agra‑Lahore route, and the Deccan corridors.

Each chowki maintained a stable of 4‑6 swift horses and a pair of trained runners (paiks) for foot‑relay in mountainous stretches. The average speed for urgent farmans (imperial orders) was 120‑150 km per day, while routine revenue reports (akhbarat) moved at 60‑80 km per day. In addition to mounted couriers, the Mughals employed carrier pigeons for the most time‑critical messages, especially during the Deccan campaigns of Aurangzeb (r. 1658‑1707). The waqia‑navis (news writers) stationed at every provincial capital compiled daily digests of market prices, troop movements, and local unrest; these digests were sealed and dispatched via the Barid to the imperial Diwan‑i‑Khas.

The intelligence function was institutionalised through the Barid‑i‑Khas, a parallel covert channel that reported directly to the emperor’s private council. Its agents, often drawn from the mansabdar class, monitored the loyalty of provincial governors (subahdars), the conduct of faujdars, and the flow of revenue from zabt assessments. During Jahangir’s reign (1605‑1627) the Barid‑i‑Khas uncovered the rebellion of Prince Khusrau (1606) within weeks, enabling rapid mobilisation. Shah Jahan (1628‑1658) expanded the network to the newly annexed Ahmadnagar and Golconda territories, adding 120 new chowkis. Aurangzeb’s Deccan wars (1681‑1707) saw the Barid stretched to its limit; the emperor relied on a dedicated dak‑e‑khas (special post) that used relay riders on Arabian horses to maintain a 200 km‑per‑day pace for strategic orders.

Financially, the Barid was funded through a dedicated jagir assigned to the Barid‑i‑Mumalik, supplemented by a small levy on merchants using the imperial highways (rahdari). The system’s efficiency declined after 1707 as central authority fragmented; regional successors (the Nizam of Hyderabad, the Maratha Peshwas) inherited the chowki infrastructure but lacked the imperial mandate to enforce uniform standards. Nevertheless, the Barid’s legacy persisted in the British Indian postal reforms of 1837, which adopted the Mughal relay spacing and the concept of a state‑controlled intelligence courier.

The Maratha Confederacy’s Fiscal Decentralisation: The Huzur Daftar, Saranjam Grants, and the Peshwa’s Revenue Administration

After Shivaji’s death (1680) the Maratha polity evolved from a centralised kingdom into a confederacy of semi‑autonomous sardars bound by the Peshwa’s fiscal authority. The institutional backbone of this arrangement was the Huzur Daftar (Central Secretariat) established at Pune by Peshwa Balaji Vishwanath (1713‑1720). The Daftar housed three principal branches: the Mahzar (audit), the Chauth‑Vahi (collection registers), and the Saranjam‑Vahi (grant ledgers). All entries were kept in the Modi script, a cursive Marathi shorthand that allowed rapid bookkeeping.

Saranjam grants were revenue assignments made to sardars (e.g., Holkar, Scindia, Bhonsle) in lieu of cash salaries. A typical saranjam comprised a cluster of villages yielding a fixed annual jama (assessed revenue) of 5‑10 lakh rupees. The grant was hereditary but subject to annual verification by the Huzur Daftar; failure to remit the stipulated chauth (one‑fourth of the produce) and sardeshmukhi (additional 10 % levy) triggered a dastak (re‑assessment) and possible resumption. The Peshwa’s Kamavisdar (revenue commissioner) toured each saranjam every two years, cross‑checking village‑level patil accounts against the central ledger.

The Chauth‑Vahi recorded the quarterly collection of chauth from territories nominally under Mughal or Nizam suzerainty but effectively controlled by Maratha agents. Collection was delegated to deshmukhs (district heads) who retained a 5 % commission (deshmukhi) and forwarded the balance to the Peshwa’s treasury (Huzur Khazana). In the Deccan, the Marathas maintained a parallel dak (postal) system — distinct from the Mughal Barid — to transmit chauth receipts from frontier outposts (e.g., Burhanpur, Aurangabad) to Pune within 15‑20 days.

Fiscal decentralisation was reinforced by the Ashta Pradhan council, where the Pant Pratinidhi (finance minister) presented the annual budget estimate (sar‑e‑sarkar) based on Huzur Daftar data. The 1719 Treaty of Delhi, which recognised Maratha claims to chauth in the six Mughal subahs of the Deccan, was negotiated using these estimates. By the 1740s the confederacy’s revenue base exceeded 1.2 crore rupees annually, yet the lack of a uniform cadastral survey meant that actual yields varied widely, prompting the Peshwa Madhavrao I (1761‑1772) to commission a limited zamin‑bandi (land measurement) in the Konkan and Desh regions.

The system’s resilience lay in its flexibility: sardars could mortgage saranjam rights to bankers (sahukars) for campaign funds, while the Huzur Daftar retained the ultimate audit trail. However, the absence of a centralised land‑tenure code led to overlapping claims, especially after the Third Battle of Panipat (1761), when the confederacy’s fiscal cohesion fractured and the British East India Company exploited the revenue vacuum through the Diwani of Bengal (1765).

Comparative Analysis Technique
Compare the Mughal Barid system and the Maratha Dak system in terms of organisational hierarchy, speed of communication, and political control.
Organisational hierarchy: The Mughal Barid was a single imperial hierarchy headed by the Barid‑i‑Mumalik, with provincial Barid‑i‑Subah officers and a covert Barid‑i‑Khas reporting directly to the emperor. The Maratha Dak operated under the Peshwa’s Huzur Daftar, with regional dak‑adhyakshas appointed by individual sardars; there was no empire‑wide postmaster general. Speed: Mughal urgent farmans achieved 120‑150 km/day using relay horses and pigeons; Maratha dak couriers covered 80‑100 km/day on foot‑relay and horse, slower because the network served revenue remittances rather than military orders. Political control: The Barid doubled as an intelligence arm (waqia‑navis, Barid‑i‑Khas) enabling the emperor to monitor mansabdars and suppress revolts swiftly. The Maratha Dak primarily facilitated fiscal flows (chauth, sardeshmukhi) and lacked a systematic espionage component; political surveillance relied on personal agents of the Peshwa rather than a dedicated postal intelligence branch.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a comparison, draw a three‑column table (Feature | Mughal Barid | Maratha Dak) in the margin. Fill each cell with a single precise fact (e.g., “Barid‑i‑Mumalik – 5,000 zat mansabdar” vs “No single postmaster; sardar‑appointed dak‑adhyakshas”). This visual structure forces you to address hierarchy, speed, and control explicitly and prevents omission of any dimension.

The Deccan Sultanates’ Naval Power: Shipbuilding, Port Control, and Maritime Trade in the Indian Ocean

The Deccan Sultanates — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, and Bidar — maintained modest but strategically significant naval forces from the late fifteenth to the late seventeenth century. Their fleets operated from fortified ports on the Konkan and Malabar coasts: Dabhol (Bijapur), Chaul and Rajapur (Ahmadnagar), Bhatkal and Goa (Golconda, after the Portuguese ceded limited access), and the island fortress of Janjira under the Abyssinian Siddi admirals who served Bijapur and later the Mughals.

Shipbuilding combined indigenous ghurab (large ocean‑going vessels, 200‑300 tons, lateen‑rigged) with the smaller, oar‑driven galivat (50‑80 tons) used for coastal patrol and riverine operations. Timber was sourced from the Western Ghats teak forests; iron fastenings and cannons were imported from Persia and the Ottoman Empire via the Red Sea route. The Bijapur admiralty, headed by the Siddi Masud (d. 1660), maintained a standing fleet of 30‑40 ghurabs and 60‑80 galivats at Dabhol, capable of mounting 12‑18 pounder bronze guns on the main deck. Golconda’s navy, smaller at 15‑20 ghurabs, specialised in protecting the diamond‑laden convoys from the Krishna‑Godavari delta to the port of Masulipatnam.

Port control was exercised through a system of customs farms (mahsul) leased to merchant‑bankers (sahukars) who paid a fixed annual zakat (2.5 % of assessed trade value) to the sultan. The Bijapur Diwan‑i‑Bandar (Port Revenue Office) collected duties on pepper, textiles, and horses; records from 1623 show a yearly intake of 1.8 lakh rupees from Dabhol alone. The Siddi admirals also levied pilgrim taxes on Muslim merchants travelling to Mecca, a lucrative source that financed the upkeep of Janjira’s massive stone walls (completed 1648).

Maritime trade linked the Deccan to the wider Indian Ocean: Bijapur exported cotton piece‑goods to Mocha and Basra, while importing Arabian horses essential for cavalry. Golconda’s diamond trade passed through the Portuguese‑controlled port of Goa under a 1589 agreement that allowed the Qutb Shahi fleet safe passage in return for a 10 % share of diamond revenue. Ahmadnagar’s navy, under Malik Ambar (d. 1626), conducted amphibious raids on Portuguese holdings at Chaul (1618) and Bassein (1620), temporarily disrupting the Estado da Índia’s supply lines.

The decline of Deccan naval power began with the Mughal annexation of Ahmadnagar (1636) and the subsequent Mughal‑Bijapur wars (1657‑1686). Aurangzeb’s forces captured Dabhol (1660) and forced the Siddi fleet into Mughal service, rebranding it the Mughal Deccan Navy. Golconda’s fleet was dismantled after the 1687 siege of Hyderabad. By 1700 the only surviving Deccan naval entity was the Janjira state, which persisted as a Mughal vassal until the British subdued it in 1840.

The Mughal‑Portuguese Diplomatic and Commercial Relations: The 1615 Treaty of Surat and the Grant of Daman and Diu

Relations between the Mughal Empire and the Portuguese Estado da Índia evolved from sporadic embassies to a formal treaty framework that shaped western Indian Ocean commerce. The first Mughal‑Portuguese contact occurred in 1573 when Akbar received the envoy Antonio de Gouvea at Fatehpur Sikri; the Portuguese sought permission to establish a factory at Surat, but Akbar deferred, preferring to keep European traders at arm’s length.

Jahangir (r. 1605‑1627) adopted a more pragmatic stance. In 1612 the English East India Company secured a farman for Surat, prompting the Portuguese to negotiate a comparable privilege. The result was the Treaty of Surat (1615), signed between Jahangir’s governor of Gujarat, Mirza Aziz Koka, and the Portuguese Viceroy Dom Jerónimo de Azevedo. Its principal clauses were:

  • Portuguese merchants received a permanent factory at Surat with exemption from the standard 2.5 % customs duty on imports of horses, arms, and European textiles.
  • In return, the Portuguese pledged naval protection for Mughal shipping in the Gulf of Cambay against piracy (notably the Malabar corsairs).
  • The Mughal emperor granted the Portuguese the right to maintain a fortified settlement at Daman (acquired 1559) and Diu (acquired 1535), confirming their sovereignty over these enclaves.
  • A joint commission was established to resolve disputes over customs valuation, meeting annually at Surat.

The treaty was ratified by a farman bearing Jahangir’s seal and a Portuguese alvará. It marked the first time the Mughals formally recognised European territorial holdings on Indian soil. Commercial data from the Surat customs house (1620‑1630) show Portuguese share of pepper exports rising from 12 % to 22 % after 1615, while Mughal revenue from the port’s mahsul increased by 15 % due to higher overall trade volume.

Subsequent Mughal rulers honoured the treaty with modifications. Shah Jahan (1628‑1658) renewed the factory privileges in 1634 but imposed a 1 % surcharge on Portuguese pepper to fund the Deccan campaigns. Aurangzeb (1658‑1707) initially confirmed the arrangement (1664 farman) but later restricted Portuguese naval movements after the 1689 capture of the Mughal ship Ganj‑i‑Sawai by English pirates, suspecting Portuguese complicity. The Portuguese enclaves of Daman and Diu remained under their control until 1961, outlasting the Mughal Empire by more than two centuries.

  • Confusing the Mughal Barid’s intelligence wing (Barid‑i‑Khas) with the Maratha dak’s revenue‑only function.
  • Assuming saranjam grants were permanent hereditary estates; they required biennial audit by the Huzur Daftar.
  • Overstating Deccan naval size; Bijapur’s fleet never exceeded 40 ghurabs even at its peak.
  • Treating the 1615 Treaty of Surat as a Mughal concession of sovereignty; it merely confirmed existing Portuguese enclaves.
  • Neglecting the role of the Siddi admirals as both Bijapur vassals and later Mughal naval commanders.
  • Equating the Maratha chauth collection mechanism with the Mughal zabt system; chauth was a negotiated tribute, not a land‑based assessment.
  • Aspirants should verify each factual claim against standard textbooks before the exam.

Mughal Land Measurement and Survey Units: The Ilahi Gaz, the Bigha, and the Zabt Cadastre

The Mughal zabt system rested on a standardized linear measure introduced by Akbar in 1580 CE: the Ilahi gaz of 33 inches (≈ 0.838 m), also called the gaz‑i‑Ilahi. This unit replaced the myriad local cubits and enabled a uniform cadastral survey across the empire. The survey instrument was the jarib, a chain of 60 Ilahi gaz (≈ 50.3 m), carried by a team of amin (surveyors) under the supervision of the diwan‑i‑zabt. Area was expressed in bigha, defined in the Ain‑i‑Akbari as a square of 60 × 60 Ilahi gaz (3 600 sq gaz ≈ 0.25 ha). Regional variants persisted — Bengal’s bigha of 20 × 20 gaz, Gujarat’s of 30 × 30 gaz — but the imperial zabt registers recorded the standard bigha for revenue calculation.

Revenue demand was fixed through the dahsala (ten‑year average) method: the amin measured each field, noted the crop (wheat, rice, cotton, indigo), and applied the zabt rate per bigha derived from the raiyat’s produce share (usually one‑third to one‑half). The paimaish (measurement) was entered in the khasra (field register) and the khatauni (village ledger). The zabt cadastre thus linked linear measure, area unit, and yield estimate in a single documentary chain. Akbar’s 1595 CE revision of the zabt rates, recorded in the Dastur‑ul‑Amal, standardized the jarib length and mandated annual re‑verification by the faujdar to prevent encroachment.

Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan the Ilahi gaz remained the legal standard, but local zamindars often used the kucha (a shorter rod) for private transactions, creating disputes that the qazi resolved by referencing the imperial gaz‑i‑Ilahi. Aurangzeb’s 1665 CE order to re‑measure the Deccan provinces with the jarib after the 1657‑1660 campaigns illustrates the state’s reliance on the same metrology for fiscal control. The survival of the Ilahi gaz in British revenue surveys (the 1793 CE Permanent Settlement used the bigha of 3 600 sq gaz) testifies to its durability as a fiscal benchmark.

Maratha Fort Administration: The Killedar, the Sabnis, and the Fort Revenue System

Shivaji’s fort network — over 300 strongholds by 1680 — operated on a tightly defined administrative triad. The Killedar (fort commander) held military and civil authority, appointed directly by the Chhatrapati or later the Peshwa. He commanded the garrison (sarnobat), oversaw the karkhana (arsenal), and adjudicated minor disputes within the fort precincts. The Sabnis (accountant) maintained the daftar (ledger) of fort revenues: killa lands cultivated by ryots, saranjam grants to officers, and the chauth collected from surrounding villages. The Havaldar (storekeeper) managed grain, ammunition, and water stores, reporting daily to the Sabnis.

Revenue from each fort flowed into the Huzur Daftar at the capital (Raigad, later Satara). The Killedar submitted a quarterly bahi (account) detailing kharif and rabi harvests, customs on goods passing through the fort gate (chowk), and the jakat levied on pilgrims. The Sabnis cross‑checked these figures against the pahani (crop inspection) conducted by the karkun (village clerk). Discrepancies triggered a jama (audit) by the Peshwa’s Mujumdar (auditor‑general).

Notable forts illustrate the system: Raigad (coronation capital, 1674) had a Killedar of the Bhosale clan, a Sabnis from the Pingle family, and a Havaldar overseeing the Mahadarwaja granary. Sindhudurg (built 1664‑1667) employed a naval Killedar (Sarkhel) to coordinate the Angre fleet. After 1714 the Peshwa centralized fort appointments, reducing hereditary Killedars and introducing saranjam grants that required biennial renewal — a shift reflected in the 1730 CE Huzur Daftar entries showing a 22 % rise in direct crown revenue from forts.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "The Maratha fort revenue system was identical to the Mughal zabt system because both used a standard bigha for assessment." Identify the error and explain the correct distinction.
The statement is false. The Mughal zabt employed a uniform Ilahi gaz and a standard bigha (3 600 sq gaz) for empire‑wide cadastral measurement, with revenue fixed as a share of estimated produce. Maratha forts used locally defined killa lands measured in regional units (often the ghumma or katha), and revenue comprised a mix of land produce, customs, and chauth — a negotiated tribute, not a land‑based assessment. The Maratha system was decentralized, with each fort’s Sabnis keeping separate accounts, whereas the Mughal zabt fed into a centralized daftar under the diwan‑i‑zabt.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question contrasts Mughal and Maratha fiscal mechanisms, first identify the measurement standard (Ilahi gaz vs. local unit), then the revenue base (produce share vs. tribute + customs), and finally the administrative hierarchy (centralized diwan vs. fort‑level Sabnis). This three‑step filter eliminates the most common conflation traps.

Deccan Sultanates' Revenue Terminology: Peshkash, Kharaj, and the Village Accountant (Kulkarni)

The Bahmani Sultanate (1347‑1527) and its five successor states — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, Berar — inherited a Persianate fiscal vocabulary that blended Islamic legal terms with local practice. Kharaj denoted the land tax levied on cultivated soil, assessed per bigha (locally defined) and collected by the amil (revenue farmer). Peshkash was the tribute paid by subordinate chiefs (nayakas, palegars) to the sultan, often a fixed cash sum or a share of their own revenue. The jizya on non‑Muslim subjects persisted until Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580‑1627) abolished it in Bijapur, replacing the loss with an enhanced kharaj rate.

At the village level the Kulkarni (also patwari in Mughal terminology) served as hereditary accountant. He maintained the bahi (ledger) of pahani (crop inspection), recorded the mahsul (revenue demand), and issued the sanad (receipt) to the raiyat. The Kulkarni’s counterpart, the Deshmukh, held the hereditary right to collect kharaj from a cluster of villages (pargana) and to remit a fixed peshkash to the provincial subahdar. The deshpande (district accountant) audited the Deshmukh’s returns.

In Golconda, the Qutb Shahi diwan (c. 1620) standardized the kharaj at ⅓ of the gross produce for wet rice and ¼ for dry crops, measured in the Golconda bigha (≈ 0.33 ha). Bijapur’s Adil Shahi records (1635) show a peshkash of 12 % of the nayaka’s revenue, payable in gold hun coins. Ahmadnagar’s Malik Ambar (d. 1626) introduced a zabt-style measurement using a 60‑gaz chain, but retained the term kharaj for the demand. The Kulkarni’s bahi survived the Mughal conquest; the 1687 CE Mughal‑Deccan revenue survey in Hyderabad still referenced Kulkarni entries for village boundaries.

Mughal Provincial Police Organization: The Kotwal, the Faujdar, and the Thanadar

Law‑enforcement in the Mughal empire operated on three nested tiers. The Kotwal headed urban police in each major city (Delhi, Agra, Lahore, Ahmedabad). Appointed by the emperor on the recommendation of the mir bakhshi, the Kotwal supervised the chowkidar (night watch), regulated markets (bazaar), enforced the muhtasib’s moral codes, and maintained the daftar‑i‑kotwali (police register). The Ain‑i‑Akbari lists his duties: census of households, fire‑prevention drills, and the arrest of dacoits within the city walls.

At the sarkar (district) level the Faujdar combined military command with rural policing. He commanded a contingent of sawars (cavalry) and bandukchis (musketeers) stationed at the faujdari (garrison). The Faujdar’s police functions included suppressing rebellions, guarding highways, and overseeing the thanadars. His judicial powers were limited to summary punishment; capital cases went to the qazi.

The Thanadar (also thanedar) headed a thana (police post) at the pargana level. He managed a small force of sipahis and the village chowkidar. The Thanadar recorded daily occurrences in the roz‑namcha (daily log) and forwarded monthly abstracts to the Faujdar. In Bengal, the 1658 CE Diwan‑i‑Bengal records show 112 thanas under 12 faujdaris, each thana covering roughly 30 villages. The mir bahr (river police) operated on the Ganga and Brahmaputra, checking piracy and collecting tolls.

Akbar’s 1582 CE Dastur‑ul‑Amal codified the hierarchy: Kotwal → Faujdar → Thanadar → Chowkidar. The system persisted under Jahangir and Shah Jahan; Aurangzeb expanded the faujdari network in the Deccan (1659‑1680) to secure supply lines. The British Police Act of 1861 retained the thana as the basic unit, a direct institutional legacy.

Vijayanagara Administrative Geography: The Rajya, Nadu, and Sthala Divisions

The Vijayanagara empire (1336‑1646) organized its territory into a three‑tier hierarchy that balanced central oversight with local autonomy. The top tier, the Rajya (province), was governed by a Mahanayakacharya or a senior Nayaka appointed by the Raya. Major Rajyas included Karnataka (capital region), Tuluva (coastal), Madurai (southern), and Gingee (northern Tamil). Each Rajya comprised several Nadu (districts), administered by a Nadugavunda (district chief) who collected the kadamai (land revenue) and maintained the nadu‑katcheri (district court).

The Sthala (village or settlement cluster) formed the base unit. A Sthanika (village headman) presided over the grama sabha (village assembly), allocated kudivari (cultivable plots), and recorded the pattayam (land grant) in the sthala‑pothi (village ledger). Temple lands (devadana) within a Sthala were managed by the archaka (priest) under the supervision of the Mahanayakacharya, linking fiscal and ritual administration.

Revenue flowed upward: the Sthanika submitted the kadamai to the Nadugavunda, who forwarded a consolidated nadu‑jama to the Mahanayakacharya. The latter rendered the rajya‑jama to the imperial treasury at Hampi. Inscriptions from the 1520 CE Krishnadevaraya period show a Rajya of Udayagiri containing 12 Nadus, each averaging 45 Sthalas. The 1565 CE Battle of Talikota disrupted this hierarchy; the Aravidu successors (1570‑1646) retained the terminology but lost effective control over southern Rajyas, which became semi‑independent Nayaka kingdoms (Madurai, Tanjore, Gingee).

  • Confusing the Mughal Ilahi gaz (33 inches) with the British “standard yard” (36 inches) when converting bigha measurements.
  • Assuming the Maratha Killedar held hereditary tenure; most appointments were revocable at the Peshwa’s pleasure after 1714.
  • Treating Deccan Sultanate peshkash as a land tax; it was a political tribute from vassal chiefs, distinct from kharaj.
  • Equating the Mughal Kotwal’s urban duties with the Faujdar’s rural military role; the Kotwal had no command over cavalry.
  • Believing the Vijayanagara Nadu corresponded exactly to the modern taluk; boundaries shifted with each Nayaka’s consolidation.
  • Overlooking the Kulkarni’s hereditary status; the post was often sold or mortgaged, affecting record continuity.
  • These traps highlight the most frequent errors aspirants make in this topic.

The Ulema's Influence on Legal and Administrative Policy in the Delhi Sultanate

The Ulema, the body of Islamic scholars and jurists, played a significant, albeit often contested, role in the Delhi Sultanate's statecraft and legal system. Their influence stemmed from their interpretation of Sharia (Islamic law), which theoretically formed the bedrock of the Sultanate's governance. The Sultan, as the protector of Islam, was expected to uphold Sharia, and the Ulema served as its custodians and interpreters.

Key positions held by the Ulema included the Sadr-us-Sudur, who was responsible for religious affairs, endowments (waqfs), and judicial appointments, and the Qazi-ul-Quzat (Chief Justice), who headed the judicial administration. Under him, numerous Qazis administered justice at provincial and local levels, adjudicating cases based on Islamic jurisprudence, primarily the Hanafi school. Muftis provided legal opinions (fatwas) when consulted by Qazis or the Sultan. The Ulema also staffed the Madrasas, shaping the intellectual and religious discourse of the era.

However, the relationship between the Sultan and the Ulema was complex. While Sultans often sought religious legitimacy through the Ulema, they also frequently prioritized political expediency and administrative necessity over strict adherence to Sharia. Alauddin Khalji, for instance, famously asserted that he would issue orders "which I consider to be for the good of the state and no one else knows what is good for the state." Muhammad bin Tughlaq also often bypassed the Ulema, appointing non-scholars to judicial posts and relying on his own judgment. This tension highlights the pragmatic nature of Sultanate rule, where the Sultan's authority, backed by military power, often superseded the Ulema's legal interpretations in matters of state policy.

Despite these challenges, the Ulema's presence ensured a degree of continuity in legal principles and provided a moral framework for governance. They acted as a check, however limited, on arbitrary rule, and their pronouncements could sway public opinion. Their role was particularly crucial in areas like personal law (marriage, inheritance), religious endowments, and the collection of religious taxes like Zakat. The Ulema's influence waned and waxed depending on the Sultan's disposition and the political climate, but their institutional presence remained a defining feature of the Delhi Sultanate's administrative and legal landscape until its eventual decline.

Mughal Imperial Ideology: From Divine Light to Universal Sovereignty

The Mughal Empire, particularly under Akbar, developed a sophisticated imperial ideology that moved beyond purely religious legitimacy to embrace a concept of universal sovereignty. This shift was crucial for governing a diverse, multi-religious subcontinent and consolidating imperial authority. The core of this ideology was articulated through the concept of Farr-i-Izadi, or "Divine Light," propagated by Akbar's court historian and ideologue, Abul Fazl. This theory posited that sovereignty was a divine emanation, a light bestowed directly by God upon the righteous ruler, making the emperor a divinely chosen guide for his people, rather than merely a temporal leader deriving authority from religious scholars or conquest.

This "Divine Light" concept elevated the emperor above sectarian divisions, allowing him to be seen as a benevolent father figure to all his subjects, irrespective of their faith. It underpinned Akbar's policy of Sulh-i-Kul (universal peace or absolute peace), which advocated for tolerance and harmony among different religious communities. The emperor, as the recipient of divine light, was deemed capable of discerning truth and justice, making him the ultimate arbiter in all matters, including religious disputes. This ideology was visually reinforced through court rituals like the Jharokha Darshan, where the emperor appeared before his subjects at sunrise, symbolizing his role as the source of light and justice.

Jahangir and Shah Jahan largely continued this ideological framework, emphasizing their lineage as recipients of the divine light and patrons of universal justice. Jahangir's autobiography, the Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri, frequently references his divine mandate. Shah Jahan's reign saw the further elaboration of imperial symbolism, with the Peacock Throne and the construction of the Taj Mahal serving as grand expressions of imperial power and divine favor. However, Aurangzeb, while still claiming divine sanction, shifted the emphasis towards a more orthodox Islamic interpretation of sovereignty, seeking legitimacy through adherence to Sharia and the patronage of Islamic institutions, which marked a departure from the inclusive universalism of his predecessors. This ideological evolution reflects the changing political and social dynamics of the Mughal Empire, from its formative period of consolidation to its later challenges.

Examiner's technique: Distinguishing sources of legitimacy
Compare and contrast the primary sources of political legitimacy for the Delhi Sultanate and the Mughal Empire under Akbar. Discuss how these differences influenced their respective administrative and religious policies.
The Delhi Sultanate primarily derived its political legitimacy from adherence to Islamic law (Sharia) and the sanction of the Ulema, often seeking recognition from the Abbasid Caliphate (e.g., Iltutmish). This meant Sultans were theoretically bound by religious injunctions and relied on the Ulema for legal interpretation and judicial administration. This often led to policies that, while sometimes pragmatic, generally aimed to uphold Islamic orthodoxy, such as the imposition of Jizya on non-Muslims and the patronage of Islamic institutions. Akbar's Mughal Empire, in contrast, developed a more expansive and inclusive ideology centered on Farr-i-Izadi (Divine Light) and Sulh-i-Kul (universal peace). This concept positioned the emperor as a divinely chosen, benevolent ruler for all subjects, transcending religious divisions. This ideological shift influenced administrative policies like the abolition of Jizya (1564 CE), the establishment of the Ibadat Khana for inter-faith dialogue, and the integration of diverse religious and ethnic groups into the Mansabdari system. While still acknowledging an Islamic heritage, Akbar's legitimacy was rooted in his perceived universal justice and divine mandate, allowing for greater religious tolerance and a more centralized, less Ulema-dependent administration compared to the Delhi Sultanate.
Exam-day strategy: Chronological nuance
When discussing broad historical trends like imperial ideologies or administrative systems, always be mindful of chronological evolution. Neither the Delhi Sultanate nor the Mughal Empire were monolithic entities. Note shifts under different rulers (e.g., Alauddin Khalji vs. Firoz Shah Tughlaq; Akbar vs. Aurangzeb) to demonstrate a nuanced understanding of institutional change and continuity. This adds depth to your answers and avoids overgeneralization.

Vijayanagara's Hydraulic Engineering and Agrarian Expansion

The Vijayanagara Empire's enduring prosperity and ability to sustain its large urban centers and military depended significantly on sophisticated hydraulic engineering and strategic agrarian expansion. The semi-arid Deccan plateau necessitated innovative water management techniques to support intensive agriculture, particularly rice cultivation, alongside millets and commercial crops. The state played a crucial role in planning, financing, and maintaining these systems, often in collaboration with local communities and temple institutions.

The capital city of Hampi itself was a marvel of water infrastructure. It was supplied by a complex network of aqueducts, canals, and reservoirs, drawing water from the Tungabhadra River. Notable examples include the Kamalapuram Tank, a massive reservoir that served both agricultural fields and the urban population, and the various canals (like the Basavanna Canal and Raya Canal) that diverted river water over considerable distances. These systems often incorporated sluice gates, feeder channels, and distribution networks to ensure equitable water supply and minimize wastage. Inscriptions frequently record the construction and repair of tanks (eri) and wells (kulam) by kings, queens, and wealthy merchants, indicating a widespread understanding of their economic importance.

Beyond the capital, the empire invested in thousands of tanks and small-scale irrigation works across its territories. These ranged from large-scale reservoirs capable of irrigating vast tracts of land to smaller village tanks that captured monsoon runoff. The maintenance of these structures was often a local responsibility, managed by village assemblies (grama sabhas) or temple trusts, which received land grants or revenue shares for their efforts. This decentralized maintenance, coupled with central oversight and investment, ensured the longevity and efficiency of the system.

The reliable water supply facilitated agrarian expansion, allowing for multiple cropping cycles and the cultivation of high-value crops like cotton, sugarcane, and various spices, in addition to staple grains. This agricultural surplus not only fed the population and supported the army but also generated significant revenue for the state and fueled a vibrant internal and external trade network. The integration of advanced hydraulic engineering with state patronage and local participation was a cornerstone of Vijayanagara's economic strength and its ability to project power across South India for over two centuries.

The Maratha Harkara System: Espionage and Strategic Intelligence

The Maratha Empire, especially under Shivaji, developed a highly effective and sophisticated intelligence network known as the Harkara system, which was pivotal to their military successes and administrative efficiency. Distinct from the general postal relays of the Dak system, Harkaras were dedicated intelligence agents, spies, and scouts whose primary function was to gather critical information about enemy movements, strengths, weaknesses, and intentions. This system was a cornerstone of Shivaji's guerrilla warfare tactics, enabling surprise attacks and strategic retreats.

Harkaras were carefully selected, often from diverse backgrounds, and trained in various skills including disguise, languages, observation, and stealth. They operated both within Maratha territories and deep inside enemy domains, particularly Mughal territories and the Deccan Sultanates. Their methods included infiltrating enemy camps, posing as merchants, mendicants, or local villagers, and establishing networks of informants. They would travel light, often on horseback, to ensure rapid communication of intelligence back to Maratha commanders.

The intelligence gathered by Harkaras was multi-faceted. It included detailed reports on the size and composition of enemy armies, their supply lines, artillery positions, and the morale of their troops. They also provided information on political dissent within enemy ranks, the layout and defenses of forts, and the economic conditions of regions targeted for raids (like the Surat sacks). This granular intelligence allowed Shivaji to plan his campaigns with precision, execute daring raids, and avoid direct confrontations with numerically superior Mughal forces when disadvantageous.

For instance, the successful surprise attack on Shaista Khan's camp in Pune (1663 CE) and the subsequent sacks of Surat (1664 CE, 1670 CE) were largely attributed to the meticulous intelligence provided by Harkaras. The system continued to be vital under the Peshwas, adapting to the changing nature of Maratha warfare, including siege operations and diplomatic maneuvers. The Harkara system thus represented a crucial institutional innovation that provided the Marathas with a significant strategic advantage, transforming raw information into actionable intelligence that shaped their military and political trajectory.

Deccan Sultanate Revenue Grants: Amaram, Mokasa, and Local Fiscal Authority

The Deccan Sultanates (Bahmani, Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Berar, Bidar) developed distinct land revenue systems that, while sharing some similarities with contemporary North Indian practices, also featured unique grant types and local administrative structures. Two prominent forms of revenue grants were Amaram and Mokasa, which were crucial for military mobilization and civil administration, respectively.

The Amaram system, particularly prevalent in the Vijayanagara Empire, was also adopted and adapted by the Deccan Sultanates. It involved granting revenue assignments from specific territories to military chiefs or commanders in exchange for maintaining a contingent of troops for the Sultan. The Amaram holder (Amara Nayaka) was responsible for collecting revenue, maintaining law and order, and providing military service when required. This system decentralized military command and revenue collection, similar in function to the Mughal Jagir system or the Maratha Saranjam, but with its own regional nuances regarding tenure and obligations.

Mokasa grants, on the other hand, were typically revenue assignments given for civil or military service, often to individuals or institutions, without the explicit obligation of maintaining a large military contingent. These grants could be for personal maintenance, as rewards for service, or to support religious establishments. Mokasa holders collected revenue from their assigned villages or lands, retaining a portion for themselves and remitting the rest to the central treasury. The terms of Mokasa grants varied, sometimes being hereditary, at other times revocable at the Sultan's pleasure.

Local fiscal authority was significantly vested in hereditary village and district officials. The Deshmukh, a prominent figure across the Deccan, was a hereditary chief or head of a district (pargana) responsible for revenue collection, maintaining land records, and local administration. Deshmukhs often commanded local militias and acted as intermediaries between the state and the peasantry. They received a share of the revenue (deshmukhi) and held considerable local power, often becoming semi-independent in times of weak central authority. The Kulkarni, the village accountant, maintained detailed records of land holdings, cultivation, and revenue assessments, providing crucial data for both the Deshmukhs and the central administration. The interplay of these grant systems and local hereditary officials created a complex fiscal architecture that balanced central control with significant regional autonomy, a characteristic feature of Deccan Sultanate governance.

  • Common Traps:
  • Confusing the Ulema's formal legal and administrative role with the Sufi orders' spiritual and political legitimizing influence; they were distinct spheres of religious authority.
  • Assuming Mughal imperial ideology remained static from Akbar to Aurangzeb; it underwent significant shifts, particularly in its emphasis on religious inclusivity versus orthodoxy.
  • Overlooking the decentralized aspect of Vijayanagara's water management, attributing all initiatives solely to the central state rather than acknowledging local community and temple involvement.
  • Equating the Maratha Harkara system (espionage) entirely with the Dak system (postal and general intelligence); while both involved communication, Harkaras were specialized spies for strategic military intelligence.
  • Treating Deccan Sultanate's Amaram and Mokasa grants as identical to Mughal Jagirs or Maratha Saranjams without highlighting their specific regional characteristics and obligations.
  • Underestimating the hereditary power of Deshmukhs in the Deccan, viewing them merely as state functionaries rather than powerful local intermediaries with significant autonomy.

Mughal Succession Wars and the Transformation of Imperial Legitimacy

The Mughal Empire lacked a codified law of succession, operating instead on the Timurid principle of taqsim (division) modified by the Chinggisid notion that sovereignty belonged to the entire royal clan. This structural ambiguity produced four major succession wars (1530, 1605, 1627–28, 1657–59) that functioned as constitutional moments, each reshaping the relationship between the throne, the mansabdari corps, and the imperial ideology. The 1627–28 war following Jahangir's death saw Shah Jahan's faction deploy the dastur-ul-amal (administrative precedent) of Akbar's sulh-i-kul to legitimize his accession against Shahryar Mirza, who relied on Nur Jahan's faction and the support of key mansabdars like Asaf Khan. The 1657–59 war between Dara Shukoh, Aurangzeb, Shuja, and Murad was the most consequential: it transformed the mansabdari from a service nobility into a political constituency whose allegiance was purchased through accelerated promotions (ziyada), enhanced zat ranks, and the promise of jagir transfers to fertile khalisa lands. Aurangzeb's victory established a new legitimizing formula: the emperor as the zill-allah (shadow of God) who enforced sharia as state policy, replacing Akbar's sulh-i-kul with din-i-ilahi rhetoric repurposed for orthodox sovereignty. The wars also institutionalized the role of the mir bakhshi as the arbiter of mansabdar loyalty during transitions, and the diwan-i-kul as the fiscal gatekeeper who could authorize emergency treasury disbursements to rival claimants. By 1659, the succession war had become a mechanism for the centralization of fiscal-military authority, but at the cost of creating a mansabdari class that expected material rewards for political support—a dynamic that fueled the jagirdari crisis of the 1680s.

Succession Wars and Institutional Outcomes
WarClaimantsKey Institutional Shift

The Mughal Mint Network: Sarrafs, Mahajans, and the Integration of Regional Monetary Zones

Beyond the metallic standards set by the imperial mints at Delhi, Agra, Lahore, and Surat, the Mughal monetary system operated through a dense network of provincial mints (taksal) and private financial intermediaries that integrated disparate regional currency zones into a unified fiscal space. The empire maintained approximately 40–50 active mints at any given time, each headed by a darogha-i-taksal (mint master) appointed by the diwan-i-kul and supervised by a mushrif (auditor) and a sayrafi (assayer). The critical innovation was the sikka (die) distribution system: master dies were cut at the capital mint and dispatched to provincial mints under sealed orders, ensuring iconographic and metrological uniformity. However, the actual flow of specie depended on the sarraf (money-changer/banker) networks—caste-based communities (Agarwal, Oswal, Shrimali Mahajans) who operated hundis (bills of exchange), discounted revenue assignments, and provided bridging finance between harvest and revenue collection. The sarrafs maintained bahi (ledger) systems using the desi nama double-entry method, and their creditworthiness was guaranteed by the imperial qabuliat (acceptance) of their hundis at the treasury. In the Deccan, the Mughals encountered the hun (pagoda) and rupiya zones of the Golconda and Bijapur sultanates; integration was achieved not by immediate demonetization but by fixing the rupiya-hun exchange rate at 1:3.5 and allowing dual circulation until the Muhiuddin rupee (Aurangzeb's coin) displaced the Ikkeri and Golconda pagodas by the 1690s. The mahajans of Surat, Ahmedabad, and Patna acted as the nodal points where imperial revenue demand (payable in rupees) met peasant production (often in local copper dams or cowries), converting kind-revenue into specie through seasonal grain markets (mandis). This monetary architecture collapsed not from design failure but from the 1700s disruption of the sarraf networks by Maratha chauth exactions and the breakdown of hundi enforceability across political boundaries.

Examiner's Trap: Conflating Mint Policy with Monetary Integration
A question asks: "The uniformity of Mughal coinage proves a centralized monetary policy." Why is this statement incomplete?
Uniformity of sikka (die) and weight standard was enforced at the mint level, but monetary integration—the actual circulation of a common medium across regions—depended on the sarraf-mahajan credit networks, hundi enforceability, and the seasonal conversion of revenue-in-kind to specie at mandis. The empire could standardize the coin but not guarantee its velocity or acceptance without the private financial infrastructure. The collapse of hundi circuits in the 18th century, not mint debasement, ended monetary unity.
Exam Strategy: Trace the Money, Not Just the Coin
When a question mentions Mughal coinage, do not stop at the rupiya-dam ratio or the ilahi standard. Ask: who moved the coin? The sarraf, the mahajan, the hundi. The examiner tests whether you understand monetary integration as a financial system, not a metallurgical standard. Cite the qabuliat of hundis at the treasury and the rupiya-hun exchange rate in the Deccan as evidence of integration mechanisms.

Vijayanagara's Military Fiscalism: The Kandachara System and Nayaka Military Obligations

The Vijayanagara state's military capacity rested on a distinctive fiscal-military arrangement—the kandachara (or kandachara-ayam) system—that linked revenue assignments directly to troop maintenance obligations in a manner more rigid than the Mughal mansabdari or the Maratha saranjam. Under this system, the nayaka (territorial governor) received a kandachara grant comprising a defined territory (nayankara) whose assessed revenue (kattubadi or varam) was calibrated to support a stipulated contingent: typically 1,000–5,000 infantry, 500–2,000 cavalry, and a quota of elephants and artillery. The kandachara holder was required to present his contingent at the annual mahanavami muster (kandachara-pariksha) where the mahapradhani (prime minister) and the senapati verified troop numbers, horse quality (asva-pariksha), and equipment standards. Failure to meet the quota triggered revenue deductions (khandana) or reassignment of the nayankara. Crucially, the nayaka could not alienate the land; he held it as a conditional military fief (amaram in Telugu, kandachara in Kannada) revocable at the ruler's pleasure. The system generated a fiscal-military census: the rayavachakamu (royal survey) recorded village-wise cultivation, irrigation sources, and expected yield, which formed the basis for the kattubadi assessment. This data was maintained by the karanika (accountant) at the rajya (province) level and cross-checked against the nayaka's own kavalgars (watchmen) reports. The kandachara system's rigidity became a vulnerability after 1565 (Talikota): the loss of the Raichur Doab's fertile revenues reduced the kattubadi base, but troop quotas remained unchanged, forcing nayakas to extract surplus through coercion, accelerating the fragmentation into the independent Nayaka kingdoms of Madurai, Tanjavur, Gingee, and Ikkeri by the 1620s.

Maratha Judicial Institutions: The Nyayadhish, the Majlis, and Village Dispute Resolution

The Maratha state developed a layered judicial architecture that combined Brahminical dharma-shastra principles with pragmatic customary law (vyavahara) and the exigencies of a mobile military confederacy. At the apex stood the nyayadhish (chief justice), one of the ashta pradhan, who presided over the mahanyaya (high court) at the capital (Satara, later Pune). The nyayadhish was typically a learned Brahmin versed in Mitakshara (Yajnavalkya-smriti with Vijnaneshvara's commentary) and the Vyavahara-mayukha (Nilakantha Bhatta's digest), but his judgments were recorded in modi script Marathi, not Sanskrit, reflecting the vernacularization of legal procedure. Below the nyayadhish operated the majlis (court) at the prant (province) level, headed by the subhedar or karyat (deputy), which heard revenue disputes, succession claims, and criminal cases involving dand (fines) up to 100 hons. The majlis applied a hybrid code: shastric rules for caste, marriage, and inheritance; rajadharma (royal ordinances) for revenue, treason, and military offenses; and deshachara (local custom) for water rights, boundary disputes, and village policing. At the village level, the patil (headman) and kulkarni (accountant) convened the grama-sabha or panchayat of five elders (pancha) to resolve petty theft, encroachment, and caste discipline. The panchayat's verdict (nyaya-patra) was enforceable through social boycott (maher) and the patil's coercive power. A critical innovation was the farad (written decree) issued by the peshwa or chhatrapati that could override shastric precedent in revenue matters—e.g., the 1730 farad declaring chauth and sardeshmukhi as state dues not subject to dharma-shastra exemption claims by Brahmin grantees. The system's weakness lay in the absence of a professional judiciary: nyayadhish appointments were often political, and the majlis lacked independent enforcement, relying on the subhedar's military contingent. By the 1770s, the Peshwa's huzur daftar began maintaining nyaya-pustak (law reports) to standardize precedent, but the confederacy's fragmentation under the saranjam holders prevented a unified legal order.

The Mughal Akhbarat System: Waqai Navis, News Writers, and the Imperial Information Architecture

Distinct from the barid (postal relay) and the harkara (courier-spy) systems, the Mughal akhbarat (news reports) constituted a daily intelligence bureaucracy that funneled verified information from every subah to the emperor's jharokha (audience window) and the diwan-i-khas. The system operated through three tiers: the waqai navis (news writer) stationed at each sarkar and major pargana; the waqai nigar (news compiler) at the subah capital who collated, redacted, and dispatched the akhbarat under the subahdar's seal; and the mir akhbar (chief news officer) at the imperial court who presented the digested reports to the emperor each morning. The waqai navis was appointed by the diwan-i-kul on the mir bakhshi's recommendation, paid from the khalisa treasury, and required to submit a daily roznamcha (diary) covering: (1) military movements and mansabdar attendance; (2) revenue collections, arrears, and zabt measurements; (3) prices of grain, cloth, and metals in local mandis; (4) weather, crop conditions, and famine signs; (5) judicial decisions, riots, and notable deaths. The akhbarat were written in Persian using a standardized formulary (dastur-ul-amal) with coded classifications: khufia (secret), aam (public), hasb-ul-hukm (as per order). The emperor's marginalia (tuzuk) on the akhbarat constituted direct executive orders—e.g., Aurangzeb's 1681 marginal note on a Deccan akhbar reporting Maratha grain raids: "Order the faujdar of Ahmadnagar to secure the mandis and hang the ringleaders." The system's integrity depended on cross-verification: the waqai navis's report was checked against the harkara's independent dispatch and the kotwal's daily roz namcha. By the 1690s, the Deccan campaign's scale overwhelmed the waqai navis network; reports arrived weeks late, prices were falsified by mansabdars inflating jagir yields, and the mir akhbar at court became a filter for factional interests. The akhbarat archive (preserved in the National Archives, New Delhi, and the British Library's Mughal Records collection) remains the densest daily administrative record of any pre-modern empire.

Examiner's Trap: Confusing Akhbarat with Barid or Harkara
A question states: "The Mughal postal system (Barid) provided the emperor with daily intelligence on prices and military movements." Identify the error.
The barid was a relay courier system for official correspondence and firmans, not a daily intelligence feed. Daily intelligence on prices, military movements, and local conditions came from the akhbarat system staffed by waqai navis at the sarkar level. The harkara was a Maratha institution (specialized spy-courier), not Mughal. The Mughal equivalent of the harkara was the waqai navis combined with the kotwal's roz namcha. The examiner tests whether you distinguish the postal infrastructure (barid) from the intelligence bureaucracy (akhbarat).
Exam Strategy: Map the Information Flow
Draw the three-tier akhbarat flow: waqai navis (sarkar) → waqai nigar (subah) → mir akhbar (court). Memorize the five mandatory report categories (military, revenue, prices, weather, judicial). The examiner often asks: "Who appointed the waqai navis?" Answer: Diwan-i-kul on mir bakhshi's recommendation, paid from khalisa. This distinguishes the intelligence bureaucracy from the military (mir bakhshi) and revenue (diwan) hierarchies.
  • Assuming Mughal succession wars were merely personal rivalries; each war restructured the mansabdari's political economy and the imperial ideology's legitimizing vocabulary.
  • Equating coinage uniformity (standard weight, die) with monetary integration; the latter required functioning sarraf networks, hundi enforceability, and seasonal mandi conversion of revenue-in-kind.
  • Treating the Vijayanagara kandachara as identical to the Mughal mansabdari; the kandachara fixed troop quotas to specific territories with annual physical muster, while the mansabdari separated rank from territory and used branding (dagh) for verification.
  • Viewing Maratha justice as purely dharma-shastra based; the nyayadhish applied a hybrid of shastric, rajadharma (royal ordinance), and deshachara (custom), with the peshwa's farad overriding precedent in revenue matters.
  • Confusing the Mughal barid (postal relay) with the akhbarat (daily intelligence bureaucracy); the barid carried orders, the akhbarat produced verified daily reports on prices, troops, and weather through a dedicated waqai navis corps.
  • Overlooking the waqai navis's fiscal independence (paid from khalisa, not jagir) as a deliberate design to prevent mansabdar capture of the intelligence stream.
  • Assuming the kandachara system's rigidity was a strength; after Talikota (1565), fixed troop quotas on a shrinking revenue base forced nayakas into coercive extraction, accelerating the Nayaka kingdoms' secession.

The Bandagan Institution and Slave Sovereignty in the Delhi Sultanate

The Turkish slave corps (bandagan, sing. banda) constituted the political backbone of the early Delhi Sultanate, transforming military households into a ruling elite. Iltutmish (r. 1211–1236) systematised the institution by purchasing young Turkic slaves from Central Asian markets — primarily Qipchaq, Qarluq, and Khalaj groups — training them in cavalry warfare, Persian administration, and court etiquette within the royal household (khana-yi khass). These slaves, manumitted upon mastery, received iqta assignments and formed the Chihalgani (the Forty), a corporate body that dominated the sultanate's first half-century. The bandagan differed from domestic slaves (ghulam) in their exclusive military-administrative function and their path to manumission through service. Balban (r. 1266–1287), himself a former banda of Iltutmish, elevated the corps' prestige by restricting high office to men of slave origin, arguing that only those forged in the royal household possessed the loyalty (wafadari) required for command. The bandagan network operated through fictive kinship: a master's bandagan became the "sons" (farzandan) of the household, creating vertical loyalty chains that bypassed tribal affiliations. This structure fractured under the Khaljis. Jalal al-Din Khalji (r. 1290–1296) and Ala al-Din Khalji (r. 1296–1316) promoted non-Turkic groups — Khaljis, Indian Muslims (hindustani), and Mongols (mughal) — diluting the bandagan monopoly. Ala al-Din's market reforms and standing army (lashkar) paid from the treasury (khazana) reduced dependence on iqta-holding slave commanders. The Tughluqs inherited a hybrid system: Muhammad bin Tughluq (r. 1325–1351) recruited widely from Persian, Afghan, and Indian Muslim backgrounds, while Firuz Shah Tughluq (r. 1351–1388) institutionalised hereditary iqta and created a separate slave corps (ghulaman-i khass) of 180,000, funded by a dedicated tax (khums on war booty). The bandagan legacy persisted in the Deccan: the Bahmani Sultanate's afaqi (foreign) nobility replicated the Turkish slave model, while the Vijayanagara nayaka system evolved a distinct indigenous equivalent. Examiner's Trap: The Chihalgani was not a fixed council of forty; its membership fluctuated, and the term denotes the bandagan elite as a corporate identity, not a constitutional body. Confusing the bandagan with the Mamluk Sultanate of Egypt overlooks the Delhi corps' tighter integration into the sultan's household and its lack of a self-perpetuating oligarchy — Delhi's bandagan never seized the sultanate for themselves, unlike their Cairene counterparts.

Mughal Imperial Painting: The Atelier System, Collaborative Production, and Jahangir's Naturalism

The Mughal kitabkhana (library-atelier) established under Akbar (r. 1556–1605) functioned as a state-sponsored workshop where Persian masters — notably Mir Sayyid Ali and Abd al-Samad, brought from Shah Tahmasp's court — trained Indian painters drawn from Gujarat, Gwalior, and the Punjab hills. The atelier operated on a collaborative model: a single folio passed through multiple hands — tarh (compositional drawing by a master), amal (colour application by junior artists), chehra-nigari (portraiture by specialists), and mohra-kashi (illumination and marginal decoration). The Akbarnama (c. 1590–1595) and Hamzanama (14 volumes, c. 1562–1577) exemplify this division of labour; the latter employed over a hundred artists and took fifteen years. Jahangir (r. 1605–1627) transformed the atelier's aesthetic priorities. His Jahangirnama records direct imperial intervention: the emperor assigned subjects, corrected drawings, and graded painters into hierarchies — ustad (master), shagird (apprentice), and kamin (assistant). Jahangir's fascination with natural history produced the Muraqqa-i Gulshan and Muraqqa-i Jahangiri albums, where Ustad Mansur's zoological studies (the dodo, the Siberian crane, the turkey cock) achieved taxonomic precision unprecedented in Islamic painting. Abu'l Hasan, titled Nadir al-Zaman (Wonder of the Age), mastered European techniques — chiaroscuro, linear perspective, and sfumato — acquired through Jesuit prints and English portraits. The durbar scenes of the Padshahnama (Shah Jahan's reign) codified a rigid iconography: the emperor on the jharokha balcony, haloed (farr-i izadi), framed by ranked nobles whose portraiture followed a physiognomic convention — Rajputs with upturned moustaches, Persians with tapered beards, Deccani Africans (habshi) with distinct features. Shah Jahan's atelier emphasised architectural settings and jewel-like colouration; Aurangzeb's disinterest caused dispersal, though provincial courts (Murshidabad, Lucknow, Faizabad) inherited the Mughal palette. The atelier's paper supply — wasli (laminated sheets), siyah qalam (black ink drawings), and European imported stock — reveals trade links. Pigment analysis confirms lapis lazuli (Afghanistan), vermilion (China), indigo (India), and orpiment (Central Asia). Examiner's Trap: Attributing the Hamzanama solely to Persian masters ignores the Indian artists who constituted the majority workforce and introduced local conventions — red horizons, lotus-petal eyes, and narrative simultaneity — into the Persianate framework. The "Mughal style" is a synthesis, not an import.

Examiner's Trap: Collaborative Attribution
A question asks: "Ustad Mansur's dodo painting (c. 1625) demonstrates Jahangir's personal interest in natural history. Identify the flaw in attributing this work solely to Mansur."
The flaw is ignoring the atelier's collaborative protocol. Mansur executed the chehra-nigari (faunal portraiture), but the folio's tarh (composition) was likely supervised by Abu'l Hasan or Aqa Riza, the marginal mohra-kashi by illumination specialists, and the paper preparation by wasli makers. Jahangir's own marginal notes (tuzuk) on the album page confirm he reviewed the work, but the physical production involved at least four named artisans recorded in the kitabkhana registers (preserved in the Royal Library, Windsor, and the National Museum, New Delhi). The examiner tests whether you understand amal as collective labour, not individual genius.
Exam Strategy: Atelier Hierarchies
Memorise the four-stage production chain: tarh → amal → chehra-nigari → mohra-kashi. Link each emperor to a diagnostic shift: Akbar = narrative manuscripts (Akbarnama, Hamzanama); Jahangir = album format (muraqqa), naturalism, European technique; Shah Jahan = formal durbar iconography, architectural precision; Aurangzeb = decline, provincial dispersal. Know two named artists per reign: Akbar — Basawan, Daswanth; Jahangir — Mansur, Abu'l Hasan, Bishandas; Shah Jahan — Payag, Balchand. The examiner often asks which painter specialised in zoological studies (Mansur) or which introduced chiaroscuro (Abu'l Hasan via Jesuit prints).

Vijayanagara Monetary System: The Varaha Standard, Temple Treasury, and Maritime Exchange

Vijayanagara's currency operated on a bimetallic standard anchored by the gold varaha (also hon or gadyana), weighing approximately 3.4 grams of 18–20 carat gold, and the silver fanam (or phanam), weighing 0.35–0.40 grams. The varaha bore the legend Sri Virupaksha (the dynastic deity) on the obverse and the king's title — Sri Pratapa Krishnaraya or similar — on the reverse, struck at multiple mints: Hampi (capital), Penukonda, Chandragiri, Vellore, and coastal ports (Bhatkal, Honavar, Mangalore). The exchange ratio fluctuated seasonally: 1 varaha = 10–12 fanam in the interior, but 1 varaha = 15–16 fanam at ports where silver influx from Portuguese carreira da India shipments depressed silver's value. Copper jital (or kasu) facilitated petty trade; 48 jital = 1 fanam. The temple of Virupaksha at Hampi functioned as a de facto central bank: its treasury (bhandara) held bullion reserves, accepted deposits from merchants (nagarattar, chetti), issued hundi-like receipts (olai), and minted coin under royal licence. The kavalgars (temple guards) escorted bullion convoys between the capital and coastal mints. Maritime trade dictated monetary policy: the Portuguese cartaz system required Vijayanagara merchants to pay customs in silver fanam or Portuguese tanga, creating a dual-currency zone in the Kanara ports. The 1565 Talikota defeat disrupted the gold supply from the Raichur Doab mines; post-1570 coinage shows debasement — varaha weight dropped to 3.1 grams, gold content to 16 carat — and the Nayaka successor states (Madurai, Tanjavur, Gingee, Ikkeri) issued their own varaha variants with distinct legends (Sri Meenakshi, Sri Rajagopala). The fanam remained stable longer, becoming the Deccan's de facto trade coin into the 18th century, imitated by the Bijapur Sultanate (fanam with Adil Shahi legend) and the English East India Company's Madras mint (1670s fanam with Company bale mark). Examiner's Trap: The varaha was not a fiduciary token; its value derived from metallic content verified by sarrafs (money-changers) using touchstones (kasauti) at every transaction. Confusing the Vijayanagara varaha with the Chola kasu or the Mughal mohur (10.95 grams, 99.9% gold) ignores the varaha's lighter weight and lower purity, calibrated for South Indian price levels and maritime exchange.

Maratha Land Tenure: Miras Rights, Upri Cultivators, and the Watandari Hierarchy

The Maratha revenue terminology distinguishes three cultivator categories whose legal status determined fiscal extraction and political allegiance. Mirasdars (from miras, hereditary right) held permanent, alienable, and partible occupancy rights in specific villages (mauza). Their tenure derived from ancestral clearance (jungle katni) or royal grant (sanad), recorded in the miras patra. They paid a fixed revenue (miras bhag) — typically 25–33% of the estimated produce — directly to the state or to a saranjamdar (grant holder), and could not be evicted so long as payment continued. Miras land was transferable by sale, mortgage, or gift, creating a land market documented in qabuliyat (acceptance deeds) and kararnama (agreement deeds) preserved in the Pune Daftar. Upri (or upari) cultivators were non-resident tenants who leased land annually (salana) from mirasdars or the state, paying a higher share — 40–50% — with no hereditary claim. They migrated seasonally from the Deccan plateau to the Konkan and vice versa, following rainfall; their contracts (patta) specified the crop, the share, and the kud (threshing floor) where division occurred. The watandari hierarchy superimposed hereditary office on this tenure: the patil (village headman), kulkarni (accountant), chaugule (assistant headman), mahar (village watchman, traditionally from the Mahar caste), and joshi (astrologer-priest) held watan (hereditary service grants) — typically 2–5% of village land revenue-free (inam) plus perquisites (hak). Watan was partible among sons but inalienable outside the lineage; disputes were adjudicated by the mamlatdar (district officer) under the wajib-ul-arz (customary record). The Peshwa's huzur daftar maintained the miras register (miras bahi) and the watan register (watan bahi), updated at each decennial settlement (peshkash). Shivaji's 1674 coronation rajyabhisheka confirmed existing miras and watan rights, but the 18th-century expansion into Malwa, Gujarat, and Bundelkhand imposed Maratha tenure categories on regions with distinct systems (Mughal zabt, Rajput bhaichara), creating hybrid records — e.g., the kamal survey in Malwa (1730s) blended miras classification with Mughal bigha measurement. Examiner's Trap: Miras is not equivalent to the Mughal khudkasht (self-cultivating peasant); khudkasht denotes cultivation status, miras denotes property right. A mirasdar could be an absentee landlord leasing to upri tenants, while a khudkasht cultivator in the Mughal system had no alienable property right, only occupancy protected by custom.

The Bhakti Sampradayas: Institutional Organisation, Matha Networks, and Political Negotiation

Between the 13th and 17th centuries, the major bhakti sampradayas (sectarian traditions) developed durable institutional forms — mathas (monasteries), gaddis (pontifical seats), and peethas (regional centres) — that negotiated sovereignty with Sultanate, Mughal, Vijayanagara, and Maratha rulers. The Sri Vaishnava mathas of the Ramanuja lineage (Srirangam, Kanchipuram, Melkote) held devadana (temple land grants) from Chola, Pandya, and Vijayanagara kings; their jeeyars (pontiffs) administered temple rituals, managed agrarian estates, and arbitrated caste disputes through sattada (assembly) courts.
