The Madhva mathas (Udupi's eight ashta mathas, founded c. 13th century) operated a rotational paryaya system — each matha governed the Krishna temple for two years — creating a corporate governance model that resisted royal appropriation. In the north, the Ramanandi akhadas (militant ascetic orders) organised into anis (regiments) — Digambar, Nirvani, Nirmohi — with mahants controlling pilgrimage routes, fair revenues (mela), and armed retainers (naga sadhus). The Mughal state engaged them strategically: Akbar granted madad-i maash (revenue-free land) to Ramanandi mahants at Ayodhya and Chitrakoot; Jahangir's tuzuk records debates with the Ramanandi leader Ramdas; Aurangzeb's 1669 order restricting matha land grants triggered the 1680s Ramanandi militarisation. The Varkari tradition (Pandharpur, Maharashtra) avoided institutional hierarchy — no matha, no gaddi — relying on the annual vari (pilgrimage) and the abhanga corpus of Jnanadev, Namdev, Eknath, and Tukaram to sustain cohesion. Shivaji's 1674 coronation invoked the Varkari saint Tukaram's blessing, but the Peshwas later patronised the Smarta mathas (Sringeri, Kanchi) for Brahminical legitimacy. The Sikh takhts (Akal Takht, Patna, Hazur Sahib, Anandpur, Damdama) evolved from Guru Hargobind's miri-piri doctrine into a parallel sovereignty structure, with the Sarbat Khalsa (biannual assembly at Amritsar) issuing gurmata (collective resolutions) binding on all misls. The Lingayat mathas (Karnataka) and the Mahanubhava peethas (Maharashtra) maintained endogamous priesthoods and distinct ritual codes, negotiating inam grants from Bahmani, Adil Shahi, and Maratha rulers. Examiner's Trap: The matha was not a retreat from politics; its landholdings, judicial authority, and pilgrimage control made it a fiscal-military node. The examiner often conflates bhakti as purely devotional; the akhada and takht demonstrate armed institutional power. Do not equate the Varkari vari with the Kumbh Mela; the former is a linear, annual, single-deity procession; the latter is a cyclical, multi-sectarian gathering at rotating sites.

  • Confusing the Delhi Sultanate's bandagan with the Mamluk Sultanate of Egypt's self-perpetuating military oligarchy; Delhi's slave corps never seized the throne for itself.
  • Attributing Mughal manuscript paintings to a single named master; the atelier's tarh–amal–chehra-nigari–mohra-kashi chain involved four or more specialists per folio.
  • Treating the Vijayanagara varaha as a fiduciary token; its value was metallic, verified by sarrafs using touchstones at every transaction.
  • Equating Maratha miras tenure with Mughal khudkasht cultivation; miras is an alienable property right, khudkasht is a non-alienable occupancy status.
  • Viewing bhakti sampradayas as apolitical devotional movements; mathas, akhadas, and takhts controlled land, arms, and judicial authority, negotiating sovereignty with every major state.
  • Assuming the Chihalgani was a fixed constitutional council of forty; it was a fluctuating corporate identity of the bandagan elite.
  • Conflating the Varkari vari with the Kumbh Mela; the vari is an annual, linear, single-deity pilgrimage to Pandharpur, the Kumbh a cyclical, multi-sectarian gathering at four rotating sites.

Mughal Imperial Ceremonial: The Jharokha Darshan, the Durbar, and the Symbolic Legitimacy of the Emperor

The daily Jharokha Darshan was the most visible ritual through which the Mughal emperor projected his presence to the populace. From a stone balcony (jharokha) facing the river or the main thoroughfare of the capital, the emperor appeared at sunrise, allowing subjects to offer nazr (tribute) and receive darshan (auspicious sight). The ceremony was institutionalised by Akbar in 1574 at Fatehpur Sikri and continued at Agra and Delhi. It served three intertwined functions: legitimising the ruler’s divine light (farr-i-izadi), providing a channel for petitions, and reinforcing the hierarchical order by requiring nobles to perform kornish (deep bow) and sijda (prostration) in the emperor’s presence.

The Durbar (court assembly) operated on two levels. The Diwan-i-Aam (Hall of Public Audience) hosted the general nobility, petitioners, and foreign envoys; the Diwan-i-Khas (Hall of Private Audience) was reserved for high mansabdars, the Mir Bakhshi, and the Wazir. Under Shah Jahan the Diwan-i-Khas acquired the Peacock Throne, its canopy inscribed with the Quranic verse “And He is the Supreme Sovereign”, turning the space into a visual manifesto of imperial authority. The ritualised exchange of khilat (robes of honour) and sarpech (turban ornaments) during these assemblies codified rank and loyalty.

Aurangzeb’s reign marks a decisive break. In 1679 he discontinued the Jharokha Darshan, citing its incompatibility with Islamic orthodoxy, and replaced the public appearance with a more secluded Jharokha-i-Azam for select nobles. The Durbar continued but lost its performative openness; the emperor’s visibility shifted to the Mujlis-i-Khas (private councils) and the Maktab Khana (translation bureau) where he projected scholarly piety. This transformation is a frequent examiner focus: the shift from a solar, universalist symbolism to a more confessional, text‑based legitimacy.

Key Comparative Points

  • Jharokha Darshan — daily, public, solar symbolism; abolished 1679.
  • Diwan-i-Aam — open petitioning, hierarchical bowing.
  • Diwan-i-Khas — elite political negotiation, Peacock Throne iconography.
  • British Imperial Durbar (1877, 1903, 1911) — staged pageantry for colonial legitimacy, not a daily ritual.

Mughal Imperial Music Patronage: The Naqqar Khana, the Court Musicians, and the Development of Hindustani Classical Traditions

The Naqqar Khana (drum house) stood at the main gate of every major Mughal palace — Agra, Delhi, Lahore — and sounded the naqqara (kettle drums) to announce the emperor’s movements, the beginning of the Jharokha Darshan, and the hours of prayer. Beyond its ceremonial role, the Naqqar Khana housed a permanent ensemble of tabla, pakhavaj, shehnai, and sarangi players who formed the core of the court’s musical establishment.

Akbar’s patronage crystallised around the figure of Tansen (c. 1500–1586), a dhrupad vocalist from Gwalior who was granted the title Mian and a mansab of 2,000. Tansen’s compositions (Raga Miyan ki Todi, Raga Darbari Kanada) became benchmarks for the dhrupad tradition. His contemporaries — Baiju Bawra, Ramdas, and the instrumentalist Lal Khan — expanded the repertoire, integrating Persian maqam structures into Indian raga grammar. Jahangir’s memoirs (Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri) record daily concerts in the Diwan-i-Khas and the emperor’s personal skill on the veena.

Under Shah Jahan the court music reached its zenith: the Sangeet Ratnakar was translated into Persian, and the Rag Darpan (Mirror of Ragas) compiled by the noble Fazlullah Khan systematised 120 ragas with their time‑theory (prahar) and emotional rasa. The khayal form, more flexible than dhrupad, began to gain currency through musicians such as Niamat Khan (Sadarang) and Firoz Khan (Adarang), who served the later Mughal emperors Muhammad Shah (r. 1719–1748) and Ahmad Shah.

Aurangzeb’s personal austerity curtailed large‑scale patronage, yet he did not ban music outright; the Naqqar Khana continued to function for state ceremonies, and a few musicians retained mansabs. The decline of central patronage after 1707 dispersed court musicians to regional centres — Lucknow, Hyderabad, Jaipur — where they seeded the distinct gharanas (lineages) that dominate Hindustani classical music today.

Examiner‑Relevant Distinctions

  • Dhrupad — rigid, devotional, court‑centric; patronised by Akbar–Shah Jahan.
  • Khayal — improvisatory, romantic, flourished under Muhammad Shah.
  • Naqqar Khana — institutionalised ensemble, not a soloist’s platform.
  • Regional gharanas — post‑Mughal diffusion, not direct imperial creation.
Comparative Analysis of Court Rituals
Compare the symbolic function of the Jharokha Darshan in the Mughal court with the British Imperial Durbar of 1877. Highlight two structural differences and one continuity.
Structural Difference 1: The Jharokha Darshan was a daily, spontaneous interaction between emperor and populace, reinforcing the ruler’s immediate accessibility. The 1877 Durbar was a one‑off, choreographed spectacle designed for colonial propaganda and the proclamation of Queen Victoria as Empress of India. Structural Difference 2: Mughal ritual required physical prostration (sijda) and hierarchical bowing (kornish) from nobles, embedding feudal loyalty. The British Durbar substituted oaths of allegiance and medal presentations, reflecting a bureaucratic, contractual loyalty. Continuity: Both ceremonies employed visual iconography — the Peacock Throne and the imperial canopy for the Mughals; the Viceroy’s throne, the Crown Jewels, and the royal standard for the British — to materialise sovereign authority in a public space.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the chronological sequence of ceremonial changes: Akbar institutionalised Jharokha Darshan (1574); Jahangir added the Jharokha‑i‑Azam; Shah Jahan expanded the Diwan‑i‑Khas with the Peacock Throne; Aurangzeb abolished Jharokha Darshan (1679). Use the mnemonic “A‑J‑S‑A” (Akbar‑Jahangir‑Shah Jahan‑Aurangzeb) to recall the four turning points. For music, link each emperor to a signature genre: Akbar–Tansen–dhrupad; Jahangir–veena; Shah Jahan–Rag Darpan; Muhammad Shah–khayal/gharana formation.

Mughal Imperial Gardens and Hydraulic Engineering: The Charbagh Layout, Water Channels, and the Politics of Paradise Imagery

The Mughal charbagh (four‑quarter garden) translated the Quranic description of paradise — rivers of water, milk, wine, and honey — into a terrestrial geometry of intersecting water channels (nahar) dividing a square enclosure into four equal plots. The design originated in Timurid Central Asia and was adapted to Indian topography by Babur, who laid out the Aram Bagh at Agra (1528) using the Yamuna’s floodplain. Subsequent emperors refined the hydraulic system: Akbar’s Shalimar Bagh at Kashmir (1619) employed a gravity‑fed cascade fed by the Lidder River; Shah Jahan’s Shalimar Bagh at Lahore (1641) introduced a marble chini khana (water pavilion) with perforated screens that atomised water into mist, enhancing the sensory evocation of jannat.

Water engineering was overseen by the Miran (chief hydraulic engineer), a mansabdar responsible for surveying gradients, constructing qanats (underground channels), and maintaining the hauz (reservoirs). The Ain-i-Akbari records the allocation of 1,200 bighas of land for the Nahr-i-Bihisht (River of Paradise) feeding the gardens of Fatehpur Sikri. The channels were lined with burnt brick and sealed with lime mortar to minimise seepage; sluice gates (band) regulated flow for irrigation of adjacent khalisa (crown) lands, linking aesthetic display to fiscal productivity.

Gardens also served political functions. The Diwan-i-Khas at the Red Fort opens onto the Hayat Bakhsh Bagh, where the emperor received foreign envoys amid fountains symbolising imperial bounty. During the 1658 succession war, Aurangzeb’s forces seized the Shalimar Bagh at Delhi to control the water supply to the Red Fort, demonstrating the strategic value of hydraulic infrastructure. After 1707, the neglect of the Miran’s office led to siltation of channels, and many gardens reverted to agricultural fields — a physical marker of imperial decline.

Key Technical Terms

  • Charbagh — quadripartite garden with axial water channels.
  • Nahar — surface water channel; often lined with brick.
  • Qanat — underground tunnel tapping groundwater.
  • Miran — imperial hydraulic engineer, mansabdar rank.
  • Chini khana — marble water pavilion with perforated screens.

Mughal Imperial Relations with the English East India Company: The 1717 Farman, the Grant of Diwani, and the Shift to Commercial Sovereignty

The Mughal‑English encounter evolved from a series of trade concessions into a transfer of fiscal sovereignty. The 1615 Treaty of Surat (already noted) granted the English a factory at Surat and exemption from customs duties in Gujarat. A century later, Emperor Farrukhsiyar issued the Farman of 1717 (often called the “Magna Carta of the Company”) after the Company’s surgeon, William Hamilton, treated the emperor’s ailment. The farman confirmed duty‑free trade for the Company in Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa, permitted the minting of Company coins at Murshidabad, and authorised the purchase of 38 villages around Calcutta (the “Zamindari of Calcutta”). Crucially, it did not confer territorial jurisdiction; the Company remained a commercial entity under Mughal suzerainty.

The turning point arrived after the Battle of Plassey (1757), where Robert Clive secured the installation of Mir Jafar as Nawab of Bengal. The subsequent Treaty of Allahabad (1765), negotiated by Clive with Emperor Shah Alam II, granted the Company the Diwani (revenue‑collection rights) of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa in return for an annual tribute of 26 lakh rupees. This transformed the Company from a merchant into the de facto fiscal sovereign of the richest Mughal province. The Diwani was formalised through a sanad bearing the imperial seal, and the Company appointed its own dewans (revenue officers) — notably Raja Shitab Rai and later Warren Hastings — to administer the zabt assessments.

The shift from commercial privilege to territorial revenue authority is a classic examiner trap. The 1717 farman is frequently misread as a grant of political power; in reality it only expanded trade immunities. The Diwani, by contrast, gave the Company control over the malguzari (land revenue) machinery, enabling it to finance its military expansion without recourse to London. The Mughal emperor retained nominal overlordship — Shah Alam II continued to issue farmans and appoint subahdars — but the fiscal sinews of empire had passed to a joint‑stock company.

Chronology of Concessions

  • 1615 — Treaty of Surat: factory at Surat, customs exemption.
  • 1717 — Farman of Farrukhsiyar: duty‑free trade in Bengal, minting rights, 38 villages.
  • 1757 — Battle of Plassey: Mir Jafar installed, Company gains political foothold.
  • 1765 — Treaty of Allahabad: Diwani of Bengal, Bihar, Orissa granted.

Mughal Imperial Frontier Policy in the Northwest: The Subah of Kabul, the Khyber Pass, and the Tribal Subsidy System

The Mughal northwest frontier was organised around the Subah of Kabul, established by Akbar in 1586 after the conquest of the Kabul Sultanate. The subah comprised the districts (sarkars) of Kabul, Peshawar, Bangash, and Swat, each governed by a faujdar responsible for military defence and revenue collection. The Khyber Pass — the principal corridor between the Indian plateau and Central Asia — was secured by a chain of forts (Attock, Jamrud, Ali Masjid) garrisoned by ahadi (gentleman troopers) and dakhili (central‑recruit) cavalry.

Direct administration of the Pashtun tribes proved impossible; the Mughals therefore instituted a tribal subsidy system. Leading maliks (tribal chiefs) received jagirs or cash allowances (tankhwah) in exchange for guaranteeing safe passage of caravans, providing levies for imperial campaigns, and suppressing raids on settled lands. The Ain-i-Akbari records an annual outlay of 12 lakh rupees for the “Khyber tribes” — a figure that rose under Jahangir and Shah Jahan as the empire sought to secure the route for the annual qafila (caravan) carrying tribute from Central Asia.

The system functioned while the central treasury was robust. After the 1739 invasion of Nader Shah, the Mughal hold on Kabul collapsed; the province was ceded to the Persian empire in 1738‑39. The loss of the subsidy network exposed the Punjab to Afghan incursions (Ahmad Shah Durrani’s invasions, 1748‑1767) and accelerated the fiscal crisis that undermined Mughal authority in the core provinces. The frontier policy thus illustrates the empire’s reliance on indirect rule and the fragility of a system predicated on continuous cash flows.

Administrative Mechanics

  • Subahdar of Kabul — overall military‑civil authority, usually a high mansabdar.
  • Faujdar of Peshawar — frontier defence, liaison with tribal maliks.
  • Malik — tribal chief receiving jagir or tankhwah; not a Mughal officer.
  • Qafila — state‑protected caravan; revenue from transit duties funded subsidies.
  • Confusing the daily Jharokha Darshan with the occasional British Imperial Durbar; the former was a routine legitimacy ritual, the latter a staged pageantry.
  • Attributing the creation of Hindustani classical gharanas directly to Mughal emperors; gharanas crystallised after 1707 when court musicians migrated to regional courts.
  • Assuming the charbagh garden was purely ornamental; its hydraulic channels simultaneously irrigated crown lands and generated revenue.
  • Reading the 1717 Farman as a grant of territorial sovereignty; it only extended trade privileges and minting rights.
  • Believing the Mughals directly administered Pashtun tribal territories; they relied on a subsidy‑based indirect rule through maliks.
  • Equating the Diwani of 1765 with the earlier farman; the Diwani transferred fiscal administration, the farman did not.
  • Overlooking the strategic loss of Kabul in 1738‑39 as a turning point that severed the subsidy chain and opened the Punjab to Afghan invasions.

The Tughlaq Token Currency Experiment: Design, Circulation, and Collapse

In 1329‑30 Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlaq ordered the minting of a bronze‑copper token coinage (the tanka) that was declared legal tender at par with the silver tanka of 178 grains. The experiment was modelled on the Chinese jiaozi paper notes described by travellers such as Ibn Battuta, but the Sultan chose metal tokens because the Delhi mint lacked a reliable paper‑production infrastructure. Coins were struck at the Delhi, Daulatabad, and Gujarat mints; each bore the legend “Sultan al‑Azam Muhammad Shah” and a nominal value of one silver tanka. The state compelled merchants, revenue officials, and the army to accept the tokens for all transactions, and the treasury promised to redeem them on demand.

The fiscal motive was twofold: to replenish a depleted silver reserve after the costly Deccan campaigns and to finance the forced migration of the capital to Daulatabad. However, the token issue suffered from three structural flaws. First, the intrinsic metal value of the bronze pieces was roughly one‑twentieth of the silver tanka, creating an enormous arbitrage incentive for counterfeiters. Second, the redemption promise was not backed by a credible reserve; the treasury held insufficient silver to honour mass conversions. Third, the administrative machinery for monitoring circulation — the diwan‑i‑wizarat and the mushrif (accountant) — was overwhelmed by the volume of token transactions across a realm that stretched from the Punjab to the Coromandel coast.

Within eighteen months the market flooded with spurious pieces. Contemporary chroniclers (Ziauddin Barani, Tarikh‑i‑Firuz Shahi) record that merchants refused the tokens, hoarded silver, and resorted to barter. The Sultan’s attempt to enforce acceptance through the kotwal and the faujdar led to riots in Delhi and Gujarat. By 1333 the token coinage was officially withdrawn; the state announced a buy‑back at a steep discount, further eroding confidence. The episode forced a return to the traditional silver‑copper bimetallic standard and contributed to the fiscal crisis that precipitated the Sultan’s later reforms, including the ill‑fated Doab revenue experiment.

For examiners, the token experiment illustrates the limits of fiat money in a pre‑modern agrarian economy where trust rests on metallic convertibility and on a credible fiscal backstop. It also shows how a centralised minting authority can be undermined by the absence of a uniform verification system — a point that resurfaces in later Mughal coinage reforms under Akbar.

The Mughal Naval Policy in the Indian Ocean: Fleet Organization, Portuguese Rivalry, and the 1615 Surat Treaty

Although the Mughal Empire is primarily remembered as a land power, from Akbar’s reign onward a modest but functional naval establishment existed to protect the western seaboard, secure the annual Hajj caravans, and project power in the Bay of Bengal. The fleet was placed under the Mir Bahr (Admiral), a mansabdar of 5,000 zat who reported directly to the emperor. Shipyards at Surat, Khambhat, and later at Dhaka produced ghurabs (large ocean‑going vessels of 300‑500 tons), gallivats (smaller coastal craft), and patamar (riverine boats). Crews were recruited from the Koli and Mappila maritime communities, and officers were often drawn from the ahadi (gentleman‑trooper) corps.

The primary strategic concern was the Portuguese Estado da Índia, which controlled the sea lanes from Goa to Hormuz and levied cartaz passes on all merchant shipping. Akbar’s 1573 diplomatic mission to Goa secured a temporary cessation of Portuguese interference with Mughal pilgrim ships, but the Portuguese continued to seize Mughal‑flagged vessels that lacked a cartaz. In response, Jahangir authorised the construction of a dedicated squadron at Surat (1612) and appointed the experienced admiral Rui Freire de Andrade (a Portuguese renegade) as Mir Bahr. The squadron escorted the annual qafila of pilgrim ships to Jeddah and patrolled the Gulf of Cambay.

The 1615 Treaty of Surat, negotiated by the English factor Thomas Roe on behalf of the Mughal court, formalised a limited maritime understanding: the Portuguese agreed to recognise Mughal sovereignty over the port of Surat and to refrain from seizing Mughal ships bearing the imperial seal, while the Mughals permitted Portuguese merchants to trade at Surat under the same customs regime as other foreign traders. The treaty did not cede territorial waters; it merely established a diplomatic protocol for the issuance of firmans authorising safe passage. Nevertheless, the Portuguese retained de facto control of the high seas, and Mughal naval capacity never matched the Portuguese carrack fleet.

Under Shah Jahan the navy was expanded to support the Bengal campaign (1632‑36), where riverine gallivats played a decisive role in the siege of Hugli and the capture of the Portuguese fort at Bandel. The fleet also transported the imperial treasury and the Mansabdari pay‑rolls to the Deccan front. By Aurangzeb’s reign the naval establishment had atrophied; the Mir Bahr post became honorary, and the empire relied on European‑chartered vessels for long‑distance trade. The decline mirrors the broader fiscal contraction that limited the state’s ability to maintain capital‑intensive shipbuilding.

Key take‑aways for the exam: the Mughal navy was a state‑run, mansabdari‑integrated force; its primary role was coastal defence and pilgrim protection, not blue‑water power projection; the 1615 Surat Treaty was a diplomatic arrangement, not a concession of maritime sovereignty; and the navy’s fortunes rose and fell with the central treasury’s health.

Technique: Distinguishing Diplomatic Protocol from Territorial Concession
A question asks: “The 1615 Treaty of Surat granted the Portuguese territorial control over the port of Surat.” True or False? Explain.
False. The treaty recognised Mughal sovereignty over Surat and regulated the issuance of firmans for safe passage. It allowed Portuguese merchants to trade under the same customs duties as other foreigners but did not transfer territorial rights. The Portuguese retained de facto naval dominance, yet the legal title to the port remained with the Mughal emperor. The examiner tests whether the candidate conflates a commercial‑diplomatic agreement with a cession of territory.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions a Mughal‑European treaty, first identify the party that issued the farman (the Mughal emperor) and the nature of the grant (trade privileges, minting rights, or revenue rights). Territorial sovereignty is only transferred in a sanad that explicitly mentions diwani or subahdari rights. Mark the distinction in the margin to avoid the common trap of reading commercial treaties as political concessions.

The Deccan Sultanates’ Persianate Bureaucracy: The Dabir, the Munshi, and the Central Diwan

The successor states of the Bahmani kingdom — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — inherited a Persianate administrative vocabulary that shaped their central and provincial governance. At the apex stood the Wazir (prime minister), who supervised the Diwan‑i‑Kul (central finance department). The Diwan was staffed by a hierarchy of Dabir (secretaries) and Munshi (clerks) who drafted farmans, maintained the daftar‑i‑mal (revenue registers), and corresponded with the Mughal court in Persian.

The Dabir‑i‑Khas (private secretary) held the sultan’s seal and authenticated all royal orders. In Bijapur, the celebrated Dabir‑i‑Khas Mulla Muhammad Qasim (d. 1625) compiled the Tarikh‑i‑Bijapur and managed the diplomatic correspondence with the Safavid and Ottoman courts. The Munshi cadre, often recruited from the Kayastha and Khatri scribal communities, kept the bahi (ledger) of land grants (inam, jagir) and the peshkash (tribute) accounts of subordinate chiefs. Their records were written in a stylised Persian chancery hand (nastaʿlīq) that became the standard for Deccani official documents.

Provincial administration mirrored the centre: each sarkar (district) was overseen by a Faujdar (military governor) assisted by a Diwan‑i‑Sarkar who reported to the central Diwan‑i‑Kul. The Kulkarni (village accountant) operated under the Munshi’s supervision, recording the kharaj (land tax) and the zakat (alms tax) collections. The Deccani sultanates also employed a distinct class of Habshi (Abyssinian) eunuchs as Mir Bahr (admiral) and Mir Atish (master of artillery), but the civilian bureaucracy remained Persianate.

Fiscal instruments such as the tanka (silver coin) and the dam (copper coin) were minted under the supervision of the Diwan‑i‑Kul, which set the metallic standard in coordination with the Sarraf (money‑changer) guilds. The Diwan also managed the ijara (revenue farming) contracts, granting ijaradars the right to collect kharaj in return for a fixed peshkash to the treasury. This system pre‑figured the Mughal ijaradari but retained a stronger role for the Dabir in auditing the farmers’ accounts.

The Persianate bureaucracy facilitated cultural exchange: the Dabir corps patronised Persian poetry (e.g., the Gulshan‑i‑Iqbal of Bijapur) and commissioned illustrated manuscripts such as the Nujum‑al‑Ulum. However, the reliance on a narrow scribal elite limited the penetration of vernacular administrative languages (Marathi, Kannada, Telugu) into the revenue apparatus, a factor that later aided the Maratha and Mughal annexations when they introduced their own record‑keeping systems.

The Ahom Land Grant System: Khel, Satra, and the Integration of Brahmaputra Valley Agrarian Networks

The Ahom kingdom (1228‑1826) developed a distinctive land‑grant framework that bound the peasantry, the nobility, and the Vaishnavite monastic institutions (satra) into a cohesive agrarian order. The basic territorial unit was the khel, a cluster of villages (typically 10‑30) administered by a khel‑phukan (village headman) who reported to the Borbarua (chief minister) or the Borphukan (commander of the western frontier). Each khel maintained a khel‑bahi (register) recording the names of paik (corvée labourers), the extent of cultivable land (bari), and the revenue demand in kind (usually paddy measured in maund).

Land grants fell into two broad categories. Khel grants (khel‑dana) were conferred on borphukan, borbarua, and senior phukan as hereditary jagir‑like holdings. The grantee collected the kharaj from the constituent villages, retained a portion for personal maintenance, and forwarded the remainder to the royal treasury (raj‑kosh). The grant deeds (sanad) were inscribed on copper plates in the Ahom script and later in Assamese, specifying the boundaries, the number of paik attached, and the obligations to supply labour for public works (roads, embankments, fortifications).

The second category comprised satra grants. From the 16th century the Neo‑Vaishnavite reformer Srimanta Sankaradeva and his disciples established satra monasteries that became centres of religious instruction, manuscript production, and agrarian management. The Ahom monarchs (notably Suhungmung, r. 1497‑1539, and Pratap Singha, r. 1603‑1641) granted extensive satra lands (satra‑bari) exempt from regular kharaj in return for the satra’s provision of spiritual services, education, and the maintenance of a reserve of paik for state labour. The satra heads (sattradhikar) exercised quasi‑judicial authority over their tenants, adjudicating disputes through a council of elders (namghar assembly).

The integration of khel and satra networks created a dual revenue stream. The royal treasury received peshkash from khel grantees and a nominal satra‑peshkash (often a symbolic offering of cloth or elephants) from the monasteries. In times of war, the Borbarua could mobilise paik from both khel and satra holdings, giving the Ahom state a flexible manpower pool that underpinned its successful resistance to Mughal invasions (e.g., the 1671 Battle of Saraighat).

After the Treaty of Yandabo (1826) the British retained the khel‑satra framework for revenue collection, converting paik obligations into a cash land tax (ryotwari) while recognising satra land tenure. The continuity of the khel register into the colonial mauza system illustrates the durability of Ahom agrarian institutions.

  • Confusing the Tughlaq token coinage with Akbar’s later Ilahi coinage; the former was a forced fiat experiment, the latter a calibrated bimetallic reform.
  • Assuming the Mughal navy possessed blue‑water capabilities comparable to the Portuguese; it was primarily a coastal and riverine force.
  • Reading the 1615 Surat Treaty as a territorial cession; it regulated diplomatic protocol and trade privileges only.
  • Equating the Deccani Dabir with the Mughal Munshi without noting the Dabir‑i‑Khas’s exclusive control of the royal seal.
  • Treating Ahom khel grants as equivalent to Mughal jagir; khel grants carried explicit corvée labour obligations and were recorded in Ahom script.
  • Overlooking the role of satra monasteries as fiscal intermediaries; they were not merely religious bodies but held revenue‑exempt lands and supplied state labour.

The Mughal Imperial Library and Translation Bureau under Akbar and Jahangir

Akbar established the imperial library (Kitab Khana) at Fatehpur Sikri in 1572, later expanding it to Agra and Lahore. The library was placed under the supervision of the chief librarian (Kitabdar), a post held successively by scholars such as Abul Fazl and later by the historian Nizamuddin Ahmad. The collection grew to an estimated 24,000 volumes, encompassing Persian, Arabic, Turkish, Sanskrit, and regional Indian languages. A dedicated translation bureau (Maktab Khana) operated alongside the library, employing a corps of multilingual scribes (munshis) and pandits. Its most celebrated output was the Razmnama, a Persian rendering of the Mahabharata completed in 1584 under the direct patronage of Akbar, which served both as a cultural bridge and a political statement of universal sovereignty (Sulh‑i‑Kul). The bureau also produced Persian versions of the Ramayana, the Rajatarangini, the Atharvaveda, and numerous scientific treatises on astronomy, medicine, and mathematics drawn from Sanskrit sources. Under Jahangir (r. 1605‑1627) the bureau continued its work, adding European texts brought by Jesuit missionaries — notably the Latin‑Persian dictionary of Jerome Xavier and the Portuguese‑Persian glossary of the Goa press. Jahangir’s personal interest in natural history prompted the translation of the Arabic “Kitab al‑Hayawan” and the commissioning of illustrated manuscripts such as the Jahangirnama, which combined autobiographical narrative with miniatures painted in the imperial atelier. The library’s cataloguing system, recorded in the “Fihrist‑i‑Kitab Khana”, listed titles, authors, languages, and physical dimensions, enabling the emperor to monitor intellectual currents across his domains. The bureau’s output reinforced the Mughal claim to be the inheritors of both the Persianate Islamic tradition and the indigenous Indian knowledge systems, a dual legitimacy that underpinned administrative integration and diplomatic outreach to the Safavid and Ottoman courts.

Mughal Coinage Reform of 1580: The Ilahi Rupee and Bimetallic Standard

In 1580 Akbar ordered a comprehensive reform of the imperial mint network, introducing the Ilahi calendar and a new silver rupee (the Ilahi rupee) that became the empire’s standard currency for the next two centuries. The rupee was struck at a weight of 11.66 grams (172 grains) of 99.9 percent fine silver, a specification maintained at the principal mints of Agra, Delhi, Lahore, and Ahmedabad under the supervision of the darogha‑i‑darul‑zarb (mint master). Gold muhrs were issued at 10.95 grams (169 grains) of 99.5 percent fine gold, establishing a bimetallic ratio of roughly 1 gold muhr = 40 silver rupees, a ratio that facilitated large‑scale revenue remittances and long‑distance trade. Copper dams (≈20 grams) continued as small‑change coinage, but their value was fixed at 40 dams = 1 rupee, eliminating the regional fluctuations that had plagued earlier Sultanate issues. The reform also standardised the legend: the obverse bore the Ilahi creed “Allahu Akbar Jalla Jalaluhu” and the regnal year in the Ilahi era, while the reverse displayed the mint name and the Hijri date. Sarrafs (money‑changers) and mahajans (bankers) were incorporated into the mint’s quality‑control process; they certified the fineness of bullion before striking and received a fixed commission (sarrafi) for each batch. The uniform rupee enabled the zabt revenue system to assess land tax in a single monetary unit, simplifying the collection of kharaj and the operation of the ijaradari (revenue‑farming) contracts. Contemporary European observers — notably the English factor William Hawkins (1609) and the Dutch merchant Pieter van den Broecke (1615) — recorded the rupee’s acceptance across the Indian Ocean littoral, from Surat to Aceh, confirming its role as a de facto trade currency. The 1580 reform thus linked fiscal centralisation, monetary stability, and commercial integration, laying the financial foundation for the empire’s peak under Shah Jahan and Aurangzeb.

Examiner's Technique: Comparative Coinage Analysis
Compare the 1580 Ilahi rupee with the earlier Sultanate silver tanka of Alauddin Khalji (c. 1300) in terms of weight, fineness, and administrative purpose. Explain why the Mughal reform succeeded where the Khalji experiment failed.
The Khalji tanka was a forced fiat issue of 11.5 grams silver but only 50 percent fine, imposed by price‑control edicts without a supporting mint network; it collapsed because merchants refused the debased coin and the state lacked a credible redemption mechanism. The Ilahi rupee, by contrast, kept a high fineness (99.9 %), a stable weight (11.66 g), and was backed by a centrally supervised mint hierarchy (darogha‑i‑darul‑zarb) and a professional sarraf guild that guaranteed convertibility. The Mughal state also tied the rupee to the zabt revenue demand, giving it a fiscal anchor absent in the Khalji experiment. Consequently the Ilahi rupee circulated widely, while the tanka was hoarded or melted down.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “the impact of Akbar’s coinage reform”, structure the answer in three layers: (1) technical specifications (weight, fineness, bimetallic ratio), (2) institutional mechanisms (mint hierarchy, sarraf role, zabt linkage), and (3) macro‑effects (revenue simplification, trade integration, European testimony). This three‑tier format matches the marking rubric for “analysis” and “evaluation” marks.

Vijayanagara Maritime Trade Networks with the Persian Gulf and Southeast Asia

From the early sixteenth century the Vijayanagara empire maintained a vibrant maritime commerce through its western ports — Bhatkal, Honavar, Mangalore, and the later Portuguese‑controlled Goa — and its eastern outlets at Pulicat and Masulipatnam. The empire’s admiral (Dandanayaka) commanded a modest fleet of armed dhows and ghurabs that protected convoy routes against piracy and Portuguese predation. Exports centred on high‑value commodities: black pepper from the Malabar hinterland, fine cotton textiles (especially the “Calico” of the Coromandel), diamonds from the Golconda mines (trans‑shipped via the Krishna‑Godavari delta), iron ore and steel (wootz) from the Deccan, and rice from the Kaveri delta. Imports comprised Arabian horses (essential for the cavalry), Persian carpets, Chinese porcelain, Southeast Asian spices (clove, nutmeg), and luxury items such as ivory and sandalwood. The trade with the Persian Gulf was mediated through the entrepôt of Hormuz, where Vijayanagara merchants maintained factor houses (kothis) and negotiated customs farms (mahsul) with the Safavid governors. Diplomatic missions — notably the 1515 embassy of the Raya Krishnadevaraya to Shah Ismail I — secured favourable tariff rates and the right to mint a limited quantity of “Vijayanagara fanams” for use in Gulf markets. On the eastern seaboard, the empire’s connection to the Melaka Strait facilitated the flow of Indonesian spices and textiles; the 1520s saw a Vijayanagara‑sponsored fleet under the Nayaka of Madurai reaching Aceh, establishing a temporary trading post. The Portuguese capture of Goa in 1510 disrupted the western network, forcing the empire to redirect pepper shipments through the inland riverine route to the Coromandel ports, a shift recorded in the “Raya Vachakam” customs registers. Despite Portuguese attempts to impose the cartaz system, Vijayanagara’s naval patrols and the diplomatic leverage of its Nayaka governors (especially the Nayaka of Ikkeri) preserved a degree of autonomy until the empire’s collapse in 1565. The maritime revenue, collected as a 10 percent ad valorem duty (sunkam) at each port, contributed an estimated 15‑20 percent of the central treasury’s income, underscoring the fiscal significance of oceanic trade.

Ahom Diplomatic Correspondence and Treaty Negotiations with the Mughal Empire

The Ahom kingdom (1228‑1826) conducted formal diplomacy with the Mughal court from the early seventeenth century, employing a bilingual chancery that drafted letters in Persian for the Mughal emperor and in Tai‑Ahom script for internal records. The first sustained exchange occurred after the Mughal governor of Bengal, Islam Khan (1608‑1613), demanded tribute; Ahom king Pratap Singha (r. 1603‑1641) responded with a letter sealed with the royal “Hengdang” (golden seal) and accompanied by gifts of elephants and musk. The Mughal farman of 1615, issued by Jahangir, recognised Ahom sovereignty over the Brahmaputra valley up to the Barnadi river in return for an annual peshkash of 20 elephants and 500 maunds of rice. This treaty, recorded in the Ahom Buranji (chronicle) as the “Ghilajharighat Treaty”, established a diplomatic protocol: Mughal envoys travelled with a “parwana” (safe‑conduct) and were received at the Ahom capital Garhgaon by the Borphukan, the western frontier commander who also functioned as chief diplomat. A second major negotiation followed the 1639 Mughal expedition under Mir Jumla; the resulting “Treaty of Asurar Ali” (1639) ceded the region west of the Barnadi to the Mughals but guaranteed Ahom control over the strategic fort of Guwahati and the right to collect customs at the riverine port of Pandu. The Ahom chancery preserved the Persian texts of both treaties in the “Raj‑Buranji” and translated them into Assamese for the council of ministers (Mantri‑Parishad). The diplomatic language emphasised mutual recognition of “sultanat” (Mughal) and “swargadeo” (Ahom) titles, avoiding subordination rhetoric. During the 1671 Battle of Saraighat, Ahom envoys used the treaty clauses to argue for Mughal withdrawal, a claim acknowledged in the Mughal “Akhbarat” reports. The continuity of this diplomatic archive — Persian farmans, Ahom sanads, and bilingual correspondence — illustrates a sophisticated statecraft that balanced military resistance with legalistic negotiation, a model later cited by British officials during the 1826 Treaty of Yandabo.

Deccan Sultanate Military Reforms under Ibrahim Adil Shah II: Artillery, Cavalry, and Fortification

Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) of Bijapur undertook a systematic overhaul of the sultanate’s military establishment to counter Mughal pressure and Portuguese naval dominance. He created a standing artillery corps (Topkhana) staffed by European gunners — primarily Portuguese deserters and Ottoman renegades — who cast bronze cannons of 12‑ to 24‑pounder calibre at the foundry in Bijapur’s citadel. The sultanate standardised gun carriages on the “Mughal‑style” four‑wheel platform, enabling rapid redeployment during sieges such as the 1595 defence of Naldurg. Cavalry reforms introduced a graded mansab‑like ranking for Deccani nobles (amirs), each assigned a fixed quota of horses (tabinan) and a stipend (jagir) paid from the provincial revenue (mahal). The “Barq‑andaz” (musketeer) regiments, recruited from the Maratha and Habshi communities, were equipped with matchlock muskets (tufang) imported from Goa and maintained in permanent barracks (thanas) at strategic nodes — Bijapur, Gulbarga, and the coastal fort of Goa‑Velha. Fortification policy shifted from hill‑top citadels to low‑profile, bastioned enceintes with angled curtain walls (trace italienne) designed to deflect cannon fire; the reconstruction of the Bijapur citadel (1590‑1600) incorporated a double‑layered glacis, a covered way, and a series of caponiers. The sultan also commissioned the “Gol Gumbaz” complex as a combined mausoleum and gunpowder magazine, its massive dome serving as a resonant chamber for signalling. These reforms were documented in the “Tarikh‑i‑Ibrahim Shahi” and reflected in the increased artillery complement at the 1616 siege of Ahmadnagar, where Bijapur fielded over 150 pieces. The integration of European technical expertise with indigenous manpower created a hybrid military system that delayed Mughal annexation until the 1686‑87 campaigns of Aurangzeb, demonstrating the durability of Ibrahim’s institutional innovations.

  • Confusing the Ilahi rupee’s 99.9 % fineness with the lower‑grade silver of the earlier Sultanate tanka.
  • Assuming the Vijayanagara navy possessed ocean‑going ships comparable to Portuguese carracks; it relied on coastal dhows and riverine craft.
  • Treating the Ahom “Buranji” as a single narrative rather than a genre of chronicles that includes diplomatic correspondence, land grants, and ritual texts.
  • Equating Ibrahim Adil Shah II’s Topkhana with the Mughal imperial artillery park without noting the former’s reliance on European mercenary gunners.
  • Overlooking the role of the Borphukan as both military commander and chief diplomat in Ahom‑Mughal relations.
  • Misreading the 1580 coinage reform as a purely monetary measure; it was integrally linked to the zabt revenue assessment and the ijaradari contract system.
  • Neglecting the fiscal contribution of maritime customs (sunkam) to the Vijayanagara treasury, which amounted to roughly one‑fifth of central revenue.

The Delhi Sultanate's Token Currency Experiment under Muhammad bin Tughlaq

In 1329‑30 Muhammad bin Tughlaq (r. 1325‑1351) ordered the minting of brass and copper tokens (tanka) at the same nominal value as the silver tanka, intending to replace scarce silver with a fiduciary issue. The coins bore the legend “Sultan al‑Azam Muhammad Shah” and were declared legal tender for all state dues, including land revenue (kharaj) and market transactions. The experiment was driven by the depletion of the imperial treasury after the costly Deccan campaigns and the desire to finance the planned capital shift to Daulatabad. However, the absence of intrinsic metallic value invited massive counterfeiting; private mints proliferated in Gujarat, Malwa, and the Doab, flooding the market with spurious tokens. Merchants refused the copper issues, hoarding silver and driving up its price. By 1332 the sultan was forced to withdraw the token currency, redeeming genuine pieces at a heavy discount and restoring the silver standard. The fiscal shock contracted revenue collection, disrupted the iqta assignments, and contributed to the rebellion of the provincial governors in the 1330s. Contemporary chroniclers such as Ziauddin Barani record that the episode eroded confidence in the sultan’s monetary authority and accelerated the fragmentation of the Sultanate’s central fiscal control.

The Vijayanagara Empire's Temple Administration: The Role of the Sthanika, the Archaka, and the Devadaya Grants

Vijayanagara temples functioned as integrated economic, ritual, and administrative units. The Sthanika (also called sthanapati) was the royal appointee responsible for the temple’s overall management: supervision of land holdings, collection of produce from devadaya villages, maintenance of the treasury, and coordination of festivals. Inscriptions from the 15th‑century Virupaksha temple at Hampi show the Sthanika issuing kattale (receipts) for grain measured in khanduga units. The Archaka (chief priest) held hereditary ritual rights, performed daily puja, and received a share of the offerings (naivedya) and a fixed cash allowance (dakshina) from the temple treasury. Devadaya grants were tax‑free land endowments made by the king or provincial governors (nayakas) to temples; the produce was earmarked for ritual expenditure, feeding of pilgrims, and maintenance of the temple staff. The Kalyana Mahotsava inscriptions (c. 1450) list over 120 devadaya villages attached to the Vitthala temple, each assessed at a standard khanduga of paddy. Temple accounts were audited annually by the Mahanayakacharya (a separate office already covered) but day‑to‑day fiscal operations remained under the Sthanika, who reported to the provincial nayaka and ultimately to the Rayasam (royal secretariat). This layered administration enabled temples to act as stable revenue nodes, supporting both state finances and the agrarian economy of the Tungabhadra basin.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which official was directly responsible for the collection of produce from devadaya villages in a Vijayanagara temple?” Options: (a) Archaka, (b) Sthanika, (c) Mahanayakacharya, (d) Nayaka.
The correct answer is (b) Sthanika. The Archaka performed ritual duties; the Mahanayakacharya oversaw multiple temples at a higher level; the Nayaka was a provincial governor. The Sthanika, as the temple’s chief administrator, handled land revenue from devadaya grants.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question distinguishes between ritual and fiscal roles in Vijayanagara temples, map each term to its function: Archaka = ritual, Sthanika = administration, Mahanayakacharya = oversight. This prevents confusion with the similarly named Mahanayakacharya section already studied.

The Maratha Confederacy's Regular Infantry Reforms under the Peshwas

From the 1720s the Peshwas, beginning with Baji Rao I (r. 1720‑1740) and consolidated under Balaji Baji Rao (r. 1740‑1761), sought to reduce reliance on irregular cavalry by creating a standing infantry corps trained in European drill. The Bargir system, originally a cavalry arrangement where the state supplied horses, was extended to infantry: the state provided muskets (tufang), uniforms, and a monthly salary (₹ 8‑10) to recruited peasants, chiefly from the Konkan and Desh regions. Simultaneously, the Pindari units — originally loose bands of plunderers — were reorganised into disciplined light infantry battalions (each 500‑800 men) under appointed sardars who answered to the Huzur Daftar (central secretariat). French officers such as Ibrahim Khan Gardi were engaged to supervise musketry practice at the Pune arsenal (established 1735). By the 1750s the regular infantry numbered roughly 25,000 men, deployed in the Carnatic wars and at the Third Battle of Panipat (1761) where they formed the defensive square that initially resisted Afghan cavalry charges. The reforms also introduced a standardized rank structure (havaldar, naik, sepoy) and a regimental colour system, laying the groundwork for the later Maratha armies that the British would confront in the Anglo‑Maratha wars.

The Deccan Sultanates' Patronage of Dakhni Urdu Literature

The Bijapur and Golconda courts cultivated a distinct literary language — Dakhni (Deccani) Urdu — that blended Persian vocabulary with local Dravidian and Marathi substrates. Sultan Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) of Bijapur commissioned the Kitab‑i‑Nauras, a treatise on music and poetry, and patronised poets such as Wajhi (author of Sab Ras, 1635) and Ghawwasi (author of Qissa‑e‑Mehar). In Golconda, Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (r. 1580‑1611) founded Hyderabad (1591) as a cultural capital and wrote poetry in Dakhni under the pen name “Kuli”. The Dakhni Masnavi tradition flourished, with works like Phul Ban (by Mulla Wajhi) and Gulzar‑e‑Ibrahim (by Ibrahim Adil Shah II himself). These texts employed the rekhta metre, used Persian ghazal forms, but incorporated Telugu and Kannada idioms, reflecting the multilingual milieu of the Deccan. Manuscript colophons indicate that royal libraries (e.g., the Adil Shahi Khana at Bijapur) maintained scribal workshops where calligraphers produced illuminated copies for distribution to Sufi khanqahs and provincial governors. The Dakhni corpus later served as a linguistic bridge for the emergence of classical Urdu in the 18th‑century Mughal courts of Delhi and Lucknow.

The Ahom Kingdom's Satra Institution: Vaishnavite Monastic Centers as Agents of State Integration

Founded by the saint‑reformer Srimanta Sankardev (1449‑1568) and institutionalised by his disciple Madhavdev, the Satra (monastic centre) became the primary vehicle for spreading Ekasarana Vaishnavism across the Brahmaputra valley. Each Satra received a satram land grant (often 100‑200 puras of wet rice land) from the Ahom king, recorded in copper‑plate sanads (e.g., the 1603 grant to the Barpeta Satra by King Pratap Singha). The Satra was headed by a Sattradhikar (abbot) who exercised judicial authority over disciples, managed the granary, and oversaw the namghar (prayer hall) where communal worship and cultural performances (bhaona) occurred. Satras also functioned as centres of education, teaching Assamese script, music, and manuscript copying; the Buranji chronicles note that the Borphukan (western frontier commander) regularly consulted Satra heads on local governance and revenue assessment. By the late 17th century, over 60 major Satras existed, forming a network that integrated tribal communities (e.g., the Mishings and Deoris) into the Ahom administrative framework, provided a stable ideological legitimation for the monarchy, and supplied manpower during the Ahom‑Mughal conflicts (notably the 1671 Battle of Saraighat, where Satra volunteers manned riverine stockades).

  • Confusing Muhammad bin Tughlaq’s token currency with the later Mughal copper dam; the former was a forced fiduciary issue, the latter a regular small‑change coin.
  • Attributing the Sthanika’s fiscal duties to the Archaka; the Archaka’s role was ritual, not revenue collection.
  • Assuming the Maratha regular infantry were entirely European‑trained; only a French‑led artillery contingent received formal drill, while most infantry retained indigenous tactics.
  • Equating Dakhni Urdu with classical Urdu; Dakhni retained strong Dravidian lexical elements absent in later North Indian Urdu.
  • Treating the Satra as a purely religious body; it held land, judicial, and military functions integral to Ahom statecraft.
  • Overlooking the chronological gap: the Satra network expanded significantly only after the 1600s, not during Sankardev’s lifetime.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise factual recall when distinguishing administrative, military, and cultural institutions across medieval Indian polities.

The Vijayanagara Poligar (Palaiyakkarar) System: Local Chiefs, Revenue Collection, and Military Service

The Vijayanagara empire delegated extensive authority to hereditary local chiefs known as poligars or palaiyakkarars, who controlled fortified hill‑top territories called palaiyams. Each poligar held a grant (palaiyam) from the raya, recorded in copper‑plate inscriptions, which entitled him to collect land revenue (kudi) from the peasantry, retain a portion for his household, and forward the remainder to the imperial treasury. In return the poligar supplied a stipulated number of cavalry and infantry for the royal army, maintained the fort and its garrison, and administered justice through a local council (panchayat) that applied customary law alongside imperial edicts.

The system emerged in the 14th century as the empire expanded into the Tamil country, where pre‑existing chieftaincies (e.g., the Nayakas of Madurai, the Sethupathis of Ramnad) were incorporated rather than replaced. The poligars were often drawn from local warrior lineages (Kallar, Maravar, Vellalar) and their legitimacy rested on both royal sanction and kinship networks. Revenue assessments were based on the khanduga (measurement) of wet and dry lands, using the kuli and ma units standardised by the imperial survey. The poligar’s share typically ranged from one‑third to one‑half of the gross produce, the rest being remitted as kudimai to the centre.

Military obligations were codified in the kaval (guard) system: each palaiyam supplied a fixed quota of horsemen (usually 10‑30) and foot soldiers (50‑200) for the annual campaign season. During the 16th‑century wars against the Deccan sultanates, poligars such as the Nayak of Gingee and the Poligar of Panchalankurichi fielded contingents that operated semi‑independently, often conducting guerrilla raids on enemy supply lines. Their forts, built on granitic outcrops with multiple concentric walls, served as logistical nodes for the imperial army.

By the late 17th century the poligar network began to fracture under Mughal pressure and internal succession disputes. The 1690s saw the Mughal subahdar of Arcot attempt to convert palaiyams into jagirs with direct revenue collection, prompting rebellions (e.g., the 1697 revolt of the Poligar of Sivaganga). The British East India Company later exploited these tensions, signing separate treaties with individual poligars (the 1799 Polygar Wars) and gradually dismantling the system. Nevertheless, the palaiyam framework persisted in revenue records well into the early 19th century, influencing the later zamindari settlements under the Permanent Settlement.

Key distinction: unlike the Mughal mansabdari where rank and revenue were centrally assigned, the poligar’s authority was hereditary, territorially bounded, and combined fiscal, judicial, and military functions in a single office. This fusion made the poligar a quasi‑sovereign within his palaiyam, a feature that both strengthened local defence and complicated central fiscal extraction.

The Ahom Buranji Tradition: Historiography, Genealogy, and Administrative Record‑Keeping

The Ahom kingdom of the Brahmaputra valley produced a distinctive corpus of chronicles known as buranjis, written in the Ahom language (later in Assamese) on sanchi (bark) manuscripts. Initiated under King Suhungmung (r. 1497‑1539) and systematised by the royal scribes (likhak) attached to the Borphukan’s office, the buranjis recorded dynastic genealogies, military campaigns, diplomatic missions, revenue settlements, and ritual calendars. Each entry was dated using the Ahom era (starting 1228 CE) and cross‑referenced with the Saka era for external correspondence.

Structurally, a buranji comprises three layers: (1) the rajavamsavali – a king‑list with regnal years and major events; (2) the katha – narrative accounts of wars, treaties, and court ceremonies; and (3) the parwana – copies of royal orders, land grants (sanad), and revenue registers. The scribes employed a standardized formulaic opening (“Sri Sri Swargadeo…”) and a closing colophon noting the scribe’s name, the date of copying, and the patron. This practice ensured textual stability across copies, allowing modern scholars to reconstruct a relatively continuous political history from the 13th to the early 19th century.

Administratively, the buranjis functioned as a reference manual for the Paik system. When a new khel (labour cohort) was formed, the Borphukan consulted the relevant buranji to verify the number of paiks owed by each village, the land allotments (pura) previously granted, and the hereditary obligations of the gaonburha (village headman). Revenue officers (Barbarua) used the parwana sections to confirm the boundaries of mauza (revenue circles) and to settle disputes over jagir‑type grants (amaram) made to nobles.

The tradition also served diplomatic purposes. During the 1615 Mughal‑Ahom negotiations leading to the Treaty of Ghilajharighat, Ahom envoys presented a buranji excerpt detailing the 1532 victory at Duimunisila, asserting sovereign parity. The Mughal chronicler Abdul Hamid Lahori noted the Ahom practice of “writing their history on bark” as evidence of a literate bureaucracy. After the 1826 Treaty of Yandabo, British officials collected buranjis for the Asiatic Society, recognising their value for land‑revenue settlement in the newly acquired province.

Scholarly caution: the buranjis are not neutral annals; they foreground the legitimacy of the ruling Swargadeo line, marginalise rival claimants, and occasionally compress chronology for rhetorical effect. Comparative work with Persian sources (e.g., Muntakhab‑ul‑Lubab) and archaeological data (coin hoards, fort inscriptions) is essential for balanced reconstruction.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “The Ahom buranjis were primarily religious texts used for ritual purposes.” Identify the error and give the correct characterisation.
The statement is false. Buranjis are secular chronicles maintained by the royal chancery for genealogical, military, diplomatic, and revenue‑administrative record‑keeping. They contain royal orders, land‑grant copies, and campaign narratives, not ritual hymns or theological treatises. The correct characterisation: “Buranjis are official historical records of the Ahom state, written in Ahom/Assamese on bark manuscripts, used for governance and legitimation.”
Exam‑day strategy
When a question cites a medieval Indian chronicle, first identify its language, patronage, and administrative function. For buranjis, remember the three‑layer structure (rajavamsavali, katha, parwana) and their role in the Paik revenue system. This lets you eliminate options that label them as purely religious or literary.

The Delhi Sultanate's Khalisa and Inam Land Grants: Fiscal Policy, Noble Patronage, and Central Control

Under the Delhi Sultanate the crown retained a portion of the conquered territory as khalisa (crown lands) where revenue was collected directly by imperial officers (amils) and deposited in the central treasury (khazana). The size of the khalisa fluctuated: Alauddin Khalji expanded it to roughly one‑third of the doab to fund his standing army, while Muhammad bin Tughlaq’s experimental token currency forced a temporary contraction as revenue assignments were converted to cash‑based iqta. The khalisa served as a fiscal buffer, allowing the sultan to meet emergency expenditures without relying on the goodwill of muqtis.

Parallel to the khalisa, the sultan granted inam (tax‑free) lands to religious scholars (ulama), Sufi shaikhs, poets, and loyal nobles. An inam grant (sanad) specified the village or parcel, the revenue exemption (usually full kharaj and jizya), and the hereditary nature of the grant. The Diwan‑i‑Wizarat maintained a register (daftar‑i‑inam) to prevent overlapping claims. Inams reinforced the sultan’s legitimacy by patronising the religious establishment and by rewarding military service without creating a permanent jagir that could become a power base.

The interaction between khalisa and inam shaped provincial administration. In the shiq (district) the shiqdar collected revenue from khalisa villages, while the amil oversaw inam holdings to ensure the grantee did not exceed the exempted area. Disputes were adjudicated by the qazi using the fatwa of the chief mufti. Notably, under Firuz Shah Tughlaq the inam register was expanded to include grants to madrasa teachers and hospital (dar al‑shifa) staff, reflecting a broader welfare orientation.

Revenue audits (mahsul) conducted every three years compared the actual yield of khalisa lands with the estimated jama (assessment). Shortfalls triggered either a reduction in the iqta assignments of the concerned muqti or a direct levy on inam holders (rare, but recorded in the 1350s). The sultan’s ability to manipulate the khalisa‑inam balance was a key lever of central authority; a shrinking khalisa often signalled weakening control, as seen in the late Lodi period when regional governors converted khalisa villages into hereditary jagirs.

Comparative note: the Mughal khalisa (crown lands) and madad‑i‑ma'ash (subsistence grants) evolved from this Sultanate framework, but the Mughals institutionalised a more systematic zabt survey for khalisa assessment and a graded hierarchy of madad‑i‑ma'ash (cash vs. land). The Sultanate’s reliance on ad‑hoc inam grants made its fiscal map more fluid and politically negotiated.

The Mughal Zabt Implementation in Gujarat: The Desai, Patel, and Village Accountants in Revenue Collection

When Akbar annexed Gujarat in 1573, the imperial administration extended the zabt (measured‑land) system to the province, adapting it to the region’s existing agrarian hierarchy. The survey, supervised by Todar Mal’s deputies, used the Ilahi gaz (33 inches) and the bigha (≈ 0.62 acre) as standard units. Village boundaries were demarcated by the patel (village headman) and the desai (revenue farmer for a cluster of villages), both of whom were confirmed in their hereditary posts provided they submitted the mahzar (revenue return) annually.

The desai acted as the primary intermediary between the diwan (provincial revenue minister) and the cultivators. He collected the assessed mal (land revenue) in cash (silver rupees) or kind (grain), retained a commission (typically 5‑10 %), and forwarded the balance to the faujdar’s treasury. The patel maintained the khasra (field register), recorded crop types (rabi, kharif), and certified the paimaish (measurement) conducted by the amin (surveyor). A separate class of village accountants, the kulkarni (or patwari in Gujarati usage), kept the bahi (ledger) of individual cultivator holdings, mutations, and remission claims.

Gujarat’s commercialised agriculture — cotton, indigo, and grain for export through Surat — meant that cash revenue was preferred. The zabt cash demand was fixed at one‑third of the estimated produce value (ain‑i‑dahsala), but the desai often negotiated a qabuliyat (agreement) allowing a lower cash payment in exchange for a share of the market surplus. This flexibility reduced peasant flight but created opportunities for desai enrichment, prompting periodic mahsul audits where the diwan compared the khasra totals with the bahi entries.

Resistance emerged in the 1620s when the desai of Baroda attempted to convert his revenue farm into a hereditary jagir. The imperial response, recorded in the Akbarnama supplement, was to appoint a karori (revenue auditor) who re‑measured the villages, dismissed the offending desai, and reinstated a loyal patel. The episode illustrates the Mughal reliance on the patel‑kulkarni duo as a check on the desai’s fiscal autonomy.

By the late 17th century, the Gujarat zabt framework eroded under Maratha chauth exactions and the rise of European trading companies that offered alternative credit to cultivators. The British later adopted the desai‑patel terminology in their own ryotwari settlements, but replaced the hereditary desai with a salaried collector, fundamentally altering the revenue chain.

The Habshi Elite in the Deccan Sultanates: From Military Command to Political Power in Bijapur and Ahmadnagar

From the late 15th century the Deccan sultanates — particularly Bijapur (Adil Shahi) and Ahmadnagar (Nizam Shahi) — recruited Abyssinian (Habshi) slaves as elite cavalry and palace guards. Purchased via the Red Sea slave trade, these men were converted to Islam, given Persian names, and trained in the mansab‑style hierarchy. Their loyalty to the sultan, unmediated by local clan ties, made them a counterweight to the Deccani (indigenous Muslim) and Afaqi (foreign Persian/Turkish) factions that dominated court politics.

In Bijapur, the Habshi commander Ikhlas Khan (originally named Habsh Khan) rose to the post of Mir Bakhshi (paymaster‑general) under Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627). He controlled the barq‑andaz (artillery corps) and the karkhana (imperial workshops), and his patronage network extended to the sufi shrines of the capital. In Ahmadnagar, the Habshi general Malik Ambar (born Chapu in Ethiopia) became the de facto ruler after 1600, reorganising the revenue system (introducing the malik‑ambari assessment), founding the city of Khadki (later Aurangabad), and leading a guerrilla resistance against Mughal incursions. His successor, Fateh Khan, continued the Habshi dominance until the Mughal annexation of Ahmadnagar in 1636.

The Habshi elite’s political influence rested on three pillars: (1) control of the khas (royal) cavalry regiments, which were the most mobile strike force in the Deccan’s hilly terrain; (2) access to the sultan’s private treasury (khazana‑i‑khas) allowing them to finance client nobles; and (3) marriage alliances with Deccani noble families, which integrated them into the regional aristocracy. Inscriptions at the Jami Masjid of Bijapur record land grants (inam) to Habshi officers, confirming their status as landed magnates.

However, their power provoked resentment. The Deccani faction, led by the Afzal Khan family in Bijapur, orchestrated the assassination of Ikhlas Khan in 1625, triggering a civil war that weakened the sultanate before the Mughal siege of 1686. In Ahmadnagar, the Mughal emperor Jahangir exploited Habshi‑Deccani rivalry by bribing Fateh Khan’s rivals, hastening the kingdom’s collapse. The Habshi legacy persisted in the military culture of the Marathas, who adopted the habshi cavalry tactics and the guerilla logistics pioneered by Malik Ambar.

Scholarly note: the term “Habshi” in Persian sources denotes any sub‑Saharan African, not exclusively Ethiopians. Contemporary European travelers (e.g., Peter Mundy) described them as “blackamoors” serving as elite archers and musketeers. Their presence illustrates the trans‑Indian Ocean slave‑military nexus that shaped Deccan statecraft well before the Atlantic slave trade’s peak.

  • Confusing the Vijayanagara poligar’s hereditary palaiyam with the Mughal jagir, which was non‑hereditary and centrally assigned.
  • Treating Ahom buranjis as purely religious chronicles rather than administrative records used for Paik revenue verification.
  • Assuming the Delhi Sultanate’s khalisa lands were static; they expanded and contracted with each sultan’s fiscal policy.
  • Equating the Gujarati desai with the Mughal zamindar; the desai was a revenue farmer for a cluster of villages, not a hereditary landlord.
  • Overlooking the Habshi elite’s role as a distinct military‑political faction separate from Deccani and Afaqi groups.
  • Believing Malik Ambar’s revenue reforms were a direct copy of Todar Mal’s zabt; they were an independent adaptation to Deccan terrain.
  • Neglecting the chronological gap: the poligar system peaked in the 16th‑17th centuries, while the British Polygar Wars occurred in the late 18th century.

Mughal Imperial Architecture: The Office of the Imperial Architect and Monumental Building Programs

The Mughal state maintained a dedicated building department headed by the Mir Imarat (Superintendent of Works), who reported directly to the emperor and coordinated with the Mir Bakhshi for labour and material logistics. The chief architect, often titled Ustad or Mimar, was responsible for design, structural engineering, and the supervision of master masons (sangtarash), stone‑cutters, inlay artisans (parchin kari), and decorative painters. Under Akbar the office was held by Ustad Isa (sometimes identified as Isa Muhammad Effendi), who oversaw the construction of Fatehpur Sikri (1571‑1585) and the early phases of the Agra Fort. Jahangir continued the tradition, appointing Ustad Ahmad to supervise the Shalimar Gardens at Lahore (1619) and the tomb of Itimad‑ud‑Daula (1622‑1628), which introduced extensive pietra dura work.

Shah Jahan’s reign saw the apogee of imperial architecture. The emperor personally reviewed plans and allocated funds from the khazana‑i‑khas (privy purse) and from revenue assignments (jagir) granted to the building department. Ustad Ahmad Lahauri is credited with the design of the Taj Mahal (1632‑1653), the Red Fort at Delhi (1639‑1648), and the Jama Masjid (1650‑1656). The Taj Mahal’s construction employed an estimated 20,000 workers, including 1,000 elephants for transport, and required the quarrying of Makrana marble, red sandstone from Fatehpur Sikri, and semi‑precious stones from Afghanistan, Persia, and the Deccan. The Red Fort’s layout followed the charbagh principle, integrating palaces, audience halls (Diwan‑i‑Am, Diwan‑i‑Khas), and a mosque within a rectangular perimeter defended by massive bastions.

The building department operated through a hierarchy of karkhanas (workshops) located at the capital and at major provincial centres such as Lahore, Agra, and Burhanpur. Each karkhana specialised: the sangtarash khana for stone‑cutting, the parchin khana for inlay, the naqqash khana for painting, and the zargar khana for metalwork. Raw materials were procured through imperial orders (farman) to provincial governors, who supplied timber from the Himalayan foothills, lime from the Deccan, and marble from Rajasthan. The accounts were audited by the diwan‑i‑buyutat (treasury of the household) and cross‑checked by the waqia‑navis (news writers) to prevent pilferage.

Architectural programmes served political ends: the Taj Mahal projected the emperor’s claim to universal sovereignty through the metaphor of paradise; the Red Fort symbolised the shift of the capital from Agra to Delhi and the consolidation of Mughal authority over the Indo‑Gangetic plain; the Jama Masjid demonstrated the emperor’s role as protector of Islam. The synthesis of Persian Timurid vaulting, Central Asian pishtaq portals, and indigenous Hindu temple ornamentation created a distinct Mughal idiom that was replicated in provincial capitals such as Lucknow, Hyderabad, and Murshidabad.

By the late seventeenth century, the building department’s budget contracted as military expenditure in the Deccan rose. Aurangzeb curtailed new monumental projects, restricting construction to mosques and utilitarian structures. Nevertheless, the institutional memory of the Mir Imarat office survived in the successor states of Awadh, Bengal, and the Maratha confederacy, where former Mughal architects and craftsmen were employed to build the Bara Imambara (1784) and the Shaniwar Wada (1732).

Mughal Legal Codification: The Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri and Its Role in Judicial Standardisation

In 1664 Emperor Aurangzeb commissioned a comprehensive compilation of Hanafi jurisprudence to serve as the authoritative legal reference for all imperial courts. The project, known as the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri (also rendered Al‑Fatawa al‑Alamgiriyya), was entrusted to a board of senior jurists headed by Sheikh Nizamuddin Burhanpuri and Mulla Mahmud Jaunpuri, both holding the rank of Qazi‑ul‑Quzat (Chief Qazi). The committee worked in the imperial library (Maktab Khana) at Delhi, consulting earlier digests such as the Hidayah of Burhan al‑Din al‑Marghinani, the Fatawa‑i‑Qazi Khan, and the Fatawa‑i‑Tatar Khaniyya. The final work, completed in 1672, comprised thirty volumes organised into books (kitab) covering ritual purity (tahara), prayer (salat), fasting (sawm), alms (zakat), pilgrimage (hajj), marriage (nikah), divorce (talaq), inheritance (mirath), contracts (muamalat), criminal law (jinayat), and revenue (kharaj).

The Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri was distributed to every provincial qazi and mufti with a royal farman mandating its use as the primary source for judicial decisions. It standardised procedural rules: the requirement for two male witnesses in hudud cases, the evidentiary value of a written deed (sahada) in property disputes, and the fixed penalties for theft (sariqa) and highway robbery (qat‘ al‑tariq). The code also addressed administrative matters, prescribing the duties of the kotwal (city prefect), the faujdar (military governor), and the amil (revenue collector) in maintaining law and order.

Aurangzeb’s motivation was twofold: to legitimise his rule as the “Caliph of the Age” by enforcing a uniform Islamic legal order, and to curb the discretionary power of local qazis who often blended customary law (urf) with Hanafi doctrine. The compilation therefore omitted many regional customs, a point later criticised by scholars such as Shah Waliullah (d. 1762), who argued that the rigid application of the Fatawa ignored the social realities of the Indian milieu.

The British East India Company, after acquiring diwani rights in Bengal (1765), adopted the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri as the basis of “Mohammedan Law” in the Anglo‑Indian courts. Warren Hastings appointed a committee of pandits and maulvis to translate selected volumes into Persian and English (the Hedaya translation by Charles Hamilton, 1791). This translation entrenched the Fatawa’s influence well into the nineteenth century, shaping the personal law of Muslims in British India and informing the later Muslim Personal Law (Shariat) Application Act, 1937.

Despite its authority, the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri never wholly displaced customary tribunals (panchayats) in rural areas, where village headmen (muqaddam) resolved disputes by local precedent. The coexistence of imperial Hanafi law and indigenous custom created a dual legal culture that persisted until the codification movements of the late colonial period.

Examiner's Trap
A question asks: “The Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri was a direct translation of the Hidayah.” Explain why this statement is incorrect and identify the actual relationship between the two works.
The statement is false. The Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri is a *compilation* (tafsir) of Hanafi legal opinions drawn from multiple classical sources, of which the Hidayah is only one. The compilers — Sheikh Nizamuddin Burhanpuri and Mulla Mahmud Jaunpuri — consulted the Hidayah, the Fatawa‑i‑Qazi Khan, the Fatawa‑i‑Tatar Khaniyya, and other authoritative texts, synthesising them into a new, encyclopaedic digest arranged by legal topics. Therefore, the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri is a derivative, multi‑source code, not a translation of a single work.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question cites a Mughal legal text, first identify whether it is a *primary* source (e.g., Hidayah, Fatawa‑i‑Qazi Khan) or a *state‑sponsored compilation* (Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri, Fatawa‑i‑Jahangiri). The latter carry imperial authority and were enforced by qazis; the former are scholarly references. This distinction often decides whether the answer should emphasise “official enforcement” or “scholarly influence”.

Mughal‑Dutch Commercial Relations: The VOC Factory at Surat, the 1662 Treaty, and Textile Export Dynamics

The Dutch East India Company (Verenigde Oostindische Compagnie, VOC) secured its first Mughal farman in 1618 from Emperor Jahangir, permitting a factory at Surat, the empire’s principal western port. The initial grant allowed the Dutch to trade duty‑free on condition that they supplied the imperial court with European curiosities — clocks, telescopes, and firearms. By 1623 the VOC had established a fortified warehouse (kothi) at Surat, a second factory at Agra (1630), and a third at Ahmedabad (1635) to procure high‑quality cotton piece‑goods (bafta, chintz) and indigo for the European market.

The relationship deepened after Aurangzeb’s accession. In 1662 the Mughal emperor ratified a formal treaty with the VOC, negotiated by the Dutch director‑general Rijckloff van Goens. The treaty confirmed the Surat factory’s extraterritorial status, exempted Dutch goods from the standard 2.5 % customs duty (mahsul), and granted the VOC the right to maintain a small garrison of European soldiers for factory defence. In return, the VOC agreed to supply the imperial arsenal with 500 matchlock muskets annually and to transport Mughal pilgrims to Mecca on its return voyages — a clause that underscored the political dimension of the commercial pact.

Textile exports dominated the trade balance. VOC records show that between 1660 and 1680 the company shipped an average of 1.2 million pieces of Gujarati cotton cloth per year from Surat to Batavia and onward to Amsterdam. The Dutch introduced the dastak (pass) system, whereby imperial officials issued sealed permits allowing duty‑free movement of VOC bales from inland weaving centres (Burhanpur, Ahmedabad) to the port. This system reduced transaction costs and gave the VOC a competitive edge over the English East India Company, which still paid full customs at Surat until the 1717 farman.

However, the partnership frayed after 1685 when Aurangzeb’s Deccan campaigns disrupted overland routes and the Mughal treasury demanded higher cash subsidies. The VOC responded by shifting its procurement to the Coromandel Coast (Pulicat, Masulipatnam) and Bengal (Chinsura, 1656), where it obtained separate farmans from the Bengal subahdar. By 1700 the Surat factory’s share of VOC textile exports had fallen below 30 %, and the Dutch increasingly relied on their fortified settlement at Chinsura for Bengal silk and saltpetre. The decline of Mughal central authority after 1707 ended the treaty’s enforcement; the VOC evacuated Surat in 1795 following the British takeover of the port.

The Mughal‑Dutch episode illustrates how European companies leveraged imperial farmans to create quasi‑sovereign enclaves, while the Mughal state used European military technology and maritime logistics to supplement its own fiscal and strategic needs.

Ahom Land Revenue Reforms under Rudra Singha: The Integration of Paik Labour with Wet‑Rice Assessment

Rudra Singha (reigned 1696‑1714), the most ambitious Ahom monarch, undertook a systematic overhaul of the kingdom’s revenue apparatus to finance his military expansion and courtly patronage. The reform centred on the Paik system, the hereditary labour obligation that underpinned Ahom statecraft. Each able‑bodied male (paik) was registered in a khel (village militia unit) and owed a fixed number of days of labour per year — either military service, public works, or agricultural work on crown lands (khas land). Rudra Singha linked this labour quota to a new land‑measurement standard: the bigha (approximately 0.33 hectare) and the pura (a fractional unit).
