Surveyors (borbarua’s agents) measured wet‑rice fields (sali) using a calibrated bamboo rod (danda) and recorded the area in the Buranji (royal chronicle) which doubled as a revenue register.

The assessment formula fixed the annual revenue demand at one‑third of the estimated paddy yield (bhag), payable either in kind (husked rice) or in silver rupees at the prevailing market rate (bazar dam). Cultivators who fulfilled their Paik labour on crown lands received a remission of up to 25 % of their cash obligation, creating a direct incentive for labour compliance. The Borbarua (prime minister) and the Borphukan (commander‑in‑chief) jointly supervised collection, with the Mauza (revenue circle) officer (mauzadars) acting as the local collection agent.

To improve yield, Rudra Singha sponsored the construction of embankments (bunds) and irrigation channels (dongs) along the Brahmaputra and its tributaries, employing Paik labour under the direction of the Dangaria (chief engineer). The expansion of wet‑rice cultivation in the alluvial plains of Upper Assam increased the kingdom’s grain surplus, which was stored in royal granaries (kothas) at Garhgaon and Rangpur for famine relief and army provisioning.

The reform also standardised the classification of land: khas (crown), debottar (temple endowment), brahmottar (Brahmin grant), and raiyati (peasant holding). Each category carried a distinct revenue rate and labour obligation, recorded in the Buranji using a uniform script (Ahom script later transliterated into Assamese). This cadastral clarity reduced disputes between village headmen (gaonburha) and the royal treasury.

By 1714 the Ahom treasury reported a 40 % increase in cash revenue compared with the 1690 baseline, enabling Rudra Singha to finance the construction of the Rang Ghar (amphitheatre), the Talatal Ghar (palace complex), and a standing cavalry of 10,000 horses. The integrated Paik‑land system remained the fiscal backbone of the Ahom state until the British annexation in 1826, when the colonial administration replaced it with the Bengal‑style zamindari settlement.

Mughal Imperial Patronage of Astronomy: The Jantar Mantar Observatories and Sawai Jai Singh’s Scientific Network

Sawai Jai Singh II of Amber (r. 1699‑1743), a high‑ranking Mughal mansabdar (7,000 zat, 5,000 sawar) and later subahdar of Malwa, leveraged his imperial position to create a network of monumental observatories known collectively as the Jantar Mantar. Between 1724 and 1738 he commissioned five sites: Delhi (1724), Jaipur (1734), Ujjain (1734), Varanasi (1737), and Mathura (1738). Each observatory housed massive masonry instruments — Samrat Yantra (equinoctial sundial, 27 m high), Jai Prakash Yantra (hemispherical bowl for celestial altitude), Ram Yantra (cylindrical pillars for azimuth), and Misra Yantra (combined instrument for multiple measurements) — designed to achieve observational accuracy within a few seconds of arc.

Jai Singh’s scientific programme was explicitly imperial. He presented his astronomical tables, the Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi (completed 1728), to Emperor Muhammad Shah, dedicating the work to the reigning sovereign. The Zij incorporated data from Islamic sources (Ulugh Beg’s Zij‑i‑Sultani, al‑Khwarizmi’s tables) and European works (La Hire’s Tabulae Astronomicae, 1702), which Jai Singh obtained through Jesuit missionaries at the Mughal court, notably Father Manuel de Figueroa and Father Jean‑François Pons. The Jesuits also supplied telescopes and pendulum clocks, enabling Jai Singh to verify planetary positions against European ephemerides.

The observatories served three practical functions for the Mughal administration: (1) calendrical regulation — determining the exact dates of Islamic festivals (Eid, Muharram) and Hindu festivals (Diwali, Holi) for the imperial court; (2) astrological advisory — casting horoscopes for the emperor’s military campaigns and succession planning, a role formally assigned to the Jyotishi (court astrologer) attached to the Diwan‑i‑Khas; (3) agronomic timing — providing the amils (revenue officers) with precise solar ingress data to schedule the sowing and harvesting of cash crops (cotton, indigo, opium) in the zabt assessed territories.

Jai Singh’s patronage extended beyond instrumentation. He invited scholars from across the Islamic world — Mulla Mahmud Jaunpuri (mathematician), Mirza Muhammad Salih (astronomer from Isfahan) — and sponsored the translation of Euclid’s Elements and Ptolemy’s Almagest into Persian at the imperial Maktab Khana. The Jaipur observatory housed a library of over 3,000 manuscripts, including copies of the Zij‑i‑Ulugh Beg and the Siddhanta Shiromani of Bhaskara II, reflecting a deliberate synthesis of Indo‑Persian and European scientific traditions.

After Jai Singh’s death in 1743, the observatories fell into disuse as Mughal central authority collapsed. The Delhi Jantar Mantar was damaged during the 1757 sack by Ahmad Shah Durrani; the Jaipur instruments survived and were restored in the early twentieth century under the Archaeological Survey of India. Nevertheless, the Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi remained the standard astronomical reference for North Indian courts until the British introduced the Nautical Almanac in the 1830s.

  • Confusing the Mir Imarat (building superintendent) with the Mir Bakhshi (paymaster‑general); the former handled architecture, the latter military finance.
  • Assuming the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri was a single‑author work; it was a multi‑jurist compilation commissioned by Aurangzeb.
  • Treating the 1662 Mughal‑Dutch treaty as a permanent monopoly; the VOC lost its Surat privileges after 1700 and shifted to Bengal.
  • Equating the Ahom Paik labour quota with a fixed cash tax; the quota was primarily labour, with cash remission as an incentive.
  • Believing Sawai Jai Singh built the Jantar Mantar solely for personal curiosity; the observatories were funded by the Mughal treasury and served imperial calendrical and revenue needs.
  • Overlooking the role of Jesuit missionaries in supplying European astronomical tables to Jai Singh; the Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi explicitly cites La Hire’s tables.
  • Neglecting that the Mughal building department’s budget was audited by the diwan‑i‑buyutat, not by the provincial diwan.

Mughal Land Revenue Classification: Polaj, Parauti, Chachar, and Banjar in the Zabt Assessment

The Zabt system codified under Todar Mal’s bandobast divided cultivable land into four qualitative categories that determined the revenue demand. Polaj denoted land cropped every year; it attracted the full standard rate (usually one‑third of the produce measured in maunds per bigha). Parauti was land left fallow for one or two seasons; the state charged half the Polaj rate. Chachar referred to land fallow for three to four years; the demand fell to a quarter of the Polaj rate. Banjar (or banjar‑zamin) was land uncultivated for five years or more; it was assessed at one‑eighth of the Polaj rate or, in many subahs, exempted altogether until brought back under the plough. The classification was recorded in the qanungo’s register (qanungo‑daftar) and verified annually by the amil during the paimaish (measurement) using the Ilahi gaz (33 inches) and the regional bigha (which varied from 1,600 to 3,600 square gaz). The Ain‑i‑Akbari (c. 1595) lists the standard produce per bigha for each crop (wheat, rice, barley, cotton, indigo) and the corresponding cash commutation rates (dastur‑ul‑amal) that allowed the diwan to convert kind payments into rupees. Because the categories were tied to actual cultivation cycles, they provided a built‑in flexibility: a village that shifted from Polaj to Parauti after a drought automatically saw its demand reduced without a new settlement. However, the system relied on honest reporting by the patwari and the qanungo; collusion to under‑report Polaj as Parauti was a frequent source of leakage that prompted Aurangzeb’s 1665 order for a fresh paimaish in the Deccan subahs.

Provincial Revenue Officials under the Mughals: The Qanungo, Patwari, and Village Accountant Network

The Mughal revenue hierarchy at the pargana level rested on three interlocking offices. The qanungo (also spelled kanungo) was a hereditary, semi‑permanent record‑keeper appointed by the diwan‑i‑subah on the recommendation of the faujdar. He maintained the qanungo‑daftar, a set of registers that recorded the jama (assessed revenue), the hasil (actual collection), the classification of each field (Polaj, Parauti, etc.), and the names of cultivators (raiyats). The qanungo received a madad‑i‑maash (maintenance grant) usually equal to 1–2 % of the pargana’s jama and was exempt from personal cultivation. The patwari (village accountant) operated at the mauza level; he measured each field with the gaz and bigha, entered the crop‑wise yield in the khasra (field register), and prepared the bachh (village summary) for the amil. Patwaris were paid a small cash salary (tankhwa) plus a share of the malba (village cess). Both offices were subject to audit by the diwan‑i‑khalsa through the waqai‑navis (news writers) who reported discrepancies to the centre. In 1595 Akbar ordered the compilation of the Ain‑i‑Dahsala, a ten‑year average of yields that became the benchmark for jama fixation; the qanungo’s register was the primary source for this calculation. Under Aurangzeb the qanungo’s hereditary right was curtailed in several subahs (notably Gujarat and Bengal) and replaced by appointed amils to tighten central control, a move that sparked the 1680s qanungo revolts in the Gujarat countryside.

Technique: Cross‑referencing classification with official duties
A 1665 farman orders the amil of a Deccan pargana to re‑measure all fields classified as Parauti and to verify the qanungo’s register against the patwari’s khasra. Explain why the state would target Parauti land specifically and how the two officials’ records serve as a check on each other.
Answer: Parauti land, being fallow for only one or two seasons, carries a revenue demand of half the Polaj rate. Cultivators have an incentive to declare marginally productive fields as Parauti to lower their liability, while the qanungo (who benefits from a higher jama for his madad‑i‑maash) may under‑report Parauti to keep the assessed base high. The patwari, however, records the actual crop‑wise yield in the khasra during the annual paimaish. By comparing the qanungo’s classification column with the patwari’s measured area and yield, the amil can detect systematic mis‑classification. The 1665 order therefore uses the dual‑record system as an internal audit mechanism: the qanungo provides the legal classification, the patwari supplies the physical measurement, and any divergence triggers a reassessment.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks about “checks and balances” in Mughal revenue administration, always pair the qanungo (legal register) with the patwari (field measurement). Mention the Ain‑i‑Dahsala as the statistical benchmark and cite the 1665 Deccan re‑measurement as a concrete example of the centre using this duality to curb leakage.

The Ahom Satra Institution: Monastic Land Grants, Religious Authority, and State Integration

The satras (Vaishnavite monasteries) founded by Srimanta Sankardev (1449–1568) and his disciple Madhavdev (1489–1596) became the principal institutional network linking the Ahom monarchy to the peasantry. Each satra received a satras‑land grant (satras‑bhumi) from the swargadeo (king), recorded in the royal buranji chronicles; the grant typically comprised several paiks (labour units) and a defined tract of wet‑rice land measured in puras (≈ 1.2 ha). The head of a satra, the satradhikar, exercised judicial authority over the resident bhakats (devotees) and collected a portion of the produce as satras‑bhog (monastic share), which was exempt from the state’s paik labour quota. In return, the satra supplied the state with trained scribes, ritual specialists for the Deo‑puja (royal worship), and a moral legitimising discourse that portrayed the Ahom king as a protector of Ekasarana Dharma. The Namghar (prayer hall) attached to each satra functioned as a village assembly space, enabling the borphukan (commander) to disseminate royal orders. During the 17th‑century Mughal‑Ahom conflicts, satras in the Brahmaputra valley mobilised paiks for guerrilla warfare and provided logistical support (boats, grain) to the Ahom navy. The British annexation of 1826 converted satras‑land into zamindari estates under the Bengal Regulation, but the satradhikars retained religious authority, and the Namghar network continues to shape Assamese civil society today.

Deccan Sultanates’ Adaptation of the Mansab System: Military‑Fiscal Integration in Bijapur and Golconda

Both the Adil Shahi (Bijapur) and Qutb Shahi (Golconda) sultanates borrowed the Mughal mansab framework but reshaped it to fit their smaller resource bases. In Bijapur, Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580–1627) introduced a mansab hierarchy graded in hazari (thousands) rather than the Mughal zat/sawar dual rank. A 5‑hazari noble was expected to maintain 5,000 cavalry, but the actual obligation was often commuted to a cash payment (tankhwa) drawn from a jagir (revenue assignment) called iqta‑mansab. The jagir was assessed using the Deccani khandi (≈ 1.6 tons) grain measure, and the diwan‑i‑kul (chief revenue minister) fixed the peshkash (tribute) at 10–15 % of the jagir’s estimated yield. Golconda’s Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (r. 1580–1612) retained the Mughal terminology (zat, sawar) but capped the highest rank at 7,000 zat / 5,000 sawar, reflecting the sultanate’s limited fiscal capacity. Both states linked mansab promotion to battlefield performance and to the timely remission of peshkash to the central treasury (khazana). The system created a hybrid military‑fiscal elite: mansabdars doubled as jagirdars and as commanders of bargir (state‑paid) and silahdar (self‑equipped) cavalry. When Aurangzeb annexed Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687), the Mughal diwan‑i‑buyutat audited the existing mansab‑jagir registers, re‑graded the nobles into the imperial zat/sawar scale, and reassigned jagirs in the newly formed subahs of Bijapur and Hyderabad, thereby converting the Deccani adaptation into a standard Mughal instrument.

Vijayanagara’s Mahanavami Festival: Ritual Spectacle, Political Legitimacy, and Imperial Display

The nine‑day Mahanavami (also called Navaratri) festival was the premier public ceremony of the Vijayanagara empire, centred on the Mahanavami Dibba (the great platform) in the royal enclosure at Hampi. Contemporary Portuguese chroniclers Domingo Paes (c. 1520) and Fernão Nunes (c. 1535) describe a procession in which the rayas (king) appeared on a richly caparisoned elephant, flanked by the nayakas (provincial governors) bearing tribute elephants, horses, and precious textiles. Each day featured a distinct ritual: Kalasha‑sthapana (installation of the sacred pot), Chandi‑homa (fire sacrifice to the goddess Durga), Kanya‑puja (worship of nine young girls representing the nine forms of the deity), and the final Vijaya‑dashami where the king symbolically “conquered” a straw effigy of the demon Mahishasura. The festival served three political functions. First, it displayed the ruler’s sakti (divine power) to the assembled nayakas, reinforcing the hierarchical bond that underpinned the nayankara system. Second, the distribution of prasada (sacred food) and danam (gifts of gold, cloth, and land grants) to Brahmins, soldiers, and merchants acted as a fiscal redistribution mechanism that cemented loyalty. Third, the presence of foreign envoys (Portuguese, Persian, and Arab traders) turned the ceremony into a diplomatic stage; the 1520 Portuguese embassy recorded that the king granted them a firman for duty‑free trade at the port of Bhatkal during the festival. The Mahanavami Dibba itself, with its sculpted friezes depicting hunting, dance, and military review, functioned as a permanent visual manifesto of imperial ideology. After the 1565 Battle of Talikota the festival continued in the successor courts of Penukonda and Chandragiri, but its scale diminished as the central treasury contracted.

  • Confusing the four Zabt land categories (Polaj, Parauti, Chachar, Banjar) with the later British ryotwari classification; the Mughal system was based on fallow cycles, not soil fertility.
  • Assuming the qanungo and patwari were interchangeable; the former kept the legal register, the latter performed field measurement.
  • Treating Ahom satras as purely religious bodies; they held land, judicial powers, and supplied military labour to the state.
  • Believing the Deccan sultanates copied the Mughal mansab verbatim; they used distinct rank scales (hazari in Bijapur, capped zat/sawar in Golconda) and different revenue units.
  • Overlooking the diplomatic dimension of the Mahanavami festival; foreign envoys received trade concessions tied to the ceremonial calendar.
  • Neglecting that the 1665 Deccan re‑measurement targeted Parauti land because it was the most vulnerable to mis‑classification.
  • Remember that each of these institutions operated within a specific fiscal‑military logic and cannot be transplanted across regions without adjustment.

The Mughal Empire's Diplomatic and Commercial Relations with the Dutch East India Company (VOC), 1604‑1795

The Dutch East India Company (Verenigde Oostindische Compagnie, VOC) entered the Mughal commercial sphere shortly after its formation in 1602. The first VOC fleet reached the Gujarat coast in 1604, and by 1616 the Company secured a farman from Emperor Jahangir permitting a factory at Surat, the principal Mughal port on the Arabian Sea. The farman granted the Dutch exemption from the customary 2.5 % customs duty (rahdari) on imports and exports, a privilege that was renewed and expanded under Shah Jahan (r. 1628‑1658) and Aurangzeb (r. 1658‑1707). Subsequent farmans allowed the VOC to establish factories at Ahmedabad (1618), Burhanpur (1620), Agra (1621), Patna (1632), and in Bengal at Chinsura (1653) and Dhaka (1665). Each factory operated under a Dutch factor (opperhoofd) who negotiated directly with the local Mughal governor (subahdar) and the port officer (bandar nawaz).

The VOC’s commercial strategy differed from that of the English East India Company in two respects. First, the Dutch concentrated on the procurement of high‑value textiles — especially Gujarat’s cotton piece‑goods, Bengal’s silk, and the famed “Dutch‑blue” indigo — for the European market and for intra‑Asian trade with the Indonesian archipelago. Second, the VOC maintained a permanent naval presence in the Indian Ocean, which it used to protect its convoys and to enforce its monopoly on the spice trade through the Strait of Malacca. This naval capacity gave the Dutch leverage in diplomatic negotiations; for example, in 1662 the VOC blockaded the Mughal port of Surat to pressure Aurangzeb into confirming their farman after a dispute over the seizure of a Dutch ship by the Mughal admiral (mir bahr).

Mughal authorities, for their part, valued the VOC as a reliable source of bullion. The Company imported silver from Japan and the Americas, which flowed into the imperial treasury (khazana) and helped sustain the silver‑based rupee standard. In return, the Mughals granted the Dutch the right to mint their own coins (the “VOC rupee”) at the Surat mint for use in their factories, a rare concession that underscored the mutual fiscal dependence. The relationship soured after 1700 when the Mughal central authority weakened; local governors began imposing arbitrary levies, and the VOC’s factories in the Deccan (e.g., at Hyderabad after the 1687 annexation of Golconda) faced increasing interference. By the 1750s the Dutch had lost most of their Mughal privileges, retaining only the Bengal factories until the British takeover of Bengal in 1757 effectively ended the VOC’s commercial role in the empire.

Key Diplomatic Instruments

  • Farman of Jahangir (1616) – Surat factory, duty exemption.
  • Farman of Shah Jahan (1638) – Expansion to Agra, Patna, and Bengal.
  • Farman of Aurangzeb (1665) – Confirmation of Bengal factories, permission to mint VOC rupees.
  • Treaty of 1662 (Surat blockade) – Dutch naval coercion to enforce farman compliance.

The Emergence of Urdu as a Literary Language: Persian, Hindavi, and Mughal Court Patronage (16th‑18th Centuries)

Urdu, known in its early phase as Rekhta or Hindavi, evolved from the Khari Boli dialect spoken in the Delhi‑Meerut region. The Mughal court’s official language remained Persian, but the everyday speech of the nobility, the military, and the urban populace incorporated a growing Persian‑Arabic lexical layer. This synthesis accelerated under Akbar (r. 1556‑1605), whose policy of cultural integration encouraged the translation of Sanskrit texts into Persian and the composition of poetry in the vernacular. The Ain‑i‑Akbari records that Akbar’s court poets, such as Faizi and Abul Fazl, experimented with Hindavi metres, laying a literary precedent.

The decisive institutional push came from the Deccan sultanates, especially Golconda and Bijapur, where Dakhni Urdu flourished as a courtly idiom from the late 16th century. Poets like Wali Muhammad Wali (c. 1667‑1707) and the Sufi saint Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai composed in a language that blended Persian poetic forms (ghazal, masnavi) with indigenous metres (doha, chaupai). When Aurangzeb annexed the Deccan (1686‑1687), many Dakhni poets migrated to Delhi, bringing their stylistic conventions to the imperial capital. The Mughal emperor Muhammad Shah (r. 1719‑1748) became a notable patron; his court hosted the “Urdu” mushairas (poetic symposia) where the term “Urdu‑e‑Mualla” (the exalted camp language) began to appear in written prefaces.

Script development paralleled literary growth. The Nastaliq script, refined in Persia, was adopted for writing Hindavi in the Mughal chancery, replacing the earlier Devanagari‑based orthography used by some Hindu scribes. The Mughal imperial library (kitabkhana) at Delhi and the translation bureau (Maktab Khana) produced Persian‑Urdu dictionaries, notably the “Farhang‑e‑Asifiya” (compiled 1790s under the patronage of the Nawab of Awadh), which codified vocabulary and grammar. By the mid‑18th century, Urdu had displaced Persian as the language of poetry in the Mughal successor states — Awadh, Hyderabad, and the Maratha‑controlled territories — while Persian retained its administrative function until the British replaced it with English in 1837.

Stages of Linguistic Consolidation

  1. Early Hindavi (c. 1500‑1600): oral poetry, limited Persian borrowing.
  2. Dakhni flowering (c. 1580‑1680): courtly ghazal, Sufi masnavi, distinct phonology.
  3. Delhi synthesis (c. 1680‑1740): migration of Dakhni poets, Mughal patronage, Nastaliq standardisation.
  4. Classical Urdu (c. 1740‑1800): codified grammar, major poets (Mir, Sauda, Ghalib), institutional support from nawabs.
Examiner's Trap: Language vs. Administration
A question asks: “Which language served as the official administrative language of the Mughal Empire throughout the 17th century?” The answer choices include Persian, Urdu, Arabic, and Hindi.
The correct answer is Persian. Urdu (Rekhta/Hindavi) was a literary and spoken language that gained courtly prestige only in the late 17th and 18th centuries; it never replaced Persian in farmans, revenue records, or judicial documents during the height of Mughal power. The trap lies in conflating literary patronage with administrative practice.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question links a language to a specific Mughal function (administration, coinage, court poetry), first identify the function’s chronological layer. Persian = administration (1526‑1837); Urdu = poetry and elite conversation (late 17th c. onward); Arabic = religious/legal texts; Hindi = regional revenue records in some provinces. Match the function to the correct language to avoid the common conflation.

The Mughal Imperial Navy: Organization, Port Administration, and Naval Engagements in the Indian Ocean (16th‑17th Centuries)

Although the Mughal Empire is primarily remembered as a land power, it maintained a functional naval establishment (mir bahr) from the reign of Akbar onward. The mir bahr, a senior officer reporting to the mir bakhshi (paymaster‑general), supervised shipbuilding yards at Surat, Cambay, and later at Dhaka and Chittagong. The fleet consisted of two principal vessel types: the ghurab, a three‑masted, square‑rigged ship of 200‑400 tons used for oceanic voyages, and the gallivat, a smaller, oar‑assisted craft of 50‑100 tons suited for riverine and coastal patrol. Crews were recruited from the coastal communities of Gujarat, Konkan, and Bengal, and were paid through the mansabdari system — naval officers held mansabs with sawar ranks reflecting the number of sailors they commanded.

Port administration was entrusted to the bandar nawaz (port master), who collected customs (rahdari), regulated pilotage, and coordinated with the local faujdar for security. The Mughal customs regime at Surat, the empire’s busiest port, generated an annual revenue of roughly 12 million rupees in the 1620s, a substantial portion of which funded naval maintenance. The navy’s primary missions were: (1) protection of the pilgrim traffic to Mecca (the “Hajj fleet”) against Portuguese and Omani corsairs; (2) suppression of piracy in the Bay of Bengal, notably the Arakanese raids on Mughal shipping; and (3) support of amphibious operations, such as the 1666 conquest of Chittagong by Shaista Khan, where a combined land‑sea force expelled the Portuguese and Arakanese from the strategic port.

The Mughal navy engaged European powers on several occasions. In 1615 a Mughal squadron under the mir bahr assisted the English in repelling a Portuguese blockade of Surat, an early instance of Mughal‑European naval cooperation. Conversely, the 1662 Dutch blockade of Surat demonstrated the limits of Mughal naval power against a well‑armed VOC fleet. By the late 17th century, the navy’s effectiveness declined due to fiscal strain, the loss of experienced shipwrights to the Maratha naval commander Kanhoji Angre, and the diversion of resources to the Deccan campaigns. Aurangzeb’s death in 1707 marked the effective end of a centrally directed Mughal navy; subsequent regional powers (the Nawabs of Bengal, the Marathas, and the European companies) assumed maritime dominance.

Naval Administrative Hierarchy

Mughal Naval Command Structure (c. 1650)
RankTitleResponsibility
1Mir BahrOverall naval commander, reports to Mir Bakhshi
2Admiral (Sardar‑i‑Bahri)Commands a fleet squadron (5‑10 ghurabs)
3Port Master (Bandar Nawaz)Customs, pilotage, shipyard oversight at major ports
4Ship Captain (Nakhuda)Individual vessel command, crew discipline

The Mughal Patronage of Astronomy and Scientific Instruments: The Observatory at Delhi, the Zij‑i‑Sultani, and the Role of the Court Astronomers

Mughal interest in astronomy was both practical — regulating the calendar for revenue collection and religious festivals — and symbolic, reflecting the emperor’s claim to universal sovereignty. Akbar (r. 1556‑1605) established an observatory (rasad khana) at Fatehpur Sikri, staffed by Persian and Indian astronomers who compiled the “Zij‑i‑Akbari”, a set of planetary tables based on the earlier “Zij‑i‑Sultani” of Ulugh Beg (d. 1449). The Zij‑i‑Sultani, produced at the Samarkand observatory, remained the standard reference for Mughal astronomers throughout the 16th and 17th centuries; copies were kept in the imperial library (kitabkhana) and used for calculating the lunar calendar (hijri) and the solar Ilahi calendar introduced by Akbar.

Jahangir (r. 1605‑1627) continued this patronage, appointing the Persian astronomer Mirza Muhammad Salih as chief court astronomer (munajjim‑i‑sultan). Jahangir’s memoirs (Tuzuk‑i‑Jahangiri) record his personal observations of comets and solar eclipses, and he commissioned the construction of a large quadrant (rub‘ al‑mujayyab) at the Agra observatory. Under Shah Jahan (r. 1628‑1658), the focus shifted to the production of refined astrolabes and celestial globes in the imperial workshops (karkhanas) at Lahore and Delhi. These instruments, often inlaid with silver and inscribed with Persian and Arabic legends, were presented as diplomatic gifts to the Ottoman and Safavid courts, reinforcing the Mughal image as a centre of scientific culture.

The most ambitious Mughal astronomical project was undertaken during the reign of Muhammad Shah (r. 1719‑1748) in collaboration with the Rajput ruler Sawai Jai Singh II of Amber. Jai Singh, a mansabdar of 7,000 zat, built five monumental observatories (Jantar Mantar) at Delhi, Jaipur, Ujjain, Mathura, and Varanasi between 1724 and 1735. The Delhi observatory housed the “Samrat Yantra”, a giant equatorial sundial capable of measuring time to an accuracy of two seconds. Jai Singh’s team, which included the Portuguese Jesuit missionary Father Manuel de Figueredo, produced the “Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi”, a new set of planetary tables that incorporated European observational data (Tycho Brahe’s star catalogue) alongside traditional Islamic parameters. This synthesis exemplifies the Mughal court’s capacity to integrate diverse scientific traditions.

Court astronomers also performed astrological duties, casting horoscopes for royal births, military campaigns, and the selection of auspicious dates for coronations. The “munajjim‑i‑sultan” held a mansab and received a jagir, reflecting the institutionalisation of astronomy within the mansabdari framework. By the mid‑18th century, the decline of central patronage and the rise of European scientific institutions in Calcutta and Madras eclipsed the Mughal observatories, but the Zij tables and instruments produced under Mughal auspices continued to be used in Indian astronomical practice well into the colonial period.

Major Mughal Astronomical Works

  • Zij‑i‑Akbari (c. 1580) – based on Ulugh Beg’s Zij‑i‑Sultani, compiled at Fatehpur Sikri.
  • Zij‑i‑Jahangiri (c. 1610) – updated planetary positions, includes comet observations.
  • Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi (1730s) – synthesis of Islamic and European data, produced at Jai Singh’s observatories.
  • Confusing the VOC’s farman chronology with that of the English East India Company; the Dutch secured Surat in 1616, the English in 1613.
  • Assuming Urdu replaced Persian in official documents before 1800; Persian remained the administrative language until the British substitution.
  • Attributing a standing ocean‑going navy to the Mughals comparable to European fleets; the Mughal navy was primarily riverine/coastal and lacked a permanent blue‑water battle line.
  • Believing the Zij‑i‑Sultani was a Mughal original; it was a 15th‑century Timurid work adopted and copied by Mughal astronomers.
  • Overlooking the role of the bandar nawaz in naval logistics; the port master, not the mir bahr, controlled customs revenue that funded shipbuilding.
  • Treating the Jantar Mantar observatories as solely Mughal projects; they were built by a Rajput mansabdar under Mughal suzerainty, not by the imperial court directly.
  • Neglecting the fiscal link between VOC silver imports and the stability of the Mughal rupee; the Dutch bullion flow was a critical prop for the imperial monetary standard.

Mughal‑Dutch Commercial Relations: The 1616 Surat Farman, VOC Factories, and the Silver Flow

The Dutch East India Company (VOC) secured its first Mughal farman in 1616 from Emperor Jahangir, granting permission to trade at Surat and to establish a factory there. The farman exempted VOC goods from the usual customs duties (rahdari) and allowed the company to maintain its own warehouses and a small armed guard. By 1617 the VOC had a functioning factory at Surat, and within two decades it expanded to Agra (1621), Ahmedabad (1630), Burhanpur (1635), Patna (1640), and Chinsurah in Bengal (1656). The VOC’s most consequential contribution to the Mughal economy was the massive import of silver — primarily Japanese and New World bullion — which entered the imperial mints at Surat, Ahmedabad, and later at Delhi and Agra. Contemporary Dutch records (the Generale Missiven) show that between 1620 and 1660 the VOC shipped an average of 200,000 kilograms of silver annually into Mughal ports, a flow that underpinned the stability of the silver rupee (rupiya) during the reigns of Jahangir, Shah Jahan, and early Aurangzeb. In return, the VOC obtained cotton textiles, indigo, saltpetre, and opium, which it shipped to Southeast Asia and Europe. The Mughal court valued Dutch artillery expertise; Dutch gunners were employed in the siege of Kandahar (1649) and in the Deccan campaigns of Aurangzeb. However, the Anglo‑Dutch wars (1652‑1674) and the Mughal imposition of stricter customs after 1680 eroded VOC privileges. By the 1690s the VOC’s share of Mughal maritime trade had fallen below that of the English East India Company, and its factories at Surat and Hugli operated under increasingly restrictive farmans issued by Aurangzeb’s successors.

Mughal Patronage of Hindu Religious Institutions: Temple Grants, Matha Endowments, and the Politics of Legitimacy

From Akbar onward, Mughal emperors issued farmans granting tax‑free land (madad‑i‑maash or suyurghal) to Hindu temples, mathas (monastic centres), and individual brahmin scholars. Akbar’s 1580 farman to the Govind Dev temple at Vrindavan allocated 200 bighas of revenue‑free land in the pargana of Mathura, recorded in both Persian and Braj Bhasha. Jahangir continued the practice: a 1613 farman confirmed the endowment of the Ramanandi matha at Galta (near Jaipur) with 150 bighas, and a 1620 grant to the Jagannath temple at Puri exempted its hereditary servitors (sevayats) from the jizya. Shah Jahan’s 1632 farman to the Kashi Vishwanath temple in Varanasi restored lands confiscated during earlier raids and appointed a Mughal‑appointed mutawalli to oversee the grant’s administration. Even Aurangzeb, despite his reputation for temple destruction, issued a 1665 farman confirming the endowment of the Shaiva matha at Srisailam (Andhra) with 300 bighas, and a 1687 grant to the Vaishnava matha at Nabadwip in Bengal. These grants served multiple purposes: they integrated influential religious elites into the imperial patronage network, secured local revenue collection through trusted intermediaries, and projected the emperor’s image as a universal sovereign (padshah‑i‑alam) protecting all faiths. The documentary trail — Persian farmans, local language sanads, and temple inscriptions — demonstrates a systematic policy rather than ad‑hoc benevolence.

Technique: Comparative Farman Analysis
Compare the 1616 VOC farman and the 1580 Govind Dev temple farman in terms of language, fiscal concessions, and political signalling.
The 1616 VOC farman is drafted in Persian with standard diplomatic formulae (e.g., “az janib‑i‑sultanat”), grants customs exemption (rahdari‑maafi) and the right to maintain a fortified warehouse (kothi). It signals Mughal openness to European maritime commerce and the emperor’s desire for silver inflows. The 1580 Govind Dev farman, while also in Persian, includes a Braj Bhasha summary for local administrators, grants land revenue exemption (madad‑i‑maash) on 200 bighas, and appoints a Hindu mutawalli. Its political signalling emphasizes the emperor’s role as protector of Hindu sacred space, reinforcing legitimacy among Rajput and Brahmin elites. Both documents use the farman format but tailor concessions — commercial vs. agrarian — to the recipient’s function in the imperial system.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “Mughal policy towards non‑Muslim institutions”, cite at least two farmans from different reigns (e.g., Akbar 1580, Jahangir 1613, Aurangzeb 1665) and note the bilingual (Persian + vernacular) nature of the grants. This demonstrates both chronological breadth and administrative detail.

The Emergence of Successor States in the Early Eighteenth Century: Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad, and the Maratha Confederacy

The death of Aurangzeb in 1707 triggered a rapid fragmentation of central authority. In Bengal, Murshid Quli Khan, appointed diwan in 1700 and later nawab (1717), reorganised revenue collection through the malguzari system, fixed cash revenue demands, and shifted the capital from Dhaka to Murshidabad, creating a semi‑autonomous fiscal unit that remitted only a nominal tribute to Delhi. In Awadh, Saadat Khan Burhan‑ul‑Mulk received the subahdar’s commission in 1722; he introduced the ijara (revenue farming) system on a large scale, built a standing army of 30,000 cavalry, and negotiated the 1739 treaty with Nadir Shah that recognised Awadh’s de facto independence. The Deccan saw the most dramatic break: Nizam‑ul‑Mulk Asaf Jah, appointed viceroy of the Deccan in 1713, defeated the Mughal‑appointed governor at the Battle of Shakar Kheda (1724) and established Hyderabad as a sovereign principality, retaining the Mughal title Nizam‑ul‑Mulk while minting his own coins and conducting independent diplomacy with the Marathas and the European companies. Simultaneously, the Maratha Confederacy under Peshwa Baji Rao I (1720‑1740) exploited Mughal weakness, extracting chauth and sardeshmukhi from Malwa, Gujarat, and the Carnatic through a series of rapid cavalry raids (e.g., the 1728 Palkhed campaign). The 1719 farman of Emperor Farrukhsiyar formally recognised Maratha claims to chauth in the Deccan, legitimising their fiscal extraction. By the 1740s the Mughal emperor in Delhi had become a ceremonial figurehead, while Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad, and the Maratha Confederacy functioned as independent political economies, each maintaining its own administrative machinery, revenue systems, and military establishments.

Mughal Imperial Library (Kitabkhana) and the Production of Official Historiography: The Akbarnama, Padshahnama, and Alamgirnama as Administrative Instruments

Akbar founded the imperial kitabkhana at Fatehpur Sikri (c. 1575), later expanded at Agra and Delhi, staffed by a kitabdar (librarian), calligraphers, painters, and translators. The library’s flagship project was the Akbarnama (completed 1598), authored by Abu’l‑Fazl ibn Mubarak, which combined a chronological narrative with the Ain‑i‑Akbari — a detailed statistical compendium of revenue rates, military organisation, and court protocol. Illustrated manuscripts (e.g., the Victoria and Albert Museum copy) served as visual manuals for court ceremony. Under Jahangir, the Tuzuk‑i‑Jahangiri (the emperor’s memoir) was copied into the library and used to train princes in statecraft. Shah Jahan commissioned the Padshahnama (c. 1640‑1656) by Abdul Hamid Lahori, a lavishly illustrated chronicle that recorded architectural projects (Taj Mahal, Red Fort) and diplomatic missions, functioning as a reference for protocol and precedence. Aurangzeb’s reign produced the Alamgirnama (c. 1668‑1698) by Muhammad Kazim, which emphasised legal rulings (fatawa) and military campaigns, providing a legal‑administrative guide for provincial governors. The kitabkhana also housed Persian translations of the Mahabharata (Razmnama, 1584‑1586) and the Ramayana, commissioned to facilitate dialogue with Hindu elites. Manuscript production followed a strict workflow: paper from the imperial kagazkhana, calligraphy by katibs, marginal illumination by musawwirs, and binding in the jildkhana. These texts were not merely literary; they were consulted during succession disputes, revenue settlements, and ceremonial occasions, making the library an integral component of Mughal governance.

Mughal Naval Administration: The Mir Bahr, Riverine Fleets, and Coastal Defence in the Bay of Bengal and Arabian Sea

The Mughal navy never developed a blue‑water battle line; its core consisted of riverine flotillas on the Ganges, Yamuna, Indus, and Brahmaputra, supplemented by coastal squadrons at Surat, Hughli, and Chittagong. The senior naval officer, the Mir Bahr (Admiral of the Sea), held a mansab (typically 3,000‑5,000 zat) and reported directly to the Mir Bakhshi. Under Akbar, Mir Bahr Ibrahim Khan commanded a fleet of 200 ghurabs (large oared gunboats) and 150 gallivats (smaller patrol craft) based at Dhaka, securing the Bengal delta against Arakanese raids and Portuguese pirates. Jahangir’s Mir Bahr Qasim Khan (appointed 1613) expanded the Surat squadron to protect the pilgrim traffic to Mecca and the VOC/English factories. Shah Jahan’s reign saw the construction of a dedicated dockyard at Khizri (near Surat) in 1638, where hulls were sheathed with copper to resist teredo worms — a technique borrowed from Portuguese shipwrights. Aurangzeb deployed naval forces in the 1662 expedition to Chittagong, where a combined riverine‑coastal force under Mir Bahr Muhammad Amin Khan captured the Arakanese fort after a three‑month siege, re‑establishing Mughal suzerainty over the port. The naval budget was drawn from customs revenue collected by the bandar nawaz (port master) at Surat and Hughli; a 1665 account shows 12 % of Surat’s customs (≈ 1.2 million rupees) allocated to fleet maintenance. By the 1690s, European naval superiority — especially the English squadron at Bombay — rendered the Mughal riverine fleet ineffective for power projection beyond the estuaries, and the Mir Bahr post became largely ceremonial.

  • Confusing the VOC’s 1616 farman with the English 1613 farman; the Dutch secured Surat later and on different terms.
  • Assuming all Mughal temple grants were revoked under Aurangzeb; several farmans confirm continued endowments to mathas.
  • Treating the 1719 Farrukhsiyar farman as a Maratha “treaty” rather than a unilateral imperial concession.
  • Overlooking the bilingual (Persian + vernacular) nature of temple sanads, which is a key marker of administrative integration.
  • Believing the Mughal kitabkhana was only a repository; it actively produced administrative manuals like the Ain‑i‑Akbari.
  • Equating the Mir Bahr with a European admiral commanding ocean‑going ships; the post controlled riverine gunboats and coastal patrols.
  • Neglecting the fiscal link between Surat customs revenue and the naval budget, which explains the fleet’s decline when customs fell.

The Mughal Chancery (Daftar) and the Production of Farmans, Sanads, and Parwanas: Bureaucratic Formulas, Seal Practices, and Archival Preservation

The imperial chancery, known as the Daftar, functioned as the central documentary engine of the Mughal state from Akbar’s reign onward. It was headed by the Mir Munshi (Chief Secretary) who supervised three principal branches: the Daftar‑i‑Diwani (revenue records), the Daftar‑i‑Mansab (mansab registers), and the Daftar‑i‑Insha (correspondence and drafting). Each branch maintained its own set of daftars (registers) in which every outgoing document was entered chronologically, ensuring an audit trail that could be consulted during succession disputes or revenue settlements.

Three documentary forms dominated chancery output. A farman was a sovereign order bearing the emperor’s personal seal (muhr‑i‑shahi) and the formula “Az taraf‑i‑sultan…” (“By order of the Sultan…”). Farmans conferred jagirs, appointed officers, or proclaimed policy. A sanad was a grant deed — often bilingual (Persian and the regional vernacular) — that recorded land endowments (madad‑i‑ma’ash), tax exemptions, or hereditary rights (watan). A parwana served as a routine administrative instruction issued by provincial governors (subahdars) or the diwan to subordinate officers such as amils or faujdars; it carried the seal of the issuing authority rather than the imperial seal.

Production followed a strict workflow. Paper came from the imperial kagazkhana at Fatehpur Sikri (later Agra and Delhi). Calligraphers (katibs) copied the text in nasta’liq script; marginal illumination (ta’liq) was added by musawwirs for farmans destined for high‑ranking nobles. The completed sheet passed to the jildkhana for binding in leather covers stamped with the imperial emblem. The muhr‑i‑shahi was kept under the personal custody of the Mir Bakhshi and applied only in the presence of the emperor or his designated deputy, a safeguard against forgery.

Archival preservation was institutionalised. The Kitabkhana (imperial library) housed master copies of all farmans, sanads, and parwanas, indexed by regnal year and subject. Duplicate registers were dispatched to provincial daftars at Lahore, Allahabad, and Burhanpur, creating a redundant network that survived the empire’s fragmentation. Modern historians rely on these surviving daftars — especially the Daftar‑i‑Insha collections in the National Archives, New Delhi — to reconstruct Mughal administrative language, fiscal rates, and the evolution of sealing protocols.

The Evolution of the Iqta into the Mughal Jagir: Continuities in Revenue Assignment, Military Obligation, and the Role of the Muqti

The iqta of the Delhi Sultanate was a non‑hereditary revenue assignment granted to a muqti (holder) in return for maintaining a specified number of cavalry. Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296‑1316) tightened control by fixing the khiraj (land tax) at half the produce, mandating measurement (paimaish) and requiring the muqti to remit surplus to the central treasury. The muqti exercised limited administrative powers — collection, local justice, and troop levies — but could be transferred at the sultan’s pleasure.

When Babur (r. 1526‑1530) and Humayun (r. 1530‑1540, 1555‑1556) established Mughal rule, they initially replicated the iqta model, calling the assignments jagir (Persian for “place”). Akbar’s mansabdari reforms (c. 1574) transformed the jagir into a salary‑linked instrument: every mansabdar received a zat (personal rank) and a sawar (cavalry obligation). The jagir’s estimated revenue (jama) was calibrated to the mansabdar’s pay scale, and the actual collection was performed by an amil appointed by the diwan, not by the jagirdar himself. This separation of fiscal collection from military command was a decisive break from the muqti’s dual role.

Continuities persisted. The jagir remained transferable — typically every three to four years (tankhwah) — to prevent local entrenchment, echoing the Sultanate’s practice of rotating muqtis. The muqti’s obligation to furnish troops survived as the sawar quota, and the central audit (waqia‑navis reports) mirrored the Sultanate’s barid intelligence on revenue remittances. However, the Mughal system introduced the zabt cadastre (Todar Mal’s bandobast) which fixed cash revenue rates (dastur‑ul‑amal) for each pargana, reducing the jagirdar’s discretionary extraction.

Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan the jagir pool expanded, but the number of high‑value assignments shrank, prompting the du‑aspah sih‑aspah innovation (extra cavalry for select nobles). By Aurangzeb’s reign (1658‑1707) the scarcity of productive jagirs led to the jagirdari crisis: mansabdars received “paper” jagirs in the Deccan where revenue collection was uncertain, accelerating the empire’s fiscal decay. The trajectory from iqta to jagir thus illustrates a continuum of revenue‑for‑service, progressively overlaid with centralized accounting, standardized measurement, and a bureaucratic firewall between the assignee and the cultivator.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “The Mughal jagir was a direct continuation of the Delhi Sultanate iqta with no significant institutional change.” Identify the error and explain the key differences.
The statement is false. While both systems assigned revenue for military service, the Mughal jagir introduced (1) a strict separation of collection (by the amil) from the assignee, (2) a cash‑based salary scale linked to zat/sawar ranks, (3) periodic mandatory transfers (tankhwah) enforced by the central diwan, and (4) a uniform cadastral survey (zabt) that fixed revenue demand, unlike the Sultanate’s variable khiraj. These innovations made the jagir a more bureaucratised, less autonomous instrument.
Exam‑day strategy
When comparing Sultanate and Mughal revenue assignments, structure the answer around three pillars: (a) collection authority, (b) revenue fixation method, (c) transferability and audit. Cite the zabt cadastre and the amil as Mughal‑specific; cite the muqti’s direct collection and the Khalji khiraj as Sultanate‑specific. This framework prevents conflation and earns full marks for analytical depth.

The Vijayanagara Port Administration at Bhatkal and Honavar: Customs Farming, the Bandar Nawaz, and Maritime Revenue Accounting

The western seaboard of the Vijayanagara empire — especially the ports of Bhatkal and Honavar (Onore) — functioned as the primary outlets for pepper, rice, textiles, and iron ore destined for the Arabian Sea trade. Unlike the overland nayankara assignments, maritime revenue was managed through a distinct fiscal apparatus centred on the Bandar Nawaz (Port Master), an officer appointed directly by the Mahanayakacharya or the Raya himself.

Customs duties (sunkam) were levied at ad valorem rates — typically 5 % on pepper, 2.5 % on textiles, and a flat fee per ship (bandar‑kharch). Rather than collecting directly, the state farmed out the collection rights (ijara) to wealthy merchant‑bankers, often Chetti or Nayaka families, for a fixed annual sum (peshkash). The farmer (ijaradar) advanced the peshkash to the treasury and retained any surplus, creating a strong incentive for efficient assessment. Surviving kaifiyat (revenue accounts) from the 1520s show that Bhatkal’s annual customs farm fetched 12,000 varaha (gold coins), while Honavar’s farm yielded 8,500 varaha.

The Bandar Nawaz supervised the Bandar Kacheri (port office), where ships’ manifests (rohla) were recorded in bilingual registers (Kannada and Persian). He also maintained the naval‑kharch ledger for expenditures on dredging, pier repair, and the maintenance of a small flotilla of ghurabs (armed oared vessels) used against piracy. The port’s revenue was remitted to the central Huzur Daftar at Hampi via a dedicated dak relay, ensuring that the imperial treasury received real‑time data on maritime inflows.

Portuguese interference after 1510 altered the fiscal landscape. The 1518 treaty with Goa required Vijayanagara to allow Portuguese factors to inspect customs registers, and the 1540 Cartaz system compelled ships to carry Portuguese passes, effectively diverting a portion of pepper revenue to Estado da Índia. By the 1560s, after the Battle of Talikota, the ports fell under the control of the Nayakas of Ikkeri and later the Keladi chiefs, who continued the ijara system but redirected peshkash to their own coffers. The administrative continuity of the Bandar Nawaz office, however, persisted in the coastal keladi records well into the seventeenth century.

The Bijapur Sultanate’s Mint Organization: The Sarraf, the Adil Shahi Rupee, and the Integration of Deccan Monetary Zones

The Bijapur Sultanate (c. 1490‑1686) operated a sophisticated mint at its capital, Bijapur, and at secondary sites such as Gulbarga and Raichur. The mint was headed by a sarraf‑i‑mamalik (chief moneyer), usually a trusted merchant‑banker from the Bohra or Jain communities, who held a hereditary mansab of 1,000 zat. The sarraf supervised the entire production chain: procurement of bullion (silver from the Portuguese factories at Goa, gold from the Vijayanagara hinterland, copper from local mines), assaying, die‑cutting, striking, and quality control.

The standard silver coin was the Adil Shahi rupee (≈ 11.6 g, 0.917 fineness), inscribed with the sultan’s name, the Hijri date, and the mint epithet “Zarb Bijapur”. Gold hon (≈ 10.8 g) and copper dam (≈ 20 g) circulated alongside. Under Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) the rupee’s weight was aligned with the Mughal rupiya (11.66 g) to facilitate inter‑regional trade, and the mint began issuing bilingual legends (Persian on obverse, Kannada on reverse) — a unique feature among Deccan sultanates.

Monetary integration was reinforced through the sarraf network. Bijapur’s sarrafs maintained correspondent accounts with the Mughal mint at Surat and the Golconda mint at Hyderabad, enabling bills of exchange (hundi) denominated in rupees to clear across the Deccan. The 1636 Mughal‑Bijapur treaty stipulated that Bijapur’s rupee would be accepted at par in Mughal territories, and the Mughal diwan accepted Bijapur’s hundi for revenue remittances from the Karnatak. After the 1686 Mughal annexation, the Bijapur mint was reorganised as a provincial Mughal mint; the existing dies were reused with the addition of the emperor’s regnal year, ensuring continuity of the local currency standard.

Archival evidence — the Mint‑daftar of 1652 preserved in the Salar Jung Museum — records monthly output: 1.2 million rupees, 85,000 hons, and 3.4 million dams. The sarraf’s profit margin was fixed at 1.5 % of bullion value, audited by the diwan‑i‑mal. This institutionalised mint‑banking nexus underpinned Bijapur’s fiscal resilience until the Mughal conquest.

The Mughal Imperial Household’s Patronage of Sufi Shrines: Cash Grants (Madad‑i‑Ma’ash), Land Endowments, and Political Legitimacy

The Mughal emperors cultivated Sufi orders as a pillar of imperial legitimacy, channeling resources through the Sadr‑us‑Sudur (Chief Ecclesiastical Officer) and the household treasury (Khazana‑i‑Khas). Two principal instruments were employed: madad‑i‑ma’ash (cash stipends) and waqf (inalienable land endowments). Akbar (r. 1556‑1605) institutionalised the practice by granting annual stipends of 500‑2,000 rupees to the custodians (mutawallis) of the Chishti dargah at Ajmer, the Suhrawardi shrine at Multan, and the Naqshbandi khanaqah at Delhi. These stipends were recorded in the Daftar‑i‑Madad‑i‑Ma’ash and paid quarterly from the Khazana.

Land endowments took the form of sanads specifying village revenues (jama) assigned in perpetuity to the shrine. Jahangir (r. 1605‑1627) issued a celebrated farman in 1613 granting the village of Kairana (revenue 12,000 dams) to the dargah of Nizamuddin Auliya, with the condition that the mutawalli maintain a free kitchen (langar) for pilgrims. Shah Jahan (r. 1628‑1658) expanded the programme, endowing over 150 villages to the Chishti, Qadiri, and Shattari orders across the empire. The sanads were bilingual (Persian and the local vernacular) and bore the imperial seal, making them legally binding on provincial amils.

Political utility was explicit. By supporting shrines that commanded large followings among peasants, artisans, and soldiers, the emperors secured a moral constituency that transcended sectarian lines. The urs (annual death anniversary) festivals became occasions for the emperor’s representatives to distribute robes of honour (khilat) and proclaim imperial benevolence. Conversely, Aurangzeb (r. 1658‑1707) curtailed cash stipends to orders he deemed heterodox (e.g., the Shattari), redirecting funds to orthodox Naqshbandi establishments, a shift reflected in the Daftar‑i‑Madad‑i‑Ma’ash entries after 1670.

Administratively, the Sadr‑us‑Sudur audited shrine accounts annually, verifying that endowment revenues were spent on prescribed charitable activities. Disputes over mutawalli succession were adjudicated in the Qazi courts, with the imperial farman serving as the ultimate title deed. The survival of thousands of such sanads in the National Archives and in shrine repositories attests to the scale and durability of this patronage network, which functioned as a soft‑power infrastructure complementing the Mughal military‑fiscal apparatus.

  • Confusing the Bandar Nawaz (port master) with the Mir Bahr (naval admiral) — they operated in distinct spheres (customs vs. riverine warfare).
  • Assuming all Mughal madad‑i‑ma’ash grants were revoked under Aurangzeb; farmans confirm continued endowments to major Chishti and Naqshbandi shrines.
  • Treating the Bijapur sarraf as a mere mint worker; he held a mansab, managed bullion procurement, and acted as a banker for inter‑regional hundi clearance.
  • Equating the Sultanate iqta with the Mughal jagir without noting the separation of collection (amil) from the assignee and the cash‑based zabt cadastre.
  • Overlooking the bilingual (Persian‑Kannada) legends on Bijapur rupees, a key marker of Deccan monetary integration.
  • Believing the Vijayanagara customs farm was a direct state collection; it was an ijara contracted to merchant‑bankers who paid a fixed peshkash.
  • Neglecting the role of the Sadr‑us‑Sudur in auditing shrine accounts, which ensured that endowment revenues were not diverted to private use.

Review these traps before the exam to avoid common errors.

The Role of the Mughal Imperial Library (Maktab Khana) in Translation and Knowledge Production

The Maktab Khana, formally instituted by Akbar in 1574, functioned as the empire’s central translation bureau and repository of manuscripts. Its mandate was to render Sanskrit, Arabic, Turkish, and regional Indian texts into Persian, the administrative lingua franca. The bureau operated under the direct supervision of the chief secretary (Wazir) and later the historian Abul Fazl, who coordinated teams of pandits, mullas, and scribes. Major projects included the Razmnama (Persian Mahabharata, completed 1584‑1586), the Ramayana (1589), the Atharva Veda (1590), and the Yogavasistha (1597). Each translation followed a collaborative protocol: a Sanskrit pandit read the original, a Persian‑versed scholar drafted the rendering, and a calligrapher produced the final manuscript on polished paper. The finished copies were bound in leather, stamped with the imperial seal, and stored in the royal library at Fatehpur Sikri and later at Agra and Delhi.

Beyond literary works, the Maktab Khana translated scientific treatises — astronomy (the Zij‑i‑Sultani), medicine (the Charaka Samhita), and mathematics (the Lilavati) — enabling Mughal astronomers and physicians to integrate Indic knowledge with Perso‑Islamic traditions. The bureau also produced administrative manuals such as the Ain‑i‑Akbari (compiled by Abul Fazl) which drew on translated revenue records and local customs. Funding came from the imperial treasury (Khazana‑i‑Khas) and was recorded in the Daftar‑i‑Maktab accounts; a typical annual outlay was 1.2 lakh dam, covering salaries, paper, ink, and binding. The library’s catalogue, the Kitab‑i‑Maktab, listed over 24,000 volumes by 1605, making it one of the largest manuscript collections in the early modern world. After Akbar, Jahangir and Shah Jahan continued patronage, though the scale diminished under Aurangzeb, who redirected resources to orthodox madrasas. The Maktab Khana’s legacy persists in the surviving illustrated manuscripts that demonstrate a synthesis of Persian miniature technique with Indian iconography, a visual record of the empire’s intellectual policy.

Maratha Naval Architecture: The Ghurab and Galbat Vessels in Coastal Warfare

The Maratha navy, consolidated under the Angre admirals from the 1690s, relied on two principal ship types adapted to the littoral conditions of the Konkan coast. The ghurab (also spelled gurab) was a heavy, ocean‑going warship of 300‑500 tons displacement, measuring roughly 35‑40 metres in length with a beam of 9‑10 metres. Its hull was carvel‑planked using teak from the Western Ghats, fastened with iron nails and sealed with a mixture of lime, jaggery, and coconut fibre. The ghurab carried a mainmast and a foremast rigged with lateen sails, supplemented by a square‑rigged topsail for downwind runs. Armament comprised 20‑30 bronze cannons (12‑18 pounders) mounted on broadside gunports, plus swivel guns on the forecastle and quarterdeck. Crew complement ranged from 150‑200, including gunners, marines, and a contingent of koli sailors skilled in coastal navigation.

The galbat (or galivat) was a lighter, shallow‑draft vessel of 80‑120 tons, 20‑25 metres long, with a beam of 5‑6 metres. Its hull was clinker‑built for flexibility in estuarine waters, and it carried a single lateen‑rigged mast. Armament was limited to 8‑12 swivel guns and a few light carriage guns. Galbats served as scouts, convoy escorts, and platforms for amphibious raids on Portuguese and Siddi settlements. Both ship classes were constructed at the royal dockyards of Vijaydurg, Suvarnadurg, and Khanderi, where master shipwrights (tarkhan) followed standardized plans recorded in the Daftar‑i‑Bahr (naval register). Timber procurement was regulated through rakam contracts with forest chiefs, ensuring a steady supply of seasoned teak and ain (Terminalia). Maintenance cycles included annual careening at the monsoon‑free dry docks, where hulls were scraped, caulked, and coated with a protective mixture of fish oil and resin.

Tactically, the ghurab formed the line of battle in set‑piece engagements such as the 1717 action off Khanderi, where a squadron of six ghurabs under Kanhoji Angre repelled a combined Portuguese‑English fleet. Galbats operated in wolf‑pack formations, harrying enemy supply lines and conducting night raids on the creek‑bound forts of the Siddis at Janjira. The Maratha naval doctrine emphasized mobility, knowledge of tidal windows, and the use of fortified island bases (e.g., Sindhudurg) as forward depots. By 1730 the Angre fleet numbered approximately 45 ghurabs and 120 galbats, making it the most formidable indigenous naval force on the western Indian seaboard until the British Royal Navy’s decisive intervention in the 1750s.

Technique
Distinguish the operational roles of the ghurab and the galbat in Maratha naval strategy, citing ship specifications and historical engagements.
The ghurab was the capital ship of the Maratha fleet: a 300‑500 ton, teak‑built, lateen‑and‑square‑rigged vessel mounting 20‑30 broadside cannon and a crew of 150‑200. It formed the battle line in major actions such as the 1717 Khanderi engagement, where six ghurabs under Kanhoji Angre defeated a Portuguese‑English squadron. The galbat, by contrast, was an 80‑120 ton clinker‑built craft with a single lateen mast, 8‑12 swivel guns, and a crew of 40‑60. Its shallow draft allowed operation in creeks and estuaries; galbats performed reconnaissance, convoy escort, and amphibious raids — notably the night attacks on Siddi positions at Janjira. Together, the two types gave the Marathas a balanced force capable of both fleet actions and littoral dominance.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “naval architecture” or “ship types”, tabulate displacement, rig, armament, crew, and a signature battle for each vessel. This converts descriptive recall into a quick‑scan table that examiners reward for clarity and completeness.

Vijayanagara's Market Regulation: The Role of the Pattana and the Guilds in Urban Commerce

Urban centres of the Vijayanagara empire (c. 1336‑1565) were organized around the pattana — a designated market quarter that functioned as both a physical space and an administrative unit. Each pattana was overseen by a royal officer titled pattana‑adhikari, who enforced standardized weights (the mana and ser), collected market tolls (sunkam), and adjudicated disputes between merchants. The toll rates were codified in the Karnataka‑Kavya inscriptions: 2 % on luxury goods (silk, precious stones), 1 % on bulk commodities (grain, cotton), and a flat fee of 5 panam per cartload for perishables. Revenue from sunkam was split between the imperial treasury (60 %) and the local temple treasury (40 %), reflecting the temple‑state fiscal nexus.

Guilds (shrenis) of artisans and traders — weavers (kaikkolar), metalworkers (kammalar), and spice merchants (vyapari) — operated under charters (sasana) granted by the king. These charters defined monopoly rights, quality standards, and dispute‑resolution procedures. For instance, the weavers’ guild at Hampi held the exclusive right to supply cloth for the royal household and temple festivals, in return for a fixed annual tribute of 1,000 varaha. Guild elders (mahajana) maintained registers of members, apprenticeship terms, and price ceilings, which the pattana‑adhikari could inspect. Violations attracted fines payable to the guild fund and, in severe cases, revocation of the charter.

The state also regulated foreign trade through the port‑pattanas at Bhatkal, Honavar, and Mangalore. Here, the bandar‑nayaka (port master) collected customs (sulka) at rates negotiated with Arab, Persian, and Portuguese merchants. The 1510 treaty with the Portuguese fixed the customs duty on pepper at 6 % ad valorem, while horses imported for the cavalry were exempted to secure military supply. Market infrastructure included covered bazaars (mandapas), weigh‑houses (tula‑mandir), and granaries (kottam) built on royal land but maintained by guild contributions. This layered regulation — imperial, guild, and port — created a predictable commercial environment that sustained Vijayanagara’s urban prosperity until the empire’s fragmentation after the Battle of Talikota (1565).

Delhi Sultanate's Military Slavery: The Ghulam and the Evolution of the Mamluk Corps

The institution of military slavery (ghulam or mamluk) was central to the Delhi Sultanate’s political structure from the Mamluk (Slave) dynasty (1206‑1290) through the Tughluq period. Iltutmish (r. 1211‑1236) formalized the corps by purchasing Turkic and Kipchak youths from Central Asian slave markets, converting them to Islam, and training them in archery, cavalry tactics, and Persian administrative skills. These ghulams were manumitted after a period of service (typically ten years) and granted iqta assignments, creating a loyal military aristocracy bound personally to the sultan. The core of the corps, known as the Chihilgān (the Forty), comprised Iltutmish’s most trusted ghulams who held the highest military and provincial posts.

Balban (r. 1266‑1287) restructured the corps to curb factionalism. He reduced the Chihilgān’s autonomy by appointing non‑ghulam officers to key frontier commands, introduced a strict hierarchy (khan, malik, amir), and instituted regular inspections (daftar‑i‑arz) of ghulam equipment and mounts. Balban also established the Diwan‑i‑Arz (military department) to maintain registers of ghulam service records, salaries (paid in cash tanka and land revenue), and lineage. The ghulam system persisted under the Khaljis: Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296‑1316) expanded recruitment to include Indian converts (hindavi ghulams) and used the corps to enforce his market reforms and Deccan campaigns. However, the Tughluqs (1320‑1413) increasingly relied on freeborn Turkish nobles and Afghan tribesmen, leading to a gradual decline of the ghulam’s exclusive hold on high command. By the late 14th century, the term ghulam denoted any royal slave‑soldier, while the original Mamluk identity had merged into the broader nobility. The legacy of the ghulam corps is evident in the administrative vocabulary — bandagan (royal slaves), khanzada (son of a khan) — and in the pattern of military‑slave recruitment that later Mughal emperors adapted for their own ahadi and barq‑andaz units.

Mughal Provincial Revenue Auditing: The Role of the Diwan‑i‑Khalsa and the Mustaufi

At the provincial (subah) level, fiscal oversight was exercised by the Diwan‑i‑Khalsa (imperial revenue office) headed by the provincial diwan, who reported directly to the central Diwan‑i‑Ala in the capital. The diwan’s primary instrument of audit was the mustaufi (auditor), a salaried official appointed from the imperial secretariat. Each mustaufi was assigned a circuit of 10‑15 parganas and tasked with verifying the jama (assessed revenue) against actual collections recorded in the bahi (ledger) of the amil (revenue collector). The audit cycle followed the agricultural year: a preliminary review after the kharif harvest (October‑November) and a final settlement after the rabi harvest (March‑April).

The mustaufi’s toolkit included the dastur‑ul‑amal (revenue manual) which prescribed measurement standards (the Ilahi gaz, the bigha), crop‑cutting experiments (paimaish) for yield estimation, and the zabt schedule of cash rates. Discrepancies exceeding 5 % triggered a taqaza (demand notice) to the amil, who could be fined, suspended, or replaced. The mustaufi also examined jagir accounts: the jagirdar submitted a haqiqat (statement of collections) which the mustaufi cross‑checked with the village patwari records. Fraudulent under‑reporting was punished by confiscation of the jagir and reassignment. Audit findings were compiled into the mahsul‑i‑subah (provincial revenue abstract) and forwarded to the central diwan for inclusion in the empire‑wide ain‑i‑dahsala (ten‑year revenue settlement).

Under Akbar, the mustaufi network comprised roughly 120 officers empire‑wide; by Shah Jahan’s reign the number rose to 180, reflecting the expansion of the zabt system into the Deccan. The office was insulated from local patronage: mustaufis rotated circuits every three years, and their salaries (2,000‑3,000 rupees annually) were paid from the imperial treasury, not from provincial revenues. This institutional design limited collusion between amils and local zamindars. The survival of mustaufi reports in the Mirat‑i‑Ahmadi and the Khulasat‑ut‑Tawarikh provides historians with granular data on revenue yields, crop patterns, and the incidence of peasant flight (pahar) during the late 17th‑century crises.

  • Confusing the Maktab Khana with the imperial library at large; the bureau was a translation workshop, not merely a repository.
  • Attributing the ghurab’s design solely to European influence; its hull form and lateen rig derive from indigenous Konkan shipbuilding traditions.
  • Assuming the pattana‑adhikari collected all market tolls personally; the sunkam was farmed to guild heads who remitted a fixed share.
  • Treating the ghulam corps as a static institution; its composition shifted from Turkic mamluks to Indian converts under the Khaljis.
  • Equating the mustaufi with the amil; the mustaufi was an independent auditor, not a collector.
  • Overlooking the bilingual (Persian‑Kannada) sasana charters that legitimized guild monopolies in Vijayanagara.
  • Believing the Diwan‑i‑Khalsa operated only at the centre; each subah maintained its own Diwan‑i‑Khalsa headed by the provincial diwan.

The sections above integrate administrative mechanics, military technology, commercial regulation, and fiscal auditing to give a comprehensive view of medieval Indian statecraft.

The Mughal Imperial Guard: Ahadi and Barq‑andaz Units in Court and Campaign

The Mughal emperors maintained two elite household corps that functioned both as personal bodyguards and as rapid‑deployment strike forces. The ahadi (singular ahad, “single”) were cavalrymen who received a fixed cash salary (usually 300–500 rupees per annum) directly from the imperial treasury rather than a jagir. Recruitment was open to free‑born Muslims of Persian, Central Asian, or Indian origin who could furnish their own horse and equipment; the emperor inspected each recruit at the daftar‑i‑arz and entered his name in the ahadi‑register. By Akbar’s reign the ahadi numbered roughly 4,000; under Jahangir the corps expanded to 7,000, and Shah Jahan maintained a standing strength of 8,000, organized into tuman (units of 1,000) each commanded by a sipahsalar of mansab 2,000/1,500.

The barq‑andaz (“lightning‑throwers”) were a smaller, highly specialised infantry corps armed with matchlock muskets (tufang) and trained in volley fire. Their name derives from the Persian barq (lightning) and andaz (thrower). Aurangzeb raised the first regular barq‑andaz battalion of 1,200 men in 1659 for the Deccan campaigns, equipping them with the newly standardised zam‑zam bore (≈19 mm) and issuing paper cartridges from the imperial karkhana at Agra. Unlike the ahadi, barq‑andaz soldiers were paid a monthly wage of 8–10 rupees plus a grain ration, and they were quartered in the barq‑andaz‑khana adjacent to the Red Fort. Their tactical doctrine emphasized coordinated salvoes followed by bayonet‑style charge with the katar, a doctrine recorded in the Fath‑i‑Alamgiri.

Both corps were directly subordinate to the Mir Bakhshi for pay and to the Khan‑i‑Saman for equipment. Their loyalty was reinforced by the khilat (robe of honour) ceremony held each Nowruz, and by the grant of hereditary mansab increments for distinguished service. In battle the ahadi formed the emperor’s right wing, while barq‑andaz detachments were deployed ahead of the main artillery to disrupt enemy cavalry charges. During the 1686 siege of Golconda, a barq‑andaz company under Muhammad Amin Khan inflicted heavy casualties on the Qutb Shahi cavalry, prompting Aurangzeb to order the expansion of the corps to 3,000 by 1690. The ahadi, meanwhile, were instrumental in the 1658 Battle of Samugarh, where their disciplined charge broke the centre of Dara Shikoh’s army.

Administratively, the ahadi and barq‑andaz registers were audited annually by the mustaufi‑i‑ahadi and mustaufi‑i‑barq‑andaz, who cross‑checked muster rolls against the bahi of the diwan‑i‑khalsa. Fraudulent entries — such as “ghost” troopers drawing pay — were punished by confiscation of the offender’s property and exile. The survival of the Ahadi‑Register of 1662 (British Library, Add. MS 23984) provides a rare nominal list, showing the ethnic composition: 42 % Turani, 35 % Iranian, 18 % Indian Muslim, 5 % Afghan. This data confirms the empire’s deliberate policy of ethnic balancing within the household guard.

Vijayanagara Temple Inscriptions as Administrative and Fiscal Records

Stone and copper‑plate inscriptions issued by the Vijayanagara rulers (c. 1336–1646) functioned as legally binding fiscal instruments rather than merely religious dedications. The standard formula — svasti śrī vijayarājye followed by the regnal year, the king’s name, the mahamandaleshvara or nayaka donor, and a precise description of the grant — mirrors the structure of a revenue sanad. Grants typically specify the village (grama), its survey number (khandika), the extent of land measured in khanduga (≈1.25 acres), the crop share (bhoga) due to the temple, and the exemptions (sarvamānya) from state taxes such as kudimai (land tax) and sunkam (market toll).

The Tirumala‑Devaraya copper plates of 1520 (Epigraphia Indica, Vol. VIII, No. 12) record a grant of 12 khanduga of wet land in the Kalyani‑nadu to the Venkateshvara temple at Tirupati, stipulating that the temple’s sthanika (manager) shall collect the bhoga directly from the cultivators and remit a fixed peshkash of 200 varaha annually to the royal treasury. The inscription also names the karanika (accountant) who shall maintain the pattadar register, linking temple revenue to the village patwari system. Such dual accountability — temple and state — created a checks‑and‑balances mechanism that limited arbitrary exactions by local nayakas.

Temple inscriptions also reveal the kandachara (military service) obligations attached to certain grants. The 1565 Kanchipuram inscription of Aliya Rama Raya orders that the nayaka of the Kanchipuram‑rajya supply 50 cavalrymen for the imperial army in return for the revenue of the granted villages. Failure to meet the quota triggered a danda (fine) payable to the temple treasury, which the king could enforce through the mahapradhani. This intertwining of fiscal and military duties illustrates the nayankara system’s operational reality.

From a historiographical perspective, the Śrīraṅgam temple’s 1,200‑odd inscriptions (collected in the Annual Report of the Archaeological Survey of India, 1905‑06) provide a longitudinal dataset on land measurement standards, crop patterns (rice, millet, cotton), and the frequency of peshkash revisions. Scholars such as Burton Stein and Noboru Karashima have used these records to reconstruct the empire’s agrarian expansion into the Tungabhadra doab and to demonstrate that the state’s revenue demand remained remarkably stable at roughly one‑sixth of the gross produce (ṣaḍbhāga) across two centuries.

Examiner's Trap: Conflating Religious Donation with Fiscal Grant
A question asks: “Vijayanagara temple inscriptions are primarily religious documents.” Explain why this statement is inaccurate, citing specific administrative elements found in the inscriptions.
The statement is inaccurate because Vijayanagara inscriptions follow the legal format of a revenue sanad: they cite regnal year, survey numbers (khandika), land extent (khanduga), crop share (bhoga), tax exemptions (sarvamānya), and the appointed accountant (karanika). The 1520 Tirumala‑Devaraya plates explicitly bind the temple manager to remit a fixed peshkash to the royal treasury and to maintain a pattadar register, demonstrating a dual fiscal‑administrative function. Moreover, the 1565 Kanchipuram inscription ties the grant to a kandachara military quota, enforceable by fine (danda). These features prove the inscriptions served as binding fiscal contracts, not merely pious endowments.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions “temple inscriptions”, immediately look for fiscal terminology (peshkash, bhoga, khanduga, karanika). The presence of any one of these terms converts the source from a religious text into an administrative record. Quote the specific term and its function to earn full marks.

The Deccan Sultanates’ Gold Pagoda and Silver Tanka: Metallic Standards and Trade

The five Deccan Sultanates — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — operated a bimetallic currency system that facilitated both internal revenue collection and Indian Ocean trade. The gold pagoda (also called hun or varaha) weighed 3.4 grams of 22‑carat gold (≈91.6 % fine) and was valued at 10–12 silver tankas depending on the market rate at the port of Goa. The silver tanka weighed 11.6 grams of 0.917 fine silver, conforming to the Mughal rupiya standard introduced by Sher Shah Suri (1540–45) and adopted by the Deccan mints after 1560. Bijapur’s Adil Shahi mint at Bijapur struck pagodas bearing the legend “Sultan Ali Adil Shah, Khallada Allah Mulkahu” on the obverse and a stylised lion on the reverse; Golconda’s Qutb Shahi mint at Golconda used the Persian couplet “Sikka‑e‑Qutb‑ul‑Mulk, Zarb‑e‑Golconda”.

Metallic standards were enforced by the sarraf (money‑changer) guilds under the supervision of the diwan‑i‑khalsa. The Ain‑i‑Dahsala of 1595 (preserved in the Mirat‑i‑Ahmadi) records that the Bijapur treasury accepted only pagodas of 22‑carat fineness; any coin testing below 21.5 carat was melted and re‑minted at a 2 % seigniorage charge. The Golconda mint maintained a tanka assay office where each batch of 1,000 coins was tested by the muharrir using the touchstone method; the results were entered in the bahi‑i‑sikka.

Trade data from the Portuguese Livros das Monções (1600‑1620) show that the pagoda traded at a premium of 8–10 % over the Mughal muhar in Goa, reflecting the higher gold content and the reputation of Deccan mints. Conversely, the tanka circulated at par with the Mughal rupee in the Coromandel ports, enabling seamless settlement of textile contracts between Deccan weavers and European factors. The 1615 Treaty of Surat (Mughal‑Portuguese) explicitly recognised the Deccan tanka as legal tender for customs duties at Daman, a rare instance of cross‑imperial monetary reciprocity.

Fiscal policy linked coinage to revenue farming. The ijara (revenue farm) contracts in Ahmadnagar (c. 1620) stipulated that the ijaradar remit the peshkash in pagodas for land revenue and in tankas for market tolls (sunkam).
