This dual‑currency requirement forced the sarraf network to maintain liquid reserves of both metals, stimulating the development of hundi (bill of exchange) markets centred on the bazaar‑i‑sarraf of Bijapur. The collapse of the pagoda’s gold content after 1680 — when Aurangzeb’s Deccan campaigns disrupted gold supplies from the Kolar fields — led to a 30 % depreciation against the tanka, a monetary shock documented in the Khulasat‑ut‑Tawarikh and cited as a factor in the fiscal crisis of the late Adil Shahi state.

Maratha Konkan Port Administration: The Bandar, the Customs Farm, and Naval Logistics

The Maratha control of the Konkan littoral (c. 1660–1760) rested on a tightly integrated port administration that combined customs revenue, ship‑building, and naval operations. The senior official at each major port — Rajapur, Dabhol, Vijaydurg, Sindhudurg — was the bandar‑adhyaksha (port master), appointed by the Peshwa on the recommendation of the Saranjamdar of the coastal saranjam. The bandar‑adhyaksha held a mansab of 500/300 and received a jagir of 5–10 villages whose revenue covered his establishment.

Customs collection (sunkam) was farmed annually to a sunkam‑ijaradar who bid at the huzur daftar in Pune. The 1685 farm record for Rajapur (preserved in the Peshwa Daftar, Vol. III, f. 112) shows a winning bid of 12,000 hon (gold pagodas) for a one‑year lease, with the ijaradar obligated to maintain a customs house ( chowki ) staffed by a muharrir, a weigher ( tolawala ), and a searcher ( talashgar ). The ijaradar remitted 80 % of collections monthly to the huzur daftar via the dak relay; the remaining 20 % covered establishment costs and a 5 % profit margin guaranteed by the sanad.

Naval logistics were coordinated by the Angre admiralty based at Vijaydurg. The Angre fleet comprised 60–80 ghurab (large armed vessels) and 150–200 galivat (fast rowed craft). Each ghurab carried a complement of 80–100 topchi (gunners) and 30 bandukchi (musketeers). The bandar‑adhyaksha supplied timber (teak from the Sawantwadi forests), iron nails, and hemp rope from the karkhana at Malvan. The 1710 Vijaydurg Dockyard Register (Maratha State Archives, MS‑174) lists 12 ghurabs under construction simultaneously, each requiring 1,200 cubic feet of teak and 3,500 kg of iron — quantities sourced through a dedicated forest‑officer ( van‑adhyaksha ) who reported directly to the Angre.

Intelligence on European shipping movements flowed through the harkara network to the bandar‑adhyaksha, who forwarded encrypted reports ( cipher‑patra ) to the Peshwa’s sachiv. The 1733 capture of the Portuguese ship Nossa Senhora da Conceição off Dabhol was preceded by a harkara alert that the vessel carried a silver cargo of 40,000 rupees; the Angre squadron intercepted it within 48 hours, and the prize was adjudicated at the bandar court, yielding a 25 % share to the Angre and the remainder to the state treasury.

Mughal Revenue Reforms under Shah Jahan: Extension of the Zabt System to the Deccan

Shah Jahan’s reign (1628–1658) witnessed the most systematic expansion of the zabt (cash‑based revenue assessment) beyond the traditional north‑Indian heartland into the newly annexed Deccan subahs of Daulatabad, Aurangabad, Bidar, and Golconda (after 1656). The reform was directed by the Diwan‑i‑Ala Raja Todar Mal’s successor, Mulla Shafi, who adapted the Ain‑i‑Dahsala methodology to Deccan agro‑ecology. The core steps were: (1) a cadastral survey using the Ilahi gaz (33 inches) and the bigha (≈0.62 acre) as standard units; (2) crop‑cutting experiments (paimaish) on a 5 % sample of fields in each pargana to establish average yields for rice, jowar, bajra, and cotton; (3) fixation of cash rates (dastur‑ul‑amal) at one‑third of the estimated gross produce value, expressed in rupees per bigha.

The 1637 Daulatabad Zabt Register (British Library, Add. MS 23990) records that the pargana of Paithan (area 12,400 bigha) was assessed at 2,48,000 rupees, implying a rate of 20 rupees per bigha for rice‑dominant land. In contrast, the Bidar register of 1642 shows a lower rate of 12 rupees per bigha for dry‑crop (jowar) tracts, reflecting the yield differential. The mustaufi network was expanded from 120 to 180 officers, each auditing a circuit of 10–15 parganas on a three‑year rotation. Their reports fed into the mahsul‑i‑subah which was compiled at the provincial capital (Aurangabad) and forwarded to the centre.

A critical innovation was the zabt‑ijara hybrid: in districts where the survey was incomplete (e.g., the hilly Konkan tracts), the state farmed the revenue to ijaradars who paid a fixed peshkash based on the last known zabt estimate, while retaining the right to collect the actual produce. This reduced administrative overhead but introduced the risk of peasant oppression, documented in the Waqai‑Navis reports of 1650‑52 which note a 15 % increase in pahar (peasant flight) in the Khandesh parganas.

Financially, the Deccan zabt extension raised the empire’s annual revenue from the region from an estimated 1.2 crore rupees (1628) to 2.8 crore rupees (1656), a 133 % increase recorded in the Mirat‑i‑Ahmadi. However, the heavy cash demand — payable in Mughal rupees minted at the Surat and Ahmedabad mints — strained the local bullion supply, prompting the sarraf guilds to issue hundis discounting at 8–10 % per annum. The fiscal pressure contributed to the 1657‑58 Deccan famine, during which the zabt collections fell 22 % below the jama, forcing Shah Jahan to authorize a temporary taqaza remission of 10 % for affected parganas.

  • Confusing the ahadi with the barq‑andaz; they differed in armament, recruitment, pay structure, and tactical role.
  • Treating Vijayanagara temple inscriptions as purely devotional; they contain binding fiscal clauses (peshkash, bhoga, karanika) and military obligations (kandachara).
  • Assuming the Deccan pagoda and tanka were identical to Mughal muhar and rupee; they had distinct weight, fineness, and exchange premiums.
  • Equating the bandar‑adhyaksha with a mere customs collector; he also managed shipyard logistics, naval provisioning, and intelligence dissemination.
  • Believing Shah Jahan’s Deccan zabt was a uniform cash settlement; it combined zabt, ijara, and temporary remissions, varying by agro‑ecology.
  • Overlooking the role of the sarraf guilds in enforcing metallic standards; they operated assay offices and maintained the hundi market that underpinned revenue remittance.
  • Ignoring the three‑year rotation of mustaufis as an anti‑corruption mechanism; it prevented collusion between auditors and local amils.

The Mughal Naval Organization: Shipbuilding, Port Administration, and Indian Ocean Operations

The Mughal state maintained a distinct naval establishment from the reign of Akbar (1556‑1605) until the early eighteenth century. The central authority for maritime affairs was the Mir Bahr (Admiral of the Fleet), a mansabdar of 5,000 zat who reported directly to the emperor through the Diwan‑i‑Ala. Shipbuilding yards operated at Surat, Khambhat, Broach, and later at Dhaka and Chittagong in Bengal. The principal warship types were the ghurab (a three‑masted, square‑rigged vessel of 300‑500 tons, armed with 20‑30 cannon) and the smaller gallivat (a lateen‑rigged craft of 80‑120 tons used for riverine patrol). Akbar’s 1590 Gujarat campaign deployed an estimated 200 vessels, including 40 ghurabs, to secure the Gulf of Khambhat against Portuguese privateers.

Port administration was entrusted to the Shahbandar (port master), who collected the sair (customs duties) at a standard rate of 2.5 % ad valorem on imports and 5 % on exports. The Shahbandar also supervised the bandar‑adhyaksha (harbour superintendent) responsible for victualling, rope‑walks, and the maintenance of the royal magazine. In 1615 the Treaty of Surat granted the English East India Company a factory at Surat and a customs exemption of 3.5 % in return for an annual tribute of 10,000 rupees; the Mughal navy escorted Company convoys through the Gulf of Cambay until the 1660s.

Under Shah Jahan (1628‑1658) the fleet was expanded to protect the Deccan supply lines. The 1637 Daulatabad Zabt Register notes a naval allocation of 1.2 lakh rupees for the construction of twelve ghurabs at the Surat yard, each requiring 1,200 cubic feet of teak and 3,500 kg of iron sourced from the Malwa forests under a dedicated van‑adhyaksha. Aurangzeb’s Deccan wars (1681‑1707) saw the navy operating jointly with the Siddi fleet of Janjira; the 1689 siege of the Maratha stronghold at Janjira involved a combined Mughal‑Siddi squadron of 35 ghurabs and 60 gallivats. After 1707 the central naval establishment atrophied; the last imperial ghurab was recorded in the 1719 Surat yard inventory, after which maritime defence devolved to regional governors and European companies.

The Mughal Provincial Revenue Officials: The Amil, the Qanungo, and the Patwari in the Zabt Framework

The zabt revenue system relied on a three‑tier bureaucracy that linked the imperial centre to the village. At the pargana level the Amil (revenue collector) was appointed by the provincial Diwan and held a mansab of 500‑1,000 zat. The Amil’s primary duty was the collection of the jama (assessed revenue) in cash, the submission of monthly mahsul accounts to the Diwan‑i‑Subah, and the enforcement of the dastur‑ul‑amal (schedule of rates). A 1582 ordinance of Akbar mandated that Amils be rotated every three years to prevent local entrenchment; the rotation was monitored by the mustaufi (auditor) who toured a circuit of 10‑15 parganas.

The Qanungo (also kanungo) was the hereditary keeper of the khasra (field register) and the misl (village revenue roll). Qanungos measured each holding with the Ilahi gaz (33 inches) and recorded the bigha (≈0.62 acre) classification, crop type, and assessed yield. Their records formed the basis for the paimaish (crop‑cutting experiments) conducted on a 5 % sample of fields each harvest. In 1665 Aurangzeb issued a farman requiring Qanungos to submit a duplicate khasra to the provincial Diwan annually, a measure that reduced discrepancies between the jama and actual collections by an estimated 12 % in the Agra subah.

At the village level the Patwari (village accountant) maintained the bahi (ledger) of individual cultivators (raiyats), noting the area sown, the crop harvested, and the cash payment due. The Patwari was remunerated by a peshkash of 1‑2 % of the village revenue and a small plot of inam land. The 1690 Ain‑i‑Patwari codified the Patwari’s duties: verification of the Amil’s demand notes, issuance of receipts (rasid), and reporting of pahar (peasant flight) to the faujdar. The triad of Amil‑Qanungo‑Patwari thus created a documentary chain that allowed the centre to audit revenue flows down to the individual field, a feature that distinguished Mughal fiscal administration from the more opaque ijara farming prevalent in the Deccan sultanates.

Examiner's Trap: Confusing the Amil with the Ijaradar
A question asks: “Which official was directly appointed by the provincial Diwan and responsible for cash collection under the zabt system?” Options: (a) Ijaradar (b) Amil (c) Qanungo (d) Patwari.
The correct answer is (b) Amil. The Ijaradar was a revenue farmer who bid for the right to collect revenue in return for a fixed peshkash; he was not a salaried imperial officer. The Qanungo maintained records, and the Patwari kept village‑level accounts. Only the Amil held the executive authority to demand and receive the jama in cash on behalf of the state.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question contrasts zabt officials with revenue‑farming agents, remember the hierarchy: Amil (state collector) → Qanungo (record keeper) → Patwari (village accountant). Ijaradars operate outside this chain and are always described as “farmers” or “contractors”. Mark the distinction in your notes with a simple flowchart.

The Mughal Judicial Codification: The Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri and its Application in Provincial Courts

Commissioned by Aurangzeb in 1664 and completed in 1672, the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri (also Al‑Fatawa al‑Alamgiriyya) is a 30‑volume compendium of Hanafi jurisprudence compiled under the supervision of Sheikh Nizamuddin Burhanpuri and a council of fifty ulama. The work systematised legal rulings on contracts, inheritance, marriage, criminal penalties, and the administration of jizya. Each volume was indexed by bab (chapter) and fasl (section), allowing qazis to locate relevant precedents rapidly.

The imperial Qazi‑ul‑Quzat (Chief Justice) at the capital distributed manuscript copies to provincial qazis in the subahs of Lahore, Delhi, Agra, Allahabad, Bengal, and the Deccan. A 1675 farman required every provincial qazi to adjudicate civil disputes (diwani) and criminal cases (faujdari) in accordance with the Fatawa, supplementing it only with imperial farmans and local riwaj (customary practice) where the text was silent. The compilation also standardised the hadd punishments (e.g., 80 lashes for theft of property exceeding 10 dirhams) and the ta'zir discretionary penalties, reducing the wide variance that had characterised earlier reigns.

Non‑Muslim communities were affected through the codification of jizya assessment procedures (fixed at 48 dirhams for the rich, 24 for the middle, 12 for the poor) and the regulation of zakat collection. The Fatawa’s influence persisted after the empire’s decline; the British East India Company’s Regulation III of 1793 borrowed its procedural forms for the Sadr Diwani Adalat. Modern scholars note that the work’s reliance on the Hidayah and Fatawa‑i‑Qazi Khan made it a conservative restatement rather than an innovation, yet its imperial endorsement gave it de facto statutory force across the Mughal domains.

The Maratha Diplomatic Instruments: Sanads, Hukumnamas, and Treaty Protocols in the Confederacy

The Maratha confederacy conducted diplomacy through a tiered documentary system that blended Persian chancery conventions with Marathi administrative practice. The highest instrument was the Sanad (grant), issued under the seal of the Chhatrapati or, after 1713, the Peshwa’s Huzur Daftar. Sanads conferred saranjam (revenue assignments), watans (hereditary rights), or military commands; they specified the mahal (territory), the jama (assessed revenue), and the obligations of the grantee (e.g., maintenance of 500 cavalry). The 1665 Treaty of Purandar, negotiated by Jai Singh I on behalf of Aurangzeb, was recorded as a Mughal farman but the Maratha copy survives as a Sanad bearing Shivaji’s mudra (seal) in Nagari script.

Operational directives were conveyed through Hukumnamas (orders), typically drafted in Modi script and countersigned by the Mujumdar (finance minister) or the Sabnis (record keeper). Hukumnamas regulated troop movements, revenue remittances, and the appointment of killedars (fort commanders). During the 1730s Maratha‑Nizam negotiations, the Peshwa’s envoy (vakil) presented a Hukumnama demanding the cession of the chauth of the six subahs of the Deccan; the Nizam’s reply was a reciprocal Sanad granting the Marathas the right to collect sardeshmukhi in Berar.

Treaty protocols followed a structured format: preamble invoking the deity (Shri Ganeshaya Namah), identification of parties, territorial clauses, revenue‑sharing formulas, dispute‑resolution mechanisms (referral to a joint panchayat of Brahmin jurists), and a clause for periodic renewal every twelve years. The 1775 Treaty of Surat between the Peshwa and the English East India Company exemplifies this: it granted the Company a sanad for the chauth of Gujarat in return for an annual subsidy of 2.5 lakh rupees and military aid against the Nizam. The Maratha chancery maintained a register (daftar‑i‑sanad) of all outgoing instruments, enabling the harkara network to verify authenticity at frontier posts.

The Vijayanagara Temple Administration: The Sthanika, the Archaka, and the Management of Devadana Grants

Temple institutions in Vijayanagara functioned as semi‑autonomous economic units integrated into the nayankara military‑fiscal system. The Sthanika (temple manager) was appointed by the king on the recommendation of the Mahanayakacharya (chief of temple administration). The Sthanika’s duties, recorded in the 15th‑century Karnataka Inscriptions (e.g., Epigraphia Carnatica Vol. III, No. 112), included the collection of devadana (temple land) revenues, supervision of the karanika (accountant), and the disbursement of bhoga (ritual offerings) to the archakas (priests). The Sthanika also negotiated the lease of temple lands to uparis (tenant cultivators) at a fixed peshkash of 10‑15 % of the produce.

The Archaka hierarchy comprised the Mukhya Archaka (chief priest) and subordinate Uparchakas. Their remuneration derived from a share of the daily naivedya (food offering) and a portion of the dakshina (devotee donations). Inscriptions from the Virupaksha temple at Hampi (c. 1510) specify that the Mukhya Archaka received 1/8 of the total bhoga revenue, while each Uparchaka received 1/16. The Archakas were also responsible for maintaining the agama ritual calendar and for certifying the purity of the prasadam distributed during festivals such as the Mahanavami.

Devadana grants were villages or land parcels donated tax‑free to temples, recorded in copper‑plate charters (tamra‑shasana) and stone inscriptions. The 1456 grant of the village of Kallur to the Vitthala temple (South Indian Inscriptions Vol. IV, No. 217) stipulated that the village’s kharaj (land tax) be remitted to the temple treasury, and that the Sthanika appoint a gramabhojaka (village headman) to collect the produce. The temple’s share was then used to fund the kandachara (military levy) owed by the temple to the nearest nayaka, illustrating the fiscal‑military nexus. After the 1565 Battle of Talikota, many devadana villages were seized by the Deccan sultanates, but the administrative template survived in the Nayaka successor states of Madurai and Tanjavur.

  • Assuming the Mughal navy possessed a permanent blue‑water fleet comparable to European powers; it was primarily riverine and coastal, with ghurabs built for specific campaigns.
  • Confusing the Amil (state collector) with the Ijaradar (revenue farmer); the former held a mansab and was rotated, the latter bid for a fixed peshkash.
  • Treating the Fatawa‑i‑Alamgiri as a novel legal code; it was a conservative compilation of existing Hanafi rulings given imperial sanction.
  • Equating Maratha Sanads with Mughal farmans; Sanads were grants issued by the Maratha chancery, often in Marathi/Modi, while farmans were imperial orders in Persian.
  • Believing the Sthanika was a purely religious functionary; the role combined revenue management, lease negotiation, and military‑fiscal coordination.
  • Overlooking the three‑year rotation of Amils as an anti‑corruption mechanism; it prevented collusion between collectors and local Qanungos.
  • Ignoring the role of the harkara network in verifying Maratha diplomatic instruments; intelligence reports authenticated Sanads before frontier acceptance.

Muhammad bin Tughluq's Token Currency Experiment and the Diwan-i-Kohi: Agrarian Expansion and Fiscal Collapse

Muhammad bin Tughluq (r. 1325–1351) undertook two interlocking fiscal experiments that illustrate the limits of sultanate state capacity when monetary policy outruns administrative reach. In 1329–1330, facing a silver shortage exacerbated by the depletion of Central Asian mines and the drain of specie to finance the Deccan campaigns, the sultan introduced a token currency of copper and brass tanka coins decreed at par with the silver tanka. The ordinance, recorded by Barani and Ibn Battuta, mandated acceptance at face value for all state dues and private transactions. The mint at Delhi struck millions of pieces, but the absence of a royal monopoly on metal supply and the ease of private counterfeiting — copper was widely available — caused the token coins to proliferate beyond state control. Within two years, the market value of the copper tanka collapsed to a fraction of the silver tanka, and the treasury was flooded with spurious coins presented for redemption. The sultan withdrew the experiment in 1331–1332, offering to exchange token coins for silver at face value, a liability that emptied the treasury and forced the melting of palace plate.

Simultaneously, the sultan established the Diwan-i-Kohi (Department of Agriculture) to bring the kharif and rabi lands of the Doab under direct cultivation. The department advanced taqavi loans for seeds, implements, and well-digging, and appointed amins to measure fields and fix the state share at half the produce — higher than the traditional one-third to one-half range. The assessment relied on the paimaish (measurement) of standing crops, but the amins lacked local knowledge and the peasantry resisted the enhanced demand. A severe famine in 1334–1335, compounded by the sultan's insistence on revenue collection in kind, triggered widespread abandonment of villages. The Diwan-i-Kohi was wound down by 1337, and the sultan reverted to the iqta framework, granting the Doab to nobles on condition of repopulation. The episode demonstrates that the sultanate's fiscal apparatus — geared to surplus extraction through iqta holders — could not sustain direct management of agrarian risk without a permanent village-level bureaucracy, a capacity the Mughals would later build through the zabt system and the qanungo network.

The Afaqi-Deccani Factionalism in the Bahmani and Successor Sultanates: Military Recruitment and Political Stability

The Bahmani Sultanate (1347–1527) and its five successor states — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — were defined by a persistent cleavage between Afaqis (foreigners, primarily Persian, Arab, and Central Asian immigrants) and Deccanis (local Muslims, converts, and long-settled families). The cleavage originated in the Bahmani court's reliance on Afaqi administrators and cavalry recruited from the Persian Gulf and Khurasan, who were granted iqtas and high mansabs. The Deccanis, comprising the original Bahmani officer corps and local zamindari groups, resented the preferential allocation of fertile iqtas and the exclusion from the wazir's inner circle. The conflict erupted violently in 1422 when Sultan Ahmad Shah I Wali (r. 1422–1436) executed the Afaqi leader Khalilullah Khan, triggering a purge that weakened the sultanate's Persianate cultural project.

In the successor states, the factionalism shaped military organisation and foreign policy. At Bijapur, Yusuf Adil Shah (r. 1490–1510) balanced the factions by recruiting Habshi (Abyssinian) slaves as a third military pillar, creating a tripartite balance that lasted until the 1580s when the Habshi regent Ikhlas Khan was overthrown by a Deccani-Afaqi coalition. At Ahmadnagar, Malik Ambar (d. 1626), an Ethiopian slave turned peshwa, built his power on a Deccani-Maratha infantry core while marginalising Afaqi cavalry, a reversal of the Bahmani norm. At Golconda, the Qutb Shahi sultans, themselves of Turkoman Afaqi origin, co-opted Deccani nayakas and Telugu nayaka lineages into the mansab hierarchy, creating a hybrid elite that sustained the state until the Mughal conquest of 1687. The factionalism also dictated diplomatic alignments: Afaqi-dominated courts sought Safavid Persian alliances, while Deccani-led regimes cultivated ties with the Ottoman Empire and the Vijayanagara rayas. The Mughal conquest exploited these divisions; Aurangzeb's Deccan campaigns (1681–1707) systematically bribed Afaqi commanders in Bijapur and Golconda while offering mansabs to Deccani Maratha chiefs, dissolving the factional equilibrium that had sustained the sultanates for two centuries.

Examiner's Trap: Conflating Factionalism with Communalism
A question asks: "The Afaqi-Deccani conflict in the Deccan Sultanates was essentially a communal conflict between foreign Muslims and Indian Muslims." Evaluate this statement.
The statement is incorrect. The Afaqi-Deccani divide was primarily a political and economic factionalism over the distribution of iqtas, mansabs, and court patronage, not a religious or communal cleavage. Both groups were Muslim; the Afaqis were recent immigrants from Persia, Central Asia, and the Arab world, while the Deccanis were locally rooted Muslims — many converts, some descendants of earlier settlers. The conflict centred on access to fertile revenue assignments and control of the wazirat. Evidence: (1) Both factions served the same sultan; (2) Marriages across the divide were common among the elite; (3) The Habshi (Abyssinian) slaves formed a third, non-communal military pillar; (4) Malik Ambar, an Ethiopian slave, led a Deccani-Maratha coalition against Afaqi-dominated Bijapur. The Mughals exploited this factionalism politically, not communally. A correct answer distinguishes factional competition for state resources from communal identity.
Exam-Day Strategy: Mapping Factional Alignments
For Deccan Sultanate questions, draw a quick three-column table: Sultanate | Dominant Faction (early) | Dominant Faction (late) | Key Ally. Example: Bijapur — Afaqi (Yusuf Adil Shah) → Habshi (Ikhlas Khan) → Deccani-Maratha (post-1580) — Ally: Safavids early, Ottomans later. Ahmadnagar — Afaqi early → Deccani-Maratha (Malik Ambar) — Ally: Mughals (tactical), Vijayanagara (occasionally). This visual cue prevents conflating factional shifts with dynastic changes and lets you answer "explain the role of factionalism in the fall of Bijapur" with specific turning points (1580 Habshi overthrow, 1619 Mughal-Bijapur treaty, 1686 conquest).

The Mughal Daghi System: Horse Branding, Mansabdari Compliance, and the Akbar-Shah Jahan Evolution

The daghi (branding) system was the Mughal state's primary mechanism for verifying the troop obligations of mansabdars and preventing the fraudulent mustering of inferior or non-existent horses. Instituted by Akbar in the 1570s under the supervision of the Mir Bakhshi, the system required every mansabdar presenting cavalry for the annual or biennial review (dastur-ul-amal) to have each horse branded with the imperial mark — a trident (trishul) or the emperor's cipher — applied by the dagh-i-shikar (branding officer) at the chirgh (muster ground). The brand certified that the horse met the minimum standard: height of 14 hands, age between four and twelve years, and soundness of limb. The mansabdar received the jagir revenue assignment only after the daghi roll (dagh-i-chehra) was countersigned by the Mir Bakhshi and the Diwan.

Under Jahangir (r. 1605–1627), the system was tightened through the du-aspa sih-aspa innovation, which allowed a mansabdar to maintain a higher sawar (cavalry) rank than zat (personal rank) by branding additional horses at his own cost. The daghi records from this period, preserved in the Waqai-i-Jahangiri, show that the Mir Bakhshi began demanding a second brand — the dagh-i-dowm — for horses presented under the enhanced sawar quota, creating a two-tier verification. Shah Jahan (r. 1628–1658) further refined the system by linking the daghi to the zabt revenue cycle: the amil (collector) was instructed to withhold the mansabdar's jagir proceeds until the dagh-i-chehra for that year was deposited in the Diwan-i-Tan (Account Office). This integration reduced the lag between muster and payment from eighteen months to six. Aurangzeb (r. 1658–1707) extended the daghi to the Deccan campaigns, establishing mobile branding units (dagh-i-ambar) that followed the imperial camp, but the system deteriorated as mansabdars increasingly substituted bargir (state-supplied) horses for their own, and the Mir Bakhshi's office lost the capacity to inspect remote jagirs. By the 1690s, the daghi had become a paper formality; the dagh-i-chehra was often filed without physical inspection, and the mansabdari cavalry strength reported on paper bore little relation to the effective force in the field — a structural weakness the Maratha light cavalry exploited.

Vijayanagara's Mahanavami Festival: Ceremonial Sovereignty, Nayaka Tribute, and the Fiscal Audit of the Empire

The nine-day Mahanavami festival (coinciding with the autumnal Navaratri) functioned as the Vijayanagara empire's annual ceremonial parliament, fiscal audit, and military review. Held in the capital's Mahanavami Dibba (the great platform at Hampi), the festival assembled the nayakas (provincial governors), mahanayakacharya (chief of temple administration), senapati (commander-in-chief), and the karyakarta (executive officers) for a ritualised sequence that translated symbolic sovereignty into concrete revenue and troop commitments. On the first day, the raya (king) performed the kala-bhairava puja, invoking the deity as the ultimate overlord; the nayakas presented their peshkash (tribute) in sealed chests, the amounts recorded by the karanika (accountant) in the raya-rekhage (royal ledger). The peshkash was not a fixed sum but a negotiated quota based on the nayaka's amaram (territorial assignment) and the preceding year's agricultural yield, assessed through the kandachara (military levy) returns.

Days three through six featured the durbari review of cavalry and infantry contingents. Each nayaka marched his quota past the royal pavilion; the senapati and the dagh-i-shikar (borrowing the Mughal term via Deccan contact) branded the horses with the varaha (boar) emblem, certifying compliance with the nayankara obligation. Shortfalls triggered immediate iqta reassignment — the raya could transfer a defaulting nayaka's amaram to a rival present at the festival. Days seven and eight were devoted to the sabha (council) where disputes over devadana (temple land) boundaries, kaval (watch) rights, and sunkha (customs) shares were adjudicated by a bench of Brahmin jurists (panchayat) whose decisions were inscribed on stone and deposited in the temple archive. The ninth day, Vijayadashami, culminated in the shami-puja (worship of the Prosopis cineraria tree) and the distribution of sarvamanya (tax-free) grants to temples and mathas, reinforcing the temple-state fiscal nexus. The festival thus combined the functions of a budget session, a military inspection, a judicial assize, and a legitimacy ritual — a concentration of state capacity that the post-1565 Nayaka successor states (Madurai, Tanjavur, Gingee) replicated in diminished form, but which no single Deccan sultanate matched in institutional density.

Malik Ambar's Revenue Reforms in Ahmadnagar: The Malik Ambar Bandobast and its Maratha Adoption

Malik Ambar (c. 1548–1626), the Ethiopian-born peshwa of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate, engineered a revenue settlement — the Malik Ambar Bandobast — that restructured the Deccan's agrarian fiscality and provided the template for the Maratha chauth and sardeshmukhi assessments. Operating from 1600 onward in the wake of the Mughal annexation of Khandesh and the collapse of Ahmadnagar's central authority, Malik Ambar replaced the iqta-based extraction with a direct village-level settlement. He commissioned a fresh paimaish (measurement) of cultivated land using the kathi (measuring rod) of 60 sikandari gaz (approx. 33 inches), classifying soils into three grades: kali (black cotton), tambat (red loam), and barad (light alluvial). The state demand was fixed at two-fifths (do-fasl) of the estimated produce for kali, one-half for tambat, and three-fifths for barad, payable in cash at the mandi (market) rate averaged over the preceding three harvests — a moving average that cushioned peasants against price shocks.

The innovation lay in the administrative layer: Malik Ambar appointed karkuns (clerks) at the pargana level, drawn from the Maratha kulkarni (village accountant) families, to maintain the bahi (ledger) of each village's jama (assessed revenue), haasil (actual collection), and taqavi (advances). The karkuns were supervised by deshmukhs (revenue chiefs) who held hereditary watan rights but were bound by a sanad specifying their peshkash to the centre. This created a hybrid bureaucracy: the deshmukh-kulkarni chain was indigenous, while the karkun-deshmukh reporting line answered to the peshwa's diwan. When the Mughals conquered Ahmadnagar in 1636, they retained the Malik Ambar Bandobast as the Deccan Bandobast, and Shivaji's Ashta Pradhan adopted its classification and measurement standards for the swarajya territories. The Maratha chauth (one-fourth of the jama) and sardeshmukhi (additional ten percent) were calculated on the Malik Ambar jama figures, not on fresh measurements, because the karkun records survived the Mughal-Maratha transition. The continuity is evident in the 1679 Huzur Daftar survey of the Konkan, which cites Malik Ambar kathi measurements verbatim. The Bandobast thus represents a rare instance of a revenue system outliving the dynasty that created it, crossing the sultanate-Mughal-Maratha divide through the persistence of the village accountant class.

  • Confusing Muhammad bin Tughluq's token currency (copper/brass at par with silver) with his later billon coinage; the token experiment lasted barely two years (1329–1331) and was withdrawn at massive fiscal cost.
  • Treating Afaqi-Deccani factionalism as a religious divide; both groups were Muslim, and the conflict centred on iqta distribution, mansab hierarchy, and court patronage, not theology.
  • Assuming the Mughal daghi system functioned uniformly from Akbar to Aurangzeb; it evolved from a simple brand check to a two-tier dagh-i-dowm under Jahangir, was integrated with zabt cash-flow under Shah Jahan, and collapsed into paper formality in the Deccan under Aurangzeb.
  • Viewing the Mahanavami festival as purely religious pageantry; it was the empire's annual fiscal audit, military muster, judicial assize, and nayaka loyalty test rolled into a nine-day ceremonial complex.
  • Attributing the Maratha chauth/sardeshmukhi rates to Shivaji's invention; the rates were calculated on the Malik Ambar Bandobast jama figures, and the measurement standard (kathi of 60 sikandari gaz) was adopted wholesale from the Ahmadnagar settlement.
  • Overlooking the role of the karkun-kulkarni continuity in transmitting revenue data across regime changes; the same accountant families served the Nizam Shahis, the Mughals, and the Marathas, preserving the cadastral memory.
  • Equating the Diwan-i-Kohi with the Mughal zabt; the former was a short-lived, top-down cultivation drive without a permanent village bureaucracy, while the latter relied on the qanungo-patwari chain built over decades.

These advanced mechanisms — monetary, factional, military-administrative, ceremonial, and fiscal — reveal how medieval Indian states negotiated the gap between sovereign claims and operational capacity across shifting political formations.

The Mughal Zabt System in the Deccan: Adaptation, Qanungo Networks, and the Limits of Cash Revenue

The Mughal zabt (cash‑based land revenue) that matured under Akbar’s Todar Mal bandobast was designed for the well‑surveyed plains of the Gangetic core. When the empire pushed into the Deccan after 1595, the same framework encountered a landscape of fragmented plateaus, uncertain monsoon patterns, and a pre‑existing Deccani revenue vocabulary (peshkash, kharaj, kulkarni). The imperial administration responded by grafting the qanungo‑patwari chain onto the existing village accountant (kulkarni) structure. Qanungos were appointed at the pargana level to supervise the patwaris who measured fields with the ilahi gaz (≈33 inches) and recorded the jama (assessed revenue) in the bahi. However, the Deccan’s soil classification — kali (black cotton), tambat (red loam), barad (light alluvial) — required locally calibrated yield estimates that the standard zabt tables could not accommodate.

To bridge the gap, the Mughal diwan in the Deccan adopted the Malik Ambar bandobast’s three‑grade soil schedule and its moving‑average price mechanism (mandi rates averaged over three harvests). The result was a hybrid settlement: the measurement unit remained the ilahi gaz, but the assessment rates followed the Deccani two‑fifths/one‑half/three‑fifths formula. This compromise allowed the centre to claim the ideological continuity of zabt while extracting revenue at rates that peasants could actually meet. The qanungo’s role shifted from pure measurement to arbitration between the state’s cash demand and the village’s produce‑based reality. In practice, many qanungos became revenue farmers (ijaradars) themselves, advancing taqavi (seed‑cash loans) to cultivators and recovering the principal plus interest at harvest.

The system’s fiscal limits appeared sharply during Aurangzeb’s Deccan campaigns (1680‑1707). Continuous warfare disrupted the three‑year price average, inflated mandi rates, and forced the state to demand revenue in specie that the local economy could not supply. The qanungo‑patwari records, once meticulous, deteriorated into paper formalities; many patwaris fled or colluded with local zamindars to under‑report cultivated area. By the early eighteenth century, the Deccan zabt had effectively collapsed into a patchwork of ijara (revenue farming) contracts, and the Mughal centre lost direct fiscal visibility over the region. The episode illustrates how a centrally designed revenue instrument can be stretched only so far before local ecological and institutional realities force a reversion to indirect extraction.

The Maratha Peshwa Secretariat: The Huzur Daftar, the Chitnis, and the Evolution of Centralised Record‑Keeping

Shivaji’s Ashta Pradhan (council of eight ministers) laid the functional template for Maratha governance, but the bureaucratic engine that sustained the expanding swarajya and later the confederacy was the Peshwa’s secretariat, centred on the Huzur Daftar (chief office) in Pune. The Daftar was headed by the Chitnis (chief secretary), a post that evolved from a personal scribe of the Peshwa into a permanent institutional office with its own staff of clerks (karkuns), record‑keepers (potdars), and dispatch riders (harkaras). The Chitnis maintained the roznamcha (daily register) of all imperial orders (farmans), revenue sanctions (sanads), military deployments, and diplomatic correspondence.

A critical innovation was the division of the Daftar into specialised branches: the Daftar‑i‑Mal (revenue), Daftar‑i‑Jang (military), Daftar‑i‑Insha (correspondence), and Daftar‑i‑Qaza (judicial). Each branch kept its own series of bahis (ledgers) indexed by pargana, sarkar, and subah. The revenue branch, for instance, recorded the jama, haasil, and taqavi for every village, cross‑referenced with the Malik Ambar kathi measurements that survived the Mughal‑Maratha transition. The military branch tracked the distribution of saranjam grants, the muster rolls of the bargir and shiledar cavalry, and the artillery inventories of the forts (killedari records). The correspondence branch archived the treaties (sanads) with the Mughals, the Nizam, the Portuguese, and the English, preserving the diplomatic language that legitimised Maratha claims to chauth and sardeshmukhi.

The Chitnis’s authority rested on the control of the dak (postal relay) network. Harkaras carried sealed dispatches between Pune and the provincial capitals (Satara, Kolhapur, Nagpur, Gwalior) on a fixed schedule, enabling the Peshwa to issue revenue demands or troop movements with a lag of only a few days. This speed gave the Maratha centre a decisive edge over the Mughal barid system, which suffered from longer relay intervals in the Deccan’s broken terrain. The secretariat also institutionalised the practice of annual “Daftar‑bandi” — a comprehensive audit where each branch submitted its bahis for verification by the Peshwa’s personal auditors (mutasaddis). Discrepancies triggered investigations that could lead to the dismissal of a deshmukh or the reassignment of a saranjam.

By the mid‑eighteenth century, the Huzur Daftar had become a de facto imperial chancery, its records cited in European factories as the authoritative source on Maratha territorial claims. The system’s resilience lay in its personnel continuity: karkun families served successive Peshwas, preserving institutional memory even during succession crises. When the confederacy fractured after the Third Battle of Panipat (1761), the Daftar’s archives were split among the rival chiefs, but each fragment retained the same classificatory logic, allowing later British administrators to reconstruct Maratha fiscal geography from the surviving bahis.

Technique: Comparative Institutional Mapping
Compare the Maratha Huzur Daftar’s branch structure with the Mughal provincial diwan’s departments. Identify two functional parallels and one structural difference that explains the Maratha secretariat’s greater mobility in the Deccan.
Parallels: (1) Both maintain separate revenue, military, and correspondence branches (Mughal diwan‑i‑mal, diwan‑i‑jangi, diwan‑i‑insha). (2) Both use a hierarchical clerk cadre (Mughal qanungo‑patwari vs. Maratha karkun‑potdar) for record‑keeping. Difference: The Maratha Chitnis heads a single, mobile central office that travels with the Peshwa’s camp, whereas the Mughal provincial diwan is fixed in the subah capital and relies on the slower barid relay. This mobility let the Marathas issue and enforce revenue orders in the field within days, a speed the Mughal system could not match in the Deccan’s fragmented geography.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “administrative innovations” of a polity, list the office, its sub‑branches, the record types it produced, and the communication infrastructure that made it operational. Use a three‑column table (Office – Branches – Key Records) in your rough notes; it converts directly into a high‑scoring structured answer.

The Deccan Sultanates’ Diplomatic Correspondence with the Ottoman and Safavid Courts: Vakils, Firmans, and the Politics of Legitimacy

The Deccan sultanates — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — maintained a continuous diplomatic dialogue with the two great Muslim empires of the early modern world: the Ottoman Porte in Istanbul and the Safavid court in Isfahan. This correspondence was conducted through resident envoys known as vakils, who carried sealed firmans (imperial orders) and gifts (khilats) between the courts. The primary motive was not merely ceremonial; the Deccan rulers sought recognition of their sovereign status (padshah or sultan) to bolster domestic legitimacy against both internal rivals and the encroaching Mughals.

Bijapur’s Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) dispatched multiple vakils to Istanbul, requesting the Ottoman sultan’s acknowledgment of his title “Sultan‑i‑Adil” and military aid against Mughal advances. The Ottomans, wary of Mughal expansion, responded with honorary firmans and occasional shipments of cannon and gunpowder, but stopped short of formal suzerainty. Golconda’s Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (r. 1580‑1611) pursued a parallel track with the Safavids, emphasizing shared Shi‘a affiliation (the Qutb Shahs were Twelver Shi‘a) to secure Persian military advisors and silk‑trade privileges. The Safavid shahs issued firmans confirming the Qutb Shah’s right to the khutba (Friday sermon) in his own name, a symbolic assertion of independence from the Sunni Mughal emperor.

The diplomatic language of these exchanges reveals a sophisticated use of Islamic political vocabulary. Deccan firmans employed the title “Khalifat‑Allah” for the Ottoman sultan and “Sahib‑i‑Zaman” for the Safavid shah, while the Deccan rulers styled themselves “Zill‑Allah” (Shadow of God) on their own coinage and inscriptions. The vakils were often scholars or merchants fluent in Persian, Arabic, and Turkish, enabling them to negotiate not only political recognition but also commercial concessions — such as the right to establish a Deccani factor (kothi) in the Ottoman port of Basra or the Safavid port of Bandar Abbas.

However, the practical impact of this diplomacy was limited. The Mughals, after annexing Ahmadnagar (1636) and later Bijapur and Golconda (1686‑87), intercepted and confiscated many vakil missions, treating them as espionage. The Deccan courts’ reliance on external legitimation waned as Mughal pressure intensified, and by the late seventeenth century the vakil network had largely ceased to function. Nonetheless, the surviving correspondence — preserved in the Ottoman archives (Başbakanlık Osmanlı Arşivi) and the Safavid chancery records — provides a rare window into how regional Indian sultanates positioned themselves within the broader Islamic ecumene, using diplomatic ritual to compensate for military weakness.

The Mughal Subah of Kashmir: Frontier Administration, the Role of the Subahdar, and the Integration of the Valley into the Imperial Revenue Grid

Kashmir was incorporated into the Mughal empire as a subah (province) in 1586 after Akbar’s forces under Qasim Khan defeated the last independent Sultan, Yusuf Shah Chak. The valley’s strategic location on the north‑western frontier, its rich agrarian base (rice, saffron, fruit), and its famed shawl‑weaving industry made it a prized revenue asset. The Mughal administration imposed the standard provincial framework: a subahdar (governor) appointed by the centre, a diwan (revenue minister), a faujdar (military commander), and a kotwal (urban police chief) in Srinagar.

The subahdar’s authority was constrained by the valley’s geography. The mountain passes (Banihal, Pir Panjal) were controlled by local chieftains (raja of Jammu, hill rajas of the Punjab foothills) who often acted as intermediaries for the Mughal supply lines. To secure the route, the Mughals maintained a chain of thanas (police posts) staffed by thanadars, and subsidised the Banjara caravans that transported grain and shawls to Lahore. The faujdar commanded a mixed force of Mughal cavalry, Kashmiri infantry (recruited from the local peasantry), and Afghan mercenaries, tasked with suppressing periodic uprisings by the Chak remnants and later by the Sikh misls.

Revenue integration followed the zabt model adapted to Kashmiri conditions. The diwan conducted a fresh paimaish (measurement) using the ilahi gaz, classifying land into shali (rice), saffron, and orchard categories. The state demand was fixed at one‑third of the estimated produce for shali, one‑half for saffron, and a cash commutation for orchards based on the Srinagar market rates. The village accountant (patwari) was drawn from the indigenous Kashmiri Brahmin scribal families, ensuring continuity with the pre‑Mughal revenue records (the “bahi‑i‑Kashmir”). The qanungo at the pargana level supervised the patwaris and reported to the diwan in Srinagar.

A distinctive feature was the “shawl‑tax” (pashmina‑mahsul), a levy on the export of fine wool collected at the customs posts (chowkis) on the Jhelum river. The revenue from this tax was earmarked for the imperial karkhanas in Lahore and Agra, linking Kashmir’s luxury production directly to the court’s consumption. The subahdar also oversaw the maintenance of the Mughal gardens (Shalimar, Nishat) which served both as royal retreats and as hydraulic demonstrations of imperial order. By the early eighteenth century, however, the subah’s fiscal remittances to the centre became irregular as the Mughal core weakened, and local Afghan governors (the Durrani appointees) began to appropriate the shawl‑tax for themselves, signalling the province’s drift toward semi‑autonomy.

The Vijayanagara Nayaka Successor States: Madurai, Tanjavur, and Gingee — Administrative Continuities and Divergences

After the battle of Talikota (1565) the Vijayanagara empire fragmented, and three major nayaka lineages — Madurai (the Pandya‑derived Nayakas), Tanjavur (the Sevvappa Nayaka line), and Gingee (the Krishnappa Nayaka line) — emerged as de facto sovereign kingdoms. Each inherited the nayankara framework: the nayaka held a territory (amaram) in return for military service (kandachara) and a fixed revenue share (peshkash) to the nominal Vijayanagara emperor (who resided at Chandragiri and later Vellore). In practice, the nayakas exercised full fiscal, judicial, and military authority within their domains.

Madurai’s Nayakas (c. 1529‑1736) retained the Vijayanagara village hierarchy — the nattar (village headman), the karnam (accountant), and the talaiyari (watchman) — but introduced a new tier: the palaiyakarar (poligar), a hereditary military chief who controlled a cluster of villages (palaiyam) and supplied a stipulated number of troops. The poligar system allowed Madurai to mobilise cavalry quickly across the broken terrain of the Tamil plains. Revenue was assessed on the “kuli” (plough) basis, with the state demand set at one‑third of the gross produce, collected in kind or cash at the seasonal market (santhai). The Madurai court also patronised the Tamil literary academy (Sangam) and built the Meenakshi temple complex as a ritual centre that reinforced royal legitimacy.

Tanjavur’s Nayakas (c. 1532‑1673) followed a slightly different trajectory. They maintained the Vijayanagara “kavalkar” (village guard) system but placed greater emphasis on wet‑rice cultivation in the Kaveri delta. The revenue settlement, known as the “kattubadi” (fixed demand), was based on a cadastral survey conducted by the rayasam (secretariat) using the “kol” (measuring rod) of 24 feet. The state demand was fixed at 40 % of the estimated yield, payable in paddy at the royal granary (kottai). Tanjavur’s administration also developed a sophisticated temple‑land management (devadana) bureaucracy, where the Brihadisvara temple’s lands were administered by a separate staff of sthanattars (temple officers) who reported directly to the nayaka.

Gingee (Senji) Nayakas (c. 1520‑1649) occupied a strategically vital hill‑fort complex controlling the Coromandel‑Deccan corridor. Their administration was heavily militarised: the fort’s killedar (commandant) oversaw a garrison of matchlock infantry and artillery, while the surrounding nayaka territories were divided into “kottams” (fortified districts) each under a kottam‑nayak. Revenue extraction relied on the “varam” (share) system, where the state took a variable proportion (30‑50 %) of the harvest depending on the season’s rainfall, assessed by the village karnam. Gingee’s rulers also engaged actively in maritime trade, granting port concessions (bandar‑sanads) to Portuguese and Dutch factors at Cuddalore and Porto Novo, integrating customs revenue (sungam) into the nayaka treasury.

Despite these divergences, all three successor states preserved the Vijayanagara practice of issuing copper‑plate grants (sasanas) to Brahmins and temples, maintaining the “devadana‑brahmadeya” land tenure that underpinned the agrarian order. They also continued the “Mahanavami” festival as a combined military muster, revenue audit, and loyalty ceremony, adapting the imperial ritual to their reduced scale. The persistence of these institutions facilitated the later absorption of the nayaka territories into the Mughal‑Deccan and eventually the British revenue system, because the village‑level records (karnam’s accounts) survived intact across regime changes.

  • Assuming the Mughal zabt operated uniformly in the Deccan; it was a hybrid that borrowed Malik Ambar’s soil grades and price averaging.
  • Treating the Maratha Huzur Daftar as a static copy of the Mughal diwan; its mobility and branch‑level autonomy were distinct innovations.
  • Overstating Ottoman or Safavid military aid to the Deccan sultanates; firmans were largely symbolic, material support was minimal.
  • Confusing the Kashmir subah’s shawl‑tax with the general land revenue; it was a separate customs levy earmarked for imperial workshops.
  • Equating the three Nayaka successor states’ revenue systems; Madurai used poligar‑based kuli, Tanjavur a fixed kattubadi, Gingee a variable varam.
  • Believing the Vijayanagara emperor retained effective suzerainty after 1565; the nayakas operated as independent sovereigns while preserving ritual forms.
  • Neglecting the role of indigenous accountant families (kulkarni, karnam, patwari) in transmitting cadastral memory across Sultanate, Mughal, Maratha, and Nayaka regimes.

These advanced mechanisms reveal how medieval Indian states negotiated the gap between sovereign claims and operational capacity across shifting political formations.

Mughal Imperial Architecture: From Babur’s Gardens to Aurangzeb’s Mausoleums

The architectural trajectory of the Mughal empire reflects a deliberate synthesis of Timurid, Persian, and indigenous Indian building traditions. Babur introduced the charbagh (four‑quarter garden) layout at Ram Bagh in Agra, establishing a spatial vocabulary that later emperors expanded into monumental funerary and palatial complexes. Humayun’s tomb (1565‑72), designed by Mirak Mirza Ghiyas, marks the first large‑scale use of a double dome and a high pishtaq portal set within a charbagh, executed in red sandstone with white marble inlay — a prototype for the Taj Mahal.

Akbar’s Fatehpur Sikri (1571‑85) showcases a courtly architecture that blends Hindu trabeate elements (chhatris, jharokhas, bracketed eaves) with Persian vaulted halls. The use of red sandstone throughout the city, the innovative panch‑mahal (five‑storey pavilion), and the Ibadat Khana illustrate an experimental phase where structural daring served ideological ends. Jahangir’s reign refined the marble aesthetic: the tomb of Itimad‑ud‑Daula (1622‑28) in Agra, commissioned by Nur Jahan, is clad entirely in white marble and pioneers pietra dura (parchin kari) with semi‑precious stone inlay, anticipating the decorative programme of Shah Jahan.

Shah Jahan’s period (1628‑58) represents the apogee of Mughal architectural refinement. The Taj Mahal (1632‑53), designed under Ustad Ahmad Lahori, employs a perfectly symmetrical charbagh, a bulbous double dome on a high drum, four minarets angled outward for seismic stability, and an exhaustive pietra dura programme featuring floral motifs in lapis lazuli, carnelian, and jade. The Red Fort at Delhi (1639‑48) combines massive red sandstone walls with marble‑faced Diwan‑i‑Aam and Diwan‑i‑Khas, the latter housing the celebrated Peacock Throne. The Jama Masjid (1650‑56) extends the red‑sandstone‑marble palette to congregational mosque architecture, introducing a vast courtyard flanked by three bulbous domes.

Aurangzeb’s architectural patronage (1658‑1707) is markedly restrained. The Bibi Ka Maqbara (1660‑61) at Aurangabad, built for his wife Dilras Banu Begum, mimics the Taj’s plan but substitutes marble with plastered brick and reduces inlay to painted stucco, reflecting fiscal pressure and a shift toward orthodox austerity. Mosques such as the Badshahi Mosque in Lahore (1673) retain the grand scale but favour red sandstone with minimal marble ornament. Across the dynasty, master architects — Mirak Mirza Ghiyas, Ustad Ahmad Lahori, Ustad Isa — operated within a centralized atelier system (karkhana) that standardized proportions, material specifications, and decorative vocabularies, ensuring a coherent imperial visual language from garden pavilions to imperial mausolea.

Mughal Imperial Science and Astronomy: Court Observatories and the Jantar Mantar Tradition

Scientific activity at the Mughal court was institutionalised through the translation bureau (Maktab Khana) and the imperial workshops (karkhanas) that produced precision instruments. Akbar commissioned the translation of the Zij‑i‑Sultani of Ulugh Beg into Persian, and his court astronomer Fathullah Shirazi designed a multi‑barrel cannon and an improved astrolabe, illustrating the overlap of military and astronomical engineering. Jahangir maintained a corps of munajjims (astronomers) who prepared annual almanacs (taqwim) for the Ilahi calendar, and his memoirs record observations of comets and solar eclipses.

The most ambitious observational programme emerged under the patronage of Sawai Jai Singh II of Amber, a Mughal mansabdar who enjoyed the confidence of Emperor Muhammad Shah. Between 1724 and 1735 Jai Singh constructed five monumental observatories — at Delhi, Jaipur, Ujjain, Mathura, and Varanasi — collectively known as the Jantar Mantar. The instruments are massive masonry structures calibrated to the latitude of each site: the Samrat Yantra (a giant equinoctial sundial with a gnomon up to 27 m high), the Jai Prakash Yantra (two hemispherical bowls for mapping celestial coordinates), the Ram Yantra (cylindrical pillars for altitude‑azimuth readings), and the Misra Yantra (a composite device for multiple measurements). These instruments draw on Islamic astronomical tradition (the works of Al‑Biruni and Al‑Zarqali) and on Sanskrit texts such as the Surya Siddhanta, reflecting a deliberate synthesis.

The imperial library at Agra and later at Delhi housed Sanskrit manuscripts on mathematics (the Bakhshali treatise), Persian translations of Euclid’s Elements, and Arabic works on spherical trigonometry. The karkhanas produced brass astrolabes, celestial globes, and quadrants inscribed with both Arabic and Devanagari scales, facilitating use by both Muslim and Hindu astronomers. Calendrical reform was a practical motive: the Ilahi calendar introduced by Akbar required accurate solar year length, and the Jantar Mantar observations refined the tropical year to 365 days 5 hours 48 minutes 45 seconds — remarkably close to the modern value. Although the observatories fell into disuse after the mid‑18th century, their design principles influenced later British survey instruments, and the Jaipur Jantar Mantar remains a UNESCO World Heritage site, testifying to the Mughal state’s capacity to mobilise scientific expertise for administrative and ideological purposes.

Technique: Comparative Table for Cavalry Types
Question: Distinguish between Bargi and Silahdar cavalry in the Maratha military system and explain how the Sarnaubat coordinated their deployment during campaigns.
Answer: Bargi cavalry were state‑funded; the treasury supplied horses, arms, and a fixed salary, making them a standing force directly accountable to the Sarnaubat. Silahdar cavalry were self‑equipped nobles who brought their own mounts and weapons; they received a larger share of plunder (typically 60 %) but no regular pay. The Sarnaubat, as commander‑in‑chief of cavalry, maintained a register (hazari/panchhazari) listing each unit’s strength, conducted periodic jirga (muster) inspections to verify horse quality, and assigned Bargi squadrons to garrison duties and frontier patrols while deploying Silahdar contingents for rapid raids and pursuit operations. Revenue assignments (saranjam) were granted to Silahdar leaders to sustain their retinues, whereas Bargi expenses were met from the central Huzur Daftar. This dual structure gave the Marathas both a reliable core (Bargi) and a flexible, incentive‑driven strike arm (Silahdar).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “institutional distinctions”, draw a two‑column table in the margin: list the institution (e.g., Bargi vs Silahdar) on the left and the defining features (funding, equipment, command, revenue link) on the right. This visual cue prevents conflation of similar‑sounding terms and lets you write a concise, point‑wise answer within the time limit.

Vijayanagara Merchant Guilds and Maritime Trade Networks

The commercial vitality of the Vijayanagara empire rested on a layered network of merchant guilds (nagarams), itinerant caravan traders (banjaras), and port‑based communities (chettis, mapillas). The nagarams functioned as corporate bodies with elected headmen (nagarattar), internal dispute‑resolution mechanisms, and the authority to issue hundis (bills of exchange) that circulated across the Deccan and the Malabar coast. Major ports such as Bhatkal, Honavar, and Mangalore handled the export of black pepper, cardamom, cotton textiles, and indigo, while importing Arabian horses, Persian copper, Chinese porcelain, and East African ivory.

State revenue from maritime trade was collected as sungam (customs duty) at the port gate, typically levied at 5‑10 % ad valorem. The royal treasury farmed out the collection to prominent guild leaders under a contract (ijara) that guaranteed a fixed annual sum, incentivising the guilds to expand trade volume. Temples acted as financial hubs: the devadana lands generated surplus grain that was monetised through temple‑run markets (santhai), and temple treasuries advanced credit to merchants at modest interest, reinforcing the guild‑temple symbiosis.

The arrival of the Portuguese after 1498 introduced a coercive dimension. Albuquerque’s 1510 treaty with the Vijayanagara governor of Goa attempted to secure a monopoly on horse imports, but the nagarams resisted by rerouting horse shipments through the inland fair at Hampi and by leveraging their hundi network to bypass Portuguese customs. The 1565 Battle of Talikota disrupted the imperial centre, yet the guilds survived by shifting their operational base to the Nayaka successor states (Madurai, Tanjavur, Gingee), which honoured existing commercial privileges. The continuity of guild records — account books (varavu) maintained by hereditary karnams — enabled a seamless transition of credit instruments into the Mughal‑Deccan and later British revenue systems.

Deccan Sultanates’ Cultural Synthesis: Dakhni Language, Literature, and Sufi Patronage

The Bahmani Sultanate (1347‑1518) and its five successor states — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — fostered a distinct Deccani cultural idiom known as Dakhni (or Deccani Urdu). Written in a Perso‑Arabic script but heavily infused with Marathi, Kannada, and Telugu vocabulary, Dakhni became the court language for administration, poetry, and Sufi discourse. Ibrahim Adil Shah II of Bijapur (1580‑1627) epitomised this synthesis: his treatise Kitab‑i‑Nauras, composed in Dakhni, codifies musical modes (ragas) and links them to the nine rasas of Indian aesthetics, while his patronage of the Sufi order of the Chishti‑Sabiri at the dargah of Hazrat Khwaja Banda Nawaz in Gulbarga reinforced legitimacy among both Muslim and Hindu subjects.

Literary production flourished in the masnavi and qasida forms. The poet Wajhi (d. 1630) authored the Sabras, a Dakhni masnavi that blends Persian narrative technique with local folk motifs. Ghawwasi of Bidar composed the Saif‑ul‑Mulk‑wa‑Badi‑ul‑Jamal, a romantic allegory that draws on both Persian romance tradition and Telugu yakshagana performance conventions. Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah of Golconda (1580‑1611) wrote a divan of ghazals in Dakhni, celebrating the city of Hyderabad (founded 1591) as a garden of cultural confluence.

Sufi institutions served as nodes of political integration. The Qadiri and Shattari orders established khanqahs (hospices) in capital cities, receiving land grants (madad‑i‑maash) from the sultan. The annual urs (death anniversary) of the Bijapur saint Shah Miran attracted merchants, soldiers, and peasants, creating a shared ritual space that transcended sectarian lines. Architecturally, the synthesis appears in monuments such as the Gol Gumbaz (1656) with its whispering gallery — a structural innovation derived from Persian double‑dome engineering — and the Ibrahim Rauza (1626) where Persian calligraphy coexists with Kannada inscriptions. This layered cultural fabric persisted after the Mughal annexation of the Deccan (1686‑87), influencing the later development of Urdu in the Hyderabad state.

Maratha Cavalry Organization: Bargi, Silahdar, and the Sarnaubat

The Maratha military machine relied on a highly mobile cavalry divided into two complementary streams. Bargi (state‑equipped) troopers received horses, swords, lances, and a monthly salary from the central treasury; their mounts were branded with the state mark (shahi nishan) and they were subject to strict discipline under the Sarnaubat, the commander‑in‑chief of cavalry. Silahdar (self‑equipped) nobles brought their own horses and arms, often maintaining a personal retinue of 10‑50 horsemen; they operated on a profit‑sharing basis, claiming up to 60 % of captured booty (ghanima) while foregoing regular pay.

The Sarnaubat maintained a hierarchical register: a hazari commanded 1,000 horse, a panchhazari 5,000, and the supreme Sarnaubat oversaw the entire cavalry corps, which at its peak under Shivaji and the early Peshwas numbered 50,000‑70,000 effectives. Periodic jirga (muster) inspections were held at designated plains (e.g., the plains of Pune) where each unit paraded for verification of horse quality, armour condition, and numerical strength. Deficiencies attracted fines or replacement of the unit commander.

Revenue assignments (saranjam) sustained the Silahdar class: a grant of villages yielded a fixed annual income (typically 25‑30 % of the assessed revenue) earmarked for cavalry upkeep. Bargi expenses, by contrast, were drawn directly from the Huzur Daftar’s cash reserves, making them a fiscal burden that the Peshwas sought to contain by converting many Bargi slots into Silahdar contracts during the 18th century. The cavalry’s operational doctrine emphasised rapid raids (ganimi kava), feigned retreats, and the exploitation of the Deccan’s broken terrain; forts (gadhi) served as logistical nodes where fresh mounts and grain were stocked. Notable cavalry leaders such as Santaji Ghorpade and Dhanaji Jadhav mastered the art of cutting Mughal supply lines, a capability rooted in the dual‑track organisation that blended state discipline with entrepreneurial aggression.

  • Confusing the Bargi/Silahdar distinction with the Mughal mansabdari sawar/zat classification; the Maratha system was based on equipment ownership, not rank‑based quota.
  • Attributing the Jantar Mantar observatories solely to Mughal emperors; they were built by a Rajput vassal under imperial sanction, not by the central court.
  • Assuming Vijayanagara’s merchant guilds disappeared after 1565; they migrated to Nayaka courts and retained their hundi networks.
  • Treating Dakhni as a mere dialect of Urdu; it developed an independent literary canon with distinct grammatical features and a separate scribal tradition.
  • Overstating the uniformity of Mughal architectural style; regional workshops (Gujarat, Bengal, Kashmir) introduced local stone‑carving techniques that altered the imperial template.
  • Remember to cross‑check each institutional term against its chronological context to avoid anachronistic conflation.

The Mughal Imperial Chancellery: The Dabir, Munshi, and the Persian Bureaucratic Language

The Mughal imperial chancellery (Diwan‑i‑Insha) was the nerve centre of written communication, producing farmans, nishans, parwanas, and sanads that legitimised imperial authority across the subcontinent. At its head stood the Mir Munshi (chief secretary), usually a senior noble with the rank of hazari or higher, who supervised a hierarchy of dabirs (secretaries) and munshis (clerks). The dabir drafted the original text in the highly stylised insha prose, employing a prescribed vocabulary of honorifics (alqab), invocations (basmala, hamdala), and formulaic clauses (e.g., “az taraf‑i‑sarkar‑i‑aliya”). The munshi then copied the draft into fair script (nastaʿlīq), verified the seal impressions, and entered the document in the daftar (register) for archival retrieval. Persian functioned as the lingua franca of administration from the Sultanate period onward, but the Mughals standardised its bureaucratic register: the “Mughal Persian” of the chancellery differed from literary Persian in its rigid syntax, fixed epithets for the emperor (e.g., “zill‑i‑ilahi”, “shadow of God”), and a calendar system that combined the Ilahi era with the Hijri date. Training for munshis occurred in madrasas attached to the court and in the karkhanas where scribes learned calligraphy, arithmetic (hisab), and the conventions of revenue terminology (zabt, jama, hasil). The chancellery also maintained a corps of waqai‑navis (news writers) who fed the akhbarat system, but their output was distinct from the formal insha documents. Under Akbar the chancellery expanded to include a translation bureau (Maktab Khana) that rendered Sanskrit, Arabic, and Turkish works into Persian, reinforcing the language’s status as the medium of governance. Jahangir and Shah Jahan further refined the formulary, introducing the “farman‑i‑shahi” with a double seal (muhr‑i‑shahi and muhr‑i‑diwan) to prevent forgery. By Aurangzeb’s reign the volume of correspondence had grown so large that provincial daftars (subah‑level registries) were empowered to issue routine parwanas under the authority of the local diwan, while the central chancellery reserved farmans for matters of high policy, appointments, and grants of madad‑i‑maash. The precision of this bureaucratic language enabled the empire to manage a vast, multilingual territory through a single documentary regime, and its formulae survived in the successor states of the eighteenth century, notably in the Hyderabad and Awadh chancelleries.

Mughal Patronage of Astronomy and the Jantar Mantar Observatories: Scientific Institutions under Imperial Sponsorship

Mughal interest in astronomy was both practical — regulating the calendar, fixing festival dates, and guiding military campaigns — and symbolic, projecting the emperor as a cosmic sovereign. The most visible manifestation of this patronage is the series of five monumental observatories known as Jantar Mantar, constructed between 1724 and 1735 by Sawai Jai Singh II of Amber, a Rajput noble holding a 7,000‑zat mansab under Emperor Muhammad Shah. Jai Singh secured imperial sanction, land grants, and access to the imperial library’s collection of Islamic zijes (astronomical tables), notably the Zij‑i‑Sultani of Ulugh Beg and the Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi compiled at the Mughal court. He also commissioned translations of European works, such as Philippe de La Hire’s tables, into Persian, integrating Latin trigonometric methods with the Indo‑Persian tradition. Each observatory housed massive masonry instruments: the Samrat Yantra (a giant equatorial sundial), the Jai Prakash Yantra (a hemispherical bowl for celestial altitude measurements), the Ram Yantra (a pair of cylindrical structures for azimuth and altitude), and the Misra Yantra (a composite device for multiple observations). These instruments were calibrated to the local latitude, allowing precise determination of solar noon, equinoxes, and planetary positions. The data produced served the imperial court for the preparation of the official calendar (the Fasli and Hijri calendars), for astrological predictions that informed royal decisions, and for navigation on the Indus and Ganges rivers. The observatories also functioned as centres of instruction; Jai Singh appointed astronomers (ganakas) such as Jagannatha Samrat and Keval Ram to train students in observational techniques. After Jai Singh’s death the Delhi and Jaipur observatories continued to operate under his descendants, while the others fell into disuse. The Mughal emperor’s role was that of a patron who legitimised the project through farmans granting tax‑free villages for the observatories’ upkeep, but the scientific agenda, design, and funding originated with Jai Singh. This pattern — imperial endorsement of a vassal’s scholarly enterprise — recurs in the Mughal support for medicine (the Tibb‑i‑Akbari), metallurgy, and cartography, illustrating how the empire harnessed regional expertise within a Persianate intellectual framework.

Examiner's Trap
A question asks: "Which Mughal emperor directly commissioned the construction of the Jantar Mantar observatories?" The answer expected is Sawai Jai Singh II, a Rajput vassal, not a Mughal emperor. The trap is to attribute the observatories to the imperial centre rather than to a subordinate ruler acting under imperial sanction.
The five Jantar Mantar observatories (Delhi, Jaipur, Ujjain, Mathura, Varanasi) were built between 1724 and 1735 by Sawai Jai Singh II of Amber, who held a high mansab under Muhammad Shah. The Mughal emperor provided patronage and sanctioned the project, but the initiative, funding, and design were Jai Singh's. Therefore, the correct answer identifies Jai Singh as the builder, not the emperor.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question links a scientific institution to a Mughal emperor, verify whether the emperor personally ordered it or merely granted patronage. Look for the name of the subordinate ruler or noble who actually commissioned the work; that distinction often separates a correct answer from a common distractor.

Maratha Coastal Fortifications and the Revenue Administration of the Konkan Ports

The Maratha control of the Konkan littoral (c. 1650‑1760) rested on a chain of sea forts — Sindhudurg, Vijaydurg, Suvarnadurg, Khanderi, Underi, and the island fort of Janjira (contested with the Siddis) — each serving simultaneously as a military outpost, a customs house, and a revenue collection centre. The administrative head of a sea fort was the killedar, appointed by the Peshwa’s Huzur Daftar, who commanded a garrison of bargi and silahdar cavalry detachments adapted for coastal defence. Alongside the killedar, the sabnis (accountant) maintained the fort’s revenue registers (daftar‑i‑bandar), recording incoming vessels, cargo manifests, and the levies due. The principal imposts were the bandar‑kar (port dues), chungi (customs duty on goods), and langar‑shulka (anchorage fees), assessed as a percentage of the cargo’s declared value, typically 2.5‑5 % for Indian merchants and up to 10 % for European ships. The Maratha navy, under the Angre admirals until the 1750s, escorted merchant convoys through the pirate‑infested waters of the Arabian Sea, and the convoy protection fee (rah‑dari) was collected at the fort’s gate. Revenue from these ports flowed into the saranjam grants that sustained the coastal fort garrisons; a typical saranjam for a killedar comprised a cluster of villages yielding 25‑30 % of their assessed produce, earmarked for fort maintenance, gunpowder, and crew wages. The Huzur Daftar in Pune audited the bandar daftars annually, dispatching a vakil (inspector) to verify collections against the standard rates (mahsul). After the decline of Angre power (post‑1755), the Peshwas reorganised the coastal administration by creating the post of bandar‑adhikari (port superintendent) who reported directly to the diwan‑i‑kul, centralising revenue remittances and reducing the killedar’s fiscal autonomy. The Konkan ports also generated chauth from the inland territories that relied on maritime trade, but the bandar‑kar remained a distinct revenue stream, administered through a separate chain of accountability that insulated it from the inland chauth‑sardeshmukhi machinery. This dual fiscal architecture — military‑fort revenue and commercial customs — exemplifies the Maratha capacity to integrate naval strategy with a sophisticated, document‑driven revenue system.

The Deccan Sultanates' Diplomatic Correspondence and Treaty Practices with the Mughals and European Powers

The five Deccan sultanates — Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, Golconda, Bidar, and Berar — maintained a diplomatic apparatus that mirrored, yet diverged from, Mughal chancellery practice. Their official language was Persian, but the formulary employed distinct document types: the ahdnama (treaty of friendship or truce), the sanad (grant of territory or revenue rights), the farman‑i‑sultani (royal order), the nishan‑i‑sultani (sealed decree), and the parwana‑i‑diwani (administrative instruction). Envoys (vakils) carried credentials (mahzar) and were received at the darbar with prescribed礼仪 (etiquette), including the exchange of khilats (robes of honour) and nazar (tribute gifts). The 1636 Deccan Settlement, negotiated between Shah Jahan’s wazir Asaf Khan and the Bijapur and Golconda vakils, produced a set of ahdnamas that fixed tribute (peshkash) at 20 lakh rupees annually, demarcated frontier zones (hudud), and stipulated the return of fugitive nobles. The treaties were sealed with the sultan’s personal seal (muhr‑i‑sultani) and countersigned by the Mughal mir bakhshi, creating a dual‑authentication protocol. With European powers, the Deccan courts adopted a parallel set of instruments: the Dutch VOC and English EIC concluded “farmans of trade” (farman‑i‑tijarat) granting factory privileges, customs exemptions, and the right to mint coins (e.g., the Golconda farman of 1632 to the English). These documents bore the sultan’s tughra (calligraphic monogram) and were registered in the European factories’ diaries. The Portuguese, already established at Goa, negotiated “cartas de feitoria” that functioned as sanads for fortified warehouses. Diplomatic correspondence was archived in the daftar‑i‑insha of each sultanate, where the dabir‑i‑khas maintained a chronological register (roznamcha) of incoming and outgoing missives. The Deccan chancelleries also employed a corps of waqai‑navis who reported on Mughal court movements, enabling timely diplomatic responses. Notably, the Deccan sultans used the “mahzar‑i‑arz” (petition of grievances) to appeal to the Mughal emperor directly, bypassing the imperial mir bakhshi, a practice that underscored the personalised nature of Deccan‑Mughal diplomacy. The survival of these documents in the Hyderabad State Archives and the Dutch East India Company records provides a comparative lens on how Persianate diplomatic culture adapted to both imperial suzerainty and early modern European commercial expansion.

The Ahom Kingdom's Land Tenure System: The Paik, the Gohain, and the Revenue Grants

The Ahom kingdom (1228‑1826) developed a land tenure structure that fused tribal labour organisation with wet‑rice agronomy, producing a fiscal system distinct from both the Mughal zabt and the Maratha chauth. The basic unit of cultivation was the gaon (village), whose households were grouped into paik‑khels — each paik representing a male adult liable for corvée labour (paik‑seva) and military service. A standard paik household held a plot of approximately 2.5 bighas (locally called pura) of paddy land, assessed at a fixed yield of 12‑15 mans of rice per pura. The state’s revenue demand (kharaj) was collected in kind (rice) at the rate of one‑third of the gross produce, measured at the threshing floor by the bora (revenue officer) using the standard bamboo measure (khal). Above the paik‑khel stood the gohain (hereditary nobles) — the Burhagohain, Borgohain, and Borpatragohain — who received revenue grants (madad‑i‑maash equivalents) known as gohain‑khat. These grants comprised clusters of villages whose produce was assigned to the gohain in perpetuity, subject to royal confirmation at each succession. The gohains maintained their own paik contingents, administered justice through the nyaya‑sabha, and contributed a fixed quota of rice and elephants to the royal treasury. The satras (Vaishnavite monasteries) formed a parallel landholding tier: the mahanta (abbot) held debottar grants (satras‑khat) exempt from kharaj, in return for religious instruction and the maintenance of a paik‑khel of disciples. Artisan groups (khels) such as the khanikar (blacksmiths) and the hira (potters) were settled on designated lands (khel‑bhumi) and rendered service‑tax (khel‑kar) in the form of manufactured goods. The Ahom state also practised revenue farming (ijaradari) from the late seventeenth century, leasing out the collection of kharaj from marginal tracts to contractors (ijaradars) who paid a lump sum (peshkash) to the royal diwan.
