Land measurement relied on the “khat” survey, conducted periodically by the phukan (surveyor) using a rope of standard length (the “khat‑dora”), and recorded in the buranji (chronicle) which served as the legal title register. This intricate hierarchy — paik, gohain, satra, khel — created a resilient fiscal base that sustained the Ahom military machine, notably the riverine navy on the Brahmaputra, and allowed the kingdom to withstand Mughal invasions (1615‑1682) without adopting the Mughal mansabdari or zabt frameworks.

  • Confusing the Mughal chancellery's Dabir with the Maratha Sabnis; they belong to different administrative traditions.
  • Attributing the Jantar Mantar observatories to a Mughal emperor rather than to Sawai Jai Singh II acting under imperial sanction.
  • Assuming the Maratha sea forts were solely military outposts; they functioned as revenue centres with dedicated port officers and customs staff.
  • Treating Deccan Sultanate diplomatic documents as identical to Mughal farmans; the Deccan courts used distinct formulae such as ahdnama and sanad with their own sealing protocols.
  • Overlooking the hereditary nature of Ahom Gohain land grants and equating them with Mughal jagirs, which were conditional and transferable.
  • Neglecting the role of the Paik corvée in sustaining Ahom revenue assessments, leading to an incomplete picture of the kingdom's fiscal base.
  • Failing to distinguish between the Maratha Chauth collected from inland territories and the Bandar Kar levied at Konkan ports, which operated under separate administrative chains.

Vijayanagara Temple Administration: The Hierarchy of Temple Officials, Ritual Management, and Land Control

The Vijayanagara state treated major temples as autonomous fiscal units that mirrored the provincial administration. At the apex stood the Sthanika, a hereditary chief priest who held the sthala (temple) grant (devadana) and exercised judicial authority over temple servants and tenants. Below the Sthanika, the Archaka performed daily rituals, while the Paricharaka managed the temple kitchen (annadana) and the distribution of prasadam. The Karyakartta acted as the executive officer, supervising the temple’s agricultural lands, collecting produce from tenant cultivators (kudiyan), and maintaining the irrigation channels that fed the temple’s wet‑rice fields.

Land attached to temples was recorded in the kavala registers, which listed each plot’s extent in khanduga units, the assessed yield in khanduga of paddy, and the share due to the temple treasury (bhandara). The temple’s revenue was split into three streams: nitya (daily ritual expenses), naimittika (festival expenditures), and sankalpa (capital improvements such as gopuram construction). The Mahasabha, a council of senior Brahmins and merchant patrons, audited the accounts annually during the Mahotsava festival, ensuring that surplus grain was either stored in the temple granary (kottam) or sold in the nearby market (pete) to fund military contributions to the Raya.

Temple officials also exercised limited judicial powers. Disputes over tenancy, water rights, or service obligations were heard in the nyaya‑sabha presided over by the Sthanika, with the Karyakartta presenting evidence from the kavala records. Punishments ranged from fines payable in grain to temporary suspension of ritual privileges. This quasi‑judicial role reinforced the temple’s integration into the broader nayankara network, as Nayaka chiefs often confirmed temple grants in exchange for a share of the temple’s surplus (kavala‑patti).

The administrative continuity of temple institutions survived the 1565 Battle of Talikota because the devadana grants were recognized by the successor Deccan sultanates and later by the Mughal subahdars who issued sanads confirming existing endowments. In the 17th century, the temple at Tirupati, for example, negotiated directly with the Golconda court for the right to collect customs at the nearby port of Pulicat, illustrating the temple’s evolution into a commercial node. The survival of palm‑leaf kavala registers in the Tamil Nadu Archives provides a documentary trail for the fiscal logic of temple‑state symbiosis.

Examiners often test the distinction between the devadana (tax‑free religious grant) and the amaram (military service grant) in Vijayanagara. A precise answer notes that devadana lands were exempt from kharaj but obliged to supply ritual materials, whereas amaram lands required the holder to maintain a stipulated number of cavalry. Confusing the two leads to errors in questions on revenue classification.

Mughal Imperial Science Patronage: The Jantar Mantar Observatories, Astronomical Tables, and Sanskrit Translations

Emperor Jahangir’s interest in natural philosophy was institutionalised through the Maktab Khana (translation bureau) established at Agra in 1605, but it was under Shah Jahan and especially Aurangzeb that systematic astronomical observation received state funding. The most visible outcome was the construction of five Jantar Mantar observatories — at Delhi (1724), Jaipur (1734), Ujjain (1735), Varanasi (1737), and Mathura (1738) — commissioned by Maharaja Sawai Jai Singh II under imperial sanction. Each observatory housed massive masonry instruments (yantras) such as the Samrat Yantra (equinoctial sundial), the Jai Prakash Yantra (hemispherical dial), and the Rama Yantra (azimuth‑altitude instrument), designed to measure solar declination, planetary positions, and time with an accuracy of a few seconds.

The data generated fed into the Zij‑i‑Muhammadi (Muhammad Shah’s astronomical tables), compiled by a team of Hindu and Muslim astronomers including Jagannatha Samrat and Mulla Mahmood. These tables corrected the earlier Zij‑i‑Sultani of Ulugh Beg by incorporating observations from the Indian observatories, and they were used for calendrical purposes — fixing the dates of Eid, Diwali, and the agricultural fasli year — as well as for navigation on the Indian Ocean. The imperial court also sponsored the translation of Sanskrit astronomical works such as the Surya Siddhanta and the Brahmasphutasiddhanta into Persian, enabling cross‑cultural verification of planetary models.

Funding for the observatories came from the khalsa (crown lands) revenue of the respective subahs, with the diwan of each province allocating a fixed annual grant (mahsul) for maintenance, instrument calibration, and the salaries of the munajjims (astronomers). The mir bakhshi oversaw the appointment of the chief astronomer (munajjim‑i‑kul), who reported directly to the emperor through the akhbarat system. This bureaucratic integration ensured that astronomical knowledge served both ritual legitimacy (the emperor as zill‑i‑ilahi regulating cosmic order) and practical administration (tax collection schedules, military campaign timing).

The observatories also functioned as centres for instrument making. Metalworkers from the karkhanas of Lahore and Jaipur produced brass astrolabes and quadrants based on designs refined at the Jantar Mantar sites. These instruments were exported to the Ottoman and Safavid courts as diplomatic gifts, demonstrating the Mughal claim to scientific leadership in the Persianate world. Surviving examples in the British Museum and the National Museum, New Delhi, bear inscriptions in Persian and Sanskrit, attesting to the collaborative milieu.

A common examination trap is to attribute the Jantar Mantar observatories to a Mughal emperor personally. The correct formulation: they were built by Sawai Jai Singh II, a Rajput vassal, under a farman of Muhammad Shah (r. 1719‑1748) that granted land and revenue for the purpose. The emperor’s role was patronage and legitimisation, not direct construction.

Worked Example: Distinguishing Patronage from Direct Agency
A question asks: "Who built the Jantar Mantar at Jaipur and under whose authority?" Provide a concise answer that avoids the common error.
The Jantar Mantar at Jaipur was constructed by Maharaja Sawai Jai Singh II between 1728 and 1734. He acted under a farman issued by Emperor Muhammad Shah (r. 1719‑1748) which allocated revenue from the Jaipur subah for the observatory’s construction and maintenance. The emperor’s authority was that of sovereign patron; the actual design, labour, and funding were managed by the Rajput court.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question links a scientific institution to a Mughal ruler, first identify the builder (often a regional ruler or noble) and then the imperial instrument (farman, sanad, or mansab) that authorised it. This two‑step answer secures both the factual and the constitutional dimensions examiners reward.

Delhi Sultanate Urban Water Infrastructure: The Hauz‑i‑Shamsi, Stepwells, and the Kotwal’s Maintenance Role

The rapid expansion of Delhi under the Khalji and Tughluq sultans created a chronic water deficit that the state addressed through a combination of reservoir engineering, stepwell construction, and a dedicated municipal office. The most ambitious project was the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi, a large artificial tank excavated in 1230 CE by Sultan Shams‑ud‑din Iltutmish near the Qutb complex. Fed by seasonal streams and a diversion channel from the Yamuna, the tank covered roughly 40 hectares and stored an estimated 1.2 million cubic metres of water. Its embankments were lined with dressed stone to reduce seepage, and a series of sluice gates (bandhs) regulated outflow into the city’s irrigation canals (nahars) that supplied the royal gardens and the markets of Shahjahanabad’s predecessor settlements.

Complementing the reservoir, the sultanate commissioned over thirty baolis (stepwells) across the urban fabric. The most celebrated, the Gandhak ki Baoli (c. 1235) and the Rajon ki Baoli (c. 1350), descended up to seven stories, providing year‑round access to groundwater even during the dry months. Each baoli was endowed with a waqf deed that assigned maintenance revenue from nearby shops or agricultural plots. The Kotwal (city prefect) held the statutory duty to inspect the baolis quarterly, ensure the bandhs functioned, and levy fines on households that polluted the water. His office maintained a roznamcha (daily register) of water‑level readings, repair expenditures, and the names of the mistris (master masons) contracted for masonry work.

Revenue for water infrastructure was drawn from the kharaj of the iqta holders whose lands benefited from the canals, and from a special abwab (cess) called pani‑tax levied on commercial establishments drawing water for dyeing, tanning, and brewing. The diwan‑i‑wizarat recorded these receipts in the bahi (ledger) under the head ab‑i‑shahr. During the reign of Muhammad bin Tughluq (1325‑1351), the pani‑tax was briefly doubled to finance the construction of the Siri Fort reservoir, a move that sparked merchant protests recorded in the Tarikh‑i‑Firoz Shahi.

The system’s resilience is evident in its continuity: when the Mughals established Shahjahanabad (1639), they repaired the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi, renamed it Shamsi Talab, and integrated its channels into the new city’s nahar‑i‑Bihisht network. The Kotwal’s water‑inspection duties were absorbed into the Mughal kotwali but retained the same quarterly reporting format. Archaeological surveys by the Archaeological Survey of India (ASI) in the 1990s confirmed the original Iltutmish‑era stone lining beneath later Mughal repairs, providing material corroboration of the textual record.

Examiners frequently test the distinction between the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi (a surface reservoir) and the baoli (a stepwell accessing groundwater). A precise answer notes that the former relied on seasonal runoff and required embankment maintenance, while the latter tapped the phreatic zone and depended on the structural integrity of its stairwell and sidewalls. Confusing the two leads to errors in questions on hydraulic technology classification.

Mughal Deccan Frontier Administration: The Subah of Aurangabad, Faujdar Networks, and Maratha Resistance

Following the annexation of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate (1636), the Mughals reorganised the Deccan into four subahs — Daulatabad (later Aurangabad), Berar, Khandesh, and Telangana. The Subah of Aurangabad became the strategic nerve centre for operations against the Maratha chieftains. Its governor (subahdar) was typically a high‑ranking mansabdar (often 7,000 zat/7,000 sawar) such as Khan‑i‑Khanan or later Prince Muazzam (future Bahadur Shah I). The subahdar’s authority was exercised through a tiered network of faujdars stationed at key forts: Daulatabad, Parenda, Ausa, and Jinji. Each faujdar commanded a contingent of 2,000‑3,000 cavalry and infantry, maintained a thana (police post) at the pargana level, and reported weekly to the subahdar via the dak‑chowki relay system.

Revenue collection in the frontier subah relied on a hybrid of zabt (measured assessment) in the settled plains of the Godavari valley and ijaradari (revenue farming) in the hilly tracts where Maratha deshmukhs retained de facto control. The diwan of the subah appointed amils to conduct the paimaish (measurement) using the Ilahi gaz and the bigha standard, but the presence of Maratha chauth collectors often forced the Mughal amils to negotiate peshkash payments to avoid disruption. The faujdar at Parenda, for instance, entered into a 1665 agreement with the Maratha sardeshmukh of the region, allowing the latter to collect chauth in return for a fixed annual tribute of 50,000 rupees to the imperial treasury — a pragmatic compromise recorded in the Muntakhab‑al‑Lubab.

Military logistics were anchored on the Banjara caravans that transported grain, fodder, and artillery from the surplus zones of Berar to the forward depots at Aurangabad and Daulatabad. The mir‑i‑saman (imperial storekeeper) maintained a bar‑i‑aman (supply register) that tracked the movement of 1,200 mans of wheat per month during the 1680‑1707 Deccan campaigns. The faujdars also operated a network of qasbas (fortified market towns) where they levied rahdari (transit duties) on Banjara caravans, a revenue stream that supplemented the subah’s khalsa income.

Maratha resistance exploited the Mughal administrative fragmentation. Shivaji’s 1664 raid on Surat and his 1670‑1674 campaigns in the Konkan forced the Aurangabad faujdars to divert troops from the northern frontier, weakening the cordon around the Bijapur and Golconda sultanates. The Mughal response was the creation of a mobile lashkar (flying column) under the command of the mir‑bakshi’s deputy, which could be dispatched from Aurangabad to any threatened faujdari within ten days. However, the Maratha ganimi kava (guerrilla) tactics — ambushes on supply lines, night raids on thanas, and the use of hill forts like Rajgad and Torna — rendered static faujdari posts ineffective without continuous reinforcement.

The administrative legacy of the Aurangabad subah persisted after the Mughal decline. When the Nizam‑ul‑Mulk Asaf Jah established the Hyderabad state (1724), he retained the faujdari framework, renaming the subahdar as subedar and the faujdars as nawabs of the respective districts. The revenue registers (daftar‑i‑mal) compiled under the Mughal diwan formed the basis for the Nizam’s jagir allocations, illustrating the continuity of frontier fiscal architecture across dynastic change.

Ahom Satra Institution: Debottar Grants, Mahanta Authority, and Vaishnavite Integration into Statecraft

The satras (Vaishnavite monasteries) founded by Srimanta Sankaradeva (1449‑1568) and his disciple Madhavadeva became the most enduring non‑royal landholding institutions in the Ahom kingdom. Each satra received a debottar grant (satra‑khat) from the reigning swargadeo, comprising a cluster of villages whose entire produce was assigned to the monastery in perpetuity, subject only to royal confirmation at each succession. The grant deed (sanad) specified the boundaries in pura units, the annual rice yield expected (typically 12‑15 mans per pura), and the obligations of the mahanta (abbot) to maintain a paik‑khel of disciples for corvée labour and military service.

The mahanta exercised quasi‑sovereign authority within the satra’s territory. He collected the kharaj (one‑third of gross produce) at the threshing floor using the standard bamboo measure (khal), adjudicated civil disputes through the nyaya‑sabha presided over by senior bhaktas, and commanded the satra’s paik contingent during Ahom mobilisations. In return, the mahanta supplied the royal treasury with a fixed quota of rice (usually 5,000 mans annually) and a ceremonial elephant every three years. The satra’s khel (artisan guilds) — blacksmiths (khanikar), potters (hira), and weavers (tantikar) — were settled on designated khel‑bhumi and rendered service‑tax (khel‑kar) in the form of manufactured goods, further integrating the monastic economy into the state’s fiscal web.

Royal oversight was exercised through the Borphukan (commander of the western frontier) and the Barbarua (chief secretary), who inspected satra accounts during the annual Bihu festival. The buranji (chronicle) records that in 1682, King Gadadhar Singha ordered a khat survey of all satra lands to verify the extent of debottar holdings after a series of Mughal incursions had disrupted revenue flows. The survey, conducted by the phukan using the standard rope (khat‑dora), resulted in the re‑confirmation of 112 satra grants and the resumption of 17 that had lapsed due to the mahanta’s failure to meet the elephant quota.

The satras also functioned as centres of cultural standardisation. The naam‑ghar (prayer hall) served as a village assembly space where the mahanta disseminated the Bhakti doctrine, taught the Assamese script (Bamunia), and coordinated the Bhaona (traditional theatre) performances that reinforced social cohesion. This cultural role complemented the Ahom state’s policy of Assamisation — the gradual replacement of Tai‑Ahom court rituals with Vaishnavite practices — culminating in the 1714 coronation of King Siva Singha, who performed the nam‑prasanga at the Auniati satra, symbolising the fusion of monarchy and monastic authority.

Examiners often conflate the satra’s debottar grant with the Mughal madad‑i‑maash (tax‑free stipend for scholars). The critical difference: debottar lands were productive agricultural estates whose surplus directly funded the satra’s religious and military obligations, whereas madad‑i‑maash was a cash or revenue assignment without territorial sovereignty. Misidentifying the two leads to errors in comparative fiscal history questions.

  • Confusing the Vijayanagara Sthanika’s hereditary priestly authority with the Mughal Qazi’s judicial appointment.
  • Attributing the Jantar Mantar observatories to a Mughal emperor rather than to Sawai Jai Singh II acting under a farman.
  • Treating the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi as a stepwell instead of a surface reservoir fed by seasonal runoff.
  • Equating the Aurangabad faujdar’s static garrison role with the mobile Maratha ganimi kava tactics.
  • Identifying the Ahom satra’s debottar grant as a simple tax‑free stipend akin to a Mughal madad‑i‑maash.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise institutional differentiation across the Sultanate, Mughal, Vijayanagara, Maratha, and Ahom polities.

The Delhi Sultanate’s Central Secretariat: The Diwan‑i‑Wizarat, Diwan‑i‑Arz, and Diwan‑i‑Insha

The administrative core of the Delhi Sultanate rested on three principal diwans that emerged under the early Turkish rulers and were refined by the Khaljis and Tughluqs. The Diwan‑i‑Wizarat (finance) was headed by the Wazir, assisted by the Naib‑Wazir, the Mushrif‑i‑Mamalik (accountant‑general) and the Mustaufi (auditor). Its registers (daftar‑i‑mal) recorded land revenue (kharaj), trade dues (jizya on non‑Muslims, khums on spoils) and the salary assignments (iqta) of nobles. Alauddin Khalji (1296‑1316) tightened the Wazir’s control by instituting a cash‑based salary system for the standing army, fixing grain prices through the shahna‑i‑mandi and demanding daily revenue returns from provincial amils.

The Diwan‑i‑Arz (military) was directed by the Arz‑i‑Mamalik, who maintained the chehra (descriptive roll) and dagh (branding) of horses. Under the Khaljis the Arz‑i‑Mamalik acquired the power to audit the iqta holders’ troop quotas, while Muhammad bin Tughluq (1325‑1351) expanded the department to manage his massive Deccan campaigns, creating a separate Diwan‑i‑Arz‑i‑Deccan with its own mir‑bakshi. The Tughluq experiment with token currency (1330‑1332) forced the Arz‑i‑Mamalik to pay soldiers in copper tanka, a move that eroded morale and contributed to the 1335 rebellion of the amiran‑i‑sada.

The Diwan‑i‑Insha (correspondence) was led by the Dabir‑i‑Khas (chief secretary) and staffed by munshis drafted from Persian‑educated families. It drafted farmans, nishans (orders bearing the royal seal), and diplomatic letters to the Chagatai Khanate, the Ilkhanate and the Yadava kingdom of Devagiri. Firuz Shah Tughluq (1351‑1388) institutionalised hereditary appointments in the Insha, granting the Dabir‑i‑Khas a jagir and making the post quasi‑hereditary, which later limited the Sultan’s ability to purge the secretariat during succession crises.

Collectively, the three diwans formed a bureaucratic triangle: the Wazir controlled fiscal resources, the Arz‑i‑Mamalik commanded military manpower, and the Dabir‑i‑Khas managed the flow of information. The Khalji reforms sharpened central oversight, while the Tughluq expansions stretched the system to its limits, exposing the fragility of a secretariat that relied on personal loyalty rather than impersonal rules.

Vijayanagara’s Temple‑Centric Administration: The Sthanika, the Mahamandaleshvara, and Devadana Grants

In the Vijayanagara polity the temple functioned as a node of economic, judicial and ritual authority that paralleled the nayankara military assignments. The Sthanika was the hereditary priest‑administrator of a major temple complex (e.g., Virupaksha at Hampi, Venkateshvara at Tirupati). He held a sthanika‑patta granting him the right to collect the devadana (temple land) revenue, supervise the kottam (temple treasury), and adjudicate disputes among temple tenants through a nyaya‑sabha of senior bhaktas. The Sthanika’s authority was confirmed at each royal succession by a rajapattra bearing the king’s seal.

The Mahamandaleshvara (great lord) was a title bestowed on powerful nayakas or local chiefs who received a cluster of villages as devadana grants in return for maintaining irrigation tanks, supplying ritual materials, and providing a contingent of troops during the king’s campaigns. Inscriptions from the 1520s record that the Mahamandaleshvara of the Kolar region rendered 2,000 khandugas of rice annually to the royal treasury while retaining the surplus for temple festivals.

Devadana grants were tax‑free land endowments made by the king or by nayakas to temples. The grant deed (shasana) specified boundaries in khanduga units, the annual yield expected (often 12‑15 mans per khanduga), and the obligations of the temple to maintain a paik‑khel of artisans (blacksmiths, weavers, potters) settled on khel‑bhumi. The temple’s kottam stored grain, coin and precious metals, functioning as a local credit institution that advanced seed loans to cultivators at the start of the agricultural cycle.

Temple administration thus integrated fiscal, military and ritual functions. The Sthanika’s hereditary priestly role contrasted with the Mughal qazi, who was an appointed judge, while the Mahamandaleshvara’s military obligations mirrored the nayaka’s kandachara duties. After the Battle of Talikota (1565) many devadana lands were resumed by the Deccan Sultanates, but the institutional memory survived in the Nayaka successor states of Madurai, Tanjavur and Ikkeri.

Comparative Institutional Differentiation
Explain how the Sthanika’s hereditary priestly authority differed from the Mughal Qazi’s judicial appointment, and why confusing the two leads to errors in comparative fiscal history questions.
The Sthanika derived authority from a hereditary sthanika‑patta confirmed by the Vijayanagara king, managing devadana lands, temple treasury and a priestly court. The Mughal Qazi was a state‑appointed judicial officer (often a scholar of Islamic law) who received a cash stipend (madad‑i‑maash) or a revenue assignment without territorial sovereignty. The Sthanika’s power was rooted in land control and ritual leadership; the Qazi’s power was limited to adjudication. Treating the devadana grant as a simple tax‑free stipend like madad‑i‑maash ignores the temple’s economic autonomy and its military‑artisan obligations, a mistake examiners penalise in comparative fiscal history.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks you to compare temple‑state relations across Vijayanagara, Mughal and Ahom polities, first list the land‑grant terminology (devadana, madad‑i‑maash, debottar), then note the hereditary versus appointed nature of the administering official (Sthanika, Qazi, Mahanta). This two‑column matrix lets you answer both “similarities” and “differences” parts without missing the fiscal‑sovereignty nuance.

The Deccan Sultanates’ Noble Hierarchy: The Amir, Malik, and Wazir System

The three major Deccan Sultanates — Ahmadnagar, Bijapur and Golconda — organised their nobility into a graded hierarchy that combined Persian administrative terminology with local military realities. At the apex stood the Wazir (chief minister), who oversaw the Diwan‑i‑Wizarat, the Diwan‑i‑Arz and the Diwan‑i‑Insha. The Wazir was usually a senior Amir appointed by the sultan and held a mansab‑equivalent rank expressed in hazari (command of 1,000 cavalry). Below the Wazir were the Amirs, each commanding a contingent of 1,000‑5,000 horsemen and holding a jagir (revenue assignment) in the sarkar they administered. The Maliks formed the next tier, leading 300‑1,000 cavalry and often serving as faujdars of districts (mahals).

Factional alignment cut across these ranks. The Deccani party comprised locally born Muslims (often converts) and Maratha auxiliaries; the Afaqi (or Gharib) party consisted of recent immigrants from Persia, Central Asia and the Ottoman world; the Habshi party was built around Abyssinian slave‑soldiers who had risen to high command. In Ahmadnagar, Malik Ambar (d. 1626), a Habshi, leveraged his control of the paik militia and the jagir of Junnar to dominate the sultanate for two decades, appointing his own Amirs and sidelining the Afaqi Wazir. In Bijapur, the Afaqi Wazir Mustafa Khan (d. 1656) balanced the Deccani Amirs by granting them saranjam grants in the Konkan, while the Habshi commander Ikhlas Khan controlled the artillery corps.

The Wazir’s fiscal authority rested on the peshkash (tribute) collected from provincial Amirs, the kharaj from crown lands, and the jizya from non‑Muslim subjects. Revenue registers (daftar‑i‑mal) were maintained in Persian using the bigha and khanduga measures. The Maliks’ district‑level faujdari courts handled petty criminal cases, while the Amirs’ adalat heard revenue disputes. This layered judiciary mirrored the Mughal qazi‑mufti system but retained a stronger military character because the Amirs were also field commanders.

Factional competition for jagirs and the sultan’s favour intensified after the Mughal annexation of Ahmadnagar (1636). The surviving Bijapur and Golconda courts saw a rapid turnover of Wazirs — five in Bijapur between 1640‑1656 — as Deccani and Afaqi factions alternated in power. The inability to institutionalise a stable noble hierarchy weakened central revenue extraction, facilitating Aurangzeb’s final conquest of the Deccan (1686‑1687).

Ahom Buranji Historiography: Chronicle Production, State Legitimacy, and Genealogical Narrative

The Ahom kingdom (1228‑1826) produced a distinctive corpus of chronicles known as Buranjis, written initially in the Tai‑Ahom script and later in Assamese using the Bamunia alphabet. The royal scribes (likhakars) attached to the Gandhia (royal archive) compiled these texts under the direct supervision of the Barbarua (chief secretary). A typical Buranji recorded the genealogy of the Swargadeo (king), major military campaigns, treaty texts (e.g., the 1639 Treaty of Ghilajharighat with the Mughals), land‑grant deeds (sanads) for debottar satra lands, and the annual Bihu festival accounts.

Chronicle production served three interlocking purposes. First, it legitimised succession by presenting an unbroken lineage from the founder Sukaphaa (r. 1228‑1268) to the reigning monarch; contested successions (e.g., the 1679‑1681 struggle between Gadadhar Singha and his rivals) were resolved by citing the Buranji passage that affirmed the primogeniture rule. Second, the Buranjis functioned as legal precedents: the 1682 khat survey of satra lands ordered by Gadadhar Singha was justified by referencing the Buranji entry that listed the original debottar grants. Third, they provided diplomatic templates — the 1639 treaty text was copied verbatim into later Buranjis to guide negotiations with the Mughal subahdar of Bengal.

The shift from Tai‑Ahom to Assamese in the 17th century reflected the kingdom’s “Assamisation” policy. Scribes translated older entries, inserting Sanskritised vocabulary while preserving the original Tai‑Ahom dating system (lak‑ni cycles). This bilingual layer allows modern historians to cross‑verify dates: the Buranji entry for the 1662 Battle of Saraighat matches the Mughal Waqai‑Navis report within a margin of three days.

Buranjis also recorded the paik levy, the khel artisan guilds, and the satras’ debottar obligations, making them indispensable for reconstructing Ahom fiscal administration. Their preservation in the Gandhia and later in the British‑era Assam Secretariat Records ensures a continuous documentary chain rare among medieval Indian polities.

Mughal Mansabdari Factionalism in the Deccan: Irani, Turani, and Deccani Parties

The Mughal mansabdari corps in the Deccan (1658‑1707) fractured into three principal ethnic‑political factions whose rivalry shaped Aurangzeb’s southern campaigns. The Irani faction comprised Persian‑born nobles (e.g., Mir Jumla, Shaista Khan) who held high mansabs (5,000‑7,000 zat) and controlled the imperial artillery and the Diwan‑i‑Arz in the Deccan. The Turani faction consisted of Central Asian (Chagatai) nobles such as Zulfiqar Khan and Asad Khan, who dominated the cavalry sawar ranks and the faujdari posts of the western Deccan. The Deccani faction — locally recruited Muslims, many of them former Bijapur and Golconda officers — filled the middle mansab tiers (2,000‑4,000 zat) and administered the sarkars of Berar, Khandesh and Aurangabad.

Factional competition manifested in three ways. First, jagir assignments: Irani nobles secured the richest khalisa lands in the Godavari valley, while Turani commanders received the less productive but strategically vital jagirs along the Maratha frontier. Deccani mansabdars were often relegated to jagirs in the arid Telangana plateau, fuelling resentment. Second, promotion bottlenecks: the mir‑bakshi (paymaster) — usually an Irani — delayed sawar increments for Turani officers, prompting the latter to seek independent alliances with Maratha chiefs (e.g., Zulfiqar Khan’s 1689 pact with Rajaram). Third, intelligence control: the akhbarat network in the Deccan was staffed by Deccani waqai‑navis who reported to the Irani‑dominated Diwan‑i‑Insha, creating a feedback loop that distorted the emperor’s picture of Maratha strength.

Aurangzeb attempted to manage the factions by rotating the subahdar of the Deccan — first the Irani prince Muhammad Azam (1681‑1685), then the Turani general Ghaziuddin Khan (1685‑1689), finally the Deccani noble Nizam‑ul‑Mulk (1690‑1707). Each rotation shifted patronage but never eliminated the underlying patronage networks. The 1707 death of Aurangzeb triggered a rapid realignment: the Irani faction backed Azam Shah, the Turani faction supported Bahadur Shah I, and the Deccani faction, led by Nizam‑ul‑Mulk, carved out the autonomous Hyderabad state (1724). The mansabdari factionalism thus accelerated the transition from a unified imperial bureaucracy to a constellation of successor states.

  • Confusing the Delhi Sultanate’s Diwan‑i‑Wizarat with the Mughal Diwan‑i‑Khas; the former was a finance ministry, the latter a private imperial treasury.
  • Treating the Vijayanagara Sthanika as a mere priest without recognising his hereditary land‑holding and judicial powers.
  • Equating the Deccan Sultanates’ Amir rank with the Mughal mansabdar’s zat rank; the Amir’s command was measured in cavalry numbers, not a dual zat‑sawar scale.
  • Assuming Ahom Buranjis were purely religious texts; they were state‑produced legal‑administrative records used in succession and diplomacy.
  • Attributing Mughal Deccan policy solely to Aurangzeb’s personal zeal, ignoring the structural Irani‑Turani‑Deccani factionalism that constrained his options.
  • Misidentifying the Mahamandaleshvara’s devadana grant as a simple tax‑free gift; it carried military, irrigation and artisan obligations.
  • Overlooking the role of the Habshi party in the Deccan Sultanates’ artillery corps, which was distinct from the Mughal Habshi slave‑soldiers in the imperial guard.

These advanced sections deepen the analytical toolkit required for comparative medieval Indian history questions.

The Mughal Imperial Navy: Organization, Shipbuilding, and the Port of Surat

The Mughal state maintained a modest but functional naval establishment primarily to protect the maritime trade that flowed through the Gujarat coast and the Bengal delta. The supreme naval officer was the Mir Bahr (Admiral of the Sea), a mansabdar of 2,000–3,000 zat who reported directly to the emperor through the Diwan‑i‑Arz. Under the Mir Bahr served a hierarchy of nawabs commanding squadrons at Surat, Cambay, Broach, and the Hugli river. Shipbuilding yards (karkhanas‑i‑bahri) were concentrated at Surat, where Mughal carpenters worked alongside Portuguese and Arab shipwrights to produce ghurabs (large ocean‑going vessels of 300–500 tons) and smaller patamar craft for riverine patrol.

Financing came from a dedicated khazana‑i‑bahri fed by a 2.5 % customs surcharge on all sea‑borne imports at Surat, the empire’s busiest port. The 1615 Treaty of Surat with the Portuguese granted the Mughal fleet the right to escort merchant convoys through the Gulf of Cambay in return for a yearly tribute of 10,000 rupees and the cession of Daman and Diu. This arrangement reduced Portuguese piracy but also tied Mughal naval operations to Portuguese goodwill. Under Shah Jahan the fleet reached its peak strength of roughly 120 vessels, capable of mounting 30–40 guns each. Aurangzeb’s Deccan campaigns diverted timber, iron, and skilled labour to land artillery, and the navy atrophied; by 1680 the Surat yard produced fewer than ten new hulls per year.

Operational doctrine emphasized convoy protection rather than power projection. The Barid intelligence network supplied the Mir Bahr with weekly reports on European fleet movements, allowing the Mughal squadron to shadow Portuguese armadas without direct engagement. In Bengal, the nawab of Dhaka maintained a riverine flotilla of 200‑odd koshas (shallow‑draft galleys) to suppress Arakanese raids on the Chittagong coast. The navy’s decline after 1700 left the western seaboard open to Maratha privateers and later to the English East India Company’s squadron, which seized Surat’s customs house in 1717.

The Mughal Imperial Armoury and Artillery Production in the Karkhanas

Artillery formed the backbone of Mughal siege warfare, and its production was centralized in the imperial karkhanas (workshops) attached to the capital and major provincial centres such as Agra, Lahore, and Burhanpur. The head of the armoury was the Mir Atish (Master of Ordnance), a mansabdar of 3,000–5,000 zat who oversaw three specialized branches: cannon casting (top‑khana), small‑arms manufacture (banduq‑khana), and gunpowder preparation (barud‑khana). Akbar’s reforms introduced a standardized calibre series — 6‑pounder, 12‑pounder, 24‑pounder, and the massive 48‑pounder Zamzama — each defined by the weight of its iron shot. The Zamzama, cast in 1605 at Lahore under the supervision of the Portuguese gun‑founder Diogo de Sousa, weighed 14 tons and required a team of 200 oxen for transport.

European expertise was systematically recruited. The firangi (Frankish) gunners, mostly Portuguese, Dutch, and English deserters, held the rank of topchi and received monthly salaries of 30–50 rupees plus a jagir of 200–500 bighas. They introduced the matchlock (toradar) with a serpentine lock, the flintlock (kaman‑i‑sang) in the 1650s, and the technique of cored gunpowder that increased muzzle velocity by roughly 15 %. Indigenous metalworkers adapted these designs, producing the shish‑par (a lightweight swivel gun) for fortification embrasures.

Logistics were managed by the Banjara caravans that moved cannon, shot, and powder from the karkhanas to the Deccan front. A typical siege train for the 1686 Bijapur campaign comprised 120 heavy pieces, 3,000 quintals of gunpowder, and 5,000 round shot, requiring 4,000 pack‑animals and a dedicated faujdar for convoy security. The karkhanas also manufactured grenades (hand‑thrown iron shells filled with gunpowder and shrapnel) used at the 1687 siege of Golconda. By the early 18th century, the decline of central funding and the loss of European masters caused a drop in casting quality; the 1719 inventory of the Delhi arsenal listed 30 % of pieces as unserviceable due to bore erosion.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Which Mughal official was directly responsible for the standardization of artillery calibres under Akbar?" Many candidates answer "Mir Bakhshi" or "Diwan‑i‑Arz". Identify the correct official and explain why the others are incorrect.
The correct answer is Mir Atish (Master of Ordnance). The Mir Atish headed the imperial armoury (top‑khana) and instituted the calibrated series of cannon (6‑, 12‑, 24‑, 48‑pounder) recorded in the Ain‑i‑Akbari. The Mir Bakhshi managed the mansabdari payroll and cavalry inspections, not artillery specifications. The Diwan‑i‑Arz dealt with military correspondence and intelligence, not technical production. Confusing these roles is a common error because all three held high mansabs and appear in the same administrative lists.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links a specific technical reform (calibre standardization, gunpowder corning, matchlock adoption) to an official, first recall the functional hierarchy: Mir Atish = ordnance, Mir Bakhshi = personnel, Diwan‑i‑Arz = intelligence. Write the official’s title in full (Mir Atish) and cite the Ain‑i‑Akbari as the source; this precision earns the full mark.

The Vijayanagara Inscriptions: Language, Script, and Administrative Vocabulary

Vijayanagara’s epigraphic corpus — over 7,000 stone and copper‑plate records — provides the most granular view of south Indian medieval administration. The majority are bilingual: a Sanskrit prashasti (eulogy) in Grantha or Devanagari script followed by a vernacular section in Kannada, Telugu, or Tamil using the respective regional scripts. This dual‑language format served both the Brahminical court elite and the local revenue officials who needed to read land‑grant boundaries, tax exemptions, and village duties.

Administrative terminology is remarkably consistent. The term Rajya denotes a province governed by a Mahanayakacharya or Nayaka; Nadu is a sub‑province comprising several Sthala (localities). Village‑level officers include the Sthanika (hereditary headman with judicial powers), the Kavalgars (watchmen), and the Kulkarni (accountant). Revenue grants use the formula devadana (god’s gift) for temple lands and brahmadeya for Brahmin donees, each carrying obligations such as kudavolai (irrigation maintenance) and vetti (forced labour). The Saka era (starting 78 CE) is the standard chronological anchor, often cross‑checked with the regnal year of the reigning Rayas.

From the mid‑15th century the court increasingly issued tamarashasana (copper‑plate charters) in a hybrid script: Sanskrit verses in Grantha, the operative clauses in Kannada using the Halegannada orthography. This shift reflects the growing influence of the Nayaka families who administered the Tamil‑speaking south (the Madurai and Tanjavur Nayakas) and required documents readable by their Tamil‑literate bureaucracy. The 1565 Battle of Talikota did not halt inscription production; the successor Aravidu rulers continued the practice at Penukonda and Chandragiri, preserving the same administrative lexicon until the final collapse in 1646.

The Mughal Land Grant System: Madad‑i‑Maash, Inam, and Waqf Grants

Beyond the jagir (conditional revenue assignment to mansabdars), the Mughal state employed three major categories of revenue‑free land grants that shaped rural society and fiscal geography. Madad‑i‑maash (literally “aid to livelihood”) were subsistence grants awarded to scholars (ulama), poets, physicians, and descendants of the Prophet (sayyids). Typically 10–50 bighas, they were recorded in the daftar‑i‑madad‑i‑maash and exempt from zabt assessment, but the grantee owed khidmat (service) such as teaching in a madrasa or maintaining a khanqah. Inam grants were larger (100–500 bighas) and given to high officials, military commanders, or local chiefs as rewards; they carried a hereditary right (miras) provided the holder performed stipulated duties (e.g., maintaining a fort, supplying cavalry). Waqf (endowment) lands were dedicated in perpetuity to mosques, tombs, madrasas, or bridges; the mutawalli (trustee) managed the produce, which funded the institution’s upkeep. Waqf deeds (waqfnama) were registered with the qazi and the diwan to prevent alienation.

The fiscal impact was significant. By Aurangzeb’s reign, madad‑i‑maash and inam lands together accounted for an estimated 12–15 % of the cultivated area in the core provinces (Agra, Delhi, Lahore), reducing the khalisa (crown) revenue base. The state attempted to curb proliferation through the farman of 1665, which required imperial sanction for any grant exceeding 20 bighas and mandated periodic tashih (verification) surveys. Nevertheless, local zamindars often secured inam confirmations by presenting forged sanads, leading to disputes recorded in the adalat registers. The distinction between jagir (conditional, transferable, tied to mansab) and inam (hereditary, service‑linked, non‑transferable) is a frequent examination focus; confusion arises because both appear as “revenue‑free” in summary tables.

The Deccan Sultanates' Diplomatic Correspondence with the Ottoman and Safavid Courts

The Deccan sultanates — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, and Bidar — maintained active diplomatic channels with the two great gunpowder empires of the west, the Ottomans and the Safavids, primarily to acquire artillery technology, military advisors, and legitimacy through recognition. The earliest surviving embassy is the 1580 Bijapur mission to Istanbul, led by the Vakil‑i‑Mulk Mustafa Khan, which presented a letter from Sultan Ibrahim Adil Shah II to Sultan Murad III requesting Ottoman master gunners and a copy of the Tophane‑i‑Amire (imperial cannon foundry manual). The Ottomans responded by dispatching a team of topçu (cannon founders) who established a foundry at Bijapur’s Asar Mahal in 1583, producing the 30‑pounder Malik‑i‑Maidan cannon.

Golconda’s engagement with the Safavids intensified after 1620 when Sultan Abdullah Qutb Shah sent a munshi (secretary) bearing a Persian‑language nameh to Shah Abbas I, seeking assistance against Mughal pressure. The Safavid court supplied 200 musketeers trained in the tufangchi corps and a shipment of sulphur and saltpetre from the Khorasan mines. In return, Golconda exported diamonds and textiles through the port of Bandar Abbas. The correspondence was conducted in Persian, the lingua franca of diplomacy, and preserved in the Munshaat‑i‑Qutb Shahi collection. These letters reveal a sophisticated protocol: each embassy carried a khilat (robe of honour), a nazar (gift of precious stones), and a sealed firman confirming the sultan’s tributary status to the respective suzerain.

The diplomatic flow declined after the Mughal annexation of Ahmadnagar (1636), Bijapur (1686), and Golconda (1687). The last recorded mission was a 1690 Golconda envoy to Isfahan pleading for military aid against Aurangzeb’s siege; the Safavid response was limited to a diplomatic protest. Nonetheless, the earlier exchanges left a lasting imprint: Deccani artillery adopted Ottoman casting techniques (the cored barrel method), and Safavid influence appears in the Shirazi style of miniature painting at the Bijapur court. The surviving munshaat collections remain primary sources for reconstructing the trans‑regional military‑technological network of the 16th–17th centuries.

  • Confusing the Mughal Mir Bahr (naval admiral) with the Portuguese Capitão‑mór; the former was a mansabdar, the latter a royal appointee of Lisbon.
  • Attributing the standardization of Mughal artillery calibres to the Mir Bakhshi instead of the Mir Atish.
  • Treating Vijayanagara’s Sthanika as a purely religious functionary, ignoring his hereditary judicial and revenue powers.
  • Equating madad‑i‑maash grants with jagirs; the former are subsistence grants to scholars, the latter are conditional military assignments.
  • Assuming Deccan sultanates’ diplomatic letters were written in Arabic; they were composed in Persian, the chancery language of both Ottoman and Safavid courts.
  • Overlooking the role of the Banjara caravans in moving siege artillery; they were the logistical backbone, not the imperial postal system.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise terminology and contextual awareness when analysing medieval Indian administrative and military institutions.

The Delhi Sultanate's Token Currency Experiment under Muhammad bin Tughlaq: Design, Failure, and Fiscal Aftermath

In 1329 CE Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlaq ordered the minting of a copper‑brass token coin (the tanka) at a nominal value equal to the silver tanka of 11.6 grams. The decree, recorded in the Tarikh‑i‑Firuz Shahi, mandated that the new coins be accepted at par in all imperial treasuries, market dues, and revenue collections across the Sultanate. The sultan’s objective was twofold: to replenish a depleted silver stock after the costly Deccan campaigns and to assert monetary sovereignty by decoupling coinage from bullion supply. The mint at Delhi produced an estimated 2 million pieces in the first year, and provincial mints at Daulatabad, Gujarat, and Bengal were instructed to follow the same standard.

The experiment collapsed within four years. Counterfeiters, exploiting the low intrinsic value of copper, flooded the market with spurious issues; contemporary chronicler Ziauddin Barani notes that “the bazaar became a sea of false coin”. Merchants refused the token at face value, hoarding silver and driving up its premium to 300 percent. Revenue officials, unable to collect the assessed kharaj in token, reported massive shortfalls; the diwan‑i‑wizarat recorded a 40 percent drop in cash receipts for 1331‑32. In 1333 the sultan issued a farman withdrawing the token, ordering its redemption at a 1:10 ratio against silver, and re‑establishing the silver tanka as sole legal tender. The redemption cost the treasury an estimated 1.2 million silver tankas, deepening the fiscal crisis that later prompted the controversial transfer of the capital to Daulatabad.

Scholars attribute the failure to three structural flaws: (1) absence of a credible backing mechanism — no state reserve of silver was pledged; (2) inadequate mint control — provincial mints operated with minimal oversight, allowing local officials to debase further; (3) lack of public trust — the sultan’s simultaneous experiments (e.g., the forced migration to Daulatabad) eroded confidence in any imperial monetary innovation. The episode is a textbook case of fiat money collapse in a pre‑modern agrarian economy and is frequently contrasted with the later Mughal rupee standard, which succeeded because it maintained a fixed silver weight, a centralized mint network, and a credible redemption promise.

Vijayanagara's Merchant Guilds: The Nakharas, the Chettis, and Long‑Distance Trade Networks

The Vijayanagara empire (1336‑1646) hosted a layered commercial architecture in which merchant guilds — collectively termed nakharas — operated alongside caste‑based trading communities such as the Chettis (also spelled Chettiyars) and the Komati merchants. Inscriptions from the Virupaksha and Hazara Rama temples record guild seals bearing the legend “Nakhara‑Sresthi” (head of the guild) and list privileges: exemption from certain sunkas (market tolls), the right to maintain warehouses (kottams) in major ports, and the authority to arbitrate commercial disputes through a guild court (nakhara‑nyaya). The most prominent guilds were the Gandhavana Nakhara (spice traders), the Karpura Nakhara (camphor and aromatic goods), and the Ashva Nakhara (horse importers from the Persian Gulf).

Long‑distance trade flowed through three maritime corridors: the Malabar coast (Calicut, Cannanore) linking to the Red Sea and Egypt; the Coromandel coast (Pulicat, Nagapattinam) connecting to Southeast Asian entrepôts such as Malacca and Aceh; and the western Deccan ports (Goa, Chaul) serving the Persian Gulf caravan routes. Guilds financed voyages through hundi (bill of exchange) networks that extended to Cairo and Hormuz; the Chetti merchants of Kanchipuram maintained correspondent houses in Malacca, evidenced by the 1512 Malacca Chetti Letter preserved in the Portuguese Arquivo Nacional. The state extracted revenue via a 2‑percent sulka on export cargo and a 5‑percent kattu on import cargo, collected at the mandapika (customs house) under the supervision of the Mahapradhana (chief minister). In return, the crown granted guilds agrahara villages whose produce supported guild temples and charitable feeding houses (annadanam).

Guild autonomy was not absolute. The Rayavachakamu (royal edicts) of Krishnadevaraya (r. 1509‑1529) show the emperor intervening to fix the price of pepper at 12 fanams per khandi during a famine, and to appoint a Nakhara‑Pradhani (guild overseer) to audit accounts. The 1565 Battle of Talikota disrupted the western corridor; Portuguese records note a 60 percent decline in horse imports, forcing the Ashva Nakhara to shift to overland routes via Bijapur. By the early 17th century, the rise of European chartered companies (VOC, EIC) eroded guild monopolies; the Dutch captured Pulicat (1608) and imposed a pass system that bypassed guild warehouses. Nevertheless, the guild structure persisted in the interior, and the Chetti network continued to finance the Nayaka polities until the Mughal absorption of the Deccan in the 1680s.

Comparative Analysis of Guild Autonomy vs State Control
Question: Explain how the Vijayanagara state balanced the autonomy of merchant guilds (nakharas) with its own fiscal and political interests, citing two specific mechanisms.
Answer: The state granted guilds institutional autonomy through guild courts (nakhara‑nyaya) and toll exemptions, allowing self‑regulation of trade disputes and lowering transaction costs. Simultaneously, it asserted control by (1) appointing a royal overseer (Nakhara‑Pradhani) to audit guild accounts, as seen in Krishnadevaraya’s 1515 edict, and (2) levying fixed customs duties (sulka and kattu) collected at state‑run mandapikas, ensuring a predictable revenue stream irrespective of guild internal decisions. This dual structure let the empire harness guild efficiency while safeguarding fiscal sovereignty.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions “merchant guilds” in medieval South India, instantly map the term to nakharas, Chettis, and Komatis; note the three maritime corridors (Malabar, Coromandel, Western Deccan) and the two state levers — guild overseers and fixed customs duties. This framework covers both economic and political angles in a single answer.

The Mughal Riverine Navy: The Mir Bahr, the Warboats of Bengal, and the Control of the Ganga‑Brahmaputra Delta

The Mughal empire maintained a distinct riverine naval establishment separate from the ocean‑going fleet. The senior officer was the Mir Bahr (Admiral of the Waters), a mansabdar of 2,000‑5,000 zat who reported directly to the Mir Bakhshi. The core of the riverine force lay in Bengal Subah, where the Nawab of Dhaka oversaw a shipyard at Narayanganj producing two principal vessel types: the ghurab (a shallow‑draft galley of 30‑40 metres, armed with 8‑12 swivel guns) and the gallivat (a smaller, oar‑propelled craft of 15‑20 metres, used for patrol and troop transport). Contemporary Ain‑i‑Akbari entries list 1,200 ghurabs and 3,500 gallivats stationed along the Ganga, Brahmaputra, and Meghna channels in 1595.

Operational doctrine emphasized control of the delta’s distributary network to secure revenue transport (the malguzari grain flotillas) and to project power against Arakanese raids, Portuguese privateers based at Chittagong, and the Ahom kingdom upstream. The 1612 campaign of Islam Khan Chishti against the Arakanese employed a combined land‑river assault: 200 ghurabs blockaded the Kaladan River while 5,000 infantry crossed the Meghna on gallivats. The Mughal victory at Mrauk‑U (1612) forced the Arakanese to cede the port of Chittagong, which was then garrisoned by a Mughal faujdar with a permanent riverine squadron.

Logistics relied on the Banjara caravans for overland supply of timber, iron, and gunpowder to the Narayanganj yard, while the Barid (imperial post) relayed intelligence on pirate movements. The Mir Bahr maintained a register (daftar‑i‑bahr) of vessel strength, crew rolls, and ammunition stocks, audited annually by the Diwan‑i‑Bengal. By the 1660s, the riverine navy declined: the loss of Bengal’s shipbuilding timber to Mughal‑Maratha conflicts, the rise of European armed merchantmen that outgunned ghurabs, and the diversion of funds to the Deccan campaigns reduced the fleet to under 300 serviceable vessels. The last major riverine operation was the 1666 expedition of Shaista Khan against the Ahom fort at Guwahati, which ended in a stalemate and marked the effective end of Mughal riverine dominance in the northeast.

The Deccan Sultanates' Dakhni Literary Culture: Language, Patronage, and the Bijapur‑Golconda Poetic Schools

Dakhni (also spelled Deccani) emerged as a literary register of Hindavi written in the Perso‑Arabic script, flourishing under the Bahmani successor states — principally Bijapur (Adil Shahi) and Golconda (Qutb Shahi) — from the late 15th to the late 17th century. Sultan Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) of Bijapur, a noted patron of music and poetry, established the Ibrahim Rauza library housing over 3,000 manuscripts, many in Dakhni. His court poet Wajhi composed the Sabras (1635), a masnavi blending Sufi allegory with local folklore, while Ghawwasi authored the Qissa‑e‑Yusuf‑Zulaikha, a romantic epic that circulated widely in the Deccan and later influenced Urdu poets in Delhi.

At Golconda, Sultan Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (r. 1580‑1611) founded the city of Hyderabad (1591) and commissioned the Kulliyāt‑e‑Muhammad Quli, a divan of ghazals, qasidas, and rubais in Dakhni. The poet‑historian Firishta records that the sultan held weekly mushairas (poetic symposiums) where Dakhni verses were recited alongside Persian classics. The Golconda school favored the ghazal form with a distinct radif (refrain) drawn from Telugu folk meters, whereas the Bijapur school excelled in the masnavi narrative mode, often adapting Sanskrit puranic themes — e.g., the Padmavat rendered in Dakhni by Mulla Wajhi (c. 1600).

Manuscript production was institutionalised through royal kitabkhanas (scriptoria) staffed by calligraphers (katibs), illuminators (musawwirs), and binders. The Bijapur Kitabkhana employed the master calligrapher Abdul Rashid, whose naskh‑style Dakhni copies of the Quran and the Yusuf‑Zulaikha survive in the British Library (Or. 12345). Patronage extended to Sufi shrines: the dargah of Gesu Daraz at Gulbarga received endowments for Dakhni recitations of the Masaib‑ul‑Awliya. By the 1680s, Mughal annexation of Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687) dispersed the courtly networks; many poets migrated to the Mughal Deccan or to the emerging Hyderabad Asaf Jahi court, where Dakhni evolved into early Urdu (Rekhta). The continuity of Dakhni prosodic conventions — the beher (meter) based on the mutaqarib pattern — can be traced in the 18th‑century works of Wali Deccani, confirming the Deccan sultanates’ pivotal role in shaping the literary lineage of Urdu.

The Ahom Kingdom's Land Revenue and the Paik‑Gohain Hierarchy: From Service Tenure to Hereditary Rights

The Ahom state (1228‑1826) organized its agrarian economy around the paik system, a corvée labour obligation that doubled as the basis for land tenure and military mobilisation. Every able‑bodied male between 15 and 50 years was registered as a paik in the buranji (state chronicle) and assigned to a khel (guild‑like unit) of 20‑30 paiks under a bora (overseer). In return for labour — constructing embankments, clearing jungle, serving in the royal army — the paik received a khats (land grant) of 2‑3 puras (≈ 0.5 hectare) cultivated tax‑free. The Gohain (nobles) — Borphukan, Borbarua, Borgohain — held larger khats (up to 50 puras) and the right to collect a portion of the produce from subordinate paiks as paik‑revenue (paik‑kar).

Revenue assessment was conducted through the paik‑survey (paik‑mela) every three years, recorded in the Buranji‑Puthi. The survey measured cultivated area using the bigha (≈ 0.13 hectare) and fixed the state’s share at one‑sixth of the gross yield (shash‑bhag), collected in kind (rice, mustard, cotton). The Borphukan, as commander of the western frontier, also supervised the khar (granary) network that stored surplus for famine relief and campaign logistics. During the reign of Rudra Singha (1696‑1714), a reform converted many paik‑khats into hereditary jagir‑like holdings (mohuri) for loyal nobles, reducing the direct corvée pool but increasing cash revenue through a 10 percent malguzari on the new holdings.

The Mughal‑Ahom wars (1615‑1682) strained the system. The 1662 Treaty of Ghilajharighat forced the Ahoms to cede the Koch territory and pay an annual tribute of 20,000 rupees and 100 elephants, paid partly through commuted paik labour. In response, King Chakradhwaj Singha (1663‑1670) expanded the paik register by 30 percent, incorporating captured Mughal peasants as paik‑prisoners. By the early 18th century, the paik obligation had become increasingly monetised; many paiks paid a cash commutation (paik‑tanka) instead of labour, a trend accelerated by the British revenue settlement of 1826 which abolished the corvée and converted all khats into ryotwari tenures. The transition from service tenure to hereditary property rights illustrates a classic trajectory of state‑controlled labour evolving into a landed aristocracy, a pattern comparable to the Mughal mansabdari‑jagir evolution but rooted in a distinct tribal‑military organisation.

  • Confusing the Delhi Sultanate’s token copper coin with the later Mughal copper dam; the former was a forced fiat issue, the latter a fractional silver‑based denomination.
  • Attributing the Vijayanagara guild court (nakhara‑nyaya) to the Mughal qazi system; they operated under distinct legal traditions.
  • Assuming the Mughal Mir Bahr commanded ocean‑going ships; his jurisdiction was limited to riverine fleets in Bengal and the Indus delta.
  • Treating Dakhni as merely a dialect of Urdu; it was a fully developed literary language with its own scriptoria and poetic conventions before the 18th‑century Urdu synthesis.
  • Equating the Ahom paik corvée with the Mughal zamindari revenue demand; the paik was a personal service obligation tied to military duty, not a land‑tax lease.
  • Overlooking the role of the Banjara caravans in supplying the Mughal riverine shipyards; they were the primary logistical link for timber and iron.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise terminology when analysing medieval Indian institutions.

The Mughal Imperial Observatory and the Patronage of Astronomy under Jahangir and Shah Jahan

The Mughal court maintained a formal astronomical establishment from the reign of Akbar, but it reached its most systematic form under Jahangir (1605‑1627) and Shah Jahan (1628‑1658). Jahangir appointed Mulla Mahmud of Jaunpur as the chief astronomer (munajjim‑i‑sultan) and tasked him with compiling a new set of planetary tables, the Zij‑i‑Jahangiri, completed around 1610. The work drew on the Zij‑i‑Sultani of Ulugh Beg, the Zij‑i‑Ilkhani of Nasir al‑Din al‑Tusi, and contemporary Ottoman tables, adapting them to the Indian meridian of Delhi (28° 39′ N). The observatory itself was housed in the Jantar Mantar‑style complex within the Red Fort, equipped with a large brass astrolabe, a mural quadrant of 2.5 m radius, and an armillary sphere calibrated to the Ilahi calendar introduced by Akbar.

Jahangir’s memoirs, the Tuzuk‑i‑Jahangiri, record personal observations of the 1618 comet (C/1618 W1) and the solar eclipse of 1620, noting the exact ghati and pal of contact. These observations were used to correct the Zij’s lunar and solar equations, reducing the accumulated error in the Ilahi year to less than three minutes. Shah Jahan continued the patronage, commissioning a revised edition, the Zij‑i‑Shahjahani (c. 1645), which incorporated the latest European data transmitted via the Portuguese factory at Surat, notably the Tycho Brahe star catalogue. The imperial library (kitabkhana) at Agra held copies of the Almagest, the Handy Tables of Ptolemy, and the Zij‑i‑Ulugh Beg in Persian translation, allowing the court astronomers to cross‑check planetary positions.

The practical output of the observatory was the annual farman fixing the dates of the Nauroz, the Eid festivals, and the agricultural kharif and rabi sowing calendars. The munajjim also advised the Mir Bakhshi on auspicious moments for military campaigns, a practice documented in the Akbarnama and continued in the Padshahnama. By the 1650s the observatory’s staff included Hindu mathematicians such as Kamalakara Bhatta, who contributed the Siddhanta‑siromani corrections for the precession of equinoxes. The integration of Indo‑Islamic and European astronomical knowledge exemplifies the Mughal state’s use of science for both ritual legitimacy and administrative precision.

The Maratha Administration of the Konkan Coast: Port Revenue, Customs, and the Deshmukh Network

After Shivaji’s capture of the coastal forts of Vijaydurg (1653), Sindhudurg (1664) and the port of Rajapur (1660), the Marathas established a distinct maritime revenue regime along the Konkan littoral stretching from Goa to Daman. The core fiscal instrument was the mahsul, a customs duty levied at a uniform rate of 2.5 percent of the declared cargo value, collected at designated bandar (customs houses) in Rajapur, Dabhol, and Malvan. Each bandar was headed by a Bandar Adhikari appointed by the Peshwa on the recommendation of the local Deshmukh, a hereditary revenue magnate who controlled a cluster of villages (mauza) and the adjacent hinterland.

The Deshmukh’s responsibilities extended beyond land revenue; he maintained the kulkarni (village accountant) registers for maritime trade, issued dastak (passes) to merchant vessels certifying payment of mahsul, and forwarded a quarterly abstract to the Huzur Daftar at Satara. The Huzur Daftar cross‑checked these abstracts against the Saranjam grants awarded to naval commanders such as Kanhoji Angre, who received a saranjam of 12 lakhs rupees in 1713 for protecting the coast from Portuguese and Siddi incursions. In return, Angre’s fleet escorted merchant convoys, and a portion of the mahsul (approximately one‑third) was earmarked for naval upkeep.

Revenue records from the 1680s show that the Konkan ports handled an average of 3,200 tons of pepper, 1,800 tons of cotton textiles, and 450 tons of indigo annually, generating an estimated 1.2 crore rupees in mahsul. The Maratha state also levied a chauth of 25 percent on the gross value of goods shipped from Portuguese Goa, collected through the Deshmukh of Pernem. The system’s efficiency rested on the Deshmukh’s local knowledge of merchant networks and the Bandar Adhikari’s direct access to the Huzur Daftar’s ledger, minimizing leakage. By the early 18th century, the Konkan customs administration had become a model for the later British Sea Customs regulations at Bombay.

Technique: Comparative Revenue Analysis
Compare the Maratha Konkan customs system (mahsul, Deshmukh, Bandar Adhikari) with the Mughal port customs at Surat under the Mir Bahr. Identify two structural similarities and two functional differences.
Similarities: (1) Both regimes levied a fixed ad‑valorem duty (Mughal mahsul at Surat was 2.5 percent, identical to the Maratha rate). (2) Both employed a hereditary local collector (Mughal mutasaddi or shahbandar; Maratha Deshmukh) who reported to a central treasury. Differences: (1) The Mughal Mir Bahr’s jurisdiction was limited to riverine and coastal patrol, whereas the Maratha Bandar Adhikari operated under a naval commander (Angre) who also received a share of the duty. (2) The Maratha system integrated the dastak pass directly into the Huzur Daftar accounting, while the Mughal Surat customs relied on the diwan‑i‑bahr ledger maintained separately from the imperial diwan‑i‑kul.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a comparison of fiscal institutions, first list the institutional actors (collector, auditor, military protector) and the flow of revenue (rate, base, sharing). Then map each actor to its counterpart in the other polity. This matrix approach prevents missing a component and makes the answer structure visible to the examiner.

The Role of Eunuchs in the Mughal Imperial Household: Administrative Functions and Political Influence

Eunuchs (khwajasara) formed a distinct administrative cadre within the Mughal imperial household from Akbar’s reign onward. Their primary duty was the guardianship of the harem (mahal), but they rapidly assumed fiscal and logistical responsibilities. Under Akbar, the chief eunuch Itimad Khan supervised the khazana (imperial treasury) and the karkhanas (royal workshops), ensuring that the daily expenditure on textiles, arms, and foodstuffs matched the diwan‑i‑khas allocations. Jahangir elevated Khwaja Abid to the rank of mansabdar (2,000 zat) and entrusted him with the diwan‑i‑buyutat (department of household stores), a post that gave him direct access to the emperor’s private seal.

During the war of succession (1657‑1658), the eunuch Fidai Khan, head of the mahal under Aurangzeb, controlled the flow of intelligence between the palace and the field armies. He intercepted correspondence of rival princes, notably Dara Shikoh’s letters to the Sadr‑us‑Sudur, and facilitated Aurangzeb’s swift occupation of Agra Fort. After coronation, Aurangzeb appointed Fidai Khan as Mir‑i‑Atish (master of artillery), a rare military command for a eunuch, illustrating the trust placed in the household corps. The eunuchs also managed the langar (royal kitchen) and the bawarchi (chief cook), overseeing a staff of over 1,200 persons and a daily grain ration of 3,500 maunds.

Their political influence waned after Aurangzeb’s death (1707) as the later emperors relied more on the wazir and the mir bakhshi. Nevertheless, the eunuch Khwaja Sabir played a decisive role in the 1719 accession of Rafi‑ud‑Darajat by securing the harem’s loyalty and controlling the khazana keys. The eunuch network thus functioned as a parallel bureaucratic channel, bypassing the regular diwan hierarchy, and its members often held mansabs and jagirs comparable to mid‑level nobles.

The Mughal Imperial Court's Allocation of Jagirs to Cultural Figures: Poets, Painters, and Musicians

The Mughal practice of granting revenue‑free land (madad‑i‑ma'ash) or suyurghal to men of letters and art began systematically under Akbar and continued through Shah Jahan. Akbar awarded the poet Faizi a madad‑i‑ma'ash of 500 bighas in the sarkar of Delhi (c. 1585), and the historian Abul Fazl received a jagir of 1,200 bighas in the subah of Lahore (1590). These grants were recorded in the diwan‑i‑insha and carried the imperial seal (muhr) of the padshah. The painter Basawan was given a mansab of 500 zat and a jagir in the pargana of Gwalior (1595), while the musician Tansen held a mansab of 1,000 zat with a jagir in the sarkar of Agra (1580).

Jahangir continued the policy, granting the Persian poet Muhammad Husain Naziri a suyurghal of 300 bighas in the subah of Allahabad (1612) and the painter Abu'l Hasan a mansab of 700 zat with a jagir in the pargana of Kashmir (1620). Shah Jahan’s reign saw the most elaborate cultural patronage: the calligrapher Abdul Rashid received a madad‑i‑ma'ash of 400 bighas in the sarkar of Delhi (1635), and the court musician Lal Khan was awarded a jagir of 600 bighas in the subah of Multan (1648). These grants were typically hereditary for one generation, after which the diwan‑i‑kul could resume the land unless renewed by a new farman.

Fiscally, the allocation of such jagirs reduced the khalisa (crown) revenue by an estimated 2‑3 percent annually, but the court regarded the expenditure as an investment in sulh‑i‑kul (universal peace) and the projection of imperial cultural authority. The diwan‑i‑insha maintained a separate register (daftar‑i‑suyurghal) to track these grants, and the mir bakhshi verified the mansab rank before the farman was issued. The practice illustrates how the Mughal state integrated cultural production into its bureaucratic machinery, using land revenue as a currency of patronage.

  • Confusing the Maratha mahsul (a uniform 2.5 % ad‑valorem duty) with the Mughal mahsul at Surat, which varied by commodity and was administered by the mutasaddi rather than a hereditary Deshmukh.
  • Attributing the Mughal observatory’s instruments solely to Islamic tradition; the Zij‑i‑Shahjahani incorporated Tycho Brahe’s European star catalogue transmitted via the Portuguese factory at Surat.
  • Assuming all Mughal eunuchs were confined to harem duties; Fidai Khan held the military post of Mir‑i‑Atish and Khwaja Sabir controlled the khazana keys during a succession crisis.
  • Treating the madad‑i‑ma'ash grants to poets as permanent hereditary estates; they were usually limited to one generation and required renewal by a fresh farman.
  • Equating the Maratha Deshmukh with the Mughal zamindar; the Deshmukh exercised both revenue collection and customs oversight at coastal bandars, a dual role absent in the Mughal inland zamindari.
  • Overlooking the role of the Huzur Daftar in auditing Konkan customs; the Maratha central ledger cross‑checked quarterly abstracts from each Bandar Adhikari, a procedure more systematic than the Mughal diwan‑i‑bahr at Surat.
  • Believing the Mughal imperial observatory ceased after Shah Jahan; the munajjim office persisted into the early 18th century, supplying the Ilahi calendar to the court until the 1730s.

The advanced sections above illustrate how fiscal, scientific, household, and cultural institutions intertwined across the Sultanate, Mughal, Maratha, and regional polities.

The Mughal Dual Diwan System: Diwan‑i‑Khalsa and Diwan‑i‑Jagir in Fiscal Management

The Mughal fiscal apparatus rested on a bifurcated diwan structure that separated crown‑land revenue (khalisa) from jagir‑assigned revenue. The Diwan‑i‑Khalsa administered the khalisa territories, collecting land tax directly into the imperial treasury (khazana) through a hierarchy of amalguzar (revenue collectors), qanungo (record‑keepers) and chaudhari (village headmen). Its accounts were audited annually by the mir bakhshi and cross‑checked by the waqai navis news‑writers. The Diwan‑i‑Jagir oversaw the revenue assignments granted to mansabdars; each jagirdar appointed his own amil and faujdar to realise the assessed produce, but the central diwan‑i‑kul retained the right to audit jagir accounts at the end of each fiscal year. Under Akbar the khalisa constituted roughly one‑fifth of total assessed revenue (Ain‑i‑Akbari, Book II), a proportion that rose to nearly one‑third during Shah Jahan’s reign as the emperor expanded direct control over the Deccan. Aurangzeb’s prolonged Deccan campaigns reversed the trend; by 1700 the khalisa share had fallen to about one‑tenth, forcing the state to rely increasingly on ijara (revenue farming) to meet cash shortfalls. The dual system permitted the centre to fund the imperial household, the standing artillery corps and large‑scale public works from khalisa receipts while delegating military maintenance to mansabdars through jagir income. Its breakdown under fiscal pressure is a key explanatory factor for the erosion of central authority in the early eighteenth century.

Maratha Confederal Military Command: The Sarnobat, Senapati, and the Peshwa’s Standing Army (Huzurat)

The Maratha confederacy operated a layered command structure that blended hereditary sardar contingents with a small standing force directly loyal to the Peshwa. The Sarnobat (commander‑in‑chief) was the senior field officer for joint operations; Shivaji appointed the first Sarnobat, and the post was revived for major expeditions such as the 1761 Panipat campaign, where Sadashivrao Bhau held the title. The Senapati functioned as chief of cavalry, supervising the bargir (state‑provided horses) and the silladar (self‑equipped horsemen) supplied by individual sardars. Distinct from these confederal posts, the Peshwa maintained the Huzurat (also called Huzuri troops), a standing army of roughly 25,000–30,000 men in the 1750s, financed from the Peshwa’s personal saranjam grants in the Konkan and the Deccan. The Huzurat comprised infantry battalions trained in European drill, a dedicated artillery park under the Mir‑i‑Atish, and a cavalry reserve directly commanded by the Peshwa’s Sarnobat‑e‑Huzurat. This force enabled the Peshwa to act independently of the great sardars (Holkar, Scindia, Bhonsle) during the 1740s‑1750s, but after the disaster at Panipat the Huzurat’s strength dwindled and the Sarnobat became largely ceremonial. The coexistence of a confederal command (Sarnobat/Senapati) and a personal standing army (Huzurat) illustrates the Maratha blend of feudal levy and centralized military entrepreneurship.

Technique
Explain how the Mughal dual diwan system allowed the emperor to finance the imperial artillery corps while delegating cavalry maintenance to mansabdars.
The Diwan‑i‑Khalsa collected revenue from khalisa lands directly into the khazana; these cash receipts funded the imperial household, the artillery corps (Mir‑i‑Atish) and public works. The Diwan‑i‑Jagir assigned revenue‑bearing territories to mansabdars, who were obliged to maintain a stipulated number of cavalry (sawar) from the jagir’s produce. Thus the centre retained cash for capital‑intensive artillery, while the recurring expense of cavalry upkeep was devolved to jagirdars through the jagir mechanism.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a fiscal‑military linkage, identify the revenue stream (khalisa vs jagir) and the corresponding military branch (artillery vs cavalry). Use the dual diwan terminology to show structural awareness.

Vijayanagara Provincial Governance: The Role of the Uttara and Dakshina Divisions in Revenue Collection

Vijayanagara’s vast territory was organised into two macro‑divisions, Uttara (northern) and Dakshina (southern), each headed by a divisional governor titled Uttara Nayaka and Dakshina Nayaka respectively. The Uttara division encompassed the Telugu‑speaking coastal Andhra and the interior Rayalaseema districts, while the Dakshina division covered the Tamil‑Kannada zones of the Kaveri delta and the Mysore plateau. Each division contained several Rajya (provinces) administered by Nayakas who held military‑fiscal obligations under the Kandachara system. Revenue collection was coordinated by divisional Karyakartas who maintained separate registers — Uttara Kacheri and Dakshina Kacheri — recording assessed produce, kudimai (land tax) and vari (customs) receipts. The divisional officers reported to the central Rayasam (secretariat) in the capital, enabling the emperor to monitor agrarian output across distinct ecological zones. After the 1520s loss of the Raichur Doab, the Uttara division’s revenue base contracted, prompting a re‑allocation of amaram grants to loyal Nayakas in the Dakshina division. This bifurcated structure facilitated targeted fiscal interventions, such as the 1540s irrigation expansion in the Kaveri delta, which was planned and financed through the Dakshina Kacheri’s surplus.

Deccan Sultanate Mansabdari Adaptations: The Bijapur and Golconda Mansab Structures Compared to the Mughal Model

Both the Bijapur (Adil Shahi) and Golconda (Qutb Shahi) sultanates adopted a mansab‑like ranking system, yet diverged from the Mughal template in three critical respects. First, the Zat–Sawar duality existed but the zat (personal rank) in Bijapur was calibrated on a cash salary scale (tankas) rather than on jagir revenue; the highest mansabdars received up to 7,000 tankas monthly, while the Mughal equivalent would have been a jagir yielding comparable produce. Second, the Deccan mansabdars were predominantly paid in cash from the sultan’s sarf (cash treasury), reducing the need for extensive jagir grants; only a minority held mokasa (revenue‑free land) as supplementary income. Third, the Deccan systems incorporated a high proportion of Habshi (Abyssinian) and Deccani commanders who received mansabs without hereditary claims, reflecting a more meritocratic military labour market. Under Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580‑1627) Bijapur maintained roughly 1,500 mansabdars, while Abdullah Qutb Shah’s Golconda roster numbered about 1,200. Both sultanates required mansabdars to furnish a fixed quota of cavalry for the sultan’s campaigns, but the obligation was enforced through periodic muster (dastur) rather than the Mughal dagh (branding) system. The cash‑based mansabdari gave the Deccan rulers tighter fiscal control over their military elite, a factor that prolonged their resistance against Mughal annexation until the late 1680s.

Ahom Administrative Hierarchy: The Borbarua, Borphukan, and the Khel System in State Formation

The Ahom kingdom’s administrative architecture rested on a dual apex: the Borbarua (chief civil minister) and the Borphukan (commander of the western frontier on the Brahmaputra). The Borbarua supervised revenue assessment, judicial administration and the royal household, while the Borphukan commanded the riverine navy and the land forces defending the strategic Guwahati corridor. Below them operated a tier of Barua (ministers) and Phukan (military governors), each presiding over a Khel — a territorial‑administrative unit that simultaneously functioned as a revenue circle and a labour‑military levy district. Each Khel comprised a fixed number of Paiks (conscripted adult males) who owed both agricultural labour (wet‑rice cultivation, embankment repair) and military service (infantry, boat‑crew).
