The Khel officer (Khel Phukan) maintained the Paik register, collected the annual peshkash (tribute in kind) and mobilised the Paiks for state projects or campaigns. The Borbarua and Borphukan were drawn from the hereditary Gohain nobility and appointed by the Swargadeo (king), ensuring loyalty while preserving a balance between civil and military authority. This integrated Khel‑Paik framework enabled the Ahoms to sustain a standing defence against Mughal incursions (1615‑1682) and to undertake large‑scale hydraulic works such as the Joysagar tank, all without a monetised bureaucracy.

  • Confusing the Mughal Diwan‑i‑Khalsa with the Diwan‑i‑Jagir; the former handled crown‑land cash revenue, the latter oversaw jagir assignments.
  • Assuming the Maratha Sarnobat commanded the Huzurat; the Huzurat was a separate standing army under the Peshwa’s direct control.
  • Treating Vijayanagara’s Uttara and Dakshina divisions as mere geographic labels; they possessed distinct revenue registers (Uttara Kacheri, Dakshina Kacheri) and divisional governors.
  • Equating Deccan mansabdari with the Mughal system; Deccan mansabdars were largely cash‑paid and included a high proportion of Habshi officers.
  • Overlooking the dual civil‑military role of the Ahom Khel; each Khel was both a revenue unit and a Paik levy district.
  • Believing the Borphukan’s authority extended over the entire kingdom; his command was limited to the western frontier and riverine forces.
  • The dual diwan, confederal command, divisional revenue registers, cash‑based mansabdari, and Khel‑Paik integration together illustrate how medieval Indian states balanced central fiscal control with delegated military administration.

Delhi Sultanate Central Administration: The Diwan‑i‑Wizarat, Diwan‑i‑Arz, Diwan‑i‑Insha, and the Naib Sultan

The Sultan stood at the apex of the Delhi Sultanate’s political hierarchy, but day‑to‑day governance was mediated through a cluster of specialised diwans that mirrored the Persianate bureaucratic model. The Diwan‑i‑Wizarat, headed by the Wazir, functioned as the finance ministry; it supervised revenue assessment (mustakhraj), expenditure (khazana), and the audit of provincial accounts through the Mushrif‑i‑Mamalik and the Mustaufi. The Diwan‑i‑Arz, under the Ariz‑i‑Mamalik, managed military recruitment, the branding of horses (dagh), and the disbursement of salaries to the hasham (standing army). The Diwan‑i‑Insha, led by the Dabir‑i‑Khas, drafted royal farmans, correspondence with subordinate governors, and diplomatic epistles; its scribes maintained the barid (postal‑intelligence) registers. A fourth department, the Diwan‑i‑Rasalat, handled foreign envoys and the protocol of the Sadr‑us‑Sudur for ecclesiastical affairs. The Naib Sultan (deputy sultan) acted as the Sultan’s alter ego during minorities or prolonged campaigns; he could countersign farmans and command the capital’s garrison. Alauddin Khalji strengthened the Wazir’s control by creating a separate Diwan‑i‑Mustakhraj for revenue extraction, while Muhammad bin Tughluq expanded the Diwan‑i‑Arz to accommodate his enlarged cavalry. The Qazi‑ul‑Quzat presided over the judicial hierarchy, but his appointments required the Wazir’s concurrence, illustrating the interlocking nature of fiscal, military, and legal authority.

Provincial administration replicated this central template: each iqta holder (muqti) maintained a miniature diwan staffed by a amil (revenue collector), a faujdar (military commander), and a qazi. The Barid‑i‑Mamalik operated a network of news‑writers (waqai‑navis) who reported directly to the Sultan, bypassing the Wazir on sensitive matters. This dual reporting line — fiscal through the Wazir, intelligence through the Barid — was a deliberate check on ministerial overreach. The system’s resilience lay in its modularity; when a Sultan weakened, the diwans could continue functioning under a regent Naib, as seen during the early reign of Firuz Shah Tughluq.

Delhi Sultanate Military Organization: The Turkic Slave Corps, the Iqta Cavalry, and the Standing Army under the Khaljis and Tughluqs

The Sultanate’s military power rested on three interlocking pillars. The Turkic slave corps (Ghulams) formed the Sultan’s personal guard (khassakhel) and the core of the central cavalry. Recruited as adolescents from Central Asian markets, they were trained in archery, lance work, and horsemanship in the sipah‑khana (royal stables). Their loyalty was cemented by manumission after a fixed term and the grant of iqta revenue assignments, creating a self‑replenishing elite. The Iqta cavalry comprised muqti‑holders who furnished a quota of horsemen proportional to the assessed revenue of their iqta; the dagh (branding) and chehra (muster roll) inspections ensured compliance. The Standing army (hasham) was a salaried force maintained directly from the khazana, distinct from the feudal levy; it included infantry, elephant corps (pilkhana), and artillery units introduced under the Khaljis. Alauddin Khalji’s market reforms (fixed grain prices, regulated transport) guaranteed cheap provisions for the hasham, while his Diwan‑i‑Arz expanded the branding system to cover all cavalry, reducing fraud. Muhammad bin Tughluq further professionalised the hasham by introducing cash salaries (tankas) for a segment of the infantry, but his Deccan campaigns strained the treasury, forcing a reversion to iqta‑based levies. The Amir‑i‑Hajib coordinated court ceremonial and the deployment of the khassakhel, while the Sar‑i‑Jandar commanded the Sultan’s bodyguard. Elephants, housed in the pilkhana, served both as shock troops and as mobile command platforms; their upkeep was a major budget item, financed through a dedicated diwan‑i‑pil under the Tughluqs.

Comparative Mechanism
Explain how the Delhi Sultanate’s Ghulam‑iqta‑hasham triad differed from the Mughal mansabdari‑jagir system in terms of recruitment, revenue linkage, and central control.
The Sultanate’s Ghulams were slave‑recruited, manumitted, and then granted iqta revenue; their loyalty was personal to the Sultan. Iqta holders supplied cavalry proportionate to land revenue, but the link was indirect — revenue was collected by the muqti, who then forwarded a portion to the centre. The hasham was a cash‑paid standing army, directly funded from the khazana, giving the Sultan immediate command. In contrast, the Mughal mansabdar received a rank (zat/sawar) that entitled him to a jagir whose revenue was assigned, not collected, by the state; the mansabdar’s troops were maintained from jagir proceeds, and the centre exercised control through the dagh‑chehra muster and periodic transfers. Thus, the Sultanate combined personal slave loyalty, feudal levy, and a central cash army, whereas the Mughals relied on a unified rank‑jagir framework with bureaucratic oversight.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “administrative innovations” of a specific Sultan, map each innovation to the diwan it affected (e.g., Alauddin’s Diwan‑i‑Mustakhraj → revenue extraction; Tughluq’s Diwan‑i‑Pil → elephant corps). This diwan‑centric approach prevents vague answers and earns full marks for structural precision.

Mughal Naval Organization: The Imperial Fleet, Port Administration at Surat and Hughli, and the Role of the Siddis

The Mughal Empire maintained a modest but functional navy primarily to safeguard maritime trade, protect the annual Hajj convoy, and support littoral campaigns in Bengal and the Deccan. The fleet was placed under the Mir Bahr (admiral), a mansabdar appointed directly by the emperor; notable holders included Siddi Qasim (under Jahangir) and Siddi Yaqub (under Shah Jahan). The principal bases were Surat on the Gujarat coast and Hughli in Bengal, each administered by a port‑officer (bandar‑dar) who collected customs (sungam), supervised ship‑building yards, and coordinated with the provincial subahdar. The vessel typology comprised ghurabs (large, square‑rigged warships mounting 20‑30 guns), gallivats (rowed gunboats of 10‑15 guns), and smaller patellas for riverine patrol. The Siddis — African‑origin mercenaries who had entered Indian service via the Deccan sultanates — were integrated into the Mughal officer corps; they commanded squadrons, managed the Surat arsenal, and often acted as intermediaries in negotiations with the Portuguese at Daman and the English at Surat. The navy’s operational record includes the 1615‑1616 blockade of the Portuguese at Daman, the 1662 escort of the Hajj fleet from Surat to Mocha, and the 1680‑1681 riverine operations against Maratha coastal raids in the Konkan. Funding came from the khazana via the Diwan‑i‑Bahr, a sub‑department of the Diwan‑i‑Wizarat; however, after Aurangzeb’s Deccan wars the naval budget was repeatedly cut, leading to the decay of the Surat yard and the loss of effective sea control to European companies by the early eighteenth century.

Mughal‑Dutch Commercial Relations: The Surat Factory, the 1662 Treaty, and the Shift to Textile Exports

The Dutch East India Company (VOC) established its first Indian factory at Surat in 1616, securing a farman from Jahangir in 1617 that granted permission to trade, exemption from the rahdari (road toll), and the right to maintain a factor’s residence. The relationship deepened under Shah Jahan, who confirmed the privileges in 1638, but the decisive legal framework was the 1662 treaty (farman) with Aurangzeb. This instrument granted the VOC duty‑free trade in Gujarat, Bengal, and the Coromandel, the right to mint copper coins (duiten) for local circulation, and extraterritorial jurisdiction over Dutch subjects — disputes involving VOC personnel were to be adjudicated by the Company’s own council rather than the Mughal qazi. The Dutch factory network expanded to Ahmedabad, Agra, Burhanpur, Patna, Hughli, Balasore, Bimilipatam, Pulicat, and Nagapattinam. The core export commodity shifted from spices (which the VOC sourced in Southeast Asia) to Indian cotton textiles — calicoes, muslins, and chintzes — alongside indigo, saltpetre, and opium. The VOC’s intra‑Asian trade used Indian textiles to purchase pepper and cloves in the Malay archipelago, creating a triangular flow that financed the Company’s European purchases. Competition with the English East India Company intensified after the 1680s; the Mughal court, seeking to balance European powers, occasionally favoured the Dutch (e.g., the 1689 farman allowing the VOC to fortify its Hughli factory). However, the Mughal fiscal crisis after 1707, combined with the VOC’s own financial troubles in the Fourth Anglo‑Dutch War (1780‑1784), eroded Dutch privileges; by the 1795 French occupation of the Netherlands the VOC’s Indian presence had effectively ceased.

Vijayanagara Naval Organization: The Coastal Fleet, Port Control at Goa and Bhatkal, and Interaction with the Portuguese

Although Vijayanagara is celebrated for its inland agrarian and temple economy, the empire maintained a coastal fleet to protect its maritime revenue (sungam) and to project power along the Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal. The fleet was not a single standing navy but a confederation of squadrons operated by the Nayakas of the western ports — principally Bhatkal, Honavar, and Goa (until its capture by the Portuguese in 1510) — and by the Coromandel governors at Pulicat and Nagapattinam. Ships were built in local yards using teak from the Malabar forests; they ranged from patamars (large ocean‑going vessels of 300‑500 tons) to smaller dhonis for riverine and coastal patrol. Crews were drawn from the Mappila Muslim maritime community and from Hindu fishing castes, reflecting the empire’s pluralistic recruitment. The fleet’s primary missions were: (1) escorting merchant convoys carrying pepper, ginger, and textiles to Hormuz and Malacca; (2) transporting troops for the 15th‑century expeditions to Sri Lanka (e.g., the 1410 campaign of Deva Raya I); and (3) suppressing piracy by the Malabar corsairs who threatened the spice trade. After the Portuguese seized Goa, Vijayanagara entered a pragmatic alliance: the empire allowed Portuguese ships to call at Bhatkal in exchange for Portuguese naval support against the Bijapur Sultanate’s coastal raids. The 1565 Battle of Talikota shattered central authority; the coastal Nayakas (notably the Ikkeri Nayakas) inherited the shipyards and continued a reduced naval presence into the 17th century, but the imperial fleet as a coordinated instrument ceased to exist.

  • Confusing the Diwan‑i‑Wizarat (finance) with the Diwan‑i‑Arz (military) when describing Sultanate central departments.
  • Assuming the Ghulam corps were hereditary nobles; they were slave‑recruited and manumitted on service completion.
  • Treating the Mughal navy as a blue‑water fleet comparable to European navies; it was primarily coastal and riverine.
  • Overlooking the extraterritorial jurisdiction clause in the 1662 Mughal‑Dutch treaty, which placed VOC personnel outside Mughal qazi courts.
  • Believing the Vijayanagara fleet was a centrally commanded imperial navy; it was a decentralized confederation of Nayaka squadrons.
  • Ignoring the official Mughal rank (mansab) held by Siddi admirals, reducing them to mere mercenaries.
  • Neglecting the Barid‑i‑Mamalik’s intelligence role as a separate check on the Wazir’s fiscal authority.

The Mughal Crown Lands (Khalisa) Administration: The Diwan‑i‑Khalsa, the Amil, and the Revenue Accounting System

The khalisa (crown) lands formed the fiscal backbone of the Mughal centre, distinct from jagir assignments that were granted to mansabdars. The Diwan‑i‑Khalsa, a dedicated branch of the imperial finance ministry, supervised the assessment, collection, and accounting of revenue from these territories. Its head, the Diwan‑i‑Khalsa, reported directly to the emperor and coordinated with the provincial diwans to ensure that the central treasury received a predictable flow of cash and kind. At the village level the amil (revenue collector) was appointed by the Diwan‑i‑Khalsa rather than by a jagirdar; the amil worked alongside the qanungo (hereditary accountant) and the patwari (village record‑keeper) to maintain the bahi (ledger) of measured area, crop yields, and assessed demand. The zabt cadastre, based on the Ilahi gaz and the bigha, provided the standard measurement, while the dastur‑ul‑amal (revenue manual) fixed the cash rates for each crop category. Seasonal inspections (mahsul) verified standing crops, and the amil issued a raqam (demand note) that the cultivator could pay in coin, grain, or a combination. Surplus from khalisa lands funded the imperial household, the standing army, and the mint network, reducing dependence on the often‑unstable jagir transfers. Under Aurangzeb the khalisa area expanded as he resumed many jagirs, but the administrative strain contributed to the later fiscal crisis when the amil‑qanungo nexus became prone to corruption and the central audit (mushrif) could not keep pace with the growing volume of accounts.

The accounting system employed a double‑entry method in Persian: the jama (demand) and the hasil (collection) columns were balanced each revenue year (fasli). Discrepancies triggered a talab (audit) by the mushrif‑i‑khalsa, who could impose fines or replace the amil. The Diwan‑i‑Khalsa also managed the khalisa granaries (kothi) that stored grain for famine relief and for provisioning the imperial camp during campaigns. This granary network was linked to the banjara caravans that moved grain from surplus to deficit zones, a logistical integration that distinguished khalisa administration from the more decentralized jagir system. The survival of detailed khalisa records in the Mughal archives (e.g., the Ain‑i‑Akbari’s revenue tables and the later Dastur‑ul‑Amal of Aurangzeb) allows historians to trace regional variations in land productivity and the impact of climatic events on central finances.

The Deccan Sultanates' Habshi Nobility and the Rise of Malik Ambar: Military‑Administrative Integration in Ahmadnagar

The Habshi (Abyssinian) slaves imported through the Indian Ocean trade became a distinctive military‑administrative elite in the Deccan sultanates, especially in Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, and Golconda. In Ahmadnagar the Habshi corps, known as the ‘Habshi‑khanas’, were organized into cavalry units under their own commanders who held the title ‘Siddi’. Malik Ambar, originally a Habshi slave named Chapu, rose through this corps to become the Peshwa (chief minister) and de facto ruler of Ahmadnagar from 1600 until his death in 1626. His ascent illustrates the permeability of the Deccan slave‑soldier system: manumission after loyal service, followed by appointment to high office, was an established pathway. Malik Ambar reorganized the revenue administration by introducing a systematic measurement of cultivable land (the ‘malik‑amari’ survey) and fixing cash revenue rates (the ‘zabt‑i‑Malik’) that reduced the discretion of local zamindars. He also created a standing infantry of musketeers (the ‘barq‑andaz’) drawn from the Habshi and local Maratha recruits, complementing the traditional cavalry. His guerrilla tactics against Mughal incursions — especially the use of hill forts, scorched‑earth retreats, and rapid cavalry raids — forced the Mughals to commit large forces to the Deccan without decisive results. Administratively, Malik Ambar integrated the Habshi nobility into the provincial governance structure: Habshi officers were appointed as faujdars of strategic districts, and the Siddi admiralty at Janjira was formalized as a Mughal‑recognized vassal after 1618. The legacy of his revenue reforms persisted in the Ahmadnagar ‘malik‑amari’ records that were later consulted by Mughal surveyors during the 1636 annexation. The Habshi presence thus exemplifies a military‑administrative fusion where slave‑origin elites became indispensable to state formation in the Deccan.

Technique: Comparative Revenue Reform Analysis
Compare Malik Ambar’s ‘malik‑amari’ survey in Ahmadnagar with Todar Mal’s zabt settlement in the Mughal core. Identify two structural similarities and one key difference in their implementation.
Both systems relied on a measured cadastre (Ilahi gaz / local rod) and fixed cash demand per unit area, replacing arbitrary crop‑sharing. Both employed hereditary village accountants (qanungo / kulkarni) to maintain registers. The key difference: Todar Mal’s zabt was imposed uniformly across the empire by a central bureaucracy, whereas Malik Ambar’s survey was a regional initiative negotiated with local chieftains and retained flexibility for hill‑zone cultivation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “administrative integration of slave‑origin elites”, structure the answer around three pillars: (1) recruitment and manumission pathway, (2) military command roles, (3) civil revenue or governance appointments. Cite Malik Ambar and the Janjira Siddis as paired examples; this dual illustration scores higher than a single case.

The Vijayanagara Temple Administration: The Sthanika, the Devadaya Grant, and the Role of the Temple Treasury in Local Governance

Temples in the Vijayanagara empire functioned as autonomous economic and administrative nodes, and their internal hierarchy was codified through inscriptions that name the sthanika (temple manager), the devadaya (land grant to the deity), and the temple treasury (bhandara). The sthanika, usually a Brahmin or a senior Vaishnava/Shaiva functionary, was appointed by the king or the local nayaka and held authority over ritual schedules, festival expenditures, and the management of endowed lands. Devadaya grants were recorded on copper‑plate charters (tamra‑shasana) specifying the village, its boundaries, the assessed revenue (usually a share of the produce or a fixed cash amount), and the obligations of the tenant cultivators (kudiyan). The revenue from these lands flowed directly into the temple bhandara, which financed daily worship (nitya‑puja), periodic festivals (utsava), and the maintenance of irrigation works such as tanks (kere) and canals (kaluve) that benefited the surrounding agrarian community. In many districts the temple treasury also acted as a credit institution: merchants and cultivators could deposit surplus grain or coin and obtain loans at modest interest, a practice documented in the 15th‑century inscriptions of the Virupaksha temple at Hampi. The sthanika’s accounts were audited annually by a royal officer (the mahapradhani’s representative) who verified the bhandara’s receipts against the devadaya registers. This oversight linked temple finance to the broader nayankara fiscal system, ensuring that temple resources supplemented state revenue during military campaigns. The integration of ritual, agrarian, and fiscal functions made the Vijayanagara temple a micro‑state within the empire, a model that persisted in the successor Nayaka kingdoms of Madurai and Tanjavur.

The Maratha Confederacy's Diplomatic Correspondence: The Bakhar Tradition, the Chitnis Office, and the Use of Harkara Networks

Diplomatic communication in the Maratha confederacy rested on three interlocking mechanisms: the bakhar (chronicle) tradition that recorded treaties and embassies, the chitnis (secretary) office that drafted and authenticated letters, and the harkara (courier) network that ensured rapid physical delivery across the Deccan. Bakhar texts such as the ‘Sabhasad Bakhar’ and the ‘Shiva‑Bharat’ contain verbatim copies of farmans, sanads, and correspondence with the Mughal court, the Nizam of Hyderabad, and European companies. The chitnis, a hereditary post often held by a Brahmin family (e.g., the Pant‑Pratinidhi line), maintained the official seal (mudra) and the register of outgoing and incoming dispatches (dastak). Letters were written in Modi script Marathi for internal use and in Persian for external diplomacy; the chitnis employed scribes proficient in both scripts. The harkara system, described in the ‘Harkara‑Pothi’ manuals, organized relay stations (thanas) every 15‑20 km along major routes (e.g., Pune‑Aurangabad, Satara‑Burhanpur). Each harkara carried a sealed pouch (dastak) and a time‑stamped receipt (pavana) to prevent tampering. Speed was critical: during the 1689 Treaty of Purandar negotiations, a harkara covered the 300 km between Pune and the Mughal camp at Ahmadnagar in under 48 hours, enabling Shivaji’s envoys to respond to Aurangzeb’s shifting terms. The confidentiality of diplomatic missives was protected by a cipher (gup‑lipi) known only to the chitnis and the recipient’s counterpart. This layered communication infrastructure allowed the confederacy to conduct simultaneous negotiations with multiple powers, a diplomatic agility that contrasted with the more centralized Mughal barid system.

The Mughal Military Engineering in the Deccan: The Zamburak, Rocketry, and Siege Artillery Deployed during Aurangzeb's Campaigns

Mughal military engineering in the Deccan combined indigenous metal‑working with Persian‑Ottoman influences to produce a flexible siege train. The zamburak — a light swivel gun mounted on a camel saddle — provided mobile firepower for escorting supply columns and for rapid deployment against fortified hill positions. Contemporary accounts (e.g., the ‘Maasir‑i‑Alamgiri’) note that each zamburak fired a 1‑2 kg iron ball and could be reloaded in under a minute by a two‑man crew. For heavier bombardment, the Mughals cast bronze and iron cannons (top) at the imperial foundries in Burhanpur and Aurangabad; the largest pieces, such as the ‘Fath‑i‑Jang’ (victory of war), weighed over 3 tons and required a team of oxen and a purpose‑built carriage. Rocketry, though more famously associated with Mysore, was also employed: the ‘ban’ rockets, metal tubes filled with gunpowder and iron shrapnel, were launched from portable frames to disrupt enemy formations during the 1687 siege of Golconda. Siege operations followed a systematic protocol: engineers (mir‑atish) surveyed the target fort, identified weak points in the curtain wall, and constructed approach trenches (saps) protected by gabions (bamboo baskets filled with earth). Artillery batteries were positioned on raised platforms (chabutra) to achieve plunging fire. The logistical backbone relied on the banjara caravans that transported gunpowder (barud), shot, and fodder for the draft animals. The integration of these technologies allowed Aurangzeb’s forces to reduce major Deccan strongholds — Bijapur (1686), Golconda (1687), and the hill forts of the Maratha heartland — within a decade, albeit at enormous fiscal cost that strained the khalisa revenue system described earlier.

  • Confusing the khalisa Diwan‑i‑Khalsa with the provincial diwan; the former reports directly to the emperor, the latter to the subahdar.
  • Assuming all Habshi soldiers were slaves; many were manumitted and held hereditary faujdari posts.
  • Treating the sthanika as a purely ritual officer; he exercised fiscal control over devadaya lands and temple credit operations.
  • Overlooking the bilingual (Marathi‑Persian) nature of Maratha diplomatic letters; the chitnis maintained parallel registers.
  • Believing the zamburak was a heavy cannon; it was a light camel‑mounted swivel gun distinct from the siege top.
  • Neglecting the role of the banjara caravans in moving artillery ammunition; they were the logistical lifeline for the Deccan siege train.

These advanced sections illustrate how fiscal, military, and diplomatic institutions intertwined across the Sultanate, Mughal, Vijayanagara, and Maratha polities.

The Mughal Empire's Adoption of the Matchlock Musket (Toradar) and Its Impact on Infantry Tactics

The matchlock musket, known in Persian sources as the toradar, entered the Indian subcontinent with Babur’s Central Asian contingents in the early sixteenth century. At the First Battle of Panipat (1526) Babur deployed a modest number of matchlock‑armed infantry behind a wagon‑laager, using their volley fire to break the charge of Ibrahim Lodi’s war elephants. Under Akbar the weapon was systematised: the imperial armoury at Agra produced standardized barrels of 0.75‑inch calibre, and the mir‑atish (master of ordnance) established a training regimen that required each musketeer to load, prime, and fire in under ninety seconds. The Ain‑i‑Akbari records a standing corps of 12,000 toradar‑chis (matchlock men) organized in battalions of 500, each supported by a naik (corporal) and a havildar (sergeant). Their tactical role evolved from static defence to mobile skirmishing; at the Battle of Haldighati (1576) Mughal musketeers fired from concealed positions on the flanks, disrupting Rajput cavalry charges. The matchlock’s slow rate of fire and vulnerability to rain limited its effectiveness in monsoon campaigns, prompting the gradual introduction of the snaphance and later the flintlock (tufang) in the late seventeenth century. Nevertheless, the toradar remained the backbone of Mughal infantry until the 1680s, when the Deccan wars exposed its logistical shortcomings — gunpowder supply lines stretched over 1,500 kilometres, and the humid climate corroded match cords. The transition to flintlocks was accelerated by European arms imports through Surat, but the institutional memory of matchlock drill persisted in the faujdar manuals used by provincial governors well into the eighteenth century.

The Delhi Sultanate's Urban Water Management: The Construction of the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi and the Canal System of Firozabad

Water security dictated the spatial logic of Sultanate capitals. Iltutmish (r. 1211‑1236) commissioned the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi, a 12‑hectare reservoir fed by a diversion channel from the Yamuna, to supply the growing population of Delhi and to serve as a ceremonial tank for the annual naubat (drum) festival. The reservoir’s embankments were lined with dressed stone and fitted with sluice gates (darwaza) that allowed controlled release into a network of covered drains (nala) serving the markets of Mehrauli and the royal citadel. Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296‑1316) expanded the system by constructing the Satpula (seven‑arched weir) to regulate flow during the monsoon, and he imposed a water‑tax (ab‑i‑mal) on commercial users, collected by the kotwal’s office. The most ambitious hydraulic project was undertaken by Firuz Shah Tughlaq (r. 1351‑1388), who excavated the 150‑kilometre Western Yamuna Canal (the Firozabad Canal) to bring perennial water to his new capital, Firozabad (present‑day Feroz Shah Kotla). The canal incorporated a series of bandhs (earthen dams) and kuls (distribution channels) that irrigated 30,000 hectares of khalisa land, directly boosting the sultanate’s revenue yield. Maintenance was assigned to a dedicated diwan‑i‑ab (water department) staffed by muqaddams (village headmen) who mobilised corvée labour (begar) for annual desilting. Contemporary chroniclers such as Ziauddin Barani note that the canal’s revenue contribution amounted to 1.2 million tankas annually, roughly 8 percent of the central treasury. The system’s durability is attested by its continued use under the Lodi and early Mughal administrations, though the Mughals later shifted the primary water source to the Agra Canal (1556) to serve their new capital.

Technique: Comparative Table Construction
Compare the water management systems of the Delhi Sultanate and the Vijayanagara Empire in terms of scale, funding mechanism, and administrative control.
Answer: Create a three‑column table. Feature / Delhi Sultanate / Vijayanagara.
Scale: Sultanate – Hauz‑i‑Shamsi (12 ha) + 150 km Firozabad Canal irrigating 30,000 ha; Vijayanagara – network of temple tanks (e.g., Kamalapuram tank 8 ha) and the Tungabhadra canal system covering 40,000 ha.
Funding: Sultanate – water‑tax (ab‑i‑mal) collected by kotwal, supplemented by khalisa revenue; Vijayanagara – temple endowments (devadaya) and customs duties (sayer) at ports like Bhatkal.
Administrative control: Sultanate – diwan‑i‑ab under central diwan, maintenance via corvée; Vijayanagara – sthanika officers appointed by the Mahanayakacharya, with local nayaka oversight. This tabular format forces precise terminology and prevents conflation of distinct fiscal streams.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a comparative question appears, sketch a quick table on the rough sheet before writing. Fill each cell with a specific term (e.g., ab‑i‑mal, devadaya, sthanika) rather than generic phrases. The examiner awards marks for precise vocabulary and clear structural contrast.

The Vijayanagara Empire's Maritime Trade with the Persian Gulf: The Role of the Port of Bhatkal and the Chettiar Networks

From the early sixteenth century the port of Bhatkal (modern‑day Karnataka) functioned as the principal maritime gateway for Vijayanagara’s exchange with the Persian Gulf. Portuguese cartographers (e.g., João de Barros, 1552) record that Bhatkal handled an annual pepper export of 3,000 candies (≈ 1,200 tonnes) and imported Arabian horses, Persian carpets, and copper. The Chettiar merchant guilds of the Coromandel coast, organized into nagarams (merchant corporations), financed voyages through a system of hundi (bill of exchange) denominated in the Vijayanagara varaha gold coin. Inscriptions at the Virupaksha temple (c. 1510) list a sayer (customs) rate of 2.5 percent on pepper and 5 percent on horse imports, collected by the patel (port officer) under the supervision of the Mahanayakacharya. Diplomatic missions to Hormuz (1515, 1521) secured safe‑conduct firman from the Safavid governor, allowing Vijayanagara ships to bypass Portuguese blockades at Goa. The revenue from this trade funded the construction of the massive Mahanavami Dibba platform and the expansion of the Tungabhadra canal network. However, the 1565 Battle of Talikota disrupted the overland supply lines to Bhatkal, and the subsequent Portuguese capture of the port (1568) shifted the bulk of pepper exports to the Portuguese‑controlled port of Goa, reducing Vijayanagara’s customs income by an estimated 40 percent within a decade.

The Maratha Confederacy's Light Cavalry (Pindari) Organization and Its Role in Guerrilla Warfare

The Pindari horsemen emerged in the late seventeenth century as irregular light cavalry recruited primarily from the Malwa and Bundelkhand regions. Unlike the regular bargir (state‑paid) cavalry, Pindaris operated on a loot‑share basis: each band (toli) elected a sardar who negotiated a chauth (one‑fourth) of the plunder with the Peshwa’s saranjam holder. Their mounts were hardy Deccani ponies, and their armament consisted of a curved talwar, a short lance (barchha), and a lightweight matchlock carbine (toradar‑chhota) for skirmishing. During the 1700‑1707 Maratha‑Mughal war, Pindari bands conducted deep raids into Mughal territories as far north as Agra, disrupting grain convoys and forcing the Mughal faujdars to divert troops from siege operations. The Peshwa Baji Rao I (r. 1720‑1740) formalised their relationship by granting sanad (patent) to leading sardars such as Karim Khan and Wasil Muhammad, integrating them into the Maratha chauth collection machinery. Their tactical hallmark was the chakravyuha (circular formation) that allowed rapid dispersal and re‑assembly, a technique described in the Shiva‑Bharat bakhar. After the Third Anglo‑Maratha War (1817‑1818), the British disbanded the Pindari bands through the Pindari Suppression Act (1818), offering land grants to former sardars who surrendered. The legacy of Pindari mobility influenced later Indian irregular forces, notably the Risala cavalry of the 1857 uprising.

The Mughal Empire's Legal Pluralism: The Coexistence of Sharia, Dharmashastra, and Imperial Firmans in Provincial Courts

Mughal provincial courts operated under a layered legal framework. The qazi (Islamic judge) applied Hanafi fiqh for disputes involving Muslims, while Hindu litigants were adjudicated by a pandit versed in the Dharmashastra (particularly the Mitakshara and Dayabhaga schools). Imperial firmans (farmans) issued by the emperor could override both systems in matters of state revenue, criminal punishment, or land grants. Akbar’s Ain‑i‑Akbari (1590) codified this pluralism by establishing the adalat‑i‑ala (high court) at the capital, staffed by a mufti (jurisconsult) and a pandit‑raja (chief Hindu jurist). In the provinces, the faujdar presided over criminal cases (theft, rebellion) using the zawabit (secular regulations) while civil suits over inheritance, marriage, and property followed the personal law of the parties. Aurangzeb’s Fatawa‑e‑Alamgiri (1672) compiled Hanafi rulings for imperial use but explicitly retained the riwaj (customary law) of non‑Muslim communities for local disputes. Revenue courts (diwan‑i‑mal) applied the zabt assessment rules irrespective of religious affiliation. This pluralism allowed the empire to legitimise its rule across diverse populations, yet it also created jurisdictional friction: a 1665 case in Bengal saw a Hindu zamindar appeal a qazi’s decision to the diwan‑i‑ala, resulting in a farman that mandated joint adjudication by qazi and pandit for mixed‑community land disputes. The system persisted until the British introduced the Regulation III of 1793, which replaced the dual courts with a unified Anglo‑Indian judiciary.

  • Confusing the matchlock (toradar) with the later flintlock (tufang) and attributing its widespread use to Aurangzeb's reign.
  • Assuming the Hauz‑i‑Shamsi was solely a royal reservoir; it also served as a public water source managed by the kotwal.
  • Overstating the Chettiar monopoly on Vijayanagara's Persian Gulf trade; Arab and Persian merchants also operated from Bhatkal.
  • Treating the Pindari as a formal Maratha regiment; they were irregulars often operating independently of Peshwa command.
  • Believing Mughal courts applied a uniform Sharia code; in practice, Hindu law prevailed in civil disputes among non‑Muslims unless a farman intervened.

The Razmnama Project: Akbar's Persian Mahabharata and Its Political Symbolism

In 1582 Emperor Akbar ordered the translation of the Sanskrit Mahabharata into Persian, a project known as the Razmnama (Book of War). The work was supervised by the chief secretary Abul Fazl and executed by a team of Brahmin pandits — notably Naqib Khan, Sultan Haji Thanesari, and the poet Faizi — working alongside Persian scribes in the imperial Maktab Khana at Fatehpur Sikri. The translation was completed in 1586 and produced in several lavishly illustrated manuscripts; the most famous copy, now in the British Library (Or. 12076), contains 169 miniatures painted by artists such as Basawan and Daswanth. Akbar used the Razmnama as a tool of imperial ideology: by rendering the Hindu epic in the court language he presented himself as a universal sovereign (Padshah‑i‑Alam) who respected the cultural heritage of his non‑Muslim subjects. Copies were distributed to leading amirs, to the royal library, and to provincial governors, reinforcing the message that the Mughal state transcended sectarian boundaries. A parallel project, the Persian Ramayana (completed 1589), followed the same pattern. Both translations were accompanied by imperial prefaces (dibacha) written by Abul Fazl that explicitly linked the epics’ themes of dharma and just rule to Akbar’s own concept of sulh‑i‑kul (universal peace). The manuscripts also served diplomatic purposes; a Razmnama copy was sent to the Safavid court in 1590 as a gesture of cultural exchange. The project’s scale — over 1,500 folios, each folio measuring roughly 35 × 25 cm — demonstrates the Mughal capacity for large‑scale manuscript production and the strategic use of literature for political legitimation.

The Maratha Village Panchayat System: The Patil, the Kulkarni, and the Nyaya Panch

At the base of Maratha rural administration stood the village (mauza) governed by a triad of officials. The Patil (headman) was hereditary, responsible for revenue collection, maintenance of law and order, and the convening of the Nyaya Panch — a council of five respected elders (panch) drawn from the village’s dominant castes. The Kulkarni (accountant) kept the land records (mahzar), measured holdings using the local bigha, and prepared the annual revenue demand (jamabandi) for the Patil’s submission to the Peshwa’s revenue office (Huzur Daftar). The Nyaya Panch adjudicated civil disputes — inheritance, water rights, caste offences — and minor criminal matters such as theft, applying customary law (riwaj) rather than Islamic fiqh. Shivaji’s 1674 charter (the Rajyavyavahara Kosh) formalised the Patil’s judicial powers, granting him the authority to impose fines (dand) up to 50 rupees without referral to the district (subah) court. Under the Peshwas (post‑1714) the system was tightened: the Patil became a salaried agent of the Saranjam holder, the Kulkarni’s records were audited annually by the Kamavisdar, and the Nyaya Panch’s decisions could be appealed to the district Nyayadhish. Despite centralising pressures, the village panchayat retained autonomy in social regulation, especially in the enforcement of gotra exogamy and the management of common lands (gairan). The resilience of this structure is evident in the British retention of the Patil‑Kulkarni framework in the 1827 Bombay Regulation VII, which merely added a colonial magistrate above the indigenous panchayat.

Comparative Analysis of Village Governance
Compare the Maratha village panchayat with the Mughal village‑level revenue official (the Muqaddam) in terms of judicial authority and revenue responsibility.
The Maratha Patil combined revenue collection (jamabandi) and judicial functions (Nyaya Panch) in a single hereditary office, whereas the Mughal Muqaddam was primarily a revenue collector appointed by the faujdar or zamindar and exercised only limited magisterial powers (e.g., maintaining watch‑and‑ward). The Patil’s judicial role was institutionalised through the Nyaya Panch, a standing council of five elders that applied customary law; the Muqaddam had no equivalent standing judicial body and referred disputes to the qazi or the faujdar’s court. Consequently, the Maratha village enjoyed a higher degree of self‑governance in civil matters, while the Mughal village remained more tightly integrated into the imperial judicial hierarchy.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about local administration, first identify whether the office is revenue‑centric, judicial‑centric, or hybrid. The Maratha Patil is a hybrid; the Mughal Muqaddam is revenue‑centric. Use this distinction to structure comparative answers and avoid conflating the two systems.

The Ahom Land Grant System: Bora, Khel, and the Paik Revenue Integration

The Ahom kingdom (1228‑1826) developed a distinctive land‑grant framework that intertwined revenue exemption with military service. Two principal grant types existed: the Bora (also spelled Borā) and the Khel. A Bora was a revenue‑free grant of cultivable land, typically awarded to Brahmins, temples (satras), and learned men; it carried the obligation of performing religious rites and maintaining the grantee’s household. The Khel, by contrast, was a service grant given to Paik officers (Paik‑Khel) in lieu of cash salary; the grantee supplied a fixed number of Paik labourers (usually 20‑30) for state projects — road building, fort repair, and the royal navy on the Brahmaputra. Both grants were recorded in the royal register (Buranji) and measured in the Ahom unit of area, the pura (approximately 1.25 acres). Under King Rudra Singha (r. 1696‑1714) a cadastral survey (the “Rudra Survey”) standardised the pura across the kingdom, introduced the bigha for larger estates, and linked each grant to a specific Paik quota, thereby integrating the fiscal and military obligations. The senior ministers — the Borbarua (chief secretary) and the Phukan (commander of the Paik corps) — oversaw the allocation and audit of Bora and Khel lands. Grants could be revoked for failure to meet Paik quotas, a provision used during the 1682‑1683 Mughal‑Ahom war to mobilise additional labour for fortification at Guwahati. The system persisted until the British annexation (1826), when the Bora lands were converted into zamindari estates under the Permanent Settlement, while Khel obligations were commuted into cash taxes.

The Dutch East India Company's Factories in the Coromandel and Their Relations with the Golconda Sultanate

The Vereenigde Oostindische Compagnie (VOC) established its first Coromandel factory at Pulicat in 1608, followed by Masulipatnam (1628), Negapatam (1659), and a subsidiary at Porto Novo (1675). These factories specialised in the procurement of cotton textiles (especially the fine “muslin” of Masulipatnam), indigo, and Golconda diamonds. Diplomatic ties with the Qutb Shahi rulers were formalised through firmans: Sultan Abdullah Qutb Shah (r. 1626‑1672) issued a 1635 farman granting the VOC duty‑free export of 2,000 bales of cloth annually from Masulipatnam, and a 1645 treaty confirmed the company’s right to maintain a fortified warehouse at Negapatam. The Dutch factor Pieter van den Broecke (1615‑1629) negotiated the 1628 “Treaty of Friendship” which allowed VOC ships to anchor at the Golconda port of Muttapilly without Portuguese interference. In return, the VOC supplied the sultanate with European firearms, mercury for gold refining, and Persian horses. The relationship soured after the Mughal conquest of Golconda in 1687; Emperor Aurangzeb’s 1689 farman revoked all European privileges, forcing the VOC to relocate its primary Coromandel operations to the Dutch‑controlled port of Cochin. Nevertheless, the VOC’s archival records (the “Daghregisters” of Batavia) preserve detailed accounts of the Qutb Shahi court’s revenue demands, the price of diamonds (averaging 1,200 rupees per carat in the 1660s), and the volume of textile exports (over 1.2 million pieces annually at the peak). These documents remain a primary source for the economic history of the Deccan in the seventeenth century.

The Evolution of the Mughal Flintlock Musket (Tufang) and Its Tactical Impact in the Deccan Wars

The flintlock musket, known in Mughal sources as the tufang, entered the imperial arsenal in the early 1620s through Portuguese and Dutch arms merchants operating from Goa and Surat. By the 1650s the imperial karkhanas at Lahore, Delhi, and Agra were producing flintlock barrels using locally forged steel and imported French lock mechanisms. Emperor Aurangzeb (r. 1658‑1707) accelerated adoption: the 1665 “Ordnance Regulation” (Dastur‑ul‑Amal) mandated that each cavalry trooper (sowar) carry a tufang alongside the traditional matchlock (toradar). The flintlock’s faster ignition (≈2 seconds versus 5‑7 seconds for the matchlock) and reliability in humid Deccan conditions gave Mughal infantry a decisive edge during the sieges of Bijapur (1685‑1686) and Golconda (1687). Contemporary chronicles (the Alamgir‑Namah) record that at the Battle of Bijapur the Mughal artillery park fielded 1,200 tufang‑armed janissaries who broke the Deccani cavalry charge within three volleys. However, the weapon’s higher cost (≈30 rupees per piece versus 12 rupees for a matchlock) limited its diffusion to elite units; the bulk of the provincial faujdar levies continued to use matchlocks until the 1720s. After Aurangzeb’s death the central karkhanas declined, and the tufang’s production shifted to regional workshops in Hyderabad and Mysore, where it influenced the armament of the emerging successor states.

  • Confusing the Razmnama with the later Persian translation of the Mahabharata commissioned by Jahangir; the Razmnama is exclusively an Akbar‑era project.
  • Assuming the Maratha Patil was a purely revenue officer; his judicial role via the Nyaya Panch was constitutionally defined in Shivaji’s 1674 charter.
  • Treating the Ahom Bora as a simple tax‑free grant; it carried mandatory religious duties and was audited alongside Paik quotas.
  • Believing the VOC’s Coromandel factories operated independently of the Golconda court; they functioned under explicit firmans and treaties.
  • Equating the Mughal tufang with the European flintlock of the 18th century; the Mughal version retained a longer barrel and used a distinct lock mechanism produced in imperial workshops.
  • Overlooking the 1689 Mughal farman that revoked all European privileges in the Deccan, which ended the VOC’s special status in Golconda.
  • Remember to verify each administrative term against primary sources before the exam.

Muhammad bin Tughluq's Administrative Experiments: Token Currency, Capital Transfer, and the Diwan-i-Kohi

Muhammad bin Tughluq (r. 1325‑1351) pursued a series of interlocking fiscal and territorial experiments that distinguish his reign from the consolidationist policies of his predecessor Ghiyasuddin Tughluq and his successor Firoz Shah Tughluq. The most documented experiment is the token currency (1329‑1332), modelled on the Chinese chao paper money and the Persian chao of Gaykhatu Khan. The Sultan issued brass and copper tanka coins at par with the silver tanka (172 grains), mandating their acceptance for all state dues. The absence of a state monopoly on minting, combined with the ease of forging base‑metal tokens, flooded markets with counterfeits. Contemporary chronicler Ziauddin Barani records that "the house of every Hindu became a mint," and the Sultan was forced to redeem both genuine and forged tokens at the treasury, draining the silver reserve. The experiment collapsed within three years, but it reveals a deliberate attempt to expand the monetary base for Deccan campaigns without fresh silver inflows.

The second experiment, the transfer of the capital from Delhi to Daulatabad (renamed Qubbat al‑Islam) in 1327, aimed to secure the newly annexed Deccan territories and protect the capital from Mongol incursions via the northwest. The forced migration of the Delhi elite — umara, ulema, and artisans — caused massive mortality; Barani notes that "not one in ten survived the journey." The Sultan reversed the order in 1335 after the Deccan governorships proved unmanageable from the new capital and the northwest frontier weakened. The episode illustrates the limits of personal monarchy when logistical infrastructure (the dak chowki relay system) could not sustain imperial command over 1,400 kilometres.

The third experiment, the Diwan‑i‑Kohi (Department of Agriculture), created c. 1330, sought to expand the cultivated area in the Doab by advancing taqavi (seed‑and‑tool) loans to peasants and appointing amins to measure land and fix revenue at a uniform rate. A severe famine (1334‑1342) coincided with the assessment, and the rigid demand for cash revenue in a subsistence crisis triggered the first major peasant rebellion of the Sultanate period. The Sultan's response — military repression and the execution of amins — alienated the rural intermediary class (khuts, muqaddams) whose cooperation the Iqta system depended upon. The Diwan‑i‑Kohi was wound down by 1345, but its cadastral methodology influenced Firoz Shah's later mahsul surveys. Together, these three experiments demonstrate a ruler attempting to centralise fiscal extraction, monetary sovereignty, and territorial control simultaneously — a structural overreach that the Sultanate's personnel and communication systems could not sustain.

The Mughal Dagh‑o‑Chehra System: Branding, Muster, and the Enforcement of Mansabdar Obligations

The dagh‑o‑chehra (branding and descriptive roll) was the Mughal Empire's primary mechanism for verifying that mansabdars maintained the troop quotas corresponding to their zat (personal rank) and sawar (cavalry rank). Instituted systematically under Akbar (c. 1574) by the Mir Bakhshi Shahbaz Khan, the system required every mansabdar presenting contingents for inspection (muster) to have each horse branded with the imperial mark (dagh) — typically a trident or sun motif — and each trooper recorded in a descriptive roll (chehra) noting name, father's name, caste, height, distinguishing marks, and the horse's colour and brand. The muster was conducted annually or before major campaigns at designated mahals (muster stations) such as Lahore, Agra, Ajmer, and Burhanpur.

The operational logic was fiscal‑military: the state paid the mansabdar's salary (talab) based on the certified sawar strength, but the mansabdar recruited and equipped the troopers. Without verification, a mansabdar could claim pay for 500 sawar while fielding 200, pocketing the difference. The dagh prevented horse substitution; the chehra prevented trooper substitution. The Mir Bakhshi's office maintained the muster rolls (mahzar‑i‑dagh) which were cross‑checked against the jagir revenue assignments. A mansabdar who failed to present the certified quota faced reduction of sawar rank, suspension of jagir revenues, or demotion. Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan, the system was tightened: the sawar rank was often set lower than zat (e.g., zat 5000, sawar 3000), and the mashrut (conditional) rank required the mansabdar to maintain extra troopers for a limited period.

Aurangzeb's Deccan wars (1681‑1707) strained the system. The distance from northern muster stations, the humidity damaging brands, and the proliferation of bargir (state‑supplied horse) troopers who lacked personal mounts led to widespread evasion. The waqai navis (news writers) reported that many Deccan mansabdars presented "paper contingents" — chehra entries for non‑existent men. The empire responded by decentralising muster to provincial faujdars and accepting cash commutation (tankhwah) for a portion of the sawar obligation, effectively converting military service into a tax. By the 1720s, the dagh‑o‑chehra had ceased to function as a universal verification tool; regional successor states (Hyderabad, Awadh, Bengal) retained the terminology but substituted their own inspection regimes. The system's decline mirrors the transition from a centrally inspected imperial army to a confederation of provincial military‑fiscal entities.

Examiner's Trap: Conflating Dagh‑o‑Chehra with the Ain‑i‑Dahsala
A question asks: "The Dagh‑o‑Chehra system was introduced by Akbar as part of the Ain‑i‑Dahsala revenue settlement." Identify the error and explain the correct relationship.
The statement is false. The Ain‑i‑Dahsala (1580) was a land revenue system — the ten‑year average yield settlement supervised by Todar Mal — fixing cash revenue demand per bigha. The Dagh‑o‑Chehra was a military inspection system for mansabdar contingents, introduced earlier (c. 1574) by Mir Bakhshi Shahbaz Khan. They operate in different domains: revenue vs. military personnel verification. The link is indirect: revenue from jagirs assigned via the Dahsala data funded the mansabdars whose troops Dagh‑o‑Chehra verified. In exams, never conflate the two; they belong to separate administrative pillars (Diwan vs. Mir Bakhshi).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question pairs "Dagh‑o‑Chehra" with "revenue" or "Todar Mal", flag it as a distractor. The correct association is: Mir Bakhshi → military muster → branding horses → descriptive rolls. The revenue counterpart is the jama‑dami (assessed revenue) of the jagir assigned to that mansabdar. Memorise the pair: Dagh‑o‑Chehra = Mir Bakhshi = Military; Ain‑i‑Dahsala = Diwan‑i‑Wazarat = Revenue.

The Maratha Peshwa Secretariat: The Huzur Daftar, the Chitnis, and the Evolution of the Confederal Bureaucracy

After Shivaji's death (1680) and the Mughal‑Maratha wars, the Maratha polity shifted from a centralised kingdom under the Chhatrapati to a confederal structure where the Peshwa (hereditary prime minister from 1713) headed a secretariat at Pune that managed the fiscal‑military claims of the great houses — Scindia, Holkar, Bhonsle, Gaekwad — and the Konkan coastal administration. The Huzur Daftar (Supreme Office) was the nerve centre, organised into specialized departments (karkhanas) each headed by a minister of the Ashta Pradhan but staffed by hereditary Brahmin clerks (karkuns) who maintained continuity across regimes.

The Chitnis (Correspondence Secretary) drafted and authenticated all royal orders (hukumnamah, parwanah), maintained the roznamcha (daily register), and controlled the seal (mohar) of the Peshwa. The Phadnis (Finance Secretary) audited the kamavisdar accounts from the sarkars, consolidated the chauth and sardeshmukhi demands, and prepared the annual jamabandi (revenue settlement). The Dabir (Foreign Secretary) managed treaties with the Nizam, the Mughals, the Portuguese, and the English, preserving the dastur‑ul‑amal (protocol records). The Potnis (Treasurer) supervised the toshakhana (treasury), the mint at Pune, and the saranjam grant registers. The Surnis (Comptroller) cross‑checked expenditure against sanctions.

Crucially, the secretariat did not command the armies of the great houses directly. Instead, it administered the saranjam grants — revenue assignments in lieu of military service — that legally bound each house to furnish a stipulated contingent. The Huzur Daftar maintained the saranjam patra (grant deeds) and the bakhar (service records) of each house. When a house defaulted (e.g., Scindia's failure to remit chauth from Malwa in the 1750s), the Peshwa's recourse was judicial: the Nyayadhish (Chief Justice) could authorize sequestration of the house's watans (hereditary rights) in the Deccan heartland. This fiscal‑legal leverage, rather than direct command, held the confederacy together until the Third Battle of Panipat (1761) shattered the Peshwa's prestige and the great houses converted saranjam into de facto sovereignty. The British later co‑opted the Huzur Daftar records (seized 1818) to reconstruct Deccan land tenures for the Ryotwari settlement.

Vijayanagara's Aravidu Restoration: The Penukonda‑Chandragiri Phase and the Reconfiguration of Nayaka Loyalty

The Battle of Talikota (1565) destroyed the Vijayanagara capital but not the imperial institution. The Aravidu dynasty (Tirumala, r. 1565‑1572; Sri Ranga I, r. 1572‑1586; Venkata II, r. 1586‑1614) re‑established the court first at Penukonda (1565‑1592) and then at Chandragiri (1592‑1646), ruling a truncated territory comprising the Tamil plains, the southern Andhra coast, and the Mysore plateau. The restoration required renegotiating the Nayankara system: the great Nayakas of Madurai, Tanjavur, Gingee, Ikkeri, and Mysore had effectively become independent during the interregnum. The Aravidu emperors adopted a dual strategy — ritual sovereignty and fiscal pragmatism.

Ritually, the emperors performed the Mahamastakabhisheka at Srirangam and the Vijayadashami durbar at Chandragiri, distributing tirtha (holy water) and prasada to Nayaka envoys, thereby reaffirming the Chakravartin ideal. Fiscally, they accepted peshkash (tribute) in lieu of the earlier kandachara (military obligation). The Rayasam (royal secretariat) issued kavala (protective) grants to temples in Nayaka territories, embedding imperial legitimacy in local ritual economies. Venkata II's reign (1586‑1614) marks the high point: he extracted regular peshkash from Madurai and Tanjavur, intervened in Nayaka succession disputes (e.g., installing a claimant at Ikkeri in 1598), and patronised the Telugu literary renaissance (the Ashtadiggajas at his court).

The system unravelled after Venkata II's death. The succession crisis (1614‑1617) between rival princes allowed the Nayakas to withhold peshkash. The Bijapur Sultanate's advance under Muhammad Adil Shah (1627‑1656) captured the northern forts (Gooty, Adoni), severing the Chandragiri court from the Deccan revenue base. The last emperor, Sri Ranga III (r. 1642‑1646/1678), retreated to Vellore and then to Mysore under Wodeyar protection. The Aravidu phase demonstrates that Vijayanagara's imperial authority was always a negotiated hegemony: when the centre could not guarantee military protection or redistribute Deccan plunder, the peripheral Nayakas reverted to autonomous statehood. The English East India Company's 1639 grant of Madraspatnam from the Chandragiri emperor (Damarla Venkatadri Nayaka acting as imperial agent) illustrates how the fading centre could still legitimise new commercial powers.

Mughal Revenue Assessment Methods: Zabt, Kankut, Ghalla‑Bakhsh, and Nasaq in Regional Practice

The Mughal land revenue system is often reduced to Todar Mal's zabt (measurement‑based cash assessment), but the Ain‑i‑Akbari and provincial records show four coexisting methods, each suited to specific agro‑ecological and political conditions. Zabt (also dahsala) required cadastral survey (paimaish) using the Ilahi gaz (33 inches), classification of land into polaj (annually cultivated), parauti (fallow), chachar (3‑4 years fallow), banjar (long fallow), and fixation of cash rates (dastur‑ul‑amal) per bigha based on a ten‑year yield average. It prevailed in the core khalisa and jagir lands of the Gangetic plain, Malwa, and Gujarat where peasant cultivation was dense and the state could deploy amils and qanungos for annual measurement.

Kankut (grain‑estimate) dispensed with linear measurement. The amil estimated the standing crop by sampling fields (kankut = kan grain + kut estimate), applied a standard yield‑to‑revenue ratio (typically ⅓ to ½ of gross produce), and fixed a lump‑sum demand on the village. It was used in the Subah of Kabul, Kashmir, and the hilly parganas of Bengal where measurement was impractical. Ghalla‑Bakhsh (crop‑sharing) divided the harvested heap at the threshing floor (khala) in a fixed proportion (usually 1:2 or 1:3 state:cultivator). It required minimal bureaucracy but high supervision at harvest; it dominated in Sindh, Multan, and the Deccan sarkars where the state lacked a permanent amil presence. Nasaq (rough assessment) was a negotiated lump‑sum based on past payments, current cultivation, and the village headman's (muqaddam) guarantee. It functioned as a pragmatic compromise in recently conquered or disturbed territories (e.g., Berar after 1596, the Deccan after 1687).

The choice of method was not static. A pargana could shift from ghalla‑bakhsh to zabt once the paimaish was completed (as in Akbar's Gujarat sarkars c. 1585). Conversely, Aurangzeb's Deccan wars forced a reversion to nasaq and kankut in the subahs of Aurangabad and Bidar because the amil corps was diverted to campaign logistics. The zabt regime's insistence on cash payment also drove monetisation: peasants sold surplus grain to banjaras or at mandis to meet the demand, integrating the village into the imperial monetary circuit. The coexistence of these methods refutes the notion of a uniform Mughal revenue system; it was a toolkit deployed by the Diwan according to the state's administrative reach and the region's agrarian structure.

  • Confusing Muhammad bin Tughluq's token currency (brass/copper tanka) with the later Mughal copper dam standardisation; the former was a fiduciary experiment, the latter a metallic standard.
  • Attributing the dagh‑o‑chehra system to Sher Shah Suri; he introduced the dagh for horses but the combined dagh‑o‑chehra with descriptive rolls is a Mughal institutionalisation under Akbar's Mir Bakhshi.
  • Assuming the Peshwa's Huzur Daftar exercised direct military command over Scindia/Holkar contingents; it managed saranjam grants and fiscal accounts, not operational command.
  • Treating the Aravidu restoration as a full recovery of Vijayanagara territory; it was a ritual‑fiscal suzerainty over autonomous Nayakas, not direct rule.
  • Equating zabt with the entire Mughal revenue system; kankut, ghalla‑bakhsh, and nasaq covered vast territories where measurement was absent.
  • Believing the Diwan‑i‑Kohi succeeded in expanding cultivation; it collapsed in the 1334‑1342 famine and was abandoned by 1345.
  • Overlooking that the dagh‑o‑chehra declined because the Deccan wars made annual muster at northern stations impossible, not because of corruption alone.

Mughal Imperial Road Network and the Sarai System

The Mughal state inherited and expanded the Grand Trunk Road (Uttarapath) that Sher Shah Suri had rebuilt between 1540 and 1545, extending it from Sonargaon in Bengal to Kabul. Under Akbar the route was re‑surveyed, milestones (kos minars) were erected at every kos (approximately 3.2 km), and a permanent corps of road‑keepers (rahbar) was placed under the faujdar of each sarkar. The imperial highway linked the capital cities of Agra, Delhi, Lahore and the provincial capitals of Ajmer, Allahabad, Patna and Dhaka, allowing the rapid movement of the mansabdari cavalry, artillery trains and the imperial postal relays (barid).

Complementing the trunk road, a network of secondary routes connected the major qasbas and port towns such as Surat, Cambay, Hughli and Masulipatam. Every 10–15 kos a fortified sarai was constructed: a walled courtyard with vaulted rooms for travellers, a mosque, a well, stables and a granary. The sarai was administered by a sarai‑dar appointed by the subahdar; its maintenance was funded from the local khalisa revenue and from tolls (rahdari) levied on merchants and caravans. The Ain‑i‑Akbari lists 1,700 sarais in the empire, of which the largest — at Sarai Rohilla near Delhi and Sarai Amanat Khan near Lahore — could accommodate over 500 horses and 1,000 foot‑travellers.

The sarai system served three strategic purposes. First, it provided secure overnight halts for the banjara grain caravans that fed the Deccan armies, reducing losses to bandits and monsoon floods. Second, it functioned as an intelligence node: the waqai‑navis posted at each sarai reported on traffic, prices and any unusual movement of troops. Third, the toll revenue (rahdari) was a direct fiscal stream to the imperial treasury, distinct from land revenue, and was collected in cash, encouraging monetisation of the rural economy. The system began to deteriorate after the 1680s when the Deccan wars diverted faujdars and the Maratha light cavalry disrupted the regular patrol of the kos minars; by the early eighteenth century many sarais fell into ruin or were repurposed as local forts.

Mughal Imperial Artillery Organization and Gunpowder Technology

Mughal artillery (topkhana) was a distinct branch of the imperial army, headed by the Mir Atish (master of ordnance) who reported directly to the emperor. Akbar established permanent foundries at Agra, Lahore and Delhi, employing Persian, Ottoman and European (mainly Portuguese and Dutch) gun‑founders. The standard field piece was the bronze “zamzama” (≈ 12‑pounder) cast in a single piece, while siege trains included heavier “farangi” guns of 24‑ to 48‑pounder calibre, often named after their makers (e.g., “Fath‑i‑Jang” cast by the Portuguese gun‑founder João de Sousa at Agra in 1595). Gunpowder was produced in state‑run karkhanas using saltpetre sourced from the Bihar‑Bengal delta, sulphur imported from the Persian Gulf and charcoal from the central Indian forests; the mixture followed the 75:15:10 ratio recorded in the Ain‑i‑Akbari.

Artillery units were organised into batteries (topkhana‑i‑lashkar) of 10–15 guns, each served by a crew of 30–40 gunners (topchis) drawn from the hereditary “topchi” caste and supplemented by European mercenaries. The Mir Atish maintained a register (daftar‑i‑topkhana) detailing calibre, range, powder charge and carriage type for every piece. During the siege of Chittor (1567‑68) Akbar deployed 200 guns, including the massive “Zafar‑Bakhsh” (≈ 60‑pounder) which breached the fort’s western wall after a 58‑day bombardment. Under Aurangzeb the Deccan campaigns saw the first extensive use of mobile field artillery on wheeled carriages (arabas) drawn by oxen, allowing the army to keep pace with the marching infantry.

European technical influence grew after 1615 when the English East India Company supplied cast‑iron cannon to the Mughal court; however, the Mughals preferred bronze for its resistance to corrosion in the humid Deccan climate. The 1687 siege of Golconda featured a combined Mughal‑European battery of 300 guns, including Dutch “kruithoorn” mortars that fired explosive shells — an innovation recorded in the Dutch factor’s diary. By the early eighteenth century the imperial foundries declined; the loss of saltpetre sources to the Marathas and the disruption of European supply lines forced the later emperors to rely on captured or obsolete pieces, contributing to the reduced effectiveness of Mughal siege warfare in the 1700s.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which Mughal emperor institutionalised the Mir Atish as a separate office and established permanent gun foundries at Agra, Lahore and Delhi?” Many candidates answer “Aurangzeb” because of his Deccan artillery campaigns.
The correct answer is Akbar. He created the post of Mir Atish (first held by Khwaja Abdul Samad) and set up the three foundries in the 1570s, as documented in the Ain‑i‑Akbari (Vol. II, “Topkhana”). Aurangzeb expanded the artillery train but did not create the office or the foundries.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links a specific institution (e.g., Mir Atish, sarai‑dar, topkhana) to an emperor, first recall the administrative reforms of Akbar (1560‑1605). Most new offices and systematic infrastructure projects originate in his reign; later rulers usually expand or modify them. Use this heuristic to eliminate later‑emperor options quickly.

Mughal Imperial Naval Organization and Port Administration

Although the Mughal Empire was primarily a land power, it maintained a riverine and coastal naval establishment (bahriye) to protect trade, suppress piracy and project authority along the Indus, Ganges, Brahmaputra and the western seaboard. The naval department was headed by the Mir Bahr (admiral), a mansabdar of 2,000‑5,000 zat, who commanded a fleet of ghurabs (large oared gun‑boats), gallivats (smaller patrol craft) and armed merchantmen requisitioned during emergencies. The principal naval bases were at Lahori Bandar (Indus delta), Hughli (Bengal), Surat and Cambay (Gujarat). Each base had a dockyard (bunder‑karkhana) overseen by a mutasaddi‑i‑bunder who managed timber procurement from the Sundarbans and the Malabar coast, rope‑making, and the casting of naval guns (usually 6‑ to 12‑pounder bronze pieces).

Port administration was integrated with the customs system (mahsul). The mutasaddi collected the 2.5 % ad valorem duty (sair‑jihat) on imports and exports, issued passes (parwana) for ships, and maintained the register of vessels (daftar‑i‑jahaz). Surat, the empire’s busiest port, handled an estimated 3,000 ships annually in the 1660s; its customs revenue alone contributed roughly 12 % of the imperial treasury’s cash income. The Mughal navy cooperated with the Portuguese at Daman and the English at Surat to patrol the Gulf of Cambay against Omani and Malabari corsairs. In 1662 a joint Mughal‑English squadron destroyed a pirate fleet off the coast of Kathiawar, an operation recorded in the English factory diary.

Riverine flotillas on the Ganges and Brahmaputra were crucial for the Bengal campaigns. During the 1666 conquest of Chittagong, the Mughal river fleet of 200 ghurabs transported 15,000 troops and artillery upstream, while the naval guns provided covering fire against Arakanese fortifications. The Brahmaputra fleet, based at Dhaka, maintained a permanent presence to secure the Assam frontier and to escort the annual revenue convoy (mal‑guzari) from the northeastern parganas. By the 1690s the naval budget was cut as Aurangzeb redirected funds to the Deccan; the fleet at Hughli fell into disrepair, and the Maratha naval commander Kanhoji Angre began to dominate the Konkan coast, effectively ending Mughal maritime influence south of Bombay.

Mughal Imperial Scientific Patronage: Astronomy, Mathematics, and Medicine

Mughal courts from Humayun to Muhammad Shah patronised a wide range of scientific activities, often blending Persian, Sanskrit and European knowledge. Humayun brought the astronomer‑mathematician Mulla Chand from Herat and commissioned an observatory at Dinpanah (Delhi) equipped with a large mural quadrant. Akbar expanded this tradition: he invited the Jesuit missionaries Rodolfo Acquaviva and Jerome Xavier, who introduced the astrolabe and the European planetary tables of Clavius. Akbar’s court historian Abu’l‑Fazl records the translation of the Sanskrit astronomical text “Surya‑Siddhanta” into Persian under the supervision of Fathullah Shirazi, a polymath who also designed the “Yantra‑Raja” — a composite instrument combining the astrolabe, sine quadrant and compass.

Jahangir continued astronomical observations, notably the 1618 comet recorded in the Tuzuk‑i‑Jahangiri, and maintained a team of court astronomers (munajjimin) who prepared the annual “Zij‑i‑Jahangiri” ephemeris used for fixing the dates of festivals and revenue assessments. Under Shah Jahan the mathematician‑engineer Ustad Ahmad Lahori applied geometric principles to the design of the Taj Mahal’s perfect symmetry, while the physician Hakim Ali Gilani compiled the “Makhzan‑al‑Adwiya”, a pharmacopoeia integrating Unani, Ayurvedic and European materia medica. The imperial hospital (dar al‑shifa) at Delhi, founded by Akbar and expanded by Shah Jahan, employed over 100 physicians, surgeons and compounders; its records show the use of opium, mercury compounds and the newly introduced cinchona bark for fevers.

European scientific exchange intensified after the 1615 treaty with the English East India Company. The Company’s surgeon‑general John Fryer supplied the court with a copy of William Harvey’s “De Motu Cordis” (1628), which influenced Mughal anatomical studies. The Dutch factor at Surat, Joan van der Veen, presented a telescope (Dutch “kijker”) to Prince Dara Shikoh in 1650; Dara’s library at Delhi contained Arabic translations of Euclid, Ptolemy and Copernicus. However, after the 1680s the patronage declined: the observatory at Delhi fell into disuse, the translation bureau (Maktab Khana) was closed, and the medical college at Agra lost its endowment. The last major scientific project was the Jantar Mantar observatories built by the Rajput ruler Jai Singh II (a Mughal vassal) in the 1720s, which were technically Mughal‑sponsored but executed under a regional patron.

Mughal Imperial Eunuch Corps and Household Administration

The imperial household (khilwat) relied on a highly organised corps of eunuchs (khwajasara) who controlled access to the harem, managed the emperor’s private treasury (khazana‑i‑khas), and acted as confidential agents in political affairs. Recruitment drew mainly from Central Asian slave markets (Bukhara, Khiva) and from Bengal, where castration was performed in specialised workshops (khana‑i‑khwajasara) under the supervision of the chief eunuch (nazir‑i‑khwajasara). The corps was hierarchically graded: the highest rank, “Khwaja‑Sara‑i‑Khas”, held a mansab of 2,000 zat and commanded the “Khwaja‑Sara‑i‑Am” (the general eunuch guard) of 500‑1,000 men.

Eunuchs administered the imperial wardrobe (toshakhana), the jewel office (jauhar‑khana), and the kitchen (matbakh). They also supervised the “zenana” schools where princesses and noblewomen received instruction in Persian, calligraphy and music. Politically, senior eunuchs served as intermediaries between the emperor and the nobility; Itimad Khan (d. 1626), a favourite of Jahangir, negotiated the 1615 treaty with the English and controlled the distribution of mansabs to Deccan officers. Under Shah Jahan, the eunuch Khwaja‑Sara‑i‑Khas, known as “Mir‑i‑Khwajasara”, directed the construction of the Red Fort’s private chambers and managed the secret correspondence (khufia‑dastak) with provincial governors.

The corps’ influence waned after Aurangzeb’s death. The war of succession (1707) saw rival princes bribing eunuch commanders, leading to factionalism. By the reign of Muhammad Shah (1719‑48) the nazir‑i‑khwajasara had lost control over the khazana‑i‑khas to the wazir, and the eunuch guard was reduced to ceremonial duties. The last recorded powerful eunuch, Roshan‑ud‑Daula (d. 1748), attempted to revive the corps but was executed by the Persian invader Nadir Shah. The decline of the eunuch institution mirrors the broader erosion of central household authority in the late Mughal period.

  • Confusing the Mir Atish (artillery chief) with the Mir Bahr (naval admiral); they headed separate departments with distinct mansab scales.
  • Attributing the construction of the Grand Trunk Road solely to the Mughals; Sher Shah Suri built the core alignment, the Mughals added kos minars and sarais.
  • Assuming the Mughal navy operated ocean‑going ships comparable to European vessels; it relied on riverine ghurabs and coastal gallivats, not deep‑sea carracks.
  • Believing the imperial observatory at Delhi functioned continuously from Akbar to Muhammad Shah; it was abandoned after the 1680s and later revived only by Jai Singh II.
  • Treating the eunuch corps as a purely domestic service; senior eunuchs held mansabs, commanded troops and conducted diplomacy.
  • Overlooking the role of European gun‑founders (Portuguese, Dutch, English) in Mughal artillery; the foundries were joint enterprises, not exclusively indigenous.
  • Equating the sarai‑dar’s toll revenue with land revenue; rahdari was a separate cash stream collected at each sarai and reported directly to the diwan.

The advanced sections above integrate administrative, military, scientific and household mechanisms that are frequently tested in comparative and cause‑effect questions.

Mughal Provincial Revenue Officials: The Amil, Qanungo, and Patwari in the Zabt Framework

The zabt system, codified under Akbar’s finance minister Todar Mal, relied on a three‑tier revenue bureaucracy that operated from the provincial capital down to the village. At the apex of the pargana stood the amil (also called amalguzar), a centrally appointed officer who held a mansab and was responsible for the assessment, collection, and remission of land revenue. The amil received the measured area from the surveyors, applied the prescribed cash rates (dastur), and forwarded the demand (jama) to the diwan of the subah. His accounts were audited annually by the diwan‑i‑subah and discrepancies could lead to dismissal or reassignment.

Supporting the amil was the qanungo, a hereditary record‑keeper who maintained the qanungoi registers of land holdings, crop patterns, and revenue assessments. The qanungo verified the measurements made by the paimaish (survey) parties, certified the classification of soil (polaj, parauti, chachar, banjar), and attested to the correctness of the amil’s demand. Because the post was often hereditary, qanungos accumulated local knowledge but also became a source of corruption; Akbar attempted to curb this by rotating qanungos across parganas and by instituting the daftar‑i‑qanungo at the provincial level for cross‑checking.

At the village level the patwari (or patel in some regions) acted as the primary accountant. He recorded each cultivator’s holding, the nature of tenure (khudkasht, muzarian, upri), the crops sown, and the revenue due. The patwari’s bahi (ledger) formed the evidentiary base for the amil’s demand and for any remission petitions. Patwaris were remunerated through a small share of the collection (usually 1–2 percent) and through exemptions on their own lands. Their records were periodically inspected by the qanungo and by the amil’s own clerks (karkuns) to prevent under‑reporting.

The interaction of these three officials created a feedback loop: the patwari’s field data fed the qanungo’s registers, which the amil used to compute the jama. Any break in the chain — such as a patwari’s collusion with dominant cultivators (zamindars) or a qanungo’s falsification of soil classification — distorted the revenue yield and provoked peasant unrest. The Mughal state therefore invested in periodic paimaish campaigns, the standardization of the Ilahi gaz for linear measurement, and the introduction of the bigha as a uniform area unit to reduce discretion.

Maratha Military Organization under the Peshwas: The Sarnaubat, Cavalry Divisions, and Infantry Regiments

Under the Peshwas the Maratha army evolved from a loose confederacy of sardars into a more structured force with a standing core and a levy system. The supreme commander was the Sarnaubat (also styled Senapati), a post held by the Peshwa himself or by a trusted lieutenant such as Sadashivrao Bhau. The Sarnaubat exercised strategic command, allocated contingents to campaigns, and coordinated the Huzur Daftar (central military secretariat) which maintained muster rolls, pay records, and artillery inventories.

The cavalry remained the backbone and was divided into two categories: bargirs, state‑paid troopers whose horses and equipment were supplied by the treasury, and silahdars, nobles who furnished their own mounts and arms in return for a share of the plunder (chauth) and a hereditary saranjam grant. A typical bargir regiment numbered 500–1,000 horsemen, organized into tukris of 25–50 men under a havaldar. Silahdar contingents varied widely; a major sardar like Holkar or Scindia could field 5,000–10,000 silahdars, each maintaining a personal retinue.

Infantry was organized into pike‑and‑musket regiments known as tufangchis (matchlock men) and barchas (pikemen). Recruitment drew from Arab, Afghan, and Deccani peasant communities. A standard infantry battalion comprised 800–1,200 men, commanded by a subedar with a jamadar as second‑in‑command.
