The Peshwa’s artillery, the Topkhana, was headed by a Mir Atish and included heavy bronze pieces (e.g., the 24‑pounder Zamzama) cast at the Pune foundry under European supervision, as well as lighter field guns (shaturnal) and mortars (gajnal). Artillery crews were organized into topchis (gunners) and bandukchis (matchlock men for close defence).

Logistics were managed by a dedicated Banjara corps that transported grain, fodder, and ammunition on bullock carts and pack animals. The Maratha intelligence wing, the harkara network, supplied real‑time information on enemy movements, enabling the cavalry’s famed ganimi kava (guerrilla raids). The combination of a professional cavalry core, disciplined infantry, and an expanding artillery park allowed the Peshwas to contest Mughal, Nizam, and British forces on relatively equal terms until the Third Battle of Panipat (1761) exposed the limits of decentralized command.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which Maratha officer commanded the state‑paid cavalry (bargirs) and was directly accountable to the Peshwa’s central treasury?” Many candidates answer “Sarnaubat” or “Senapati”. The correct answer is the havaldar of each bargir tukri, because the Sarnaubat was the overall strategist, not the immediate paymaster of individual regiments.
The bargir system placed fiscal responsibility at the regimental level. Each tukri of 25–50 bargirs was led by a havaldar who drew the troopers’ salaries from the Huzur Daftar and reported muster strength monthly. The Sarnaubat allocated campaigns but did not handle day‑to‑day payroll. Therefore, the officer directly accountable to the treasury for bargir pay was the havaldar.
Answer‑writing strategy
When a question distinguishes between strategic command and fiscal administration, identify the tier at which money flows. In Maratha military structure, the Sarnaubat = strategy; the havaldar = payroll; the silahdar = self‑financed. Map the hierarchy before selecting the answer.

Deccan Sultanates’ Diplomatic Correspondence with the Ottoman and Safavid Courts: Protocols, Gifts, and Strategic Alliances

The five Deccan sultanates — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — maintained regular diplomatic channels with the two great Muslim empires of the early modern period: the Ottoman Empire centred on Istanbul and the Safavid Empire based in Isfahan. Correspondence was conducted in Persian, the lingua franca of the Islamic chancery, and followed a formal protocol: a royal farman bearing the sultan’s seal (muhr) was dispatched with a qasid (envoy) who travelled in a protected caravan, often accompanied by merchants to defray costs. The letters opened with the basmala and the sovereign’s titles, proceeded to the purpose — request for military technicians, recognition of sovereignty, or mediation in inter‑Deccan disputes — and closed with a list of gifts.

Gifts functioned as both diplomatic currency and status markers. Bijapur’s Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580–1627) sent Persian manuscripts, Deccani textiles, and prized Arabian horses to Sultan Murad III (r. 1574–1595) and later to Ahmed I, while requesting Ottoman cannon founders to upgrade his artillery. Golconda’s Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (r. 1580–1611) dispatched envoys to Shah Abbas I (r. 1588–1629) bearing Golconda diamonds, rose‑water, and copies of the Quran illuminated in the Deccani style, seeking Safavid acknowledgment of his Shi’a affiliation and occasional military aid against the Mughals. The Safavids, in turn, sent Persian poets, calligraphers, and Shi’a theologians who influenced Deccani court culture.

Strategic alliances were fragile. The Ottomans, preoccupied with the Habsburgs and the Mediterranean, rarely committed troops but supplied gun‑founders and military manuals (e.g., the Tufang‑nama) that were translated at the Bijapur court. The Safavids, engaged in Ottoman‑Safavid wars, could spare only limited detachments; nevertheless, Safavid advisers helped reorganise Golconda’s topkhana and introduced the zamzama casting technique. Deccani rulers also used these ties to legitimise their rule domestically: the adoption of the title Padshah and the inclusion of Ottoman‑style tughra in royal seals signalled parity with the great empires.

The correspondence declined after the Mughal annexation of Ahmadnagar (1636) and Bijapur (1686), when the Deccan sultanates lost independent foreign policy capacity. Surviving archives in the Topkapi Palace (Istanbul) and the Golestan Palace (Tehran) preserve dozens of farmans, gift registers, and envoy itineraries, providing historians with a rare window into early modern Islamic diplomacy beyond the Mughal sphere.

Vijayanagara Urban Planning: The Layout of Hampi, Water Management, and Market Quarters

The capital city of Vijayanagara, known as Hampi, exemplifies a planned imperial metropolis that integrated sacred geography, royal ceremony, and commercial vitality. The city was divided into three principal zones: the Sacred Centre along the Tungabhadra’s northern bank, dominated by the Virupaksha Temple, the Hemakuta hill shrines, and the massive Mahamandapa; the Royal Centre to the south‑west, containing the Lotus Mahal, the Elephant Stables, the Queen’s Bath, and the Mahanavami Dibba (ceremonial platform); and the Urban‑Commercial Core stretching along the main north‑south artery (the “Chariot Street”) flanked by the Sule Bazaar, the Pan Supari Bazaar, and the Vitthala Temple complex with its iconic stone chariot.

Water management was the engineering backbone. A network of tanks (kunds) — notably the Kamalapuram Tank, the Anantapur Tank, and the massive Raya (royal) tank — captured monsoon runoff from the surrounding hills. Stone‑lined canals (kaluves) conveyed water to the royal gardens, the temple precincts, and the residential quarters. The aqueduct crossing the Tungabhadra near the Virupaksha Temple, built of dressed granite blocks, supplied the Sacred Centre year‑round. Inscriptions record the appointment of a jaladhyaksha (water superintendent) responsible for desilting, sluice regulation, and equitable distribution among cultivators and urban households.

Market quarters were laid out on a modular grid. Each bazaar street consisted of uniform shop units (approximately 3 m × 4 m) with a front verandah, a storage cellar, and a rear courtyard for livestock. Guilds (nagaram) regulated weights, measures, and quality standards; the patel of each bazaar collected a daily market toll (sayer) that flowed to the royal treasury. Foreign merchants — Portuguese, Arab, and Chinese — occupied designated quarters near the river port, where customs officials (mahaldar) levied ad valorem duties on pepper, textiles, and horses. Archaeological surveys estimate the intra‑city population at 400,000–500,000 at its zenith (c. 1550), making Hampi one of the largest pre‑modern cities in the world.

Fortification comprised seven concentric walls with massive gateways (gopurams) that doubled as ceremonial arches. The outermost wall enclosed an area of roughly 40 km², incorporating agricultural land, reservoirs, and satellite settlements. The strategic placement of watchtowers and the integration of natural hillocks (Matanga, Malyavanta) into the defensive circuit reflect a synthesis of military engineering and landscape cosmology. The city’s layout thus served simultaneously as a ritual mandala, a hydraulic machine, and a commercial hub — a triad that sustained Vijayanagara’s imperial authority for two centuries.

Mughal Siege Artillery: European Gunfounders, Indigenous Casting, and the Evolution of Heavy Ordnance

The Mughal siege train (topkhana) was a hybrid enterprise that combined European metallurgical expertise with indigenous casting traditions. From the reign of Akbar, Portuguese gunfounders such as Francisco de Almeida and later Dutch and English artisans were employed at the imperial foundries in Agra, Lahore, and Delhi. They introduced the reverberatory furnace for bronze casting, the use of cascabel (rear knob) for elevation control, and the standardization of calibres (e.g., 12‑pounder, 24‑pounder, 48‑pounder). The most famous product of this collaboration was the Zamzama (also called Bhangra), a 24‑pounder bronze cannon cast in 1628 at the Lahore foundry under the supervision of the Dutch master Jan van der Veen; it later saw action at the Battle of Panipat (1761) under Afghan command.

Indigenous Mughal artisans, organized into the ustad‑i‑topkhana guild, mastered the lost‑wax method for bronze and the sand‑casting technique for iron. They produced the Fathullah cannon used by Akbar at the Siege of Chittor (1567), a 30‑pounder that could breach stone walls at 300 m. The Mughal artillery corps was headed by the Mir Atish, a mansabdar of 5,000 zat, assisted by a Darogha‑i‑Topkhana (storekeeper) and a Ustad‑i‑Topkhana (chief gunner). Each heavy piece required a crew of 12–15 gunners (topchis), a team of 8–10 elephants for transport, and a purpose‑built wooden carriage with iron brackets.

Siege doctrine emphasized battering breaches followed by escalade. At the Siege of Golconda (1687), Aurangzeb deployed over 100 heavy guns, including 48‑pounders, arranged in batteries protected by gabions and fascines. The artillery barrage lasted 40 days, firing an estimated 15,000 rounds, before the final assault. Logistics were managed by the Banjara corps, which moved powder (stored in sealed copper barrels), shot (stone and later iron), and spare carriages along the Grand Trunk Road and dedicated military roads (rah‑dari) built for the Deccan campaigns.

After the 1680s the quality of Mughal ordnance declined. The death of experienced European masters, the loss of access to high‑grade copper from the Khetri mines, and the fiscal strain of the Deccan wars reduced the foundries to producing lighter field pieces (shaturnal) and mortars (gajnal). By the early eighteenth century the once‑formidable siege train could no longer sustain prolonged bombardments, a factor that contributed to the Mughal inability to reduce Maratha hill forts such as Raigad and Pratapgad. The legacy of the Mughal‑European artillery partnership, however, survived in the successor states — Hyderabad, Mysore, and the Maratha confederacy — which continued to employ European gunfounders well into the 1790s.

  • Confusing the amil (pargana revenue collector) with the qanungo (hereditary record‑keeper); they operated at different levels and had distinct accountability.
  • Attributing the command of bargir cavalry to the Sarnaubat instead of the regimental havaldar who handled payroll and muster.
  • Assuming Deccan sultanates received regular Ottoman troop contingents; in reality only technical experts and manuals were exchanged.
  • Overstating the grid regularity of Hampi’s Sacred Centre; the religious zone grew organically around temples, while the Royal Centre followed a planned layout.
  • Equating the Mughal Mir Atish with the Mir Bahr (naval admiral); they headed separate departments with different mansab scales.
  • Believing the Zamzama was a Mughal‑only creation; it was a joint Mughal‑Dutch casting later captured by Afghan forces.
  • Neglecting the role of the Banjara logistics corps in moving heavy artillery; without them the siege train could not operate beyond the capital region.

These advanced sections equip aspirants with the mechanistic detail and comparative nuance that high‑scoring answers demand.

The Mughal Imperial Library (Kitabkhana) and Manuscript Culture: Patronage, Cataloguing, and Circulation

The Mughal imperial library, known as the Kitabkhana, functioned as the central repository for manuscripts produced, collected, and translated under imperial patronage from Akbar’s reign onward. Unlike the more informal collections of earlier Sultanate courts, the Kitabkhana was administered by a dedicated Darogha‑i‑Kitabkhana who reported directly to the Mir Bakhshi and, in later periods, to the Wazir. The library housed works in Persian, Arabic, Turkish, Sanskrit, and regional languages such as Braj Bhasha and Dakhni, reflecting the empire’s multilingual character. Catalogues were maintained in Persian using a systematic classification by subject (e.g., ulum‑i‑hikmat for philosophy, tarikh for history, adab for literature) and by script, enabling scholars to locate texts efficiently.

Akbar’s establishment of the Maktab Khana (translation bureau) in 1574 institutionalised the rendering of Sanskrit classics — Mahabharata, Ramayana, Atharvaveda — into Persian. The bureau employed a team of munshis (secretaries), pandits, and Persian‑speaking scholars who worked collaboratively; the Razmnama (Persian Mahabharata) and the Tarikh‑i‑Alfi are the most celebrated products. Jahangir continued the practice, commissioning the Jahangirnama illustrations and the Persian translation of the Yogavasistha. Shah Jahan expanded the library’s physical space in the Agra Fort and later in the Red Fort, adding a dedicated khana‑i‑naqsh for illuminated manuscripts. The imperial atelier (karkhana‑i‑musawwir) supplied miniatures, while the kitabdar (bookbinder) ensured durable bindings using leather, lacquer, and gold tooling.

Circulation of manuscripts extended beyond the court. Copies were gifted to provincial governors, Sufi khanqahs, and Hindu mathas, creating a network of textual exchange that reinforced imperial legitimacy. The farman granting a manuscript often carried a seal (muhr) and a colophon noting the scribe, date, and patron, which modern scholars use for provenance. The library also served as a reference for the Ain‑i‑Akbari and the Tuzuk‑i‑Jahangiri, both of which cite specific volumes held in the Kitabkhana. By Aurangzeb’s reign, the library’s growth slowed as resources were diverted to Deccan campaigns, but the catalogue survived and was later consulted by the British East India Company’s Orientalists in the late eighteenth century.

The Evolution of the Mughal Revenue Survey under Shah Jahan: The Ain‑i‑Dahsala and Regional Adaptations

Shah Jahan’s revenue reforms built upon Akbar’s Dahsala (ten‑year average) system but introduced the Ain‑i‑Dahsala, a more granular survey that combined measured area (bigha) with crop‑specific yield estimates (mahsul). The survey was conducted by a corps of amils assisted by qanungos who recorded village‑level data in the daftar‑i‑mal. Unlike the earlier zabt which relied on a uniform gaz (Ilahi gaz ≈ 33 inches), Shah Jahan permitted regional variations: the bigha in Bengal (≈ 1,600 sq yd) differed from that in Gujarat (≈ 2,500 sq yd) and the Deccan (≈ 3,200 sq yd). This flexibility reduced peasant resistance in newly annexed territories such as the Deccan sultanates, where local measurement customs were respected.

The Ain‑i‑Dahsala also introduced a three‑tier classification of land: polaj (annually cultivated), parauti (fallow for one‑two years), and chachar (fallow longer). Revenue demand was fixed at one‑third of the estimated produce for polaj, one‑fourth for parauti, and one‑fifth for chachar. The state retained the right to reassess every ten years, but in practice the amil often negotiated a qabuliyat (agreement) with the village headman (muqaddam) that locked rates for longer periods, especially in the fertile Gangetic plains.

Regional adaptations are evident in the Subah of Bengal, where the diwan‑i‑Bengal incorporated the zamindari intermediary layer into the survey, recording zamindar claims alongside peasant holdings. In the Deccan, the survey integrated the existing kharaj and peshkash registers of the Bijapur and Golconda administrations, allowing a smoother fiscal transition after the 1636 annexation. The Ain‑i‑Dahsala data fed directly into the imperial khazana through the diwan‑i‑tan, enabling the emperor to monitor revenue trends and adjust military allocations. Surviving daftar‑i‑mal fragments from the 1640s show detailed entries for each pargana, including crop yields (wheat, rice, cotton, indigo) and the number of halak (ploughs), providing historians with a quantitative base for agrarian studies.

Technique
Explain how the Ain‑i‑Dahsala differed from Akbar’s Dahsala in terms of measurement standards and land classification, and why these changes mattered for revenue collection in the Deccan.
The Ain‑i‑Dahsala retained the ten‑year averaging principle but replaced the uniform Ilahi gaz with region‑specific bigha sizes, acknowledging local measurement customs in Bengal, Gujarat, and the Deccan. It also introduced a three‑tier land classification (polaj, parauti, chachar) with graded revenue rates (1/3, 1/4, 1/5 of produce) instead of a flat one‑third demand. In the Deccan, this flexibility allowed the Mughal administration to incorporate pre‑existing Bijapur and Golconda kharaj registers, reducing peasant unrest and facilitating smoother fiscal integration after 1636.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “differences” between two revenue systems, structure the answer in a two‑column table (Feature | Akbar’s Dahsala | Shah Jahan’s Ain‑i‑Dahsala). Highlight measurement units, land categories, and the treatment of regional customs. This visual cue earns easy marks for clarity and completeness.

Deccan Sultanates' Bureaucratic Lexicon: The Dabir, the Munshi, and the Persianate Chancery

The administrative machinery of the Deccan sultanates — Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar — relied on a Persianate chancery whose terminology mirrored but also diverged from the Mughal model. The senior secretary, the Dabir‑i‑Khas (or Dabir‑i‑Mulk), headed the Daftar‑i‑Insha (chancery) and drafted all royal farmans, nishans, and diplomatic correspondence. Below him, the Munshi corps managed day‑to‑day record‑keeping: the Munshi‑i‑Mal for revenue, the Munshi‑i‑Jang for military orders, and the Munshi‑i‑Siyasat for political intelligence. Each munshi maintained a daftar (register) written in a cursive nastaʿliq script, sealed with the sultan’s muhr.

A distinctive feature was the Dabir‑i‑Sadr, a judicial secretary who recorded qazi judgments and issued parwanas for land grants (inam, madad‑i‑maʿash). The Dabir‑i‑Sadr often held a dual role as the chief qazi’s deputy, blurring the line between executive and judicial documentation. In Golconda, the Dabir‑i‑Khas also supervised the Karkhana‑i‑Insha, a workshop producing illuminated manuscripts for diplomatic gifts, linking scribal work to artistic production.

The chancery’s vocabulary incorporated Deccani (Dakhni) terms for local offices: Deshmukh (district chief), Deshpande (accountant), Kulkarni (village accountant). These vernacular designations appeared alongside Persian titles in official documents, reflecting a bilingual administrative culture. The Daftar‑i‑Mal (revenue register) used a hybrid columnar format: Persian headings (jama, kharch) with Deccani marginal notes. This dual‑language practice persisted until the Mughal annexation, after which the Mughal diwan imposed a standardized Persian‑only format, though many Deccani terms survived in local revenue manuals (dastur‑al‑amal) used by amils into the eighteenth century.

Maratha Coastal Defence beyond the Angre Dynasty: Fortified Ports, Privateering, and the Konkan Trade Network

While the Angre family dominates narratives of Maratha naval power, the broader coastal defence system comprised a chain of fortified ports — Suvarnadurg, Vijaydurg, Sindhudurg, Kolaba, and Jaigad — each garrisoned by a killedar (fort commander) supported by a sarnobat (naval captain) and a complement of gallivats (small armed vessels). These forts were strategically placed at river mouths (Savitri, Vashishti, Shastri) to control inland‑sea trade routes and to levy chauth on merchant shipping. The Maratha admiralty issued patents (sanad) to privateers (jang‑i‑bahri) authorising them to capture enemy (Portuguese, Siddi, English) vessels in return for a share of the prize (ganimah).

The Konkan trade network thrived on the export of rice, coconut, timber, and pepper, and the import of horses, Persian Gulf textiles, and European firearms. Maratha customs houses (chowkis) at each port collected a 2.5 % ad valorem duty (mahsul) and a fixed portage fee per ton. Revenue from these dues funded the maintenance of the fleet and the construction of new fortifications, such as the 1664 expansion of Sindhudurg under Shivaji’s direct supervision. The Maratha navy also coordinated with the Deshmukh of the coastal parganas to mobilise kolis (fisher‑warriors) for amphibious operations, notably the 1679 raid on the Portuguese factory at Bassein.

After the decline of the Angre line in the 1730s, the Peshwa’s administration reorganised the naval establishment under the Sarnobat‑i‑Bahri, a centrally appointed admiral who reported to the Peshwa via the Huzur Daftar. The new structure introduced standardized ship‑building specifications at the Dockyard of Ratnagiri, where teak from the Western Ghats was seasoned and assembled into 30‑gun gurabs. Despite these reforms, the Maratha navy never achieved blue‑water capability; its strength remained coastal defence and commerce raiding, a factor that limited its ability to contest European naval dominance in the Arabian Sea after the 1750s.

Mughal Imperial Astronomy and Cartography: The Jantar Mantar Observatories and the Atlas of the Empire

Mughal patronage of astronomy culminated in the construction of five Jantar Mantar observatories — Delhi (1724), Jaipur (1734), Ujjain (1735), Varanasi (1737), and Mathura (1738) — commissioned by Sawai Jai Singh II, a Mughal vassal who held the title Mirza Raja. These masonry instruments, notably the Samrat Yantra (giant sundial), the Jai Prakash Yantra (hemispherical dial), and the Rama Yantra (cylindrical altitude measurer), were designed to improve the accuracy of the Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi, the imperial astronomical tables used for calendrical calculations, astrological prognostication, and the timing of military campaigns.

Parallel to observational astronomy, the Mughal court sponsored a cartographic project known as the Atlas‑i‑Hind (c. 1650‑1700). Under the supervision of the Mir‑i‑Bahr (chief geographer) and a team of naqsh‑nawis (map‑makers), the atlas compiled regional maps at a scale of 1 : 2,000,000, integrating data from the Ain‑i‑Akbari revenue surveys, the Barid intelligence reports, and Portuguese coastal charts. The maps depicted subah boundaries, major roads (rah‑dari), river courses, and fortified places, and were annotated in Persian with qalam‑i‑siyah (black ink) for administrative use and qalam‑i‑surkh (red ink) for military routes.

The practical impact of these scientific endeavours is evident in the 1687 Deccan campaign, where Aurangzeb’s artillery commanders used latitude‑derived firing tables from the Zij‑i‑Muhammad Shahi to calibrate mortar trajectories. Similarly, the Atlas‑i‑Hind guided the placement of sarais (caravanserais) along the Grand Trunk Road, ensuring logistical support for the imperial army. After the Mughal decline, the Jaipur observatory continued to function under the Kachwaha rulers, and its instruments were later studied by British surveyors such as William Lambton during the Great Trigonometrical Survey (1802‑1871), illustrating a direct line of technical continuity from Mughal astronomy to colonial geodesy.

  • Confusing the Dabir‑i‑Khas (chief secretary) with the Munshi‑i‑Mal (revenue clerk); they operated at different hierarchical levels.
  • Assuming the Ain‑i‑Dahsala applied a uniform bigha across the empire; regional variations were explicitly permitted.
  • Attributing all Maratha naval activity to the Angre family; the coastal fort chain and privateer system predated and outlasted Angre dominance.
  • Equating the Jantar Mantar instruments with purely ceremonial structures; they produced data used in military artillery tables.
  • Neglecting the bilingual (Persian‑Deccani) nature of Deccan chancery documents; many revenue registers retain Deccani marginalia.
  • Overstating the Mughal atlas’s coverage of the far south; the Atlas‑i‑Hind focused on imperial territories north of the Tungabhadra.
  • Remember that precise terminology and chronological sequencing are essential for high scores.
